

# SECTION: Cardiovascular System (Count: 140 Questions - ADVANCED)


### Question 1
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**
The nurse receives the shift report on a step-down cardiac unit. Which client experiencing a cardiovascular complication should the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with a hypertensive crisis on a nicardipine continuous infusion who reports a mild headache and has a current blood pressure of 168/92 mmHg.
B) A client 48 hours post-myocardial infarction who has suddenly developed bilateral crackles midway up the lung fields and a new S3 gallop.
C) A client with chronic atrial fibrillation receiving a diltiazem infusion who has a heart rate of 95 bpm and a blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg.
D) A client 2 days post-pericardiocentesis for cardiac tamponade who has accumulated 30 mL of serous fluid in the pericardial drain over the last 4 hours.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
<b>Rationale:</b> This client is exhibiting signs of acute pulmonary edema and heart failure, which are hallmarks of cardiogenic shock following a myocardial infarction. The presence of an S3 heart sound and bilateral crackles indicates severe left ventricular pump failure, making this client highly unstable and at immediate risk of respiratory and cardiovascular collapse. Option A is expected; the client's blood pressure is elevated but gradually coming down safely, and a mild headache is a known side effect of nicardipine (a calcium channel blocker). Option C represents a controlled heart rate for atrial fibrillation. Option D describes expected, stable serous drainage after a pericardiocentesis.
</details>

### Question 2
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**
A client is brought to the emergency department unresponsive with a palpated heart rate of 180 bpm. The cardiac monitor displays a wide-complex, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with twisting of the QRS complexes around the isoelectric line. The client has a history of alcohol use disorder. Which laboratory value is the primary driver of this lethal arrhythmia?

A) Serum potassium 3.6 mEq/L
B) Serum calcium 9.0 mg/dL
C) Serum sodium 132 mEq/L
D) Serum magnesium 1.1 mg/dL

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: D</b><br>
<b>Rationale:</b> The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes, a specific and lethal form of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. The twisting of the QRS complexes around the isoelectric line is the defining ECG characteristic. A primary cause of Torsades de Pointes is a prolonged QT interval, which is frequently precipitated by severe hypomagnesemia (normal: 1.5–2.5 mg/dL). Alcohol use disorder is a major risk factor for magnesium depletion. Option A (potassium 3.6 mEq/L) is within the normal range. Option B (calcium 9.0 mg/dL) is normal. Option C (sodium 132 mEq/L) is mildly hyponatremic but does not typically cause Torsades de Pointes. The immediate treatment involves intravenous magnesium sulfate.
</details>

---

### Question 3
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
A client in a hypertensive crisis is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside at 4 mcg/kg/min. Over the last 48 hours, the client's blood pressure has decreased from 230/130 mmHg to 145/88 mmHg. During the current assessment, the client exhibits sudden profound confusion, hyperreflexia, and a mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) of 85% (normal 60-75%). What is the nurse's priority action?

A) Increase the nitroprusside infusion rate to treat impending hypertensive encephalopathy.
B) Administer a rapid intravenous fluid bolus to increase cerebral perfusion.
C) Discontinue the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.
D) Document the findings as an expected physiological response to rapid blood pressure lowering.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
<b>Rationale:</b> Sodium nitroprusside metabolism produces cyanide. Prolonged infusions (typically >48 hours) or high doses can lead to cyanide toxicity. Cyanide blocks cellular respiration, preventing tissues from extracting oxygen from the blood. This results in an artificially elevated mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2), metabolic acidosis, and profound neurological changes (confusion, hyperreflexia, seizures). The priority action is to immediately stop the infusion and administer the antidote, which is sodium thiosulfate (or hydroxocobalamin). Increasing the infusion (Option A) would worsen the toxicity. Options B and D are incorrect because this is a life-threatening toxicological emergency, not a fluid deficit or an expected response.
</details>

---

### Question 4
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**
A client is in the intensive care unit with profound cardiogenic shock following an anterior wall ST-elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI). The client is supported by an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) and continuous norepinephrine infusion. Which clinical finding best indicates that the primary cardiovascular failure has progressed to secondary structural organ failure?

A) Hourly urine output drops to 10 mL/hr with a rising serum creatinine and muddy brown granular casts in the urinalysis.
B) Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) increases to 40 mg/dL while the serum creatinine remains at 1.1 mg/dL.
C) The client complains of severe, sudden flank pain radiating to the groin with gross hematuria.
D) The central venous pressure (CVP) increases from 8 to 14 mm Hg over a two-hour period.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
<b>Rationale:</b> Multi-system organ dysfunction syndrome (MODS) frequently begins with acute kidney injury (AKI) when severe, prolonged cardiogenic shock occurs. Option A describes Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN), a structural intra-renal failure caused by prolonged ischemic injury. The presence of muddy brown granular casts, alongside rising creatinine and oliguria, confirms actual parenchymal damage rather than simple hypoperfusion. Option B describes pre-renal azotemia (a BUN:Cr ratio > 20:1), which is a physiological response to hypoperfusion, not yet structural organ failure. Option C describes nephrolithiasis, unrelated to shock. Option D indicates right-sided heart failure or fluid overload, representing primary cardiovascular strain rather than secondary structural failure of a different organ system.
</details>

### Question 5
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**
A client is 4 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. The nurse notes the mediastinal chest tube drainage has abruptly decreased from 100 mL/hr to 10 mL/hr over the past hour. The client's blood pressure is 112/74 mmHg and heart rate is 98 bpm. Which subtle assessment finding should immediately alert the nurse to the development of cardiac tamponade before overt cardiovascular collapse occurs?

A) An abrupt increase in the systolic blood pressure accompanied by profound bradycardia.
B) Development of a harsh, high-pitched holosystolic murmur audible at the cardiac apex.
C) A drop in systolic blood pressure of 15 mmHg during spontaneous inspiration.
D) Flattening of the jugular veins when the client is placed in a supine position.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
<b>Rationale:</b> The abrupt cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage in a post-CABG client strongly suggests a blockage (clot) in the tube, which can quickly lead to blood accumulating in the pericardial space (cardiac tamponade). Pulsus paradoxus—a drop in systolic blood pressure of >10 mmHg during inspiration—is an early, highly specific sign of cardiac tamponade. It occurs because the increased right ventricular filling during inspiration bows the interventricular septum into the left ventricle, severely compromising left ventricular stroke volume due to the restricted pericardial sac. Option A describes Cushing's triad (increased ICP), not tamponade. Option B describes mitral regurgitation. Option D indicates hypovolemia; tamponade restricts right heart filling, which would cause jugular venous distention (JVD), not flattening.
</details>
### Question 6
A client in a hypertensive crisis is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. Over the last hour, the client's blood pressure has dropped from 210/115 mmHg to 100/60 mmHg. The client suddenly reports ringing in the ears, severe abdominal pain, and exhibits acute delirium. Which of the following is the nurse's priority action?

A. Decrease the sodium nitroprusside infusion rate by half and monitor.
B. Administer PRN intravenous labetalol to stabilize blood pressure.
C. Immediately stop the infusion and prepare to draw a thiocyanate level.
D. Initiate a rapid 500 mL bolus of 0.9% sodium chloride.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Immediately stop the infusion and prepare to draw a thiocyanate level.

**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolizes into cyanide and thiocyanate. Signs of severe thiocyanate/cyanide toxicity include profound hypotension, tinnitus, abdominal pain, altered mental status (delirium/confusion), and metabolic acidosis. Because this is a life-threatening high-alert pharmacological complication, the immediate priority is to stop the medication drip. Decreasing the rate by half is insufficient when acute toxicity is occurring. Labetalol would further worsen the hypotension. While IV fluids may be needed for hypotension, stopping the offending toxic agent is the absolute first step.

**Cognitive Level:** Application / Analysis
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY
**NCLEX Client Need:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 7
A client with severe heart failure is receiving intravenous amiodarone for atrial fibrillation, alongside a continuous furosemide infusion. The central telemetry monitor suddenly alarms, displaying a ventricular rhythm exceeding 200 beats per minute with a shifting, sinusoidal polymorphic waveform. The client is lethargic but has a weak palpable pulse. Which intervention should the nurse anticipate immediately?

A. Administer a synchronized electrical shock at 100 Joules.
B. Prepare for the immediate administration of IV magnesium sulfate.
C. Give a rapid IV push of atropine 1 mg.
D. Rapidly infuse 500 mL of lactated Ringer's to correct hypovolemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Prepare for the immediate administration of IV magnesium sulfate.

**Rationale:** The rhythm described—a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a shifting sinusoidal waveform—is *Torsades de Pointes*. The client is at high risk because amiodarone prolongs the QT interval, and continuous furosemide can cause hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia, further exacerbating QT prolongation. The first-line pharmacological treatment for Torsades de Pointes with a pulse is intravenous magnesium sulfate. Synchronized cardioversion is difficult because the machine cannot identify a single QRS complex; if the client becomes pulseless, *unsynchronized* defibrillation is required. Atropine is for bradycardia, and a fluid bolus does not treat the lethal electrical irritability.

**Cognitive Level:** Analysis / Evaluation
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS
**NCLEX Client Need:** Physiological Adaptation
</details>

---

### Question 8
A client is in the intensive care unit in cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior myocardial infarction. The client is intubated, receiving a continuous dobutamine infusion, and has an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) in place. Which of the following assessment findings best indicates that the client's compensatory mechanisms have failed and multi-system organ dysfunction is occurring?

A. Mean arterial pressure (MAP) stabilizes at 68 mmHg.
B. Pulmonary artery wedge pressure (PAWP) remains elevated at 18 mmHg.
C. Mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) decreases to 55%.
D. Hourly urine output drops to 12 mL/hr alongside a rapidly rising serum creatinine.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Hourly urine output drops to 12 mL/hr alongside a rapidly rising serum creatinine.

**Rationale:** Cardiogenic shock represents a profound failure of the heart to pump, leading to systemic hypoperfusion. When the cardiovascular system fails to maintain forward flow, other organ systems become ischemic. A drop in urine output to 12 mL/hr (oliguria/anuria) combined with a rising serum creatinine indicates acute kidney injury (AKI) secondary to severe renal hypoperfusion. This is a hallmark sign that shock is progressing into multi-system organ dysfunction syndrome (MODS). A MAP of 68 is borderline acceptable. Elevated PAWP and low SvO2 are expected baseline hemodynamic values in active cardiogenic shock, indicating poor cardiac output, but the renal failure specifically signals the failure of a secondary system.

**Cognitive Level:** Analysis
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION
**NCLEX Client Need:** Reduction of Risk Potential
</details>

---

### Question 9
A client who underwent a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) three hours ago suddenly experiences a drop in blood pressure to 82/40 mmHg. The nurse notes that the previously steady mediastinal chest tube drainage has abruptly ceased. Assessment reveals jugular venous distension (JVD) and distant, muffled heart sounds. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A. Prepare the client for an immediate bedside echocardiogram and pericardiocentesis.
B. Aggressively strip and milk the mediastinal chest tubes to dislodge any clots.
C. Administer IV epinephrine to increase systemic vascular resistance and blood pressure.
D. Place the client in a high-Fowler's position to decrease venous return to the heart.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Prepare the client for an immediate bedside echocardiogram and pericardiocentesis.

**Rationale:** The abrupt cessation of chest tube drainage post-cardiac surgery, combined with hypotension, JVD, and muffled heart sounds (Beck's triad), are classic signs of cardiac tamponade. Blood is pooling in the pericardial sac, compressing the heart and preventing ventricular filling (obstructive shock). The immediate life-saving emergency rescue is decompression of the pericardial sac via pericardiocentesis or surgical reopening of the chest. Stripping/milking chest tubes aggressively is contraindicated as it generates dangerously high negative pressure that can damage tissue. Epinephrine will not fix the mechanical obstruction, and high-Fowler's would dangerously impair what little venous return the client has left.

**Cognitive Level:** Application
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE
**NCLEX Client Need:** Management of Care / Physiological Adaptation
</details>

---

### Question 10
A client with profound cardiogenic shock has an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) inserted via the left femoral artery to augment coronary perfusion and decrease afterload. To prevent a catastrophic complication specific to the presence of this invasive device, which advanced nursing intervention is mandatory?

A. Maintain the client's bed in a reverse Trendelenburg position at a 45-degree angle.
B. Briefly disconnect the balloon from the console when turning the client to avoid kinking.
C. Flush the central IABP lumen vigorously with 50 units of heparin every hour.
D. Assess the left pedal pulse, capillary refill, and extremity temperature every 1 to 2 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Assess the left pedal pulse, capillary refill, and extremity temperature every 1 to 2 hours.

**Rationale:** The insertion of an IABP catheter into the femoral artery poses a severe risk of peripheral ischemia due to arterial occlusion, thrombosis, or embolism. A primary nursing responsibility to prevent life-altering complications (like limb loss) is performing frequent neurovascular assessments of the affected extremity. The head of the bed must not be elevated more than 30 degrees to prevent kinking the IABP catheter in the groin, making 45 degrees unsafe. Disconnecting the balloon from the console can cause immediate blood backflow or device failure. IABP lumens are maintained with a continuous pressurized flush system, not vigorous hourly manual boluses.

**Cognitive Level:** Application / Analysis
**Lens:** COMPLICATION PREVENTION
**NCLEX Client Need:** Reduction of Risk Potential
</details>
### Question 11
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**
The charge nurse in the Cardiac Intensive Care Unit (CICU) is reviewing the status of four assigned clients. Which client should the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with cardiogenic shock on a continuous dobutamine infusion who has a capillary refill of 4 seconds and cool, mottled extremities. 
B) A client admitted for a hypertensive emergency reporting a new, severe "tearing" sensation radiating between their shoulder blades. 
C) A client with chronic atrial fibrillation receiving a diltiazem infusion who has an apical heart rate of 96 beats per minute. 
D) A client 2 days post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) with a mediastinal chest tube draining 40 mL of serosanguineous fluid over the last hour. 

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
A tearing or ripping pain radiating to the back or between the shoulder blades in a client with severe hypertension is the hallmark, classic presentation of an acute aortic dissection. This is an immediate, life-threatening cardiovascular emergency that requires rapid medical and surgical intervention to prevent complete aortic rupture and death. The client in Option A has expected findings of poor peripheral perfusion secondary to cardiogenic shock. The client in Option C has a controlled heart rate for atrial fibrillation. The client in Option D is exhibiting expected, normal chest tube drainage parameters (less than 100-150 mL/hr) for a post-CABG client. 

</details>

### Question 12
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
A client in a hypertensive crisis is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The client's blood pressure drops rapidly from 225/125 mm Hg to 130/70 mm Hg within 10 minutes of initiating the drip. The client now reports acute lightheadedness, nausea, and diaphoresis, and their mentation is becoming sluggish. Which action should the nurse take IMMEDIATELY?

A) Decrease the sodium nitroprusside infusion rate by 50%.
B) Administer an IV fluid bolus of 500 mL normal saline.
C) Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion completely.
D) Obtain a stat 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) to check for ischemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
Sodium nitroprusside is a highly potent, rapid-acting direct vasodilator with a half-life of less than 2 minutes. The therapeutic goal in a hypertensive crisis is a controlled reduction of mean arterial pressure (MAP) by no more than 20-25% in the first hour. Dropping the blood pressure too rapidly compromises cerebral, coronary, and renal perfusion, leading to symptomatic hypotension and potential ischemic strokes or myocardial infarctions. Because the client is exhibiting severe symptoms of hypoperfusion (sluggish mentation, lightheadedness, diaphoresis), the immediate life-saving action is to stop the infusion entirely. Lowering the rate (Option A) is insufficient for symptomatic cerebral hypoperfusion. Giving fluids (Option B) or obtaining an ECG (Option D) delays the primary corrective intervention.

</details>

---

### Question 13
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**
A client who sustained a blunt chest injury in a motor vehicle collision is being continuously monitored in the trauma ICU for potential cardiac tamponade. Which early, subtle clinical finding should alert the nurse to the deterioration of the client's hemodynamic status BEFORE the development of the classic Beck's triad?

A) A decrease in systolic blood pressure of 14 mm Hg during spontaneous inspiration.
B) Jugular venous distention visible when the head of the bed is elevated to 45 degrees.
C) Muffled and distant S1 and S2 heart sounds upon auscultation of the apical impulse.
D) A sudden, progressive widening of the pulse pressure on the arterial line monitor.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
Pulsus paradoxus—defined as an exaggerated drop in systolic blood pressure of greater than 10 mm Hg during inspiration—is one of the earliest and most sensitive signs of impending cardiac tamponade. As pericardial fluid accumulates, right ventricular expansion during inspiration pushes the intraventricular septum into the left ventricle, critically reducing left ventricular stroke volume. Beck's triad (hypotension, jugular venous distention [Option B], and muffled heart sounds [Option C]) are late, classic signs that indicate the tamponade is already fully developed and the client is in extremis. Option D is incorrect; cardiac tamponade causes a *narrowing* of the pulse pressure due to severely restricted cardiac output, not widening. 

</details>

---

### Question 14
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**
A client in profound cardiogenic shock secondary to a massive anterior myocardial infarction is receiving continuous milrinone and norepinephrine infusions. The nurse notes the client's urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr over the last 3 hours, and the serum creatinine has acutely increased from 1.0 mg/dL to 2.4 mg/dL. Which pathophysiological mechanism best explains this multi-system complication?

A) Systemic vasodilation from the milrinone infusion causing primary intrarenal toxicity.
B) Direct nephrotoxic effects of massive endogenous catecholamines released during the myocardial infarction.
C) Postrenal obstruction caused by profound right ventricular volume overload and severe venous congestion.
D) Severe left ventricular pump failure leading to decreased cardiac output and subsequent prerenal acute kidney injury.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
Cardiogenic shock is characterized by severe impairment of ventricular pumping action, most often due to an extensive anterior myocardial infarction causing left ventricular failure. The catastrophic drop in cardiac output results in profound systemic hypoperfusion. When the kidneys are deprived of adequate blood flow and perfusion pressure, the glomerular filtration rate (GFR) drops rapidly, resulting in prerenal acute kidney injury (AKI). This manifests as oliguria (low urine output) and a rapid rise in serum creatinine. It is a secondary failure of the renal system driven entirely by the primary failure of the cardiovascular system.

</details>

---

### Question 15
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**
An intensive care nurse is analyzing the cardiac rhythm of a client admitted with recurrent syncope and a serum magnesium level of 1.1 mEq/L (0.45 mmol/L). The telemetry monitor suddenly displays a rapid, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a cyclic, twisting QRS morphology. The nurse enters the room and finds the client unresponsive and completely pulseless. What is the nurse's PRIORITY intervention?

A) Administer a rapid intravenous push of 150 mg amiodarone.
B) Initiate synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules.
C) Begin high-quality CPR and prepare for immediate defibrillation.
D) Hang an immediate continuous intravenous infusion of magnesium sulfate.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (TdP), a highly unstable lethal polymorphic ventricular tachycardia heavily associated with severe hypomagnesemia (normal magnesium is 1.5 - 2.5 mEq/L). While intravenous magnesium sulfate is the specific pharmacological antidote for TdP, this client is currently **pulseless and unresponsive**. According to Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support (ACLS) guidelines, a pulseless polymorphic VT is treated exactly the same as Ventricular Fibrillation (VF). The absolute priority is immediate high-quality CPR and unsynchronized defibrillation to restore a perfusing rhythm. Synchronized cardioversion (Option B) cannot be used as the machine cannot find a stable R-wave in TdP, and amiodarone (Option A) can actually prolong the QT interval, worsening TdP.

</details>
### Question 16
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)
**System Focus:** Lethal Arrhythmias

A client admitted with an acute anterior wall myocardial infarction suddenly becomes unresponsive while the nurse is assessing their peripheral IV site. The nurse glances at the telemetry monitor and observes wide, bizarre QRS complexes at a rate of 180 beats per minute with no visible P waves. The client has no palpable carotid pulse. Which of the following is the priority nursing action?

A) Administer 1 mg of Epinephrine via rapid intravenous push.
B) Deliver a synchronized electrical shock at 100 Joules.
C) Initiate high-quality chest compressions and call for a defibrillator.
D) Administer 300 mg of Amiodarone via rapid intravenous push.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is in pulseless ventricular tachycardia (VT), a lethal arrhythmia that rapidly leads to death if not treated immediately. The definitive treatment for pulseless VT is immediate defibrillation. However, while the defibrillator is being brought to the room and charged, high-quality cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) must be initiated immediately to maintain perfusion to the brain and vital organs. Synchronized cardioversion (Option B) is contraindicated because the client has no pulse; an unsynchronized shock (defibrillation) is required. Epinephrine (Option A) and Amiodarone (Option D) are part of the Advanced Cardiac Life Support (ACLS) algorithm for pulseless VT/VFib, but they are administered *after* CPR and the initial defibrillation attempts have begun.

</details>

### Question 17
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)
**System Focus:** Hypertensive Crisis

A client in a hypertensive emergency is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside at 3 mcg/kg/min. After 48 hours of therapy, the nurse observes that the client has become acutely disoriented, exhibits muscle twitching, and has a respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min. A newly drawn arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals a pH of 7.28, HCO3 of 16 mEq/L, and a serum lactate of 5.2 mmol/L. Which action should the nurse take first?

A) Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate by half and draw a thiocyanate level.
B) Administer an intravenous bolus of sodium bicarbonate to correct the metabolic acidosis.
C) Administer a 500 mL bolus of 0.9% normal saline to increase renal clearance of the drug.
D) Stop the nitroprusside infusion immediately and notify the healthcare provider.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside contains cyanide groups. Prolonged infusions or high doses can lead to cyanide and thiocyanate toxicity, which prevents cells from using oxygen and causes severe lactic acidosis (metabolic acidosis). The classic presentation of toxicity includes altered mental status, hyperreflexia, muscle twitching, seizures, and unexplained metabolic acidosis. The absolute priority is to immediately discontinue the medication and notify the provider. Antidotes such as sodium nitrite, sodium thiosulfate, or hydroxocobalamin may need to be administered. Decreasing the rate (Option A) is insufficient because the client is actively showing signs of life-threatening toxicity. Treating the acidosis with bicarbonate (Option B) or administering saline (Option C) does not remove the offending agent causing the cellular asphyxiation.

</details>

### Question 18
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of 'failure to rescue')
**System Focus:** Cardiac Tamponade

A client is recovering in the intensive care unit four hours after a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. The nurse is monitoring the client's hemodynamic status and mediastinal chest tubes. Which assessment finding represents the *earliest* indication of impending cardiac tamponade?

A) A widening pulse pressure with an acute increase in central venous pressure (CVP).
B) Sudden cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage accompanied by unexplained tachycardia.
C) The presence of muffled, distant heart sounds and severe jugular venous distention.
D) A precipitous drop in blood pressure from 115/70 mmHg to 85/50 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Cardiac tamponade post-cardiac surgery often occurs because blood or fluid accumulates in the mediastinal space, usually due to a blocked or clotted chest tube. The sudden cessation of previously active chest tube drainage, combined with a compensatory tachycardia, is the earliest and subtlest sign of impending tamponade. Muffled heart sounds, severe JVD, and profound hypotension (Options C and D) represent Beck's Triad, which are *late* and ominous signs of decompensated cardiac tamponade. Cardiac tamponade causes a *narrowed* pulse pressure, not a widening pulse pressure (Option A), because the heart cannot fill properly during diastole, causing stroke volume and systolic blood pressure to drop.

</details>

### Question 19
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting hemodynamics)
**System Focus:** Cardiogenic Shock

A client admitted with a massive myocardial infarction develops severe shortness of breath and altered mentation. The client is intubated, and a pulmonary artery (PA) catheter is inserted. The nurse records the following hemodynamic parameters:
- Cardiac Output (CO): 2.8 L/min (Normal: 4-8 L/min)
- Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP): 26 mmHg (Normal: 6-12 mmHg)
- Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR): 1,800 dynes/sec/cm⁻⁵ (Normal: 800-1,200)

Based on this hemodynamic profile indicating cardiogenic shock, the nurse should anticipate orders for which combination of intravenous medications?

A) Dobutamine continuous infusion and furosemide IV push.
B) Norepinephrine continuous infusion and 0.9% normal saline bolus.
C) Phenylephrine continuous infusion and metoprolol IV push.
D) Vasopressin continuous infusion and diltiazem IV push.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** This hemodynamic profile represents cardiogenic shock with left ventricular failure. The low CO indicates poor pump function. The significantly elevated PAWP (26 mmHg) reflects severe left ventricular preload and volume overload (pulmonary edema). The high SVR indicates intense compensatory systemic vasoconstriction, which further increases afterload and makes it harder for the failing heart to pump. The optimal treatment includes an inotrope like Dobutamine, which increases cardiac contractility (raising CO) and causes mild vasodilation (decreasing SVR and afterload). Diuretics like furosemide are needed to reduce the fluid overload, thereby lowering the elevated PAWP. Administering fluids (Option B) would worsen the severe pulmonary edema. Vasopressors like phenylephrine or vasopressin (Options C and D) would further increase the already elevated SVR, increasing afterload and worsening the pump failure. Beta-blockers and calcium channel blockers (Options C and D) negatively affect contractility and are contraindicated in acute cardiogenic shock.

</details>

### Question 20
**Lens:** COMPLEX DELEGATION (Deciding what tasks are UNSAFE to delegate)
**System Focus:** Lethal Arrhythmias / Unstable System Environment

A client with severe ischemic heart disease suddenly develops third-degree atrioventricular (AV) block. The client’s blood pressure is 70/40 mmHg, and the heart rate is 30 beats/min. The rapid response team is at the bedside initiating transcutaneous pacing. Which task assigned by the charge nurse is **UNSAFE** and requires immediate intervention?

A) Directing the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) to obtain the emergency crash cart.
B) Assigning the Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN/VN) to insert a strictly monitored indwelling urinary catheter.
C) Delegating the Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN/VN) to titrate a continuous dopamine infusion to maintain a systolic BP > 90 mmHg.
D) Asking the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) to continuously observe and report the client's oxygen saturation values.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Titrating high-alert, vasoactive continuous intravenous infusions (such as dopamine) requires advanced clinical judgment, continuous hemodynamic assessment, and specialized training. This falls squarely within the scope of practice of the Registered Nurse (RN) and is UNSAFE to delegate to an LPN/VN, making Option C the correct answer. The LPN/VN can safely insert a standard indwelling urinary catheter (Option B). The UAP is safely and appropriately utilized in a crisis by obtaining emergency equipment like a crash cart (Option A) and observing/reporting standard vital signs or continuous oxygen saturation to the RN (Option D).

</details>
### Question 21
The nurse assumes care for four clients on the step-down cardiac unit. Which client requires the nurse's immediate attention?

A. A client with chronic atrial fibrillation who is receiving a diltiazem drip, currently displaying an irregular apical pulse of 112 beats/min.
B. A client 12 hours post-coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) who has a sudden cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage and muffled heart sounds.
C. A client admitted with acute decompensated heart failure who reports resting orthopnea and a 4-pound weight gain over the last 3 days.
D. A client with infective endocarditis receiving IV antibiotics who developed splinter hemorrhages and a loud systolic murmur.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** This client is exhibiting classic signs of acute cardiac tamponade, a life-threatening emergency. A sudden stop in chest tube drainage post-CABG often indicates tube occlusion, which leads to blood accumulating in the pericardial sac. This exerts immense pressure on the heart, restricting ventricular filling and causing a severe drop in cardiac output. Symptoms include Beck's Triad: muffled heart sounds, hypotension, and jugular venous distention. This requires immediate intervention (e.g., clearing the chest tube or emergency sternotomy). The other clients have expected or chronic findings for their respective diagnoses that, while requiring ongoing management, do not signify an immediate, acute system collapse. 

**Lens Used:** PRIORITIZATION
</details>

---

### Question 22
A client is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside at 3 mcg/kg/min for a hypertensive emergency. The client's initial blood pressure was 230/130 mm Hg, and the current blood pressure is 185/105 mm Hg. During assessment, the client reports a severe, throbbing headache, and the nurse notes acute confusion, muscle twitching, and an almond-like odor on the client's breath. Which intervention is the nurse's priority?

A. Draw a stat serum thiocyanate level to confirm the suspicion of toxicity.
B. Increase the infusion rate to reach the target mean arterial pressure (MAP).
C. Discontinue the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately.
D. Administer a PRN dose of intravenous labetalol.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside is a potent, rapid-acting vasodilator used in hypertensive crises, but it metabolizes into cyanide and thiocyanate. Acute confusion, muscle twitching, severe headache, and the classic "bitter almond" breath odor are profound signs of cyanide toxicity, a life-threatening complication. The absolute priority is to stop the offending agent immediately to prevent anoxic brain injury and death. Drawing a lab level (A) delays life-saving action. Increasing the infusion (B) will worsen the toxicity. Administering labetalol (D) might be needed later to control blood pressure once the nitroprusside is stopped, but the immediate rescue action is to stop the toxic drip.

**Lens Used:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY
</details>

---

### Question 23
The intensive care nurse analyzes the telemetric electrocardiogram (ECG) of a client admitted with acute decompensated heart failure. The monitor displays a prolonged QT interval that suddenly degrades into a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a twisting QRS morphology around the isoelectric line. Reviewing the client's laboratory data, which finding is most likely responsible for this lethal rhythm?

A. Serum magnesium 1.1 mEq/L
B. Serum potassium 5.2 mEq/L
C. Brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) 940 pg/mL
D. Serum calcium 9.8 mg/dL

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (TdP), a specific and highly lethal form of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by a prolonged QT interval and a QRS complex that twists around the baseline. A primary and highly common cause of a prolonged QT interval leading to TdP is hypomagnesemia. The normal serum magnesium level is 1.5–2.5 mEq/L; a level of 1.1 mEq/L is critically low and severely impairs cardiac repolarization. While hyperkalemia (B) can cause lethal arrhythmias (like sine waves or V-fib), it does not classically cause prolonged QT with TdP. The BNP level (C) is a marker of heart failure, not an electrical trigger. The serum calcium level (D) is within normal limits (8.6–10.3 mg/dL).

**Lens Used:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS
</details>

---

### Question 24
A client diagnosed with a massive pericardial effusion develops cardiac tamponade and undergoes an emergent bedside echocardiogram-guided pericardiocentesis. The nurse is evaluating the client's hemodynamic response 15 minutes post-procedure. Which assessment finding provides the best evidence that the crisis rescue intervention was successful?

A. The QRS amplitude on the continuous electrocardiogram begins exhibiting electrical alternans.
B. Jugular venous distention (JVD) becomes more pronounced during inspiration.
C. The client develops a 15 mm Hg drop in systolic blood pressure during inspiration.
D. Central venous pressure (CVP) decreases from 18 mm Hg to 8 mm Hg.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** Cardiac tamponade restricts right ventricular filling, which causes blood to back up into the venous system, leading to a severely elevated Central Venous Pressure (CVP). Normal CVP is 2-8 mm Hg. A decrease in CVP from 18 mm Hg to 8 mm Hg indicates that the pressure on the right side of the heart has been relieved, allowing normal venous return and ventricular filling. Electrical alternans (A) is a sign of a swinging heart in a large effusion, indicating unresolved or worsening tamponade. An increase in JVD during inspiration (Kussmaul's sign) (B) and a significant drop in systolic blood pressure during inspiration (pulsus paradoxus) (C) are classic signs of cardiac tamponade; their presence indicates the procedure was unsuccessful or fluid is re-accumulating.

**Lens Used:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS
</details>

---

### Question 25
A client is in cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. Despite receiving a continuous infusion of dobutamine, the client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) remains 55 mm Hg. The nurse notes a sharp decline in urine output to 10 mL/hr over the past 4 hours, and the latest serum creatinine has rapidly increased to 2.4 mg/dL. Which pathophysiological mechanism best explains this secondary organ failure?

A. Severe reduction in cardiac output triggers intense sympathetic and renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS) activation, causing profound renal vasoconstriction.
B. Massive systemic vasodilation leads to functional hypovolemia, resulting in blood pooling and impaired renal perfusion.
C. Dobutamine-induced nephrotoxicity directly damages the renal tubules, resulting in an acute intrarenal failure.
D. Profound third-spacing of intravascular fluid results in bilateral post-renal obstruction.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** In cardiogenic shock, profound left ventricular failure causes a massive drop in cardiac output and systemic hypoperfusion (MAP < 60 mm Hg). To compensate for the poor perfusion, the body heavily activates the sympathetic nervous system and the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS). This results in extreme peripheral and renal vasoconstriction. The combination of low cardiac output and severe renal arterial constriction dramatically decreases the glomerular filtration rate (GFR), causing pre-renal acute kidney injury (AKI), termed Cardiorenal Syndrome. Option B describes distributive shock (e.g., sepsis or anaphylaxis), not cardiogenic shock. Dobutamine (C) does not cause direct tubular necrosis; it is an inotrope used to *improve* cardiac output. Post-renal obstruction (D) is caused by a physical blockage in the urinary tract (e.g., enlarged prostate, stones), not fluid shifting.

**Lens Used:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION
</details>
### Question 26
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A client is 12 hours postoperative following a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The nurse observes that the mediastinal chest tube drainage has abruptly decreased from 100 mL/hr to 0 mL over the last hour. The client's central venous pressure (CVP) has increased from 6 to 16 mm Hg, the blood pressure has dropped from 118/72 to 88/74 mm Hg, and the heart tones are distant. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse must take?

A) Strip the mediastinal chest tubes forcefully to dislodge the obstructing clot.
B) Initiate a continuous dopamine infusion to maintain a mean arterial pressure greater than 65 mm Hg.
C) Notify the provider immediately and prepare the client for an emergency pericardiocentesis.
D) Administer a rapid 1,000 mL bolus of 0.9% Normal Saline to increase cardiac preload.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Emergency Rescue** lens. The client is exhibiting classic signs of **Cardiac Tamponade**, a life-threatening complication of CABG characterized by Beck's Triad: hypotension with a narrowed pulse pressure (88/74 mm Hg), elevated central venous pressure/jugular venous distention (CVP 16 mm Hg), and muffled heart tones. The sudden cessation of chest tube drainage indicates fluid/blood is accumulating in the pericardial sac, compressing the heart. The immediate life-saving rescue action is to evacuate the fluid via an emergency pericardiocentesis or surgical re-exploration. 

**Why other options are incorrect:**
- *Option A:* "Stripping" chest tubes is contraindicated because it creates excessively high negative pressure that can damage lung tissue, disrupt surgical anastomoses, and worsen bleeding. "Milking" may be done cautiously per facility policy, but stripping is unsafe.
- *Option B:* While vasopressors/inotropes might temporarily support blood pressure, they do not resolve the mechanical obstruction caused by the tamponade. The heart physically cannot fill.
- *Option D:* Fluid resuscitation may transiently increase right-sided filling pressures, but it does not fix the underlying obstructive shock and can worsen right ventricular strain in severe tamponade. Immediate decompression is required.
</details>

---

### Question 27
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**

The nurse is closely monitoring a client admitted with acute decompensated heart failure. Suddenly, the telemetry monitor displays a run of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a constantly changing QRS amplitude and axis. The client appears lethargic but currently has a palpable radial pulse of 140 beats per minute. Which laboratory finding most likely precipitated this lethal rhythm?

A) Serum potassium 5.5 mEq/L
B) Serum magnesium 1.1 mg/dL
C) Serum calcium 10.5 mg/dL
D) Serum sodium 132 mEq/L

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Advanced Data Analysis** lens. The ECG description of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a fluctuating QRS axis is classic for **Torsades de Pointes**. This lethal arrhythmia is highly associated with a prolonged QT interval, which is frequently caused by severe hypomagnesemia. A normal serum magnesium level is 1.5 to 2.5 mg/dL. A level of 1.1 mg/dL is critically low and directly precipitates Torsades de Pointes. The immediate treatment for this rhythm, provided the patient has a pulse, includes the administration of IV magnesium sulfate.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
- *Option A:* Hyperkalemia (5.5 mEq/L) typically presents with tall, peaked T waves, widened QRS complexes, and can lead to ventricular fibrillation or asystole, but it is not the classic precipitator of Torsades de Pointes.
- *Option C:* A serum calcium level of 10.5 mg/dL is within normal limits (usually 9.0 to 10.5 mg/dL). Hypocalcemia, not normo- or hypercalcemia, can prolong the QT interval.
- *Option D:* Hyponatremia (132 mEq/L) causes neurological symptoms (confusion, seizures) and fluid shifts but is not a primary driver of Torsades de Pointes.
</details>

---

### Question 28
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client in severe cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction is receiving continuous intravenous infusions of dobutamine and norepinephrine. The nurse is titrating the vasoactive drips to optimize cardiac output. Which clinical assessment indicates the need to immediately *decrease* the dobutamine infusion rate?

A) The client experiences 6 to 8 premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) per minute.
B) The client's central venous pressure (CVP) decreases from 14 mm Hg to 10 mm Hg.
C) The client's mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) increases from 52% to 65%.
D) The client’s urine output increases from 20 mL/hr to 45 mL/hr.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **High-Alert Pharmacology** lens. Dobutamine is a potent synthetic catecholamine with strong beta-1 agonist properties, used to increase cardiac contractility (inotropy) in cardiogenic shock. However, it significantly increases myocardial oxygen demand and is highly arrhythmogenic. The appearance of frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) indicates severe myocardial irritability and ischemia. The nurse must recognize this adverse effect and immediately decrease the dobutamine dose to prevent deterioration into lethal arrhythmias like ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
- *Option B:* A decrease in CVP from 14 to 10 mm Hg indicates improved forward flow and reduced right-sided heart congestion. This is an expected and desired therapeutic effect.
- *Option C:* An SvO2 increase from 52% to 65% demonstrates improved tissue perfusion and oxygen delivery. A normal SvO2 is 60-80%; this indicates the shock state is improving.
- *Option D:* An increase in urine output indicates improved renal perfusion secondary to enhanced cardiac output and mean arterial pressure. This is a positive outcome of the therapy.
</details>

---

### Question 29
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A client is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside for a hypertensive crisis. Over the last 48 hours, the blood pressure has been successfully maintained at 150/85 mm Hg. During the morning assessment, the nurse notes several new clinical findings. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the highest risk of acute deterioration and a "failure to rescue" if not immediately addressed?

A) The client's heart rate increases from 82 to 98 beats per minute.
B) The client states they have a mild, throbbing headache.
C) The client’s urine output over the last hour is 40 mL.
D) The client becomes progressively restless and exhibits hyperreflexia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Risk of Deterioration** lens. Sodium nitroprusside is a potent, rapid-acting vasodilator. A major, life-threatening complication of prolonged nitroprusside infusion (especially beyond 48 hours or in clients with renal impairment) is **thiocyanate or cyanide toxicity**. The earliest and most subtle signs of this toxicity are central nervous system changes, including progressive restlessness, confusion, hyperreflexia, muscle twitching, and eventually seizures or coma. The nurse must recognize this immediately, stop the infusion, and notify the provider to prevent fatal deterioration.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
- *Option A:* A mild increase in heart rate (reflex tachycardia) is a common, expected compensatory response to systemic vasodilation and dropping blood pressure.
- *Option B:* A mild, throbbing headache is a very common and expected side effect of potent cerebral vasodilation caused by nitroprusside. 
- *Option C:* A urine output of 40 mL/hr is adequate (greater than 30 mL/hr or 0.5 mL/kg/hr) and does not indicate an acute decline in renal perfusion.
</details>

---

### Question 30
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is admitted with cardiogenic shock secondary to acute myocardial infarction. The failing cardiovascular system has now triggered acute kidney injury (urine output of 15 mL/hr, BUN 45 mg/dL) and acute pulmonary edema (bilateral crackles in the mid-to-upper lung fields, SpO2 86%). The client's current mean arterial pressure (MAP) is 54 mm Hg. The provider orders a continuous intravenous furosemide infusion. What is the most appropriate multi-system integration action by the nurse?

A) Administer the furosemide as ordered to rapidly clear the pulmonary edema and protect the respiratory system.
B) Hold the furosemide and administer a rapid 500 mL normal saline bolus to urgently increase renal perfusion.
C) Prepare to administer a prescribed inotropic agent to stabilize the MAP before initiating the diuretic.
D) Request an order to initiate continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) instead of using diuretics.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Multi-System Integration** lens. The client is experiencing a cascade of multi-organ failure driven by the primary cardiovascular collapse (pump failure). While the pulmonary edema (respiratory) and acute kidney injury (renal) need management, administering a powerful diuretic like furosemide when the MAP is 54 mm Hg will cause catastrophic hypotension, further destroying renal and coronary perfusion. The nurse must integrate hemodynamics with renal and respiratory needs: the MAP *must* be stabilized first (typically with inotropes like dobutamine or vasopressors) to ensure organ perfusion before a diuretic can be safely administered or be effective.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
- *Option A:* Giving furosemide to a patient with a MAP of 54 mm Hg will exacerbate shock, leading to cardiovascular collapse and worsening acute kidney injury due to hypoperfusion. 
- *Option B:* Giving a 500 mL fluid bolus is strictly contraindicated in cardiogenic shock with acute pulmonary edema; the failing heart cannot pump the extra volume, which will rapidly worsen the pulmonary edema and hypoxia.
- *Option D:* While CRRT may eventually be required if the kidneys do not respond to stabilized perfusion and diuretics, the immediate physiological priority is correcting the cardiogenic shock (raising the MAP to > 65 mm Hg) to support all organ systems.
</details>
### Question 31
A client is 6 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. The nurse observes that the mediastinal chest tube drainage, which was previously 120 mL/hr, has abruptly decreased to 5 mL over the past hour. Which subsequent assessment finding represents the *earliest* indicator of a developing life-threatening complication?

A. Muffled, distant heart sounds and profound jugular venous distention.  
B. An inspiratory drop in systolic blood pressure of 14 mm Hg.  
C. Equalization of right atrial and pulmonary artery wedge pressures on the monitor.  
D. A sudden, dramatic widening of the client's pulse pressure.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. An inspiratory drop in systolic blood pressure of 14 mm Hg.

**Lens:** Risk of Deterioration
**System Focus:** Cardiac Tamponade

**Rationale:** The sudden cessation of chest tube drainage in a post-CABG client is a major red flag for blood pooling in the mediastinum, leading to cardiac tamponade. The earliest and subtlest clinical sign of impending cardiac tamponade is *pulsus paradoxus*, which is defined as a decrease in systolic blood pressure greater than 10 mm Hg during inspiration. Muffled heart sounds and jugular venous distention (Beck's triad) (Option A) and equalization of intracardiac pressures (Option C) are classic but *later* signs indicating that severe hemodynamic compromise has already occurred. A widening pulse pressure (Option D) is seen in increased intracranial pressure (Cushing's triad); tamponade classically presents with a *narrowing* pulse pressure. Recognizing pulsus paradoxus allows the nurse to intervene before full cardiovascular collapse occurs.

</details>

---

### Question 32
A client in a hypertensive emergency is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The client's blood pressure has decreased from 220/130 mm Hg to 160/90 mm Hg over the last 4 hours. Upon assessment, the nurse notes that the client is newly disoriented, restless, and has hyperreflexia. What is the nurse's priority pharmacological intervention?

A. Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate by half and reassess in 15 minutes.  
B. Administer PRN intravenous haloperidol to manage the client's acute agitation.  
C. Prepare to administer intravenous labetalol to transition the client off the drip.  
D. Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately and notify the provider.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately and notify the provider.

**Lens:** High-Alert Pharmacology
**System Focus:** Hypertensive Crisis

**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside is a potent vasodilator used in hypertensive emergencies. However, its metabolism produces cyanide, which is converted to thiocyanate in the liver. Prolonged or high-dose infusions can lead to thiocyanate/cyanide toxicity. Early signs of toxicity include altered mental status (confusion, restlessness, disorientation), hyperreflexia, tinnitus, and muscle spasms. The immediate priority when toxicity is suspected is to stop the infusion to prevent lethal cellular hypoxia and cardiovascular collapse. Decreasing the rate (Option A) is unsafe when active toxicity is occurring. Administering haloperidol (Option B) inappropriately masks the symptoms of toxicity. While transitioning to another antihypertensive like labetalol (Option C) will eventually be necessary, the *immediate* life-saving action is discontinuing the toxic agent.

</details>

---

### Question 33
The nurse is caring for a client with a history of prolonged QT interval due to hypomagnesemia. The telemetry monitor suddenly alarms, displaying a rapid, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a twisting QRS morphology. Upon entering the room, the nurse finds the client unresponsive with no palpable carotid pulse. What is the immediate life-saving action?

A. Initiate high-quality cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) and prepare for defibrillation.  
B. Administer 2 grams of intravenous magnesium sulfate over 10 minutes.  
C. Prepare the client for immediate synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules.  
D. Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of 300 mg of amiodarone.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Initiate high-quality cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) and prepare for defibrillation.

**Lens:** Emergency Rescue
**System Focus:** Lethal Arrhythmias

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting Torsades de Pointes (a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia). Because the client is unresponsive and pulseless, this is treated identically to ventricular fibrillation. The immediate life-saving actions are high-quality CPR and early, unsynchronized defibrillation. Intravenous magnesium sulfate (Option B) is the pharmacological treatment of choice for Torsades de Pointes, but if the client is *pulseless*, CPR and defibrillation take absolute priority over medication administration. Synchronized cardioversion (Option C) is impossible because the defibrillator cannot synchronize to the constantly changing, polymorphic R-waves of Torsades. Amiodarone (Option D) may be used later in the cardiac arrest algorithm, but it does not supersede CPR and defibrillation.

</details>

---

### Question 34
A client is in severe cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. Despite intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) therapy and maximal vasopressor support, the client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) has remained between 50-55 mm Hg for the past 12 hours. Which laboratory finding most strongly indicates that the cardiovascular failure has precipitated an *intrarenal* acute kidney injury (acute tubular necrosis)?

A. A progressively increasing blood urea nitrogen (BUN) to creatinine ratio of 25:1.  
B. A calculated fractional excretion of sodium (FENa) of less than 1%.  
C. The presence of muddy brown granular casts in the microscopic urinalysis.  
D. A markedly elevated urine specific gravity of 1.030.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. The presence of muddy brown granular casts in the microscopic urinalysis.

**Lens:** Multi-System Integration
**System Focus:** Cardiogenic Shock

**Rationale:** In severe cardiogenic shock, prolonged hypoperfusion to the kidneys initially causes *prerenal* acute kidney injury (AKI). If the MAP remains critically low, the ischemic injury damages the renal tubules, progressing to *intrarenal* AKI, specifically Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN). The hallmark diagnostic sign of ATN is the presence of muddy brown granular casts in the urine, representing sloughed, necrotic tubular epithelial cells. Options A, B, and D (BUN:Cr ratio > 20:1, FENa < 1%, and high specific gravity) are all compensatory signs of *prerenal* azotemia, where the tubules are still intact and attempting to desperately hold onto sodium and water in response to poor cardiac output. Once ATN (intrarenal failure) occurs, the kidneys lose this concentrating ability. 

</details>

---

### Question 35
The charge nurse in the cardiovascular intensive care unit is reviewing the status of four unstable clients. Which client must the nurse assess FIRST?

A. A client with a pericardial effusion who exhibits a 12 mm Hg drop in systolic blood pressure during inspiration.  
B. A client in a hypertensive crisis receiving clevidipine who suddenly reports severe, tearing pain radiating to the upper back.  
C. A client in cardiogenic shock receiving dobutamine whose hourly urine output has decreased from 35 mL to 25 mL.  
D. A client with chronic heart failure who has developed a new run of four premature ventricular contractions (PVCs).  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. A client in a hypertensive crisis receiving clevidipine who suddenly reports severe, tearing pain radiating to the upper back.

**Lens:** Prioritization
**System Focus:** Mixed Cardiovascular (Hypertensive Crisis priority)

**Rationale:** A client in a hypertensive crisis who complains of severe, tearing, or ripping pain radiating to the upper back or between the shoulder blades is exhibiting classic signs of an acute aortic dissection. This is an immediate, catastrophic surgical emergency with an extremely high risk of imminent rupture and death; blood pressure and heart rate must be aggressively controlled immediately. The client in Option A is showing signs of pulsus paradoxus (early tamponade), which is urgent, but an actively dissecting aorta takes absolute precedence. The client in Option C has a minor drop in urine output expected in shock/hypoperfusion, requiring ongoing monitoring and titration of hemodynamics. The client in Option D has a short run of non-sustained ventricular tachycardia (4 PVCs), which warrants assessment of electrolytes and oxygenation, but does not immediately threaten life compared to an aortic dissection.

</details>
### Question 36
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology**
**System Focus: Hypertensive Crisis**

A client is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside for a hypertensive emergency. Upon initial assessment, the blood pressure was 230/130 mmHg. Fifteen minutes later, the blood pressure reads 140/85 mmHg. The client is now intensely restless, diaphoretic, and reporting sudden, severe chest pain. Which advanced pharmacological intervention is the priority?

A) Stop the infusion completely to prevent imminent cardiogenic shock and notify the provider.
B) Administer a rapid 500 mL bolus of 0.9% sodium chloride to treat the induced hypotension.
C) Decrease the infusion rate, as rapid blood pressure reduction reduces coronary perfusion and causes ischemia.
D) Maintain the current rate, as the blood pressure has successfully reached the target normotensive range.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: C</b><br>
During a hypertensive emergency, blood pressure should be lowered gradually (typically no more than a 20-25% reduction in Mean Arterial Pressure within the first hour). Dropping the pressure too rapidly, as seen in this scenario (a drop from 230/130 to 140/85 in 15 minutes), causes sudden hypoperfusion to vital organs, leading to cerebral, renal, or coronary ischemia (manifesting here as sudden severe chest pain). The nurse must decrease the rate to allow for a gradual reduction. Stopping the medication completely (Option A) is incorrect because sodium nitroprusside has an extremely short half-life (about 2 minutes), and an abrupt cessation can cause dangerous rebound hypertension. Administering a fluid bolus (Option B) is contraindicated for a client already suffering from a hypertensive emergency and potential heart failure. Maintaining the rate (Option D) will continue to cause ischemic damage.
</details>

---

### Question 37
**Lens: Risk of Deterioration**
**System Focus: Cardiac Tamponade**

A client is 6 hours post-operative following a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). Hemodynamics reveal a heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure 105/65 mmHg, and a central venous pressure (CVP) of 8 mmHg. Which assessment finding should alert the nurse to the highest risk of impending cardiac tamponade?

A) A gradual widening of the pulse pressure over the last two hours.
B) An abrupt cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage accompanied by tachycardia.
C) New-onset dependent pedal edema and jugular venous distention when flat.
D) Occasional premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) on the telemetry monitor.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
In the immediate post-operative period following cardiac surgery, mediastinal chest tubes are placed to drain blood and prevent it from accumulating around the heart. An abrupt cessation of drainage often indicates that the tube is clotted or kinked. When this occurs, blood rapidly pools in the pericardial space, compressing the heart and leading directly to cardiac tamponade. Tachycardia is an early compensatory sign. Option A is incorrect because cardiac tamponade causes a *narrowing* of the pulse pressure, not widening (widening pulse pressure is associated with increased intracranial pressure). Option C is incorrect because dependent edema takes days to develop, and JVD is an expected finding when a client is lying flat. Option D is a common and relatively benign finding post-cardiac surgery due to myocardial irritability, not a direct predictor of tamponade.
</details>

---

### Question 38
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis**
**System Focus: Lethal Arrhythmias**

An older adult client admitted with an exacerbation of heart failure is receiving intravenous furosemide and oral digoxin. The telemetry monitor suddenly displays a run of Torsades de Pointes (polymorphic ventricular tachycardia). The nurse quickly evaluates the client's morning laboratory profile. Which laboratory finding most likely precipitated this lethal arrhythmia?

A) Serum potassium 5.2 mEq/L, Magnesium 1.8 mg/dL, Digoxin 1.0 ng/mL.
B) Serum potassium 4.5 mEq/L, Magnesium 2.2 mg/dL, Digoxin 2.8 ng/mL.
C) Serum potassium 3.8 mEq/L, Magnesium 2.0 mg/dL, Calcium 11.2 mg/dL.
D) Serum potassium 2.9 mEq/L, Magnesium 1.1 mg/dL, Calcium 8.5 mg/dL.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: D</b><br>
Torsades de Pointes is a specific, lethal form of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia that is classically triggered by a prolonged QT interval. The most common electrolyte derangements that cause QT prolongation are severe hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, and hypocalcemia. Aggressive loop diuretic therapy (furosemide) aggressively depletes potassium and magnesium. Option D shows significant hypokalemia (normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L) and hypomagnesemia (normal 1.5-2.5 mg/dL), creating the perfect physiological setup for Torsades de Pointes. Option B shows digoxin toxicity (>2.0 ng/mL), which typically causes atrial tachycardias, AV blocks, frequent PVCs, or bidirectional VT, but is not the classic trigger for Torsades de Pointes.
</details>

---

### Question 39
**Lens: Multi-System Integration**
**System Focus: Cardiogenic Shock**

A client in the intensive care unit has profound cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior myocardial infarction. Current hemodynamics show a Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) of 54 mmHg and a Cardiac Index (CI) of 1.5 L/min/m². Over the last 4 hours, urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr, and serum lactate has risen to 4.2 mmol/L. Which physiological mechanism is directly responsible for the secondary organ dysfunction, and what is the priority advanced nursing action?

A) Severe hypoperfusion leading to acute tubular necrosis; titrate prescribed positive inotropes to optimize cardiac output and restore renal blood flow.
B) Decreased renal perfusion activating the RAAS system; administer a continuous infusion of a loop diuretic to force diuresis and clear lactate.
C) Systemic inflammation causing microvascular thrombosis; prepare the client for continuous renal replacement therapy (CRRT) and heparinization.
D) Right ventricular failure causing severe venous congestion; administer rapid IV fluid boluses to increase right-sided cardiac preload.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: A</b><br>
Cardiogenic shock represents severe "pump failure," evidenced by a critically low Cardiac Index (< 2.2 L/min/m²) and MAP (< 65 mmHg). This primary system failure leads to severe systemic hypoperfusion. The kidneys, highly sensitive to drops in blood flow, develop prerenal acute kidney injury that rapidly progresses to acute tubular necrosis (ATN) if perfusion is not restored (evidenced by oliguria and elevated lactate). The priority intervention for this multi-system failure is to fix the primary cause: improving cardiac output by titrating positive inotropes (e.g., dobutamine, milrinone) to increase myocardial contractility and restore end-organ perfusion. Option B is incorrect because forcing diuresis in a severely hypotensive, shocked client will further deplete intravascular volume and precipitate death. Option C describes the mechanism for sepsis/DIC, not primary cardiogenic shock. Option D is deadly; rapid fluid boluses in cardiogenic shock will overwhelm the failing left ventricle and cause fulminant pulmonary edema. 
</details>

---

### Question 40
**Lens: Emergency Rescue**
**System Focus: Cardiac Tamponade**

A client arrives at the emergency department after a high-speed motor vehicle collision. The client is lethargic, diaphoretic, and severely dyspneic. Vital signs are BP 70/40 mmHg, HR 135 bpm, and SpO2 88%. The nurse observes severe jugular venous distention and muffled heart sounds. The cardiac monitor displays sinus tachycardia with electrical alternans. Which emergency rescue intervention should the nurse anticipate as the definitive life-saving action?

A) Administration of intravenous atropine 1 mg to optimize heart rate.
B) Immediate bedside pericardiocentesis to relieve pericardial pressure.
C) Synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules to correct the electrical alternans.
D) Insertion of a large-bore chest tube at the 5th intercostal space for a hemothorax.

<details>
<summary>Rationale</summary>
<b>Correct Answer: B</b><br>
The client is exhibiting the classic Beck's triad (hypotension, jugular venous distention, and muffled heart sounds), which indicates acute cardiac tamponade. The presence of electrical alternans on the EKG (alternating QRS amplitude from beat to beat) is highly specific for tamponade, caused by the heart "swinging" back and forth within a fluid-filled pericardial sac. The definitive and immediate life-saving rescue intervention for cardiac tamponade is a pericardiocentesis (or pericardial window) to aspirate the fluid, relieve the mechanical compression on the heart, and restore cardiac output. Option A is incorrect as the client is already tachycardic as a compensatory response. Option C is incorrect; electrical alternans is an artifact of mechanical movement, not an arrhythmia requiring electrical cardioversion. Option D is used for a pneumothorax or hemothorax, which would present with absent breath sounds and tracheal deviation rather than muffled heart sounds and electrical alternans.
</details>
### Question 41
The nurse is analyzing the telemetry data of a client admitted with heart failure who suddenly becomes unresponsive and pulseless. The monitor displays a rapid, irregular ventricular rhythm with QRS complexes that continuously change in amplitude, appearing to twist around the isoelectric line. Based on this advanced data analysis, which laboratory value most likely precipitated this specific lethal dysrhythmia?

A) Potassium 6.2 mEq/L  
B) Magnesium 1.1 mEq/L  
C) Calcium 11.5 mg/dL  
D) Sodium 128 mEq/L  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Magnesium 1.1 mEq/L  

**Rationale:** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes, a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by QRS complexes that "twist" around the isoelectric line. Hypomagnesemia (normal range: 1.5–2.5 mEq/L) is a primary trigger for Torsades de Pointes, often secondary to a prolonged QT interval. While hyperkalemia (A) can cause lethal arrhythmias like V-Fib or asystole, it typically presents with peaked T waves and widened QRS complexes, not Torsades. Hypercalcemia (C) shortens the QT interval. Hyponatremia (D) primarily causes neurological changes rather than lethal cardiac arrhythmias. This question applies the **Advanced Data Analysis** lens by requiring the nurse to interpret a lethal EKG rhythm and correlate it with its exact biochemical trigger.

</details>

---

### Question 42
A client is recovering in the cardiovascular intensive care unit 4 hours after a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The chest tube drainage, previously 120 mL/hr, has abruptly ceased over the last 45 minutes. Which subtle clinical assessment finding would be the *earliest* indicator that the client is experiencing a failure to rescue due to impending cardiac tamponade?

A) Muffled, distant heart sounds auscultated over the precordium.  
B) A sudden, significant drop in systolic blood pressure accompanied by bradycardia.  
C) An exaggerated decrease in systolic blood pressure greater than 10 mm Hg during inspiration.  
D) Widening of the pulse pressure on the continuous arterial line monitor.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) An exaggerated decrease in systolic blood pressure greater than 10 mm Hg during inspiration.  

**Rationale:** Sudden cessation of chest tube output post-CABG strongly suggests a clot blocking the tube, leading to blood accumulation in the pericardial space (cardiac tamponade). The earliest and most sensitive sign of impending tamponade is *pulsus paradoxus*—a drop in systolic blood pressure of >10 mm Hg during inspiration. Muffled heart sounds (A) are part of Beck's Triad (hypotension, JVD, muffled heart sounds), which is a classic but *late* sign of tamponade. Tamponade causes tachycardia, not bradycardia (B), as the heart attempts to compensate for a dropping cardiac output. Tamponade produces a *narrowing* pulse pressure, not a widening one (D). This question applies the **Risk of Deterioration** lens by requiring the nurse to identify the subtlest, earliest sign of a life-threatening complication.

</details>

---

### Question 43
A client in a hypertensive crisis is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. Over the last hour, the client's blood pressure has decreased from 230/125 mm Hg to 160/90 mm Hg. The client suddenly reports profound fatigue, tinnitus, and confusion. The nurse notes the client's breath has a faint almond-like odor. Which high-alert pharmacological intervention should the nurse anticipate executing *immediately*?

A) Pause the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.  
B) Increase the nitroprusside infusion rate to treat rebound reflex tachycardia.  
C) Administer a rapid fluid bolus of 0.9% normal saline to increase tissue perfusion.  
D) Administer intravenous labetalol to successfully transition from the nitroprusside drip.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Pause the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.  

**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside is a potent vasodilator used in hypertensive emergencies. A rare but lethal complication of nitroprusside infusion is cyanide toxicity, especially if infused at high rates or in clients with renal impairment. Signs of cyanide toxicity include altered mental status, tinnitus, hyperreflexia, and classically, an almond smell to the breath. The immediate life-saving action is to stop the infusion and administer the antidote, sodium thiosulfate (or amyl nitrite/sodium nitrite). Increasing the rate (B) is contraindicated and will worsen toxicity. Administering fluids (C) or transitioning to labetalol (D) ignores the acute toxicological emergency occurring. This question applies the **High-Alert Pharmacology** lens by testing the nurse's ability to manage an adverse event related to a critical care IV drip.

</details>

---

### Question 44
A client admitted with a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction progresses into cardiogenic shock requiring an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP). The nurse notes the client’s urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr, serum creatinine has risen to 2.8 mg/dL, and arterial blood gases reveal a pH of 7.28, PaCO2 of 32 mm Hg, and HCO3 of 15 mEq/L. Which pathophysiological mechanism best explains this multi-system complication?

A) Impaired right ventricular contractility has caused venous congestion in the liver, leading to a secondary lactic acidosis.  
B) Fluid resuscitation for the cardiogenic shock has caused pulmonary edema, resulting in a primary respiratory acidosis.  
C) The intra-aortic balloon pump has mechanically hemolyzed red blood cells, causing a pre-renal acute kidney injury.  
D) Severe left ventricular dysfunction has triggered systemic hypoperfusion, leading to acute tubular necrosis and a metabolic acidosis.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Severe left ventricular dysfunction has triggered systemic hypoperfusion, leading to acute tubular necrosis and a metabolic acidosis.  

**Rationale:** Cardiogenic shock represents a severe failure of the heart to pump adequately (especially the left ventricle in an anterior MI), resulting in profoundly decreased cardiac output and systemic hypoperfusion. This lack of perfusion to the kidneys causes ischemic damage to the renal tubules (intrarenal acute kidney injury / acute tubular necrosis), indicated by the oliguria and rising creatinine. The failing kidneys are unable to excrete acid or reabsorb bicarbonate, leading to a metabolic acidosis (low pH, low HCO3, with a compensatory drop in PaCO2). The ABGs do not show a respiratory acidosis (B). While IABPs can cause hemolysis, this causes intrarenal, not pre-renal, AKI (C). Right ventricular failure causes systemic congestion, but the anterior MI and ABG picture strongly point to left-sided failure causing hypoperfusion and renal failure. This question applies the **Multi-System Integration** lens by assessing how cardiac failure precipitates renal failure and acid-base derangement.

</details>

---

### Question 45
A client on a step-down telemetry unit experiences a sudden cardiac arrest. The emergency crash cart is immediately at the bedside, and the rhythm displayed on the defibrillator pads reveals chaotic, disorganized waves with no identifiable P waves, QRS complexes, or T waves. The client has no palpable pulse. Which sequence of emergency rescue actions is the highest priority?

A) Administer epinephrine 1 mg IV push, followed by continuous chest compressions.  
B) Deliver an immediate unsynchronized shock, followed instantly by chest compressions.  
C) Perform synchronized cardioversion, check for a pulse, and then begin chest compressions.  
D) Intubate the client, establish intravenous access, and administer amiodarone.  

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Deliver an immediate unsynchronized shock, followed instantly by chest compressions.  

**Rationale:** The rhythm described is coarse ventricular fibrillation (V-Fib), a lethal arrhythmia requiring immediate defibrillation. According to ACLS guidelines, if a witnessed cardiac arrest occurs and the defibrillator is immediately available, the absolute highest priority is to deliver an unsynchronized shock (defibrillation) as quickly as possible. Every minute of delay in defibrillation drastically reduces the chance of survival. Instantly after the shock is delivered, high-quality chest compressions must be initiated for 2 minutes before checking a pulse or rhythm. Synchronized cardioversion (C) is inappropriate because there are no recognizable R-waves to synchronize with in V-Fib. Medications and intubation (A, D) are secondary interventions that follow CPR and early defibrillation.
