This question applies the **Emergency Rescue** lens by testing the definitive, immediate life-saving action during a sudden systemic collapse.

</details>
### Question 46
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION**

The nurse receives the shift handoff report for four clients on the cardiovascular step-down unit. Which client should the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with chronic heart failure who reports a dry cough, increased fatigue, and a weight gain of 1 kg over the last 24 hours.  
B) A client with permanent atrial fibrillation receiving a continuous diltiazem infusion who has a heart rate of 98 beats/min and a blood pressure of 110/70 mm Hg.  
C) A client 2 days post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) who has developed muffled heart sounds, jugular venous distention, and a narrowed pulse pressure.  
D) A client admitted with a hypertensive crisis whose blood pressure decreased from 220/120 mm Hg to 175/95 mm Hg after receiving intravenous labetalol.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
- **Option C is correct.** This client is exhibiting Beck’s triad (muffled heart sounds, jugular venous distention, and narrowed pulse pressure), which are hallmark clinical signs of cardiac tamponade. Cardiac tamponade is a life-threatening emergency where fluid accumulates in the pericardial sac, compressing the heart and severely dropping cardiac output. This requires immediate life-saving intervention (pericardiocentesis or surgical window).
- **Option A is incorrect.** While weight gain and a cough indicate an exacerbation of heart failure requiring intervention (such as a diuretic adjustment), it is a subacute issue compared to cardiac tamponade.
- **Option B is incorrect.** A heart rate of 98 bpm and normal blood pressure indicate that the diltiazem drip is successfully controlling the ventricular rate in this client with atrial fibrillation. The client is stable.
- **Option D is incorrect.** The goal in a hypertensive crisis is a controlled, gradual reduction in blood pressure (typically no more than 20-25% in the first hour) to prevent cerebral, coronary, or renal ischemia. This client's blood pressure is trending appropriately and safely.

</details>

---

### Question 47
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client in a hypertensive emergency is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The nurse notes that the client's blood pressure has dropped precipitously from 230/130 mm Hg to 135/75 mm Hg over 15 minutes. The client suddenly reports profound weakness, ringing in the ears (tinnitus), and exhibits acute confusion. Which action should the nurse take immediately?

A) Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion and notify the rapid response team.  
B) Decrease the infusion rate by half and monitor the client's neurological status closely.  
C) Administer a rapid PRN bolus of 500 mL normal saline to correct the sudden hypotension.  
D) Obtain a stat 12-lead electrocardiogram to assess for silent myocardial ischemia.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
- **Option A is correct.** Sodium nitroprusside is a highly potent vasodilator. Dropping the blood pressure too rapidly (more than 25% in the first hour) can lead to severe cerebral hypoperfusion and ischemia, manifesting as profound weakness and altered mental status. Furthermore, tinnitus, confusion, and weakness are early signs of thiocyanate/cyanide toxicity, a known, life-threatening complication of nitroprusside therapy. The immediate required action is to stop the high-alert medication and escalate to the rapid response team.
- **Option B is incorrect.** Decreasing the rate by half is insufficient. The client is demonstrating signs of severe adverse effects (possible cerebral ischemia and thiocyanate toxicity) requiring immediate cessation of the drug.
- **Option C is incorrect.** While fluids may eventually be ordered, blindly bolusing a patient who recently had a blood pressure of 230/130 mm Hg poses a massive risk for flash pulmonary edema. Stopping the offending agent is the primary intervention.
- **Option D is incorrect.** An ECG may be necessary later to rule out ischemia, but it delays the immediate life-saving action of stopping the continuous medication causing the acute decompensation.

</details>

---

### Question 48
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is in cardiogenic shock following an extensive anterior wall myocardial infarction and is currently receiving continuous infusions of dobutamine and norepinephrine. Which assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the cardiovascular failure is now causing a secondary organ system failure?

A) A central venous pressure (CVP) reading of 14 mm Hg.  
B) An arterial blood gas (ABG) showing a PaO2 of 88 mm Hg on a 2L nasal cannula.  
C) Sinus tachycardia at 115 beats per minute with occasional premature ventricular contractions (PVCs).  
D) Urine output dropping to 15 mL/hr over two hours with an acutely increasing serum creatinine.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
- **Option D is correct.** Cardiogenic shock causes profound systemic hypoperfusion. The kidneys are highly sensitive to decreased cardiac output and MAP. A urine output of less than 30 mL/hr (or <0.5 mL/kg/hr) combined with an increasing serum creatinine indicates Acute Kidney Injury (AKI), representing failure of the renal system secondary to cardiovascular pump failure.
- **Option A is incorrect.** A CVP of 14 mm Hg is elevated (normal 2-8 mm Hg) and reflects right-sided heart pressures and volume overload, which is an expected primary finding in severe cardiogenic shock, not a sign of a secondary organ failing.
- **Option B is incorrect.** A PaO2 of 88 mm Hg is within normal limits (80-100 mm Hg) and does not indicate respiratory failure.
- **Option C is incorrect.** Sinus tachycardia with PVCs is a cardiac manifestation related to the myocardial infarction, cardiogenic shock, and the inotropic/vasopressor effects of dobutamine and norepinephrine. It does not indicate failure of a secondary organ system.

</details>

---

### Question 49
**LENS: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**

A client admitted with suspected acute myocardial infarction suddenly becomes unresponsive. The nurse looks at the bedside telemetry monitor and notes a wide-complex tachycardia where the QRS complexes gradually change in polarity and amplitude, appearing to twist around the isoelectric line. The client has no palpable pulse. What is the nurse's interpretation of the rhythm and immediate priority action?

A) Ventricular fibrillation; immediately administer 1 mg of epinephrine intravenous push.  
B) Torsades de Pointes; initiate cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) and prepare for unsynchronized defibrillation.  
C) Polymorphic ventricular tachycardia; prepare the client for immediate synchronized cardioversion.  
D) Pulseless electrical activity (PEA); initiate high-quality CPR and assess for the H's and T's.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
- **Option B is correct.** The description of wide QRS complexes changing in amplitude and polarity, "twisting" around the isoelectric line, is the classic hallmark of Torsades de Pointes (a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia). Because the client is pulseless, this is treated as a cardiac arrest. The immediate ACLS priority is high-quality CPR and unsynchronized defibrillation. 
- **Option A is incorrect.** The description is not ventricular fibrillation (which appears as chaotic, undulating waves with no discernible QRS complexes). Furthermore, in a shockable rhythm arrest, CPR and defibrillation take priority over epinephrine administration.
- **Option C is incorrect.** While Torsades is a polymorphic VT, synchronized cardioversion is contraindicated for a pulseless client. Pulselessness requires unsynchronized defibrillation.
- **Option D is incorrect.** The rhythm described is an organized, albeit lethal, electrical tachycardia, not PEA. PEA is defined by any organized rhythm lacking a palpable pulse that is *not* VT or VFib. 

</details>

---

### Question 50
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A client admitted with an acute inferior myocardial infarction develops sudden dizziness, heavy diaphoresis, and severe dyspnea. The bedside cardiac monitor displays independent atrial activity at 90 beats/min and a wide ventricular escape rhythm at 30 beats/min with no relationship between the P waves and QRS complexes. The client is lethargic, and the blood pressure is 68/40 mm Hg. What is the most critical immediate life-saving action the nurse must take?

A) Administer intravenous atropine 1 mg via rapid push.  
B) Prepare to administer a continuous intravenous dopamine infusion.  
C) Apply transcutaneous pacing pads and initiate pacing.  
D) Administer a 500 mL normal saline fluid bolus over 15 minutes.  

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
- **Option C is correct.** The client is exhibiting a Third-Degree (Complete) Atrioventricular Block (independent atrial and ventricular rates) complicated by profound hemodynamic instability (lethargy, severe hypotension). According to ACLS guidelines, for severe symptomatic bradycardia or complete heart block with wide QRS complexes, immediate transcutaneous pacing is the definitive life-saving intervention. 
- **Option A is incorrect.** Atropine is typically ineffective for Third-Degree AV blocks with a wide QRS complex (infranodal block) and should not delay pacing in a profoundly unstable client who is lethargic and hypotensive.
- **Option B is incorrect.** While a dopamine or epinephrine infusion may be used as a second-line measure if pacing fails or while awaiting a transvenous pacemaker, applying transcutaneous pacing pads is faster and the immediate priority for an acutely crashing patient.
- **Option D is incorrect.** While right ventricular infarction (common in inferior MI) can cause hypotension requiring fluids, the primary driver of this client's shock is profound bradycardia from the complete heart block. Fixing the heart rate via pacing is the absolute priority to restore cardiac output.

</details>
### Question 51
The nurse is reviewing the telemetry monitor of an unresponsive client admitted with severe alcohol use disorder and palpitations. The EKG displays a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with the QRS complexes continuously twisting around the isoelectric line. Laboratory data obtained 30 minutes prior reveals a Potassium of 3.1 mEq/L, Magnesium of 1.0 mg/dL, and Calcium of 8.2 mg/dL. The client has no palpable pulse. Applying advanced data analysis, which intervention must the nurse execute immediately?

A) Administer IV amiodarone 300 mg rapid push to stabilize the ventricular rhythm.
B) Deliver synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules to restore an organized rhythm.
C) Defibrillate the client immediately and prepare to administer IV magnesium sulfate.
D) Push IV calcium gluconate to stabilize the myocardium followed by continuous CPR.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
This client is experiencing Torsades de Pointes, a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by QRS complexes twisting around the isoelectric line. It is highly associated with a prolonged QT interval, often caused by severe hypomagnesemia (normal: 1.5-2.5 mEq/L). Because the client is pulseless, this is a lethal arrhythmia requiring immediate defibrillation (unsynchronized shock) and high-quality CPR, followed by the specific pharmacological antidote: IV magnesium sulfate. Synchronized cardioversion (Option B) is impossible as the monitor cannot sync to polymorphic waves. Amiodarone (Option A) can further prolong the QT interval and is contraindicated in Torsades de Pointes. Calcium gluconate (Option D) is used for hyperkalemia or hypocalcemia, but it does not correct the underlying magnesium deficit causing this specific lethal rhythm.

**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting lethal EKG rhythms, compensated ABGs, or toxic labs)
</details>

---

### Question 52
A client is 4 hours post-coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG). The nurse notes that the mediastinal chest tube drainage, which had been steadily measuring 150 mL/hr, has abruptly ceased over the last 30 minutes. The client's blood pressure drops from 118/72 mmHg to 86/68 mmHg, the heart rate increases to 124 bpm, and the central venous pressure (CVP) rises rapidly from 8 mm Hg to 18 mm Hg. Heart sounds are distant on auscultation. What is the nurse's immediate emergency rescue action?

A) Prepare the client for an immediate sternotomy or pericardiocentesis while notifying the surgeon.
B) Aggressively strip or milk the mediastinal chest tubes to dislodge the suspected clot.
C) Administer a rapid 500 mL normal saline bolus to compensate for the sudden drop in cardiac output.
D) Initiate a continuous IV infusion of norepinephrine to maintain a mean arterial pressure > 65 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting classic signs of acute cardiac tamponade (Beck's triad: hypotension, jugular venous distention/elevated CVP, and muffled heart sounds), combined with a sudden cessation of chest tube drainage, indicating an accumulation of blood in the pericardial space. This is a life-threatening emergency causing immediate cardiovascular collapse. The definitive life-saving rescue action is decompression of the pericardium via pericardiocentesis or surgical reopening of the sternotomy. Aggressively stripping chest tubes (Option B) is contraindicated as it generates dangerously high negative pressures that can damage tissue and exacerbate bleeding. While fluids (Option C) or vasopressors (Option D) might be temporarily used to support blood pressure as a bridge, they do not resolve the mechanical compression of the heart and are not the primary, definitive rescue actions.

**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)
</details>

---

### Question 53
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with a hypertensive emergency, presenting with a blood pressure of 240/140 mmHg, confusion, and papilledema indicating hypertensive encephalopathy. The provider orders a continuous IV infusion of sodium nitroprusside. Which advanced pharmacological principle must strictly guide the nurse's titration of this high-alert medication?

A) Titrate the infusion to achieve a target blood pressure of 120/80 mmHg within the first 2 hours.
B) Monitor closely for cyanide toxicity, which presents primarily as profound bradycardia and hypokalemia.
C) Ensure the IV bag is wrapped in an opaque cover to prevent crystallization of the medication in the tubing.
D) Decrease the mean arterial pressure (MAP) by no more than 20-25% in the first hour to prevent cerebral ischemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
In a hypertensive crisis, rapidly lowering the blood pressure can lead to severe hypoperfusion of the brain, heart, and kidneys, resulting in ischemic stroke, myocardial infarction, or acute renal failure. The standard of care is to reduce the MAP by no more than 20% to 25% within the first hour of treatment. Option A is dangerous because dropping the blood pressure to normotensive levels (120/80) too rapidly causes massive ischemia. Option B is incorrect in its symptom description; while sodium nitroprusside can cause cyanide/thiocyanate toxicity, it primarily presents as altered mental status, lactic acidosis, and almond breath, not profound bradycardia and hypokalemia. Option C is partially true in that nitroprusside requires an opaque cover to protect it from light, but light exposure degrades the medication into cyanide, it does not cause "crystallization." 

**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips like Heparin, Insulin, or Nitro in a crisis)
</details>

---

### Question 54
A client is deteriorating from severe cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. The client is receiving continuous IV dobutamine and norepinephrine. The nurse assesses bilateral crackles halfway up the lung fields, a respiratory rate of 34/min, a PaO2 of 54 mmHg on a 100% non-rebreather mask, and a urine output of 15 mL/hr over the last two hours. BUN is 45 mg/dL and Creatinine is 2.8 mg/dL. Recognizing the multi-system cascade currently occurring, what is the most appropriate integrated management strategy?

A) Prepare for endotracheal intubation to decrease the work of breathing and anticipate therapies like ultrafiltration or CRRT.
B) Rapidly infuse a 1000 mL bolus of lactated Ringer's to increase renal perfusion and reverse the pre-renal acute kidney injury.
C) Discontinue the dobutamine infusion immediately to reduce myocardial oxygen demand and prevent further pulmonary edema.
D) Administer high-dose IV furosemide push to aggressively treat the fluid overload causing the anuria and hypoxemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
The client is experiencing severe multi-system failure stemming from cardiogenic shock. Left ventricular failure has caused fluid backup leading to pulmonary edema/acute respiratory failure (hypoxia refractory to oxygen therapy) and severe forward-flow reduction causing acute kidney injury (oliguria, elevated BUN/Cr). Intubation and mechanical ventilation are necessary to improve oxygenation, reduce the massive work of breathing, and decrease overall metabolic demand. Because the kidneys are failing, high-dose loop diuretics (Option D) are unlikely to be effective and could further compromise intravascular volume/perfusion; ultrafiltration or Continuous Renal Replacement Therapy (CRRT) is indicated. IV fluid boluses (Option B) are strongly contraindicated in cardiogenic shock and pulmonary edema, as they will drown the client. Discontinuing dobutamine (Option C) would be fatal, as this inotrope is currently keeping the severely damaged left ventricle pumping.

**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)
</details>

---

### Question 55
A client with symptomatic third-degree AV block has just returned to the critical care unit following the emergent placement of a temporary transvenous pacemaker. The client's underlying rhythm is inherently unstable. The registered nurse (RN) is working with an experienced Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN) and an Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP). Which delegation decision by the RN is UNSAFE and requires immediate intervention?

A) Directing the UAP to assist the client with passive range of motion exercises strictly on the non-insertion side.
B) Assigning the LPN to independently evaluate the telemetry tracing for continuous 1:1 pacemaker capture and sensing.
C) Having the LPN administer a scheduled subcutaneous dose of enoxaparin for deep vein thrombosis prophylaxis.
D) Instructing the UAP to obtain and document the client's continuous vital signs every 15 minutes.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
Evaluating complex, potentially lethal telemetry rhythms—especially confirming continuous capture and sensing in a newly placed temporary transvenous pacemaker—requires advanced clinical judgment, assessment, and rapid intervention. This is strictly within the scope of practice of the RN and is UNSAFE to delegate to an LPN, regardless of their experience. If the pacemaker loses capture, the client will immediately revert to a lethal third-degree block. Options A and D are safe to delegate to the UAP, as they involve routine, standard procedures (vital signs and positioning) that do not require nursing judgment. Option C is a safe task to delegate to the LPN, as medication administration (subcutaneous injections) falls within their defined scope of practice.

**Lens:** COMPLEX DELEGATION (Deciding what tasks are UNSAFE to delegate in an unstable system environment)
</details>

---
### Question 56
A client is in a hypertensive emergency with a blood pressure of 240/130 mm Hg and acute encephalopathy. The nurse is titrating a continuous IV sodium nitroprusside infusion in the intensive care unit. Which assessment finding requires the nurse to immediately halt the infusion and notify the healthcare provider?

A) The blood pressure decreases to 180/100 mm Hg within the first 60 minutes of the infusion.
B) The client complains of a mild, generalized headache and drowsiness.
C) The client's mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) unexpectedly rises to 88% alongside a rising serum lactate.
D) The client exhibits a steady heart rate increase from 85 bpm to 102 bpm.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips like Nitroprusside in a crisis)
**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolism naturally produces cyanide. Prolonged or high-dose infusions can lead to cyanide toxicity, which paralyzes cellular respiration (cytochrome c oxidase), preventing cells from extracting oxygen from the blood. This results in an abnormally high mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2 > 80%) because the oxygen returns to the venous system unused, alongside a profound lactic acidosis and altered mental status. Option A is the targeted therapeutic goal (dropping MAP by no more than 20-25% in the first hour). Option B can be related to the underlying encephalopathy or the vasodilatory effect. Option D represents expected reflex tachycardia from rapid vasodilation.
</details>

---

### Question 57
A client in profound cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior myocardial infarction is receiving continuous dobutamine and has an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) in place. Over the last three hours, the nurse notes a urine output of 12 mL/hr, a serum creatinine spike to 2.4 mg/dL, and newly developed fine crackles in the bilateral lung bases. What is the pathobiological explanation for these integrated multi-system failures?

A) The IABP is positioned too high in the aorta, occluding the renal arteries and causing pre-renal azotemia.
B) Profound left ventricular pump failure has caused forward hypoperfusion to the kidneys and backward failure into the pulmonary circuit. 
C) The dobutamine infusion is causing profound splanchnic vasoconstriction, leading to acute tubular necrosis.
D) Rapid fluid shifting from the interstitial space into the intravascular space has overloaded both the kidneys and the lungs.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)
**Rationale:** Cardiogenic shock is characterized by severe pump failure of the left ventricle. This causes a dual-sided crisis: a lack of "forward flow" leading to systemic hypoperfusion (triggering acute kidney injury, evidenced by oliguria and elevated creatinine), and a "backward backup" of hydrostatic pressure into the left atrium and pulmonary veins (causing pulmonary edema, evidenced by crackles). Option A is incorrect; an IABP placed too *low* occludes the renal arteries, while one placed too *high* occludes the left subclavian. Option C is incorrect; dobutamine is an inotrope and mild vasodilator, not a severe splanchnic vasoconstrictor (unlike high-dose vasopressin or norepinephrine). Option D describes an osmotic shift, not the hallmark pump-failure mechanism of cardiogenic shock.
</details>

---

### Question 58
A client is recovering in the cardiovascular ICU 12 hours after a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The mediastinal chest tubes, which previously drained 100 mL/hr of serosanguinous fluid, have suddenly drained 0 mL in the last hour. Which early assessment finding should the nurse identify as the most definitive indicator of impending cardiac tamponade?

A) A decrease in systolic blood pressure of 16 mm Hg during inspiration.
B) Development of distant, muffled heart sounds on auscultation.
C) A widening pulse pressure with bounding peripheral pulses.
D) The abrupt appearance of prominent U waves on the bedside telemetry monitor.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')
**Rationale:** A sudden cessation of chest tube drainage after open-heart surgery strongly suggests a clot or blockage, risking blood accumulation in the pericardial space (cardiac tamponade). Pulsus paradoxus (an exaggerated drop in systolic BP >10 mm Hg during inspiration) is a hallmark, *early* sign of cardiac tamponade indicating compromised ventricular filling. Muffled heart sounds (Beck's triad) (Option B) are a classic but usually *late* sign of severe tamponade. Tamponade produces a *narrowing* pulse pressure, not a widening one (Option C). Prominent U waves (Option D) indicate hypokalemia, not tamponade.
</details>

---

### Question 59
A client with a history of severe heart failure is admitted for an acute exacerbation and is receiving aggressive IV furosemide therapy. The bedside monitor suddenly alarms, displaying a rapid, irregular ventricular rhythm with QRS complexes that appear to twist around the isoelectric line. What is the nurse's immediate priority intervention?

A) Administer a rapid IV push of amiodarone 300 mg to stabilize the ventricular rate.
B) Prepare to administer IV magnesium sulfate and immediately assess the client for a pulse.
C) Initiate synchronized biphasic cardioversion at 100 Joules.
D) Push IV atropine 1 mg to increase the underlying sinus node automaticity.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting lethal EKG rhythms)
**Rationale:** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (polymorphic ventricular tachycardia), which is often triggered by a prolonged QT interval due to severe electrolyte imbalances (hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia) secondary to aggressive loop diuretic therapy (furosemide). The priority is to assess for a pulse (as this rhythm frequently causes hemodynamic collapse) and prepare IV magnesium sulfate, the first-line pharmacological treatment. Amiodarone (Option A) is contraindicated because it prolongs the QT interval and can worsen Torsades. Synchronized cardioversion (Option C) is impossible because the monitor cannot easily identify a single R wave in polymorphic VT; if the client is pulseless, immediate *unsynchronized* defibrillation is required. Atropine (Option D) is used for symptomatic bradycardia.
</details>

---

### Question 60
A client with severe cardiac tamponade has just undergone an emergency bedside pericardiocentesis. Which combination of hemodynamic parameters provides the most definitive evidence that the life-saving intervention was successful?

A) Increased heart rate, increased pulmonary artery wedge pressure (PAWP), and flattened jugular veins.
B) Decreased cardiac output, increased right atrial pressure (RAP), and a widening pulse pressure.
C) Decreased systemic vascular resistance (SVR), elevated central venous pressure (CVP), and absent pulsus paradoxus.
D) Increased mean arterial pressure (MAP), decreased central venous pressure (CVP), and increased hourly urine output.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS (Identifying physical signs that a rescue intervention was successful)
**Rationale:** Cardiac tamponade severely compresses the heart chambers, preventing adequate venous return (resulting in elevated CVP/RAP) and drastically reducing cardiac output (resulting in low MAP and poor organ perfusion/oliguria). A successful pericardiocentesis instantly relieves this compression. The nurse will evaluate success by observing a decrease in central venous pressure (blood can now enter the right atrium freely), an increase in mean arterial pressure (stroke volume is restored), and signs of restored end-organ perfusion (increased urine output). Options A, B, and C describe worsening hemodynamics or incorrect pathological responses for a resolving tamponade.
</details>
### Question 61
A client with a large pericardial effusion is recovering in the intensive care unit immediately following an emergent pericardiocentesis for acute cardiac tamponade. When evaluating the success of this crisis intervention, which physical assessment finding provides the best evidence that the client's hemodynamics have stabilized?

A) Narrowing of the pulse pressure occurs during inspiration.
B) Muffled heart sounds persist but distant murmurs become audible.
C) Pulsus paradoxus resolves and systolic blood pressure remains consistent.
D) Jugular venous distention (JVD) increases when the head of the bed is lowered.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS (#8)

**Rationale:** 
Cardiac tamponade occurs when fluid accumulates in the pericardial space, restricting ventricular filling and decreasing cardiac output. Classic signs include Beck's Triad (hypotension, JVD, muffled heart sounds) and pulsus paradoxus (a drop in systolic blood pressure of >10 mm Hg during inspiration). A successful pericardiocentesis relieves the pressure on the heart. Therefore, the resolution of pulsus paradoxus and stabilization of systolic blood pressure are the primary indicators that normal ventricular filling and stroke volume have been restored. Narrowing pulse pressure (A) and persistent muffled heart sounds (B) are signs of ongoing or worsening tamponade. Increased JVD when supine (D) is a normal physiologic finding or indicates continued right-sided failure, not a sign of successful resolution.

</details>

---

### Question 62
A client in a hypertensive emergency is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The client's initial blood pressure was 230/130 mm Hg (MAP: ~163 mm Hg). Thirty minutes after initiation of the drip, the blood pressure drops to 140/80 mm Hg (MAP: ~100 mm Hg), and the client reports new-onset dizziness, slurred speech, and lethargy. Which action should the nurse take immediately?

A) Prepare to administer IV thiocyanate to reverse potential cyanide toxicity.
B) Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate to allow a gradual rise in blood pressure.
C) Administer an IV bolus of 0.9% normal saline to quickly increase intravascular volume.
D) Increase the infusion rate as the blood pressure is not yet within the normal baseline range.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (#6)

**Rationale:** 
When managing a hypertensive emergency, the clinical goal is to lower the Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) by no more than 20-25% within the first hour. A rapid or excessive drop in blood pressure can cause cerebral, coronary, or renal ischemia because autoregulatory mechanisms in chronically hypertensive clients require higher pressures to maintain perfusion. The client's MAP dropped from ~163 to ~100 (a nearly 40% drop in 30 minutes), and the new-onset dizziness, slurred speech, and lethargy are signs of acute cerebral hypoperfusion (ischemia). The immediate action is to decrease the rate of the potent vasodilator to allow the pressure to rise to a safer level. Cyanide/thiocyanate toxicity (A) is a risk with prolonged nitroprusside use, but does not occur within 30 minutes. Fluid boluses (C) are not the primary treatment for titratable drug-induced hypotension. Increasing the rate (D) would cause a lethal ischemic stroke or myocardial infarction.

</details>

---

### Question 63
A client is admitted to the cardiovascular intensive care unit with profound cardiogenic shock following an extensive anterior wall myocardial infarction. The nurse notes new-onset oliguria (15 mL/hr), an elevated blood urea nitrogen (BUN) of 45 mg/dL, and a serum creatinine of 2.6 mg/dL. Which pathophysiological mechanism best explains how this primary cardiovascular failure is driving the failure of the renal system?

A) Aggressive diuretic therapy used for the myocardial infarction has directly caused acute tubular necrosis.
B) Compensatory parasympathetic nervous system activation is causing profound systemic vasodilation and organ pooling.
C) Right ventricular failure is causing systemic venous congestion, leading to renal vein thrombosis and post-renal failure.
D) Decreased forward cardiac output stimulates the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS), exacerbating renal ischemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (#5)

**Rationale:** 
Cardiogenic shock involves severe failure of the heart's pumping ability (often due to an anterior MI), resulting in a drastically decreased forward cardiac output. This lack of forward flow leads to hypoperfusion of the kidneys (pre-renal acute kidney injury). In response to low renal blood flow, the kidneys secrete renin, activating the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS). Angiotensin II causes intense systemic vasoconstriction, which shunts blood away from the kidneys (worsening renal ischemia) while simultaneously increasing afterload, which further strains the already failing heart. Shock causes sympathetic (not parasympathetic) activation (B). While right-sided failure causes venous congestion, the acute pre-renal failure in left-sided cardiogenic shock is primarily driven by lack of forward flow, not sudden renal vein thrombosis (C). Diuretics (A) can cause hypovolemia, but the primary driver of this multi-system failure in active cardiogenic shock is pump failure leading to RAAS-mediated hypoperfusion.

</details>

---

### Question 64
The central telemetry monitor alarms with a critical alert for a client admitted with severe hypomagnesemia. The nurse enters the room and observes polymorphic ventricular tachycardia (Torsades de Pointes) on the bedside monitor. The client is unresponsive, apneic, and has no palpable carotid pulse. What is the nurse's immediate, life-saving rescue action?

A) Perform immediate unsynchronized cardioversion (defibrillation) and begin CPR.
B) Administer an IV bolus of 2 grams of magnesium sulfate over 5 minutes.
C) Prepare for synchronized cardioversion starting at 50 Joules to organize the rhythm.
D) Administer Amiodarone 300 mg rapid IV push to chemically convert the arrhythmia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (#2)

**Rationale:** 
Torsades de Pointes is a lethal form of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. Although magnesium sulfate is the pharmacological treatment of choice for *pulsatile* or stable Torsades de Pointes, this client is unresponsive, apneic, and pulseless. A pulseless client with ventricular tachycardia or ventricular fibrillation requires immediate high-quality CPR and unsynchronized cardioversion (defibrillation) according to Advanced Cardiac Life Support (ACLS) guidelines. Synchronized cardioversion (C) is impossible in polymorphic VT because the machine cannot identify a single, consistent R-wave to synchronize with, and it is contraindicated in pulseless arrest. Medications such as Magnesium (B) or Amiodarone (D) are secondary interventions administered only *after* CPR and defibrillation have been initiated.

</details>

---

### Question 65
The charge nurse receives the shift report on a high-acuity cardiac step-down unit. Which unstable cardiovascular client must the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with cardiogenic shock on a dobutamine infusion whose urine output was 25 mL over the last hour.
B) A client with chronic atrial fibrillation reporting sudden palpitations with an irregular apical pulse of 115 beats per minute.
C) A client recovering from coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) who has new-onset distant, muffled heart sounds and narrowed pulse pressure.
D) A client recovering from a myocardial infarction who exhibits occasional, unifocal premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) on telemetry.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION (#1)

**Rationale:** 
Using the prioritization framework of ABCs and immediately life-threatening conditions, the client recovering from CABG with new-onset muffled heart sounds and narrowed pulse pressure (C) is exhibiting classic signs of acute cardiac tamponade. This is a rapid, life-threatening emergency that leads to cardiovascular collapse and pulseless electrical activity (PEA) if not treated immediately with pericardiocentesis or surgical re-exploration. While the client in cardiogenic shock (A) has low urine output, this is an expected, chronic finding of poor perfusion being managed with an inotrope (dobutamine); they are unstable but being actively managed. Palpitations with a heart rate of 115 in a client with known atrial fibrillation (B) requires assessment but is not immediately lethal. Occasional unifocal PVCs (D) are a common and largely benign finding post-myocardial infarction as the myocardium is irritable. 

</details>
### Question 66
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?)**

The nurse receives the shift report on a step-down cardiac intensive care unit. Which client should the nurse assess **first**?

A) A client with a hypertensive emergency receiving an esmolol infusion who complains of a sudden onset of severe, tearing pain radiating to the back.
B) A client with cardiogenic shock on a continuous dobutamine infusion whose urine output was 20 mL over the last hour.
C) A client two days post-myocardial infarction whose cardiac monitor shows an occasional premature ventricular contraction (PVC).
D) A client with acute pericarditis who is leaning forward to relieve chest pain, with a current blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** A sudden onset of severe, "tearing" back pain in a client with a hypertensive emergency is the hallmark clinical presentation of an acute aortic dissection. This is a catastrophic, rapidly fatal event that requires immediate surgical intervention and aggressive, targeted blood pressure control to prevent aortic rupture. While the client with cardiogenic shock (Option B) requires close monitoring for poor organ perfusion (oliguria), this is an expected finding of their ongoing condition and is secondary to a potential systemic rupture. Occasional PVCs (Option C) are common post-MI and do not require immediate intervention unless they become frequent or multiform. Leaning forward to relieve pain (Option D) is an expected finding and a typical compensatory position for acute pericarditis.
</details>

---

### Question 67
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)**

A client admitted in a hypertensive crisis (initial blood pressure 240/130 mmHg) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The nurse monitors the client closely for signs of severe drug toxicity. Which clinical manifestation requires the nurse to **immediately** discontinue the infusion and notify the rapid response team?

A) A decrease in blood pressure to 160/90 mmHg over the first hour of the infusion.
B) Flushed, warm skin and client complaints of a mild headache.
C) A gradual heart rate increase from 82 beats per minute to 98 beats per minute.
D) Unexplained altered mental status, absent reflexes, and profound metabolic acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside is a potent vasodilator metabolized into cyanide and thiocyanate in the body. Prolonged use or high doses can lead to severe cyanide toxicity. Hallmark signs of cyanide toxicity include unexplained altered mental status, absent reflexes, seizures, and profound metabolic acidosis (lactic acidemia) due to the blockade of cellular aerobic metabolism. The infusion must be stopped immediately and the antidote (such as sodium thiosulfate) prepared. A decrease in BP (Option A) is the intended therapeutic effect. Flushing and mild headache (Option B) are common, expected side effects of potent vasodilation. A reflex increase in heart rate (Option C) is a normal physiological compensatory response to a rapid drop in blood pressure.
</details>

---

### Question 68
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting hemodynamics in a system crisis)**

A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with profound cardiogenic shock following an extensive anterior wall myocardial infarction. A pulmonary artery (PA) catheter is placed for hemodynamic monitoring. Which set of baseline hemodynamic parameters should the nurse identify as strongly correlating with this client's condition?

A) Cardiac Output (CO) 6.5 L/min; Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR) 600 dynes/sec/cm⁻⁵; Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP) 8 mmHg.
B) Cardiac Output (CO) 2.5 L/min; Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR) 1800 dynes/sec/cm⁻⁵; Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP) 24 mmHg.
C) Cardiac Output (CO) 3.0 L/min; Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR) 1400 dynes/sec/cm⁻⁵; Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP) 4 mmHg.
D) Cardiac Output (CO) 8.0 L/min; Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR) 800 dynes/sec/cm⁻⁵; Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP) 12 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** In cardiogenic shock, the heart fails as a pump, leading to a significantly reduced Cardiac Output (CO < 4 L/min). As blood backs up into the pulmonary circulation due to left ventricular failure, the Pulmonary Artery Wedge Pressure (PAWP) elevates significantly (normal is 6-12 mmHg; > 18 mmHg indicates left-sided failure/congestion). To compensate for the low cardiac output and maintain perfusion pressure, the sympathetic nervous system triggers intense peripheral vasoconstriction, leading to a highly elevated Systemic Vascular Resistance (SVR > 1200 dynes/sec/cm⁻⁵). Option A describes distributive (septic) shock. Option C describes hypovolemic shock. Option D reflects a hyperdynamic state, not cardiogenic shock.
</details>

---

### Question 69
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)**

The nurse is caring for a client with a temporary transvenous pacemaker inserted for third-degree atrioventricular (AV) block. Suddenly, the cardiac monitor displays coarse ventricular fibrillation, and the client becomes unresponsive with no palpable central pulse. What is the nurse's **most** immediate life-saving action?

A) Increase the output (mA) on the temporary pacemaker generator to capture the ventricles.
B) Administer 1 mg of epinephrine via rapid intravenous push.
C) Initiate high-quality cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) and prepare for immediate defibrillation.
D) Check the pacemaker leads for secure connections and proper electrical grounding.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** Ventricular fibrillation is a highly lethal arrhythmia characterized by completely disorganized, chaotic electrical activity in the ventricles resulting in zero cardiac output. The definitive, primary life-saving rescue for pulseless ventricular fibrillation is early defibrillation combined with high-quality CPR. A pacemaker (Option A) provides isolated electrical impulses and cannot override or terminate ventricular fibrillation; in fact, the pacer will fail to capture the chaotic myocardium. Epinephrine (Option B) is secondary to CPR and defibrillation. Troubleshooting the pacemaker (Option D) wastes precious time during a sudden cardiac arrest and is entirely inappropriate for a lethal V-Fib rhythm.
</details>

---

### Question 70
**Lens: EVALUATION OF CRISIS (Identifying physical signs that a rescue intervention was successful)**

A client presenting with a penetrating chest wound develops rapid-onset cardiac tamponade. The emergency physician performs an urgent bedside pericardiocentesis, draining 150 mL of non-clotting blood. Which clinical finding indicates to the nurse that the intervention was successful in reversing the hemodynamic crisis?

A) The client exhibits a paradoxical blood pressure drop of 18 mmHg during inspiration.
B) The client's pulse pressure widens and jugular venous distention (JVD) resolves.
C) The client's central venous pressure (CVP) increases from 8 mmHg to 14 mmHg.
D) The client's apical heart sounds become distinctly muffled and distant upon auscultation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Cardiac tamponade severely restricts ventricular filling, leading to Beck's triad: hypotension with narrowed pulse pressure, muffled heart sounds, and jugular venous distention (JVD). Successful pericardiocentesis relieves the intrapericardial pressure, allowing the heart chambers to fill properly. A widening pulse pressure (indicating improved stroke volume) and the resolution of JVD (indicating decreased right-sided backup) are critical physical signs that the rescue intervention was successful. Pulsus paradoxus > 10 mmHg (Option A), increased CVP (Option C), and muffled heart sounds (Option D) are all signs of active, worsening, or unresolved cardiac tamponade.
</details>
### Question 71
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?)**

The nurse receives the shift report on a step-down cardiovascular unit. Which of the following clients must the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with acute pericarditis who is leaning forward in bed and has an oxygen saturation of 93% on room air.
B) A client 12 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) whose chest tube drainage abruptly decreased from 150 mL/hr to 10 mL/hr, accompanied by muffled heart sounds.
C) A client 2 days post-myocardial infarction reporting a new onset of palpitations with a heart rate of 115 bpm.
D) A client with decompensated heart failure and a B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) of 950 pg/mL who is complaining of a persistent dry cough.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** This client is exhibiting classic signs of developing cardiac tamponade, an emergency complication post-cardiac surgery. An abrupt cessation of heavy chest tube drainage indicates the tubes may be clotted or kinked, leading to blood accumulating in the pericardial sac. Muffled heart sounds, along with hypotension and jugular venous distention (Beck's triad), are hallmark signs of cardiac tamponade, which severely restricts ventricular filling and cardiac output. This requires immediate life-saving intervention (e.g., clearing the chest tube, echocardiogram, pericardiocentesis, or return to the OR). 
*Option A* is incorrect because leaning forward is an expected compensatory position that relieves pain for a client with pericarditis, and an SpO2 of 93% is acceptable. 
*Option C* is incorrect because while new-onset palpitations and tachycardia post-MI are concerning and require an EKG to rule out arrhythmias like atrial fibrillation or ventricular tachycardia, it does not supersede the immediate hemodynamic collapse risk of cardiac tamponade. 
*Option D* is incorrect because a highly elevated BNP and a dry cough are expected, chronic findings in decompensated heart failure, not immediate life-threatening emergencies compared to tamponade.
</details>

---

### Question 72
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)**

The telemetry monitor alarms for a client admitted with severe hypomagnesemia. The rapid response nurse enters the room and finds the client unresponsive, apneic, and without a palpable carotid pulse. The monitor displays a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia (Torsades de Pointes). What is the nurse's immediate life-saving action?

A) Administer IV Magnesium Sulfate 2g over 10 minutes.
B) Perform synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules.
C) Initiate high-quality chest compressions and call for the defibrillator.
D) Administer an immediate IV bolus of Amiodarone 300 mg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is in pulseless ventricular tachycardia (Torsades de Pointes is a specific type of polymorphic VT). According to Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support (ACLS) guidelines, the immediate priority for any pulseless lethal ventricular arrhythmia (VT or VF) is high-quality CPR and early unsynchronized defibrillation. 
*Option A* is incorrect because while Magnesium Sulfate is the specific pharmacological treatment for Torsades de Pointes, it is secondary to CPR and defibrillation in a *pulseless* client. It would be administered during the cardiac arrest algorithm, not as the very first action.
*Option B* is incorrect because synchronized cardioversion is indicated for *unstable but pulsing* clients with tachyarrhythmias. Delivering a synchronized shock to a pulseless client delays life-saving defibrillation, and the machine may not even fire if it cannot identify an R-wave in the chaotic rhythm.
*Option D* is incorrect because antiarrhythmic medications (like Amiodarone) are given later in the pulseless arrest algorithm (typically after the third shock), not as the immediate initial action.
</details>

---

### Question 73
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)**

A client in a hypertensive crisis is receiving an intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The blood pressure has decreased from 235/135 mmHg to 165/90 mmHg over the last 3 hours. The nurse notes the client is becoming increasingly disoriented, agitated, and reports a ringing in their ears and a "metallic taste" in their mouth. Which of the following is the most appropriate advanced nursing action?

A) Stop the nitroprusside infusion immediately and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.
B) Administer PRN intravenous haloperidol to manage the acute agitation.
C) Increase the nitroprusside infusion rate as the client's blood pressure is still above target.
D) Request a prescription for a stat CT scan of the head to rule out hemorrhagic stroke.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside is a potent vasodilator used in hypertensive emergencies. However, its metabolism produces cyanide and thiocyanate. Disorientation, agitation, tinnitus (ringing in the ears), and a metallic taste are classic early manifestations of thiocyanate/cyanide toxicity. The priority is to stop the infusion immediately and administer an antidote, such as sodium thiosulfate or sodium nitrite.
*Option B* is incorrect because treating the symptom (agitation) with an antipsychotic masks the underlying, potentially fatal toxicity.
*Option C* is incorrect because increasing the infusion rate will exacerbate the cyanide toxicity, potentially leading to profound metabolic acidosis, seizures, and death.
*Option D* is incorrect because while hypertensive clients are at risk for hemorrhagic strokes, the specific combination of metallic taste, tinnitus, and altered mental status in the context of a nitroprusside drip overwhelmingly points to toxicity, requiring immediate cessation of the drug rather than delaying care for a scan.
</details>

---

### Question 74
**LENS: RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')**

A client is recovering in the intensive care unit following a massive anterior myocardial infarction. The nurse is closely monitoring the client's hemodynamics to prevent cardiogenic shock. Which finding represents the earliest, most subtle indicator of impending cardiogenic shock?

A) An abrupt drop in systolic blood pressure to 82 mmHg.
B) Cool, clammy, and profoundly mottled extremities.
C) Development of a new S3 heart sound and coarse crackles up to the mid-scapula.
D) A narrowing pulse pressure from 45 mmHg to 25 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** In the early stages of cardiogenic shock, cardiac output and stroke volume begin to fall. To compensate, the sympathetic nervous system triggers widespread vasoconstriction to maintain systemic blood pressure. This causes the diastolic blood pressure to rise while the systolic blood pressure drops or remains normal, resulting in a narrowing pulse pressure (the difference between systolic and diastolic pressure). A pulse pressure of less than 30 mmHg is an extremely subtle, early warning sign of decreasing stroke volume that occurs *before* frank hypotension develops.
*Option A* is a late sign; frank hypotension indicates compensatory mechanisms have already failed.
*Option B* is a late sign reflecting severe peripheral hypoperfusion and shunting of blood away from the skin to vital organs.
*Option C* indicates established heart failure and pulmonary edema, which are profound and obvious clinical signs, not the *earliest* or subtlest sign of impending hemodynamic collapse compared to narrowing pulse pressure.
</details>

---

### Question 75
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)**

A client is admitted with profound cardiogenic shock following a massive myocardial infarction. Over the last 12 hours, the client's urine output has dropped to 12 mL/hr, serum creatinine has risen from 0.9 mg/dL to 2.4 mg/dL, and BUN is 52 mg/dL. The client's central venous pressure (CVP) is 18 mmHg. How should the nurse best interpret and manage this multi-system complication?

A) The client has developed postrenal failure due to urinary retention; prepare to insert an indwelling Foley catheter.
B) The client is experiencing intrarenal failure from nephrotoxic drugs; prepare the client for emergent hemodialysis.
C) The client has developed prerenal failure due to decreased cardiac output; administer prescribed dobutamine to improve contractility.
D) The client is in hypovolemic shock causing prerenal acute kidney injury; administer a rapid 500 mL crystalloid fluid bolus.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is experiencing prerenal acute kidney injury (AKI) secondary to profound cardiogenic shock. In cardiogenic shock, the "pump" fails, leading to drastically reduced forward cardiac output and subsequent hypoperfusion to the kidneys. The elevated CVP (normal is 2-8 mmHg) indicates significant right-sided fluid overload and rules out hypovolemia. The definitive treatment for prerenal AKI caused by pump failure is to optimize cardiac output, typically using positive inotropes (like dobutamine) or mechanical assist devices (like an intra-aortic balloon pump) to restore renal perfusion.
*Option A* is incorrect; there is no indication of obstruction (postrenal). The oliguria is due to a lack of renal blood flow.
*Option B* is incorrect; the sudden onset of these labs in the context of cardiogenic shock points to a prerenal etiology (hypoperfusion), not necessarily intrarenal damage from nephrotoxins, though prolonged prerenal AKI can evolve into intrarenal acute tubular necrosis (ATN).
*Option D* is incorrect; the client has an elevated CVP (18 mmHg), indicating severe fluid backup and hypervolemia due to pump failure. Administering a fluid bolus would acutely worsen the cardiogenic shock and pulmonary edema.
</details>
### Question 76
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**
The nurse is receiving the shift report for four clients in the cardiovascular intensive care unit. Which client presents the greatest immediate risk to life and should the nurse assess first?

A) A client with an acute dissecting aortic aneurysm whose blood pressure is 105/60 mmHg on a continuous labetalol infusion.
B) A client with cardiogenic shock receiving milrinone whose continuous mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) has improved to 62%.
C) A client in a hypertensive emergency whose mean arterial pressure (MAP) has decreased by 15% in the first 45 minutes of a sodium nitroprusside infusion.
D) A client with acute pericarditis who exhibits a muffled first heart sound and a systolic blood pressure drop of 18 mmHg during inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Option D describes pulsus paradoxus (a drop in systolic blood pressure >10 mmHg during inspiration) coupled with muffled heart sounds. These are classic, cardinal signs of developing cardiac tamponade (Beck's triad), a life-threatening emergency causing obstructive shock and rapid cardiovascular collapse. This client requires immediate assessment and preparation for an emergency pericardiocentesis. Option A is stable; for a dissecting aortic aneurysm, maintaining a low blood pressure (SBP typically 100-120 mmHg) is the desired goal to prevent further dissection. Option B is showing clinical improvement, as a normal SvO2 is typically 60-80%, indicating the milrinone is successfully augmenting cardiac output. Option C is experiencing an expected and highly desired outcome; in a hypertensive emergency, the initial goal is to reduce the MAP by no more than 25% within the first hour to prevent ischemic organ damage.
</details>

---

### Question 77
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
A client presents to the emergency department in a hypertensive emergency with a blood pressure of 240/140 mmHg (MAP 173 mmHg). The healthcare provider prescribes a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside with a goal to reduce the MAP by no more than 25% over the first hour. Forty-five minutes into the infusion, the client's blood pressure is 150/90 mmHg (MAP 110 mmHg). What is the *priority* nursing action?

A) Decrease the rate of the sodium nitroprusside infusion and closely assess the client for neurological deficits.
B) Increase the infusion rate to reach a completely normotensive target systolic blood pressure of 120 mmHg.
C) Stop the infusion immediately, aspirate the IV line, and notify the rapid response team.
D) Administer PRN sublingual nitroglycerin to stabilize the blood pressure decline.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** In a hypertensive emergency, autoregulation mechanisms in the brain, heart, and kidneys have adapted to high perfusion pressures. Rapidly reducing the MAP by more than 20-25% in the first hour can lead to profound cerebral, coronary, or renal ischemia. The client's MAP has dropped from 173 mmHg to 110 mmHg, which is a roughly 36% reduction—too much, too fast. The nurse's priority is to decrease the titration rate of the nitroprusside to allow the MAP to safely rise closer to the 130 mmHg target range, while assessing for signs of stroke or ischemia. Option B would dangerously worsen the systemic ischemia. Option C is an overreaction; abruptly stopping a short-acting agent like nitroprusside can cause severe rebound hypertension unless the client is actively hypotensive or in shock. Option D is contraindicated, as giving another vasodilator will drop the blood pressure even further.
</details>

---

### Question 78
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**
The nurse is analyzing the continuous telemetry strip of a client in cardiogenic shock who recently had a transvenous pacemaker placed for third-degree atrioventricular block. The nurse observes pacing spikes occurring at regular, programmed intervals, but immediately following several of the spikes, there are no QRS complexes. The client's blood pressure has dropped to 82/50 mmHg. Which technical intervention is required *first* to address the device malfunction interpreted from this rhythm?

A) Prepare the client for immediate synchronized cardioversion.
B) Increase the pacemaker's electrical output (mA) to regain ventricular capture.
C) Increase the sensitivity setting (mV) so the pacemaker recognizes the intrinsic rhythm.
D) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of amiodarone.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** The EKG interpretation is "failure to capture," indicated by pacing spikes that are not followed by the appropriate myocardial depolarization (a QRS complex). Because the client's cardiac output is dependent on this pacing (evidenced by the drop in blood pressure), the immediate intervention is to increase the electrical output (milliamperes or mA) on the pacemaker generator until a QRS complex consistently follows every pacing spike. Option A is incorrect; synchronized cardioversion is used for tachyarrhythmias (like unstable VT or SVT), not for bradycardia/pacing failure. Option C addresses "failure to sense," which would present as pacing spikes landing randomly throughout the cardiac cycle, rather than regular spikes failing to produce a QRS. Option D is an antiarrhythmic used for ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation, which would further suppress the ventricles and worsen the shock in a client with a pacing failure.
</details>

---

### Question 79
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**
A client is recovering in the cardiac intensive care unit following a massive anterior myocardial infarction. The nurse is closely monitoring the client for the development of cardiogenic shock. Which subtle assessment finding represents the *earliest* indicator that the client's compensatory mechanisms are failing to rescue the cardiovascular system?

A) A widening pulse pressure with an increasing systolic blood pressure.
B) A sudden onset of coarse crackles audible halfway up both lung fields.
C) A decrease in urine output from 45 mL/hr to 25 mL/hr and mild restlessness.
D) A steady decrease in the central venous pressure (CVP) from 8 mm Hg to 2 mm Hg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** In the earliest stages of cardiogenic shock, as cardiac output drops, the body's compensatory mechanisms (sympathetic nervous system and RAAS) attempt to maintain vital organ perfusion by vasoconstricting peripheral beds. This leads to early, subtle signs of compromised tissue perfusion, notably a drop in renal perfusion (oliguria, <30 mL/hr) and decreased cerebral oxygenation (mild restlessness, anxiety, or altered mentation). Recognizing these subtle shifts is critical to preventing 'failure to rescue'. Option A describes Cushing's triad (increased ICP), whereas cardiogenic shock causes a *narrowing* pulse pressure. Option B represents frank pulmonary edema; while a hallmark of left-sided heart failure and cardiogenic shock, it is a later, decompensated sign compared to oliguria and restlessness. Option D indicates profound hypovolemia (distributive or hypovolemic shock). In cardiogenic shock, the CVP typically *increases* due to the backflow of blood from the failing pump.
</details>

---

### Question 80
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit in profound cardiogenic shock following a massive myocardial infarction. Despite maximum inotropic and vasopressor support, the client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) remains at 55 mmHg. Recent morning laboratory results reveal a sudden, dramatic spike in serum AST, ALT, and bilirubin levels, alongside an escalating prothrombin time (PT). The nurse understands that this multi-system complication is primarily the result of which pathophysiological process?

A) Ischemic hepatopathy ("shock liver") resulting from profound, prolonged systemic hypoperfusion.
B) Acute right-sided heart failure causing direct fluid overload and third-spacing into the peritoneal cavity.
C) Medication toxicity caused by the continuous administration of high-dose adrenergic vasopressors.
D) Acute obstructive jaundice secondary to gallstone formation from a prolonged fasting state.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting signs of "shock liver" (ischemic hepatopathy), a severe complication of multi-system integration failure. The profoundly low cardiac output and persistent hypotension (MAP <60 mmHg) seen in cardiogenic shock lead to massive hepatic ischemia and hepatocyte necrosis. This is chemically marked by a sudden, severe elevation in aminotransferases (AST/ALT) often reaching thousands of units, elevated bilirubin, and coagulopathy (prolonged PT/INR) due to the liver's failure to synthesize clotting factors. Option B describes congestive hepatopathy from right-sided failure, which typically elevates liver enzymes mildly and chronically, not with a sudden, dramatic spike seen in acute ischemic shock liver. Option C is incorrect; while vasopressors can cause peripheral ischemia, the primary driver of this massive hepatic failure is the underlying low-flow state (cardiogenic shock) failing to perfuse the splanchnic bed. Option D is unrelated to the acute, life-threatening hemodynamics of cardiogenic shock.
</details>
### Question 81
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')**

A critical care nurse is managing a client 6 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. Which combination of assessment data provides the *earliest* and most critical warning of developing cardiac tamponade requiring immediate rescue?

A. Engorged neck veins, muffled, distant heart sounds, and a mean arterial pressure (MAP) of 55 mm Hg. 
B. A sinus tachycardia of 118 bpm, a widening pulse pressure, and bounding peripheral pulses.
C. An abrupt cessation of mediastinal chest tube output accompanied by a narrowing pulse pressure.
D. An increase in central venous pressure (CVP) from 8 to 10 mm Hg with a urine output of 45 mL/hr.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. An abrupt cessation of mediastinal chest tube output accompanied by a narrowing pulse pressure.

**Rationale:**
- **Option C is correct:** In the post-cardiothoracic surgery client, an abrupt cessation of chest tube drainage often indicates that the tubes are clotted or obstructed. Blood then rapidly accumulates in the pericardial/mediastinal space, leading to cardiac tamponade. As fluid compresses the heart, stroke volume decreases, leading to a compensatory systemic vasoconstriction that presents as a narrowing pulse pressure. This is a subtle, early indicator of "failure to rescue" before profound shock sets in.
- **Option A is incorrect:** While jugular venous distention (JVD), muffled heart sounds, and hypotension constitute Beck's Triad, these are *late* signs of cardiac tamponade. By the time the MAP drops to 55 mm Hg, the patient is already in cardiogenic shock. 
- **Option B is incorrect:** Cardiac tamponade typically presents with a *narrowing* pulse pressure due to decreased stroke volume, not a widening pulse pressure with bounding pulses (which is more indicative of distributive shocks like sepsis or neurogenic shock).
- **Option D is incorrect:** A CVP of 8 to 10 mm Hg is relatively normal or expected in a post-CABG patient receiving fluids, and a urine output of 45 mL/hr indicates adequate renal perfusion. 

**Advanced Concept:** Always monitor chest tube patency in post-open heart patients. A sudden drop in output is a massive red flag for impending cardiac tamponade, long before Beck's Triad fully manifests.

</details>

---

### Question 82
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)**

A client in severe cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior myocardial infarction is receiving continuous infusions of norepinephrine and dobutamine. The client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) is currently 68 mm Hg. However, the nurse notes severe respiratory crackles up to the apices, a PaO2 of 55 mm Hg on an FiO2 of 80%, and an hourly urine output of 15 mL/hr. How should the nurse accurately interpret this multi-system presentation?

A. The low urine output is causing systemic fluid overload, which should be managed by aggressively titrating up the norepinephrine infusion.
B. The client is experiencing transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) requiring immediate discontinuation of all IV fluids and blood products.
C. The vasopressor infusions are causing extreme pulmonary vasoconstriction, resulting in isolated right-sided heart failure and systemic congestion.
D. Profound left ventricular failure has caused a severe increase in pulmonary capillary wedge pressure, leading to refractory alveolar edema.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Profound left ventricular failure has caused a severe increase in pulmonary capillary wedge pressure, leading to refractory alveolar edema.

**Rationale:**
- **Option D is correct:** Cardiogenic shock typically stems from severe left ventricular (LV) dysfunction. The forward failure of the LV causes decreased cardiac output and poor renal perfusion (resulting in the oliguria of 15 mL/hr). Simultaneously, the backward failure causes pressure to build up in the left atrium and pulmonary vasculature (elevated pulmonary capillary wedge pressure). This hydrostatic pressure forces fluid into the alveoli, resulting in severe pulmonary edema (crackles to the apices) and refractory hypoxemia (low PaO2 despite high FiO2).
- **Option A is incorrect:** Norepinephrine is a vasopressor that increases systemic vascular resistance (SVR). Increasing SVR in a failing heart further increases afterload, making it even harder for the left ventricle to pump, which could worsen the cardiogenic shock. 
- **Option B is incorrect:** There is no indication in the scenario that the client received blood products, making TRALI highly unlikely. The presentation is classic for cardiogenic pulmonary edema.
- **Option C is incorrect:** While vasopressors can affect vascular tone, the primary driver of this client's pulmonary congestion is left ventricular failure (backward failure), not isolated right-sided failure. 

**Advanced Concept:** Cardiogenic shock requires a delicate balance of managing "pump failure." You must integrate the understanding that the heart's inability to eject blood forward directly destroys the lungs' ability to oxygenate due to backward hydrostatic flooding. 

</details>

---

### Question 83
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips like Heparin, Insulin, or Nitro in a crisis)**

A client admitted for a hypertensive emergency (initial BP 245/140 mm Hg) has been receiving a sodium nitroprusside continuous IV infusion for 48 hours to maintain a MAP of 90 mm Hg. During the current shift, the client becomes severely agitated, confused, and begins hyperventilating. Stat laboratory results reveal a serum lactate of 5.6 mmol/L and an uncompensated metabolic acidosis. What is the nurse's *priority* action?

A. Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately, maintain the IV line, and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.
B. Increase the infusion rate of sodium nitroprusside to rapidly manage an assumed spike in intracranial pressure.
C. Administer PRN intravenous haloperidol to treat the client's acute agitation and decrease metabolic oxygen demand.
D. Prepare the client for emergent intubation and mechanical ventilation to reverse the respiratory alkalosis caused by hyperventilation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately, maintain the IV line, and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.

**Rationale:**
- **Option A is correct:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolism produces cyanide. Prolonged infusions (typically >48 hours) or high doses can lead to cyanide toxicity. Cyanide halts cellular respiration (inhibits cytochrome oxidase), forcing the body into anaerobic metabolism, which causes severe lactic acidosis (lactate 5.6 mmol/L). Symptoms include altered mental status, hyperventilation, and metabolic acidosis. The life-saving priority is to stop the offending drug and administer the antidote, sodium thiosulfate.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Increasing the medication rate would worsen the lethal cyanide toxicity. 
- **Option C is incorrect:** While the client is agitated, haloperidol only masks the symptoms of toxicity and prolongs the QT interval. It does not address the underlying life-threatening cause.
- **Option D is incorrect:** The hyperventilation is a compensatory mechanism (Kussmaul-like breathing) attempting to blow off CO2 in response to the severe metabolic acidosis. Intubating without correcting the underlying toxicity could cause fatal acid-base derangement.

**Advanced Concept:** When titrating potent vasodilators like nitroprusside, the nurse must monitor for thiocyanate and cyanide toxicity. Unexplained metabolic acidosis and confusion in a patient on this drip is toxicity until proven otherwise.

</details>

---

### Question 84
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting lethal EKG rhythms, compensated ABGs, or toxic labs)**

A client is admitted to the progressive care unit with a history of recurrent ventricular arrhythmias. The nurse reviews the client’s telemetry strip and current medication administration record (MAR), which includes scheduled oral sotalol, newly prescribed IV azithromycin, and PRN IV ondansetron. The morning 12-lead EKG reveals a QTc interval of 0.58 seconds and frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs). Based on advanced data analysis, what is the most critical intervention?

A. Prepare the client for immediate synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules to suppress the ectopic beats.
B. Administer an IV bolus of amiodarone to suppress the premature ventricular contractions and stabilize the myocardium.
C. Administer the PRN ondansetron proactively to prevent vagal stimulation from potential nausea during the PVCs.
D. Withhold the sotalol and azithromycin, initiate continuous cardiac monitoring, and notify the provider of the high risk for Torsades de Pointes.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Withhold the sotalol and azithromycin, initiate continuous cardiac monitoring, and notify the provider of the high risk for Torsades de Pointes.

**Rationale:**
- **Option D is correct:** A normal QTc interval is generally less than 0.44 seconds in men and 0.46 seconds in women. A QTc of 0.58 seconds is significantly prolonged and places the client at imminent, severe risk for the "R-on-T" phenomenon, which triggers Torsades de Pointes (a lethal polymorphic ventricular tachycardia). Sotalol (a Class III antiarrhythmic) and azithromycin (a macrolide antibiotic) both prolong the QT interval. The priority is to withhold these offending agents and notify the provider to prevent a lethal cardiac arrest.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Synchronized cardioversion is used for unstable tachyarrhythmias with a pulse (e.g., unstable SVT, unstable A-fib, unstable VT). It is not indicated for isolated PVCs or simply a prolonged QT interval.
- **Option B is incorrect:** Amiodarone is a Class III antiarrhythmic that significantly *prolongs* the QT interval. Administering it to a client with a QTc of 0.58 seconds could be a fatal error, directly precipitating Torsades de Pointes.
- **Option C is incorrect:** Ondansetron is another medication notorious for prolonging the QT interval. Administering it would compound the toxicity and increase the risk of a lethal arrhythmia.

**Advanced Concept:** Polypharmacy involving QT-prolonging drugs is a major cause of preventable in-hospital cardiac arrest. Nurses must meticulously cross-reference EKG findings (prolonged QTc) with the MAR before administering medications.

</details>

---

### Question 85
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?)**

The nurse assumes care for four clients in the cardiac intensive care unit. Which client must the nurse assess *first*?

A. A client post-pericardiocentesis for cardiac tamponade who has had 30 mL of serosanguineous fluid drain into the pericardial collection system over the last hour.
B. A client with a hypertensive crisis receiving an intravenous labetalol drip who acutely reports severe, tearing pain radiating to the upper back.
C. A client with cardiogenic shock on a milrinone infusion whose cardiac index (CI) is 2.1 L/min/m² and mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) is 60%.
D. A client with a history of persistent atrial fibrillation whose telemetry shows a ventricular rate of 110 bpm and a blood pressure of 105/70 mm Hg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. A client with a hypertensive crisis receiving an intravenous labetalol drip who acutely reports severe, tearing pain radiating to the upper back.

**Rationale:**
- **Option B is correct:** This client is exhibiting classic symptoms of an acute aortic dissection (severe, tearing/ripping pain radiating to the back) in the setting of a hypertensive crisis. This is a catastrophic, life-threatening emergency that requires immediate reduction of blood pressure and sheer stress on the aorta, as well as emergent surgical intervention. This client is at the highest risk for imminent death.
- **Option A is incorrect:** Following a pericardiocentesis, it is normal and expected for the pericardial drain to output small amounts of serosanguineous fluid (e.g., 30 mL/hr) as the residual fluid evacuates. This client is currently stable.
- **Option C is incorrect:** A cardiac index (CI) of 2.1 L/min/m² is at the lower limit of normal (normal 2.5-4.0 L/min/m²), but is an acceptable, improved finding for a patient being actively managed for cardiogenic shock with an inotrope (milrinone). An SvO2 of 60% is slightly low (normal 65-75%), but expected in this disease process. 
- **Option D is incorrect:** A ventricular rate of 110 bpm in a client with persistent atrial fibrillation indicates mild lack of rate control, but the client is normotensive and not actively decompensating. They require assessment and medication adjustments, but do not take priority over a dissecting aorta.

**Advanced Concept:** In prioritization, new onset of "tearing" or "ripping" back/chest pain in a severely hypertensive patient must instantly be recognized as an aortic dissection. Death can occur within minutes if the aorta ruptures.

</details>
### Question 86
A client experiencing a hypertensive crisis is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. During hourly assessments, the nurse notes that the client has become acutely confused, is tachycardic with a bounding pulse, and the most recent arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals an unexplained metabolic acidosis. Based on these findings, what is the nurse's priority action?

A) Administer intravenous sodium bicarbonate to correct the metabolic acidosis. 
B) Discontinue the nitroprusside infusion immediately and notify the healthcare provider.
