C) Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate by half and reassess in 15 minutes. 
D) Increase the infusion rate to manage the escalating heart rate and bounding pulse. 

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Discontinue the nitroprusside infusion immediately and notify the healthcare provider.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
Sodium nitroprusside is a potent vasodilator used in hypertensive emergencies. However, its metabolism produces cyanide, which can accumulate and cause potentially fatal cyanide toxicity. Early signs of cyanide toxicity include altered mental status (confusion), tachycardia with a bounding pulse, and metabolic acidosis (lactic acidosis due to impaired cellular respiration). The priority action is to immediately stop the infusion to prevent further toxicity and notify the provider so that antidotes (such as sodium thiosulfate) can be administered. Administering bicarbonate (Option A) treats the symptom but not the underlying toxic cause. Altering the rate (Options C and D) is unsafe and will exacerbate or prolong the toxic exposure.

</details>

---

### Question 87
The nurse is caring for a client who is recovering from a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. The nurse monitors the client closely for the development of cardiogenic shock. Which subtle clinical finding represents the *earliest* indicator that the client is experiencing a failure of compensatory mechanisms and deteriorating into shock?

A) A sudden onset of pink, frothy sputum with agonal respirations.
B) Profound hypotension with a systolic blood pressure reading of 78 mm Hg.
C) An abrupt decline in Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score from 15 to 8.
D) A narrowing pulse pressure accompanied by an increasing resting heart rate.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) A narrowing pulse pressure accompanied by an increasing resting heart rate.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION (Failure to Rescue)**
Cardiogenic shock occurs when the heart loses its pumping ability, leading to decreased cardiac output. Before frank hypotension (Option B) or severe pulmonary edema (Option A) occurs, the body attempts to compensate for the falling cardiac output. This compensatory phase is marked by sympathetic nervous system activation, which causes tachycardia and systemic vasoconstriction. The vasoconstriction raises the diastolic blood pressure while the failing heart generates a lower systolic pressure, resulting in a narrowing pulse pressure. Identifying this subtle, early sign allows for life-saving interventions before the client progresses to multi-organ failure or severe neurological decline (Option C).

</details>

---

### Question 88
An intensive care nurse is analyzing the telemetry and laboratory data for a client with advanced heart failure who is receiving aggressive intravenous loop diuretics. The telemetry monitor suddenly displays a rapid, irregular rhythm with a shifting QRS axis that appears to twist around the isoelectric line. Which laboratory value should the nurse analyze *first* to determine the primary etiology of this lethal rhythm?

A) Serum magnesium level of 1.2 mEq/L (0.60 mmol/L).
B) Serum potassium level of 5.6 mEq/L (5.6 mmol/L).
C) Serum sodium level of 131 mEq/L (131 mmol/L).
D) Brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) level of 1,200 pg/mL.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Serum magnesium level of 1.2 mEq/L (0.60 mmol/L).

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**
The rhythm described—a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a shifting QRS axis twisting around the baseline—is Torsades de Pointes. This lethal arrhythmia is frequently triggered by a prolonged QT interval. Aggressive diuresis with loop diuretics commonly depletes electrolytes, notably potassium and magnesium. Severe hypomagnesemia (normal range: 1.5 - 2.5 mEq/L) is a hallmark trigger for Torsades de Pointes. While hyperkalemia (Option B) can cause fatal arrhythmias like ventricular fibrillation or asystole, it typically presents with peaked T waves and widened QRS complexes, not Torsades. Hyponatremia (Option C) and elevated BNP (Option D) are expected in heart failure but do not directly cause this specific twisting lethal arrhythmia.

</details>

---

### Question 89
A client is 6 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. The nurse notes that the mediastinal chest tube drainage has abruptly ceased. Upon assessment, the client has distended neck veins, muffled heart sounds, and a paradoxical blood pressure drop of 18 mm Hg during inspiration. What is the *immediate* life-saving nursing action?

A) Forcefully strip the mediastinal chest tubes to dislodge the suspected clot.
B) Place the client in a high-Fowler's position with the legs dependent.
C) Rapidly administer a prescribed intravenous crystalloid bolus while preparing for emergency surgical intervention.
D) Administer PRN intravenous furosemide to reduce the central venous congestion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Rapidly administer a prescribed intravenous crystalloid bolus while preparing for emergency surgical intervention.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE**
The client is exhibiting Beck's triad (muffled heart sounds, jugular venous distention) and pulsus paradoxus (BP drop >10 mm Hg on inspiration) alongside a sudden cessation of chest tube output. This indicates acute cardiac tamponade due to blood accumulation in the pericardial sac. The immediate rescue action is to prepare for emergency decompression (pericardiocentesis or emergency sternotomy/re-exploration). While awaiting the surgeon, rapidly administering an IV fluid bolus is crucial because it increases right-sided ventricular filling pressures, temporarily overcoming the compressive forces of the tamponade to maintain cardiac output. Stripping chest tubes (Option A) is contraindicated as it generates dangerously high negative pressure that can damage cardiac tissue. Diuretics (Option D) are strictly contraindicated as they will reduce filling pressures and cause cardiovascular collapse.

</details>

---

### Question 90
The charge nurse in the cardiovascular intensive care unit is reviewing the status of four assigned clients. Which client must the nurse assess *first*?

A) A client with cardiogenic shock on a milrinone infusion whose mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) has slowly decreased from 65% to 58%.
B) A client with a hypertensive crisis on an esmolol drip who reports a sudden, tearing sensation radiating to the upper back.
C) A client recovering from cardiac tamponade post-pericardiocentesis whose pericardial drain output is 30 mL over the last hour.
D) A client with chronic atrial fibrillation who has developed a heart rate of 115 bpm and a blood pressure of 102/68 mm Hg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) A client with a hypertensive crisis on an esmolol drip who reports a sudden, tearing sensation radiating to the upper back.

**Rationale:** 
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**
A client with a hypertensive emergency who develops sudden, tearing back or chest pain is exhibiting classic signs of an expanding or rupturing aortic dissection. This is an immediate, life-threatening emergency that requires strict blood pressure and heart rate control, and likely emergent surgery to prevent exsanguination. This client takes highest priority. While a decreasing SvO2 in cardiogenic shock (Option A) indicates worsening tissue oxygenation/cardiac output, it does not supersede an acute aortic rupture. A pericardial drain output of 30 mL/hr (Option C) is an expected finding post-procedure. A heart rate of 115 bpm in chronic atrial fibrillation (Option D) requires evaluation but the client's blood pressure remains stable, making them a lower priority than a dissecting aorta.

</details>
### Question 91
**PRIORITIZATION**

The nurse is receiving the morning handoff report for four unstable clients on the cardiovascular step-down unit. Which client should the nurse assess **FIRST**?

A. A client in cardiogenic shock receiving a dobutamine infusion whose mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) has dropped to 58%.
B. A client with a hypertensive emergency receiving an IV clevidipine infusion who reports a severe, throbbing headache and blurred vision.
C. A client admitted with acute pericarditis who has a newly developed paradoxical pulse of 18 mmHg and increasingly distant heart sounds.
D. A client with chronic heart failure exhibiting bilateral pitting edema, an S3 gallop, and a weight gain of 4 lbs (1.8 kg) over the last 48 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click for Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:**
**C is the correct answer.** This client is exhibiting classic, ominous signs of cardiac tamponade (pulsus paradoxus >10 mmHg, muffled/distant heart sounds, and likely narrowed pulse pressure/hypotension). Cardiac tamponade is a life-threatening complication of pericarditis that severely restricts ventricular filling, leading to acute obstructive shock and circulatory collapse. This client requires an immediate emergency pericardiocentesis to save their life. 
**A is incorrect.** An SvO2 of 58% (normal is 60-80%) is abnormally low, reflecting poor tissue perfusion, but this is an *expected* finding for a client already being actively managed for cardiogenic shock. While serious, it does not immediately precede imminent death like the untreated tamponade in option C.
**B is incorrect.** A severe headache and blurred vision are classic manifestations of a hypertensive emergency. While the nurse must assess the effectiveness of the clevidipine drip, the client in option C is experiencing a rapid structural obstruction to cardiac output, which takes higher physiological priority.
**D is incorrect.** An S3 gallop, edema, and weight gain indicate an acute exacerbation of chronic heart failure. This client requires diuretics and fluid management, but they are not the most critically unstable client in this group. 
</details>

---

### Question 92
**HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client admitted for a severe hypertensive crisis is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside at 5 mcg/kg/min. After 48 hours of therapy, the client becomes profoundly disoriented, exhibits diffuse muscle twitching, and an arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals a severe, unexplained metabolic acidosis. What is the nurse's **priority** intervention?

A. Immediately stop the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer intravenous sodium thiosulfate.
B. Increase the infusion rate to 6 mcg/kg/min as the symptoms indicate worsening hypertensive encephalopathy.
C. Administer a rapid IV bolus of sodium bicarbonate to correct the profound metabolic acidosis.
D. Temporarily halt the infusion and assess the client for a sudden onset of ischemic stroke.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click for Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:**
**A is the correct answer.** Sodium nitroprusside metabolizes to cyanide, which is then converted by the liver to thiocyanate. Prolonged administration or high doses (>3 mcg/kg/min) dramatically increase the risk of cyanide toxicity. The hallmark signs of cyanide toxicity include altered mental status, seizures, muscle twitching, and severe metabolic acidosis (due to a shift to anaerobic metabolism when cyanide poisons cellular respiration). The priority is to stop the drug and administer the antidote, sodium thiosulfate (or hydroxocobalamin).
**B is incorrect.** Increasing the infusion rate will deliver more cyanide to the client, rapidly worsening the toxicity and likely causing cardiovascular collapse and death. The symptoms are not due to elevated blood pressure.
**C is incorrect.** While sodium bicarbonate is used to treat severe metabolic acidosis, the primary cause of the acidosis here is cellular asphyxia from cyanide poisoning. Without stopping the offending agent and administering the antidote, sodium bicarbonate will be ineffective. 
**D is incorrect.** While a stroke could cause disorientation, it does not explain diffuse muscle twitching combined with severe metabolic acidosis. Stopping the drug is necessary, but the nurse must also recognize the immediate need for the antidote, not just a neurological assessment.
</details>

---

### Question 93
**ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**

A client with severe hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia secondary to hyperemesis is admitted to the progressive care unit. The nurse observes the central cardiac monitor and sees a sudden rhythm change: a rapid, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia where the QRS complexes appear to continually twist around the isoelectric line. The client is lethargic but currently has a weak pulse. Which action should the nurse take **FIRST**?

A. Prepare for immediate synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules.
B. Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of amiodarone 300 mg.
C. Administer an intravenous bolus of 10% calcium gluconate.
D. Administer an intravenous bolus of magnesium sulfate.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click for Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:**
**D is the correct answer.** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (TdP), a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by QRS complexes that twist around the isoelectric line. It is strongly associated with a prolonged QT interval, which is frequently caused by severe hypomagnesemia or hypokalemia. The first-line pharmacological treatment for TdP with a pulse is IV magnesium sulfate, as it stabilizes the myocardial membrane and shortens the QT interval.
**A is incorrect.** Synchronized cardioversion relies on regular, identifiable R waves for the defibrillator to synchronize with. Because TdP has continually varying QRS morphologies and amplitudes, the machine cannot accurately synchronize, making synchronized cardioversion inappropriate. If the patient becomes pulseless, immediate *unsynchronized* defibrillation is required. 
**B is incorrect.** Amiodarone is an antiarrhythmic that significantly prolongs the QT interval. Administering it to a client in Torsades de Pointes will worsen the underlying pathology and can precipitate a fatal cardiac arrest. 
**C is incorrect.** Calcium gluconate is the antidote for hypermagnesemia or calcium channel blocker toxicity and is used to stabilize the myocardium in severe hyperkalemia. It is not indicated for the treatment of Torsades de Pointes. 
</details>

---

### Question 94
**EVALUATION OF CRISIS**

A client is in profound cardiogenic shock secondary to a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. An intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) is inserted via the femoral artery, and a continuous infusion of norepinephrine is initiated. Which assessment finding provides the **best** evidence that the IABP is achieving its primary therapeutic goal?

A. The central venous pressure (CVP) increases from 12 mm Hg to 16 mm Hg.
B. The client's hourly urine output increases from 15 mL/hr to 45 mL/hr.
C. The client's heart rate steadily increases from 102 bpm to 125 bpm.
D. The client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) decreases from 70 mm Hg to 60 mm Hg.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click for Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:**
**B is the correct answer.** The primary goals of an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) in cardiogenic shock are to decrease afterload (decreasing cardiac workload) and increase coronary and systemic perfusion (by inflating during diastole). The most objective, reliable indicator of improved systemic end-organ perfusion is an increase in urine output. An increase from 15 mL/hr to 45 mL/hr shows that the kidneys are being adequately perfused. 
**A is incorrect.** An increase in central venous pressure (CVP) indicates worsening fluid overload, right-sided heart failure, or impending right ventricular collapse. In successful cardiogenic shock therapy, CVP should decrease as the forward flow of blood improves.
**C is incorrect.** An increasing heart rate is a sign of compensatory tachycardia or increased myocardial oxygen demand, which is exactly what the IABP is designed to prevent. This indicates worsening cardiac stress, not improvement.
**D is incorrect.** A decrease in mean arterial pressure (MAP) to 60 mm Hg (below the therapeutic goal of >65 mm Hg) indicates worsening systemic perfusion and failing compensatory mechanisms, requiring immediate intervention. 
</details>

---

### Question 95
**RISK OF DETERIORATION**

The nurse is caring for a client who is 8 hours post-operative following a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The client has two mediastinal chest tubes connected to continuous wall suction. The nurse notes the following recent trend in the client's vital signs and assessments over the last 30 minutes:
- Heart rate: Increased from 88 bpm to 118 bpm
- Blood pressure: Decreased from 112/70 mm Hg to 94/78 mm Hg
- Chest tube drainage: Decreased abruptly from 100 mL/hr to 5 mL in the last 30 minutes.

Based on these subtle and rapid changes, the nurse suspects the client is developing which life-threatening complication?

A. A tension pneumothorax secondary to a disconnected drainage system.
B. Hypovolemic shock resulting from massive internal postoperative hemorrhage.
C. Impending cardiac tamponade due to intrathoracic fluid accumulation.
D. Right ventricular failure related to perioperative myocardial infarction.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click for Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:**
**C is the correct answer.** The triad of abruptly ceased mediastinal chest tube drainage, narrowed pulse pressure (from 42 down to 16 mmHg), tachycardia, and hypotension in a post-CABG patient are the cardinal early signs of cardiac tamponade. Blood and fluid are accumulating in the pericardial space because the drainage tubes are likely clotted off. This prevents ventricular filling, rapidly leading to obstructive shock.
**A is incorrect.** While tension pneumothorax causes tachycardia and hypotension, the specific presentation of suddenly stopped mediastinal chest tubes combined with narrowed pulse pressure post-CABG points directly to cardiac tamponade. Mediastinal tubes drain the pericardial space, not the pleural space where a pneumothorax would occur.
**B is incorrect.** In massive internal hemorrhage, the nurse would likely see a surge in bloody chest tube output, rather than a sudden cessation. Furthermore, hypovolemic shock typically presents with a decreased, but not drastically narrowed, pulse pressure until very late stages. 
**D is incorrect.** Right ventricular failure would present with elevated central venous pressure, JVD, and systemic edema. While it causes hypotension, it does not explain the sudden cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage, which is the mechanical trigger in this scenario.
</details>
### Question 96
A client in profound cardiogenic shock is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of norepinephrine through a peripheral IV while awaiting central line placement. Ten minutes after the nurse titrates the dose upward to maintain a mean arterial pressure (MAP) > 65 mmHg, the client reports severe burning at the IV site. The nurse assesses the site and notes it is pale, cold, and hard to the touch. What is the priority nursing action?

A) Elevate the extremity above the level of the heart and apply a cold compress.
B) Decrease the infusion rate and monitor the site closely for blistering.
C) Immediately remove the IV catheter and apply firm direct pressure for 5 minutes.
D) Stop the infusion, leave the cannula in place to aspirate residual drug, and prepare to administer phentolamine.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Stop the infusion, leave the cannula in place to aspirate residual drug, and prepare to administer phentolamine.

**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips like Norepinephrine in a crisis)
**System Focus:** Cardiogenic Shock

**Rationale:** Norepinephrine is a potent alpha-adrenergic agonist and a known vesicant. Extravasation causes severe localized vasoconstriction, leading to tissue ischemia and necrosis. The priority is to immediately stop the infusion. The IV cannula must be left in place initially to attempt aspiration of the residual vesicant drug from the tissue/catheter. Following aspiration, the antidote phentolamine (an alpha-adrenergic antagonist) is injected subcutaneously into the affected area to promote vasodilation and restore local blood flow. Elevating the extremity or applying cold compresses (Option A) can worsen ischemia. Removing the catheter immediately (Option C) prevents the ability to aspirate the vesicant. Decreasing the rate (Option B) is inappropriate as the extravasation will continue to cause tissue death.
</details>

---

### Question 97
A client is 4 hours postoperative following a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The client has two mediastinal chest tubes. The nurse notes that the chest tube drainage, which was steadily measuring 120 mL/hr, has abruptly dropped to 5 mL over the past hour. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the highest risk for an impending, life-threatening complication?

A) Bilateral fine crackles in the lung bases and the presence of an S3 heart sound.
B) A 14 mmHg decrease in systolic blood pressure during spontaneous inspiration.
C) Abrupt development of severe, tearing chest pain radiating to the interscapular area.
D) A sudden increase in hourly urine output accompanied by sinus bradycardia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) A 14 mmHg decrease in systolic blood pressure during spontaneous inspiration.

**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')
**System Focus:** Cardiac Tamponade

**Rationale:** The abrupt cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage post-CABG strongly suggests tube occlusion, which traps blood in the pericardial space and rapidly leads to cardiac tamponade. A key early and subtle hemodynamic sign of cardiac tamponade is *pulsus paradoxus*—an abnormally large decrease in systolic blood pressure (greater than 10 mmHg) during inspiration. This occurs because increased right ventricular filling during inspiration causes the intraventricular septum to bow into the left ventricle due to the restricted pericardial space, drastically reducing left ventricular stroke volume. Option A indicates heart failure. Option C is classic for aortic dissection. Option D is irrelevant to tamponade, which typically causes decreased urine output and tachycardia due to falling cardiac output.
</details>

---

### Question 98
A client with a history of severe heart failure is admitted with a serum magnesium level of 1.0 mEq/L. While being evaluated, the client suddenly loses consciousness. The cardiac monitor displays a rapid, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a shifting sinusoidal waveform. The nurse palpates the carotid artery and finds no pulse. What is the *immediate* life-saving action?

A) Deliver an unsynchronized shock (defibrillation) immediately.
B) Administer a rapid IV push of 2 grams of magnesium sulfate.
C) Prepare the client for immediate synchronized cardioversion.
D) Administer Amiodarone 300 mg via rapid IV push.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Deliver an unsynchronized shock (defibrillation) immediately.

**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)
**System Focus:** Lethal Arrhythmias

**Rationale:** The client is experiencing Torsades de Pointes (polymorphic ventricular tachycardia) and is pulseless. The immediate, definitive life-saving intervention for *any* pulseless ventricular tachycardia or ventricular fibrillation is high-quality CPR and early unsynchronized defibrillation. While IV magnesium sulfate (Option B) is the specific pharmacological treatment for Torsades de Pointes, it is secondary to defibrillation and CPR in a pulseless client. Synchronized cardioversion (Option C) is contraindicated because the monitor cannot synchronize to a chaotic polymorphic rhythm, and it is entirely inappropriate for a pulseless client. Amiodarone (Option D) is not a first-line therapy for Torsades and can sometimes prolong the QT interval further; regardless, defibrillation must come first.
</details>

---

### Question 99
A client presents to the emergency department with a hypertensive crisis (blood pressure 250/140 mmHg) and severe headache. The provider orders a continuous intravenous labetalol infusion. Thirty minutes later, the client's blood pressure reads 135/75 mmHg. The client rapidly exhibits acute confusion, slurred speech, and urine output drops to 5 mL over the next hour. Which pathological mechanism is occurring, and what is the priority nursing action?

A) Fluid volume deficit causing prerenal failure; administer a 500 mL normal saline bolus.
B) Intracranial hemorrhage; request a stat non-contrast CT scan of the head.
C) Organ hypoperfusion from rapid pressure drop; decrease the labetalol infusion rate.
D) Acute tubular necrosis; prepare the client for emergency hemodialysis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Organ hypoperfusion from rapid pressure drop; decrease the labetalol infusion rate.

**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)
**System Focus:** Hypertensive Crisis

**Rationale:** In a hypertensive emergency, the vascular beds in target organs (brain, kidneys) adapt to chronic high pressures. If blood pressure is lowered too rapidly (dropping from 250/140 to 135/75 in 30 minutes is a massive overcorrection), these adapted vessels cannot dilate enough to maintain perfusion. This results in profound cerebral hypoperfusion (confusion, slurred speech) and renal ischemia (oliguria). The standard of care is to reduce the MAP by no more than 20% to 25% within the first hour to prevent iatrogenic multi-system failure. The priority action is to immediately decrease or stop the antihypertensive drip to allow a permissive rise in blood pressure, restoring organ perfusion.
</details>

---

### Question 100
A client with end-stage ischemic cardiomyopathy and a valid "Do Not Resuscitate" (DNR) order is admitted in severe cardiogenic shock. The client has a permanent Implantable Cardioverter-Defibrillator (ICD) in place. The client becomes completely unresponsive, and the bedside monitor shows coarse ventricular fibrillation. The client's body jolts violently as the ICD delivers a shock, but the rhythm remains pulseless ventricular fibrillation. The ICD begins charging for a second shock. What is the nurse's priority ethical and legal action?

A) Initiate high-quality chest compressions immediately because the ICD is failing to restore a rhythm.
B) Administer IV amiodarone to medically suppress the ventricular fibrillation and assist the device.
C) Override the DNR order because the device is providing automated, mechanical resuscitation.
D) Place a clinical magnet over the ICD generator to deactivate the shock therapy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Place a clinical magnet over the ICD generator to deactivate the shock therapy.

**Lens:** LEGAL/ETHICAL ADVOCACY (Handling DNR, Informed Consent, or Advocacy in a system-related crisis)
**System Focus:** Cardiogenic Shock / Lethal Arrhythmias

**Rationale:** The client has a valid DNR order, which means they have legally elected not to receive life-prolonging resuscitative measures, including defibrillation, in the event of cardiac arrest. However, an active ICD will automatically fire when it detects a lethal arrhythmia, causing painful jolts to the dying client and directly violating the intent of the DNR. The ethical and priority nursing action is to advocate for a peaceful, natural death by immediately placing a clinical magnet over the ICD pulse generator. This temporarily suspends the device's tachyarrhythmia detection and shock therapies. Initiating compressions (Option A) or giving antiarrhythmics (Option B) violates the DNR order. An ICD does not invalidate a DNR (Option C).
</details>
### Question 101
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**
A client is 6 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. The nurse notes a sudden cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage, which had previously been 100 mL/hr. The client's blood pressure drops from 118/72 mm Hg to 84/50 mm Hg, heart sounds become distant and muffled, and the jugular veins are significantly distended. What is the nurse's immediate life-saving action?

A. Administer IV norepinephrine to maintain a mean arterial pressure greater than 65 mm Hg.
B. Strip the mediastinal chest tubes aggressively to dislodge the suspected clot.
C. Prepare the client for an emergency echocardiogram and pericardiocentesis.
D. Administer a rapid 1,000 mL normal saline fluid bolus to increase preload.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting Beck's triad (hypotension, muffled heart tones, and jugular venous distention) combined with a sudden stop in chest tube output, which is the classic presentation of acute cardiac tamponade. Blood is accumulating in the pericardial sac, compressing the heart and severely restricting cardiac output. The immediate life-saving intervention is emergency decompression via pericardiocentesis or surgical re-exploration. Stripping chest tubes (Option B) is contraindicated as it creates dangerous negative pressure that can damage mediastinal tissues. While fluids (Option D) or pressors (Option A) might temporarily support blood pressure, they do not resolve the mechanical compression and delay the definitive rescue action.
</details>

---

### Question 102
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with a hypertensive crisis (BP 240/130 mm Hg) and is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside at 4 mcg/kg/min. After 48 hours, the client becomes profoundly lethargic, develops hyperreflexia, tinnitus, and an ABG reveals a worsening metabolic acidosis. Which action should the nurse take immediately?

A. Decrease the nitroprusside rate by half and assess a STAT comprehensive metabolic panel.
B. Initiate an IV infusion of labetalol to replace the nitroprusside.
C. Administer IV flumazenil immediately to reverse the suspected neurotoxicity.
D. Discontinue the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolizes into thiocyanate and cyanide. Prolonged infusions, especially at higher doses or in clients with renal/hepatic impairment, can lead to cyanide toxicity. Signs include altered mental status, hyperreflexia, tinnitus, seizures, and unexplained metabolic acidosis (due to lactic acidosis from inhibited cellular respiration). The nurse must immediately stop the infusion and prepare the antidote, which is sodium thiosulfate (or sodium nitrite). Simply decreasing the rate (Option A) or switching medications without addressing the toxicity (Option B) is unsafe. Flumazenil (Option C) is the antidote for benzodiazepines, not nitroprusside toxicity.
</details>

---

### Question 103
**LENS: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**
A client with decompensated heart failure and a history of chronic severe alcoholism becomes suddenly unresponsive. The cardiac monitor displays a rapid, wide-complex polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a continuously changing QRS axis that appears to twist around the isoelectric line. The client has a faint carotid pulse. Which diagnostic finding is the most likely cause of this lethal arrhythmia?

A. Serum magnesium 1.1 mEq/L
B. Serum potassium 6.1 mEq/L
C. Brain natriuretic peptide 1,200 pg/mL
D. PaO2 55 mm Hg on room air

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes, a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by QRS complexes that "twist" around the baseline. It is uniquely and most commonly precipitated by a prolonged QT interval, which is classically caused by severe hypomagnesemia (normal Mg is 1.5–2.5 mEq/L). Clients with chronic alcoholism are at an exceptionally high risk for severe magnesium depletion. Hyperkalemia (Option B) typically causes peaked T waves, a widened QRS, or sine-wave ventricular tachycardia, but not the characteristic "twisting" of Torsades. Elevated BNP (Option C) confirms heart failure but does not cause arrhythmias, and while hypoxia (Option D) can trigger premature beats, it is not the primary mechanism of Torsades.
</details>

---

### Question 104
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**
A client is in profound cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction and is currently managed with intravenous dobutamine and norepinephrine. Which clinical finding best indicates to the nurse that the primary cardiovascular failure is now resulting in acute secondary organ dysfunction?

A. Central venous pressure (CVP) reading of 14 mm Hg.
B. Urine output of 15 mL/hr for the past 3 consecutive hours.
C. Mean arterial pressure (MAP) fluctuating between 60 and 65 mm Hg.
D. Cool, clammy extremities with a capillary refill time of 4 seconds.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Cardiogenic shock causes profound systemic hypoperfusion. When cardiac output falls critically low, the body shunts blood away from non-vital organs (like the kidneys) to preserve the brain and heart. A sustained urine output of less than 30 mL/hr (Option B) is a hallmark sign of failing compensatory mechanisms and indicates the onset of acute kidney injury (AKI), demonstrating multi-system organ dysfunction. A CVP of 14 mm Hg (Option A) and cool extremities (Option D) are direct consequences of the primary cardiogenic shock (fluid backing up and peripheral vasoconstriction), while borderline MAP (Option C) reflects the ongoing shock state itself rather than a new secondary organ failure.
</details>

---

### Question 105
**LENS: EVALUATION OF CRISIS**
A nurse is managing a client who received an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) 12 hours ago for refractory cardiogenic shock. Which hemodynamic assessment finding provides the best evidence that the therapeutic goal of the mechanical intervention has been successfully achieved?

A. The client exhibits an increased pulmonary artery wedge pressure (PAWP).
B. The client's systolic blood pressure decreases by 15 mm Hg during inspiration.
C. The mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) increases from 45% to 65%.
D. The cardiac monitor displays a new onset of sinus tachycardia at 115 bpm.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The therapeutic goal of an IABP is to increase coronary artery perfusion (via diastolic inflation) and decrease cardiac workload/afterload (via systolic deflation), thereby improving overall cardiac output and tissue perfusion. Normal mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) is 60-80%. In cardiogenic shock, cardiac output is poor, leading tissues to extract a higher percentage of oxygen from the sluggish blood, resulting in a low SvO2 (<50%). An increase in SvO2 from 45% to 65% indicates that cardiac output and systemic oxygen delivery have significantly improved. An increased PAWP (Option A) indicates worsening left ventricular failure. A drop in BP during inspiration (Option B) is pulsus paradoxus (seen in cardiac tamponade). Tachycardia (Option D) increases myocardial oxygen demand and indicates ongoing stress or compensation, not a successful rescue.
</details>
### Question 106
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**

A client with a history of alcohol use disorder and severe heart failure is admitted to the cardiac intensive care unit. The client reports sudden, intense palpitations and dizziness. The nurse notes the cardiac monitor displays a prolonged QT interval that rapidly deteriorates into a twisting, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. Based on the client's rhythm change, which of the following laboratory results most likely precipitated this lethal arrhythmia?

A) Serum potassium 5.6 mEq/L
B) Serum magnesium 1.1 mg/dL
C) Serum calcium 10.9 mg/dL
D) Serum digoxin 2.4 ng/mL

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Serum magnesium 1.1 mg/dL

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Advanced Data Analysis** lens by requiring the interpretation of a lethal EKG rhythm (Torsades de Pointes) and connecting it to the specific toxic or deficient lab value that caused it. 
*   **Option B is correct:** Torsades de Pointes is a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by a "twisting of the points" around the isoelectric line. It is highly associated with a prolonged QT interval. Hypomagnesemia (normal: 1.5–2.5 mg/dL) is a primary trigger for prolonged QT intervals and Torsades de Pointes, especially in clients with a history of alcohol abuse or diuretic use for heart failure. 
*   **Option A is incorrect:** Hyperkalemia typically presents with peaked T waves, widened QRS complexes, and can progress to ventricular fibrillation or asystole, but it is not the classic trigger for Torsades de Pointes.
*   **Option C is incorrect:** Hypercalcemia usually causes a *shortened* QT interval, not a prolonged one. Hypocalcemia would be associated with a prolonged QT interval.
*   **Option D is incorrect:** Digoxin toxicity (levels >2.0 ng/mL) can cause various arrhythmias, including bradycardia, heart blocks, and premature ventricular contractions, and is characterized by a "scooped" ST segment, but it does not classically cause Torsades de Pointes.

</details>

---

### Question 107
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client is admitted in a hypertensive crisis with a blood pressure of 245/135 mmHg. The nurse is titrating a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. Four hours into the infusion, the client suddenly becomes agitated, reports a severe headache, and is highly confused. The nurse assesses the client and notes an almond-like odor to the breath. Arterial blood gases (ABGs) reveal profound metabolic acidosis. What is the priority nursing action?

A) Decrease the sodium nitroprusside infusion rate by half and notify the provider.
B) Elevate the head of the bed to 45 degrees and administer a PRN dose of IV hydralazine.
C) Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.
D) Prepare for immediate endotracheal intubation to compensate for the metabolic acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Stop the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately and prepare to administer sodium thiosulfate.

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **High-Alert Pharmacology** lens by managing a life-threatening complication of a continuous vasoactive drip. 
*   **Option C is correct:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolism produces cyanide. Prolonged or high-dose infusions, especially in clients with renal or hepatic impairment, can lead to cyanide toxicity. Classic signs include altered mental status, severe metabolic acidosis, and an almond odor on the breath. The immediate life-saving action is to stop the infusion and administer the antidote, sodium thiosulfate (or hydroxocobalamin).
*   **Option A is incorrect:** Decreasing the rate by half is inadequate; the infusion must be stopped completely due to active, life-threatening cyanide poisoning.
*   **Option B is incorrect:** While blood pressure must be managed, administering hydralazine does not address the acute cyanide toxicity, which is the immediate threat to the client's life.
*   **Option D is incorrect:** While the client has metabolic acidosis, intubation is not the primary intervention for cyanide toxicity. The root cause (nitroprusside) must be stopped, and the antidote administered to restore cellular oxygen utilization.

</details>

---

### Question 108
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A client is 4 hours post-coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. Over the last hour, the nurse notes that the mediastinal chest tube drainage has abruptly decreased from 110 mL/hr to 10 mL/hr. The client's heart rate has increased from 88 to 112 bpm. Which subtle assessment finding is the earliest indicator that the client is developing a failure to rescue situation related to cardiac tamponade?

A) A decrease in systolic blood pressure of 14 mmHg during normal inspiration.
B) A sudden onset of a widened pulse pressure and irregular respirations.
C) A loud, harsh holosystolic murmur auscultated at the cardiac apex.
D) Completely flattened jugular veins when the head of the bed is elevated to 45 degrees.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) A decrease in systolic blood pressure of 14 mmHg during normal inspiration.

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Risk of Deterioration** lens by challenging the nurse to identify the earliest, subtlest sign of a life-threatening complication (Cardiac Tamponade) before full cardiovascular collapse occurs. 
*   **Option A is correct:** The abrupt cessation of mediastinal chest tube drainage post-cardiac surgery is a red flag for a clotted tube, which can cause blood to accumulate in the pericardial sac, leading to cardiac tamponade. Pulsus paradoxus—a drop in systolic blood pressure of >10 mmHg during inspiration—is one of the earliest and most reliable signs of cardiac tamponade. It occurs because the fluid accumulation prevents the right ventricle from expanding outward, pushing the septum into the left ventricle during inspiratory venous return, thus dropping cardiac output.
*   **Option B is incorrect:** Widened pulse pressure, bradycardia, and irregular respirations represent Cushing's triad, a sign of increased intracranial pressure, not cardiac tamponade. Tamponade presents with a *narrowed* pulse pressure.
*   **Option C is incorrect:** A holosystolic murmur at the apex indicates mitral regurgitation or a ventricular septal defect, not cardiac tamponade. Tamponade typically presents with *muffled* or distant heart sounds.
*   **Option D is incorrect:** Flattened jugular veins indicate severe hypovolemia. Cardiac tamponade causes impaired right-sided heart filling, which leads to venous congestion and *distended* jugular veins (JVD).

</details>

---

### Question 109
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is in profound cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. Despite the placement of an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) and maximum titration of norepinephrine and dobutamine, the client's cardiac index remains critically low. The nurse evaluates the client for secondary organ dysfunction caused by this severe systemic hypoperfusion. Which assessment finding best indicates the client is developing concurrent acute hepatic failure (shock liver)?

A) A progressive increase in serum creatinine from 1.0 mg/dL to 2.8 mg/dL over 24 hours.
B) Development of new-onset bilateral coarse crackles extending into the upper lung lobes.
C) A sudden inability to maintain core body temperature, dropping to 95.2°F (35.1°C).
D) A rapid, massive elevation in serum aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH).

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) A rapid, massive elevation in serum aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH).

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Multi-System Integration** lens by asking how a primary failure in the cardiovascular system (cardiogenic shock) leads directly to the secondary failure of another critical system (the liver). 
*   **Option D is correct:** "Shock liver," or ischemic hepatitis, occurs as a direct result of profound systemic hypoperfusion and hypoxia to the hepatocytes due to severely depressed cardiac output. It classically presents with a rapid, massive spike in transaminases (AST and ALT) and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), often into the thousands, shortly after a hypotensive or shock event.
*   **Option A is incorrect:** An increase in creatinine indicates acute kidney injury (AKI) secondary to poor renal perfusion, which is a complication of shock, but does not indicate hepatic failure.
*   **Option B is incorrect:** Bilateral crackles indicate pulmonary edema, which is a direct sign of the primary left-sided heart failure (cardiogenic shock), not secondary hepatic failure.
*   **Option C is incorrect:** Hypothermia can occur in shock due to poor peripheral perfusion and metabolic failure, but it is a systemic sign and not a specific diagnostic indicator of acute ischemic hepatic failure.

</details>

---

### Question 110
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**

The critical care nurse receives handoff report for four unstable clients on the cardiovascular unit. Which client should the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client 2 days post-myocardial infarction exhibiting premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) at a rate of 4 per minute.
B) A client with a hypertensive emergency receiving an IV labetalol drip who reports a sudden onset of "tearing" pain in the upper back.
C) A client with cardiogenic shock on a continuous norepinephrine infusion whose distal extremities are cool and mottled.
D) A client with acute pericarditis who has an audible friction rub and reports sharp pleuritic chest pain that worsens upon deep inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) A client with a hypertensive emergency receiving an IV labetalol drip who reports a sudden onset of "tearing" pain in the upper back.

**Rationale:** 
This question applies the **Prioritization** lens by requiring the nurse to identify the most immediately life-threatening systemic crisis among four unstable or complex cardiovascular presentations. 
*   **Option B is correct:** A sudden onset of severe, "tearing" or "ripping" back or chest pain in a client with severe hypertension is the classic presentation of an acute aortic dissection. This is a catastrophic, life-threatening emergency that requires immediate surgical intervention and aggressive blood pressure lowering to prevent aortic rupture and sudden death. This client is the highest priority.
*   **Option A is incorrect:** Occasional PVCs (4 per minute) are a common and generally expected finding following a myocardial infarction due to myocardial irritability. While they require monitoring, they are not immediately life-threatening.
*   **Option C is incorrect:** Cool, mottled extremities in a client with cardiogenic shock on norepinephrine (a potent alpha-agonist vasoconstrictor) are expected findings. The medication shunts blood from the periphery to vital organs. While the client is critically ill, this is an expected, managed state, not an acute new emergency.
*   **Option D is incorrect:** Sharp, pleuritic chest pain that worsens on inspiration and an audible pericardial friction rub are classic, expected findings in a client with acute pericarditis. This client requires anti-inflammatory medication and pain management, but does not take priority over an impending aortic rupture.

</details>
### Question 111
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis (Interpreting Lethal EKG Rhythms & Toxic Labs)**

A client admitted for acute decompensated heart failure is receiving continuous intravenous bumetanide. The cardiac monitor suddenly alarms, and the nurse observes a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia where the QRS complexes appear to twist around the isoelectric line. The client is unresponsive, apneic, and pulseless. Based on the analysis of this lethal rhythm and the client's current pharmacotherapy, which immediate intervention should the nurse anticipate?

A) Administer a rapid 1 mg bolus of intravenous epinephrine and prepare for synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules.
B) Administer intravenous atropine 1 mg rapidly while initiating transcutaneous pacing. 
C) Initiate immediate unsynchronized defibrillation followed by the administration of intravenous magnesium sulfate.
D) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of amiodarone 300 mg and prepare for emergent intubation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** C (Initiate immediate unsynchronized defibrillation followed by the administration of intravenous magnesium sulfate.)

**Rationale:** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (TdP), a specific type of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia characterized by QRS complexes that twist around the isoelectric line. It is highly associated with a prolonged QT interval, often triggered by electrolyte disturbances such as hypokalemia or hypomagnesemia (exacerbated here by the continuous loop diuretic, bumetanide). Because the client is unresponsive and pulseless, the rhythm must be treated as ventricular fibrillation with immediate, high-quality CPR and unsynchronized defibrillation. Synchronized cardioversion (Option A) is impossible because the monitor cannot accurately identify a consistent R wave. Intravenous magnesium sulfate is the definitive pharmacological treatment to stabilize the cardiac membrane and terminate TdP. Epinephrine is used in cardiac arrest, but synchronized cardioversion is contraindicated. Amiodarone (Option D) can further prolong the QT interval and is generally avoided in TdP. Atropine and pacing (Option B) are for symptomatic bradycardia.
</details>

---

### Question 112
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology (Titrating IV Drips in a Crisis)**

A client in a hypertensive emergency (initial blood pressure 245/140 mm Hg) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. After 48 hours on the infusion at a high titration rate, the client develops acute confusion, hyperreflexia, and a mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) of 88% (normal: 60-75%). Which is the priority nursing action?

A) Decrease the sodium nitroprusside infusion rate by half and notify the healthcare provider.
B) Obtain an arterial blood gas to assess for respiratory alkalosis caused by hyperventilation.
C) Increase the nitroprusside infusion to lower the cerebral perfusion pressure and relieve confusion.
D) Discontinue the nitroprusside infusion immediately and anticipate administering sodium thiosulfate.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** D (Discontinue the nitroprusside infusion immediately and anticipate administering sodium thiosulfate.)

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic, advanced signs of cyanide toxicity, a severe and potentially fatal complication of prolonged or high-dose sodium nitroprusside therapy. Nitroprusside metabolism produces cyanide ions. Toxicity manifests as altered mental status, hyperreflexia, seizures, and a falsely elevated SvO2. The elevated SvO2 (88%) occurs because cyanide binds to cytochrome oxidase in the mitochondria, preventing the tissues from extracting and utilizing oxygen, leading to profound cellular hypoxia and lactic acidosis despite oxygen being present in the blood. The absolute priority is to stop the offending agent immediately to prevent lethal cellular asphyxiation. Sodium thiosulfate (or hydroxocobalamin) is the specific antidote for cyanide toxicity. Decreasing the rate (Option A) is insufficient given the life-threatening nature of the toxicity.
</details>

---

### Question 113
**Lens: Risk of Deterioration (Earliest Sign of Failure to Rescue)**

A client is 6 hours postoperative following a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The mediastinal chest tube drainage was 150 mL/hr for the first 3 hours but has abruptly decreased to 15 mL over the last hour. The client's central venous pressure (CVP) is steadily rising. Which subtle assessment finding should the nurse recognize as the earliest indicator of impending cardiac tamponade?

A) A drop in systolic blood pressure of 14 mm Hg during inspiration.
B) The sudden appearance of a loud, harsh pericardial friction rub.
C) A profound widening of the arterial pulse pressure on the monitor.
D) A dramatic and rapid increase in hourly urine output.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** A (A drop in systolic blood pressure of 14 mm Hg during inspiration.)

**Rationale:** The scenario describes a client at high risk for cardiac tamponade due to sudden cessation of chest tube drainage (likely a clot in the tube) and rising CVP (indicating back-up of right-sided heart pressures). The earliest, most subtle hemodynamic indicator of impending cardiac tamponade is *pulsus paradoxus*, defined as an exaggerated decrease in systolic blood pressure (>10 mm Hg) during inspiration. This occurs because the accumulating pericardial fluid restricts right ventricular expansion; during inspiration, increased venous return to the right side of the heart forces the intraventricular septum to bow into the left ventricle, critically reducing left ventricular stroke volume and systemic blood pressure. A pericardial friction rub (Option B) indicates pericarditis, not necessarily tamponade (tamponade produces *muffled* heart sounds). Tamponade causes a *narrowed* (not widened) pulse pressure (Option C) and decreased (not increased) urine output due to falling cardiac output (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 114
**Lens: Multi-System Integration (Cardiogenic Shock & Secondary System Failure)**

A client is in the intensive care unit experiencing profound cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior myocardial infarction. The client is intubated, receiving a high-dose continuous infusion of norepinephrine, and has an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) in place. Over the past 4 hours, urine output has dropped to 5 mL/hr, and the serum creatinine has doubled. The nurse understands that the cardiovascular failure is triggering an acute kidney injury (AKI). Which collaborative intervention best addresses the integrated pathophysiology of the failing cardiovascular and renal systems?

A) Administering a rapid 1,000 mL bolus of 0.9% Normal Saline to forcefully increase renal artery perfusion.
B) Anticipating the addition of an inotrope like dobutamine to improve forward cardiac output while carefully weaning the norepinephrine.
C) Administering a high-dose continuous intravenous infusion of furosemide to force diuresis and flush the renal tubules.
D) Preparing the client for the emergent placement of bilateral percutaneous nephrostomy tubes.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** B (Anticipating the addition of an inotrope like dobutamine to improve forward cardiac output while carefully weaning the norepinephrine.)

**Rationale:** Cardiogenic shock causes profound pump failure, leading to severely reduced cardiac output (CO) and subsequent prerenal acute kidney injury (AKI). Furthermore, high-dose norepinephrine (a potent alpha-agonist) causes severe peripheral and splanchnic vasoconstriction, which can further clamp down on the renal arteries and exacerbate the AKI. Adding a positive inotrope (such as dobutamine or milrinone) increases myocardial contractility and forward flow (cardiac output). As cardiac output improves, systemic perfusion improves, allowing the critical care team to down-titrate the norepinephrine. This combined approach reduces extreme renal vasoconstriction while delivering adequate blood volume to the kidneys, addressing both the primary cardiac failure and the secondary renal failure. A fluid bolus (Option A) is contraindicated in cardiogenic shock as it will worsen pulmonary edema and right-sided heart failure. High-dose diuretics (Option C) in a hypotensive, shock state will further deplete intravascular volume and worsen the prerenal AKI. Nephrostomy tubes (Option D) treat postrenal (obstructive) AKI, not prerenal AKI.
</details>

---

### Question 115
**Lens: Prioritization (Who do you see first among 4 unstable clients?)**

After receiving the shift report in the cardiac intensive care unit, which client should the nurse assess **FIRST**?

A) A client with a hypertensive emergency receiving an esmolol infusion whose blood pressure has decreased from 210/115 mm Hg to 170/95 mm Hg over the last 2 hours.
B) A client with an implantable cardioverter-defibrillator (ICD) that fired once during the previous shift, who is now resting comfortably in a paced rhythm.
C) A client 3 days post-myocardial infarction who suddenly developed extreme dyspnea and a new, loud holosystolic murmur best heard at the cardiac apex.
D) A client with acute pericarditis who is sitting upright and leaning over the bedside table to relieve sharp, pleuritic chest pain.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>
**Correct Answer:** C (A client 3 days post-myocardial infarction who suddenly developed extreme dyspnea and a new, loud holosystolic murmur best heard at the cardiac apex.)

**Rationale:** A new, loud holosystolic murmur at the apex accompanied by sudden extreme dyspnea in a client recent to a myocardial infarction is a classic, red-flag presentation for papillary muscle rupture. This catastrophic structural complication leads to acute, massive mitral valve regurgitation, rapid pulmonary edema, and sudden cardiogenic shock. This client requires immediate emergency surgical intervention and hemodynamic support. The client on the esmolol drip (Option A) is demonstrating an expected and desired gradual reduction in blood pressure (lowering blood pressure too rapidly in a hypertensive emergency can cause cerebral or cardiac ischemia). The ICD client (Option B) had an expected device response to an arrhythmia and is currently stable. The client with pericarditis (Option D) is exhibiting the classic presentation of the disease, and the position of leaning forward is a well-known, expected compensatory mechanism to reduce pericardial friction and pain; they do not represent an immediate threat to life compared to the papillary muscle rupture.
</details>
### Question 116
**Multi-System Integration**

A client is in the intensive care unit in cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior myocardial infarction. The client is receiving continuous intravenous dobutamine and norepinephrine. The nurse notes the sudden development of profound hypoxemia, bilateral dense infiltrates on the portable chest radiograph, and a rapidly declining PaO2/FiO2 ratio of 115 (normal > 400). The client is currently on a 100% non-rebreather mask. Which advanced intervention should the nurse anticipate next to manage this multi-system complication?

A) Administration of a rapid 500 mL normal saline fluid bolus to augment cardiac preload.
B) Initiation of a continuous bumetanide infusion to rapidly mobilize pulmonary fluid.
C) Immediate preparation for endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation with PEEP.
D) Stat administration of intravenous atropine sulfate to increase chronotropic demand.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale & Strategy</b></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** This client is exhibiting severe left ventricular forward failure (cardiogenic shock), which has caused backflow into the pulmonary circulation, precipitating acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) or refractory pulmonary edema. A PaO2/FiO2 ratio less than 200 indicates severe shunting and hypoxemia that will not respond simply to supplemental oxygen via a non-rebreather mask. The immediate life-saving intervention for this secondary respiratory failure is endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation with Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) to stent open collapsed alveoli, improve oxygenation, and decrease the work of breathing, which in turn decreases myocardial oxygen demand. 

**Incorrect Options:**
- **A:** Administering a rapid fluid bolus is strictly contraindicated in cardiogenic shock complicated by pulmonary edema, as it will exacerbate fluid overload and worsen respiratory failure.
- **B:** While diuretics like bumetanide are used for cardiogenic pulmonary edema, this client is in profound shock (requiring norepinephrine) with a severely low PaO2/FiO2 ratio. Diuretics alone will not resolve the immediate refractory hypoxemia and may worsen the shock state by depleting intravascular volume too rapidly. Intubation is the immediate priority.
- **D:** Atropine is indicated for symptomatic bradycardia. Increasing the heart rate in a failing, ischemic heart without addressing the primary severe hypoxemia will exponentially increase myocardial oxygen demand and worsen the infarction. 

**Test-Taking Strategy:** Identify the primary problem: Cardiogenic shock leading to secondary respiratory failure (ARDS/severe pulmonary edema). Focus on the "PaO2/FiO2 ratio of 115," which demands mechanical ventilation. 
</details>

---

### Question 117
**Advanced Data Analysis**

A client admitted with a hypertensive crisis and a history of heart failure is placed on continuous telemetry. The monitor suddenly displays polymorphic ventricular tachycardia (Torsades de Pointes). The nurse rapidly reviews the client's morning arterial blood gases, current medications, and laboratory profile. Which paired data set provides the most accurate explanation for the etiology of this lethal dysrhythmia?

A) PaCO2 55 mmHg and a serum potassium level of 4.8 mEq/L.
B) Serum calcium 10.2 mg/dL and a serum digoxin level of 1.5 ng/mL.
C) PaO2 88 mmHg and a serum sodium level of 131 mEq/L.
D) Serum magnesium 1.1 mg/dL and concomitant administration of intravenous ondansetron.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale & Strategy</b></summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** Torsades de Pointes is a specific form of polymorphic ventricular tachycardia that is classically precipitated by a prolonged QT interval. Hypomagnesemia (normal magnesium is 1.5 - 2.5 mEq/L or 1.8 - 2.6 mg/dL) is a primary trigger for prolonged QT intervals and Torsades. Furthermore, ondansetron is a high-risk medication known to prolong the QT interval. The combination of critically low magnesium and a QT-prolonging medication creates a highly toxic environment that triggers this lethal dysrhythmia. 

**Incorrect Options:**
- **A:** A PaCO2 of 55 mmHg indicates respiratory acidosis, and a potassium level of 4.8 mEq/L is normal (3.5 - 5.0 mEq/L). While acidosis can cause arrhythmias, this specific pairing does not classically cause Torsades de Pointes.
- **B:** A serum calcium of 10.2 mg/dL is within normal limits (9.0 - 10.5 mg/dL), and a digoxin level of 1.5 ng/mL is within the therapeutic range (0.8 - 2.0 ng/mL). Digoxin toxicity typically causes premature ventricular contractions, bradycardia, or heart blocks, not Torsades.
- **C:** A PaO2 of 88 mmHg is normal, and a sodium level of 131 mEq/L indicates mild hyponatremia, which can cause neurological changes (confusion, seizures) but is not a direct trigger for Torsades de Pointes.

**Test-Taking Strategy:** When you see "Torsades de Pointes," immediately associate it with "prolonged QT interval." Look for the option containing hypomagnesemia, hypokalemia, hypocalcemia, or QT-prolonging drugs (like antiemetics, antipsychotics, or class III antiarrhythmics). 
</details>

---

### Question 118
**Emergency Rescue**

A client is 12 hours postoperative following a coronary artery bypass graft (CABG). The nurse notes a sudden cessation of drainage from the mediastinal chest tubes. Hemodynamic monitoring reveals a blood pressure drop from 110/70 mmHg to 82/65 mmHg, pulsus paradoxus, and muffled heart sounds. The client appears highly restless. What is the immediate life-saving nursing action?

A) Aggressively "milk" or strip the mediastinal chest tubes to dislodge the suspected clot.
B) Prepare the bedside for an emergency sternotomy or pericardiocentesis.
C) Initiate a continuous infusion of epinephrine to rapidly increase cardiac contractility.
D) Position the client flat and elevate the lower extremities to augment venous return.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale & Strategy</b></summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** This client is presenting with classic signs of acute cardiac tamponade (Beck's triad: hypotension, muffled heart sounds, elevated venous pressure/JVD), complicated here by pulsus paradoxus and a sudden cessation of chest tube drainage (indicating blood is pooling in the mediastinum/pericardial sac, compressing the heart). This is an absolute surgical emergency. The nurse must immediately notify the provider and prepare for life-saving decompression via an emergency bedside sternotomy or pericardiocentesis. 

**Incorrect Options:**
- **A:** Aggressively milking or stripping chest tubes is strictly contraindicated. It can generate extreme negative pressure (up to -400 cm H2O), which can damage the delicate graft sites or myocardial tissue, leading to fatal hemorrhage.
- **C:** Epinephrine is a vasopressor/inotrope, but it will not fix the mechanical compression of the heart. The heart cannot expand to fill during diastole due to the fluid surrounding it; administering inotropes without relieving the pressure is futile and wastes precious time.
- **D:** Positioning the client flat with elevated legs (modified Trendelenburg) increases venous return. In cardiac tamponade, the right heart is already failing to accommodate incoming volume due to external compression; this position can dangerously increase intracardiac pressures and worsen the client's respiratory and hemodynamic distress.

**Test-Taking Strategy:** Recognize the complication: Sudden stop in chest tube drainage + Beck's Triad = Cardiac Tamponade. Tamponade is a *mechanical* problem requiring a *mechanical* solution (drainage of the fluid/blood). 
</details>

---

### Question 119
**High-Alert Pharmacology**

A client admitted with a hypertensive emergency (initial BP 245/140 mmHg) has been receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside for the past 48 hours. The client’s blood pressure is currently 155/88 mmHg. During assessment, the client is profoundly lethargic, confused, and reports ringing in the ears. The nurse notes a mixed venous oxygen saturation (SvO2) of 89% (normal 60-80%). What is the nurse's priority clinical action?

A) Discontinue the sodium nitroprusside infusion immediately.
B) Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate by 50% and reassess the client in 15 minutes.
C) Administer a PRN dose of intravenous labetalol to ensure the blood pressure remains stable.
D) Draw a stat arterial blood gas to assess for compensatory respiratory alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale & Strategy</b></summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolizes into cyanide and thiocyanate. Prolonged infusions (especially >48 hours or at high doses) place the client at high risk for cyanide toxicity. A hallmark of cyanide toxicity is *histotoxic hypoxia*—the cells are unable to extract and use oxygen from the blood. This results in a paradoxically high venous oxygen saturation (SvO2 > 80%), alongside altered mental status, tinnitus, hyperreflexia, and metabolic acidosis. The absolute priority is to stop the toxic infusion immediately and prepare to administer an antidote (like sodium thiosulfate).

**Incorrect Options:**
- **B:** Decreasing the rate is insufficient. The client is already exhibiting systemic toxicity (neurological changes and impaired cellular oxygenation). The drug must be stopped entirely.
- **C:** Administering labetalol without stopping the nitroprusside ignores the active poisoning occurring in the client. The priority is halting the offending agent.
- **D:** Drawing an ABG may show metabolic acidosis (lactic acidosis due to anaerobic metabolism), but assessing is not the priority when clear signs of a life-threatening pharmacological complication are present. The priority is intervention (stopping the drip).

**Test-Taking Strategy:** Apply the rule of High-Alert Pharmacology: If a client displays specific signs of severe toxicity (tinnitus, high SvO2 with nitroprusside = cyanide toxicity), the immediate action is ALWAYS to stop the infusion before calling the provider or performing further assessments.
</details>

---

### Question 120
**Risk of Deterioration**

The nurse is caring for a client recovering from an extensive anterior wall myocardial infarction. The client is currently stable but remains at high risk for cardiogenic shock. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the earliest hemodynamic deterioration and a potential "failure to rescue" if missed?

A) An isolated mean arterial pressure (MAP) reading of 63 mmHg.
B) Intermittent sharp chest pain rated 2/10 that worsens with deep inspiration.
C) A progressive narrowing of the pulse pressure to 20 mmHg with cool extremities.
D) A slight increase in the client's respiratory rate from 16 to 20 breaths per minute.

<details>
<summary><b>Rationale & Strategy</b></summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** Cardiogenic shock occurs when the heart loses its pumping ability, resulting in a severely decreased stroke volume. The body's initial compensatory response is massive sympathetic nervous system activation, which causes vasoconstriction (increasing systemic vascular resistance [SVR] and diastolic blood pressure) while the failing heart continues to produce a low systolic blood pressure. This results in a severely narrowed pulse pressure (systolic minus diastolic). A pulse pressure dropping to 20 mmHg, combined with peripheral vasoconstriction (cool extremities), is the earliest, most insidious hallmark of impending cardiogenic shock, occurring well before overt systemic hypotension (drop in MAP) manifests.

**Incorrect Options:**
- **A:** While a MAP of 63 mmHg is slightly below the normal >65 mmHg threshold, an *isolated* reading is not as reliable or deeply indicative of early compensatory failure as a dynamically narrowing pulse pressure coupled with sympathetic shunting (cool extremities).
- **B:** Sharp chest pain that worsens with deep inspiration is characteristic of acute pericarditis, a common complication post-MI (Dressler's syndrome), but it does not specifically indicate impending cardiogenic shock or immediate life-threatening hemodynamic collapse.
- **D:** A respiratory rate of 20 is at the upper limit of normal and is non-specific. It could be related to pain, anxiety, or early pulmonary congestion, but it is not the most definitive, system-specific early warning sign of failing cardiac output compared to a severely narrowed pulse pressure.

**Test-Taking Strategy:** When evaluating for early signs of shock, look for compensatory mechanisms. Narrowing pulse pressure means the body is clamping down the blood vessels (raising diastolic) because the heart is failing to push blood forward (lowering systolic). This is a vital early warning sign of 'failure to rescue' in cardiology.
</details>

---
### Question 121
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?)
**System Focus:** Cardiac Tamponade / Hypertensive Crisis / Cardiogenic Shock / Arrhythmias

The charge nurse on a cardiac step-down unit has just received shift report. Which client must the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with acute pericarditis reporting sharp, pleuritic chest pain that significantly improves when leaning forward.
B) A client 3 days post-myocardial infarction exhibiting sudden muffled heart sounds and a narrowed pulse pressure.
C) A client in a hypertensive emergency on a nicardipine continuous infusion whose blood pressure decreased from 220/120 to 195/105 mmHg in one hour.
D) A client with chronic atrial fibrillation and a ventricular rate of 118 bpm who is waiting for a scheduled diltiazem dose.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** 
- **B is correct:** This client is exhibiting Beck's triad (muffled heart sounds, narrowed pulse pressure/hypotension, and jugular venous distention), which is the classic presentation of **cardiac tamponade**. Post-MI clients are at risk for ventricular free wall rupture leading to tamponade, which is a rapidly fatal emergency requiring immediate pericardiocentesis.
- **A is incorrect:** Sharp chest pain that worsens with inspiration and improves upon leaning forward is the expected, classic presentation of acute pericarditis. While painful, it is not an immediate life-threatening emergency.
- **C is incorrect:** In a hypertensive emergency, the initial goal is to reduce the mean arterial pressure (MAP) by no more than 20% to 25% within the first hour to prevent cerebral, coronary, or renal ischemia. Dropping from 220/120 to 195/105 mmHg is an expected and therapeutic response to the nicardipine drip.
- **D is incorrect:** A heart rate of 118 bpm in atrial fibrillation (rapid ventricular response) requires intervention, but the client is chronic and awaiting scheduled medication. This does not take priority over impending cardiovascular collapse from cardiac tamponade.

</details>

---

### Question 122
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)
**System Focus:** Hypertensive Crisis

The nurse is titrating a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside for a client in a severe hypertensive crisis. The client has been on the infusion at a high dose for 48 hours. Which assessment finding requires the nurse to immediately halt the infusion and notify the rapid response team?

A) A decrease in mean arterial pressure (MAP) by 15% over the first two hours of therapy.
B) Serum creatinine increasing from 0.9 mg/dL to 1.1 mg/dL over the past 24 hours.
C) Client reports of a mild throbbing headache and visibly flushed skin on the face and neck.
D) Sudden disorientation, profound unexplained metabolic acidosis, and an almond-like odor on the breath.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** 
- **D is correct:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolism produces cyanide and thiocyanate. Prolonged infusions, high doses, or renal/hepatic impairment can lead to **cyanide toxicity**. Classic signs of cyanide toxicity include altered mental status, profound metabolic acidosis, and the scent of bitter almonds on the breath. The infusion must be stopped immediately, and antidotes (e.g., sodium thiosulfate) administered.
- **A is incorrect:** A gradual decrease in MAP by 15-25% is the strict therapeutic goal in treating a hypertensive emergency to safely lower BP without causing sudden end-organ ischemia.
- **B is incorrect:** A mild fluctuation in creatinine is not a life-threatening emergency requiring the immediate cessation of a life-saving drip, though it warrants monitoring since thiocyanate is renally excreted.
- **C is incorrect:** Throbbing headaches and facial flushing are expected, benign side effects of the potent, rapid systemic vasodilation caused by nitroprusside.

</details>

---

### Question 123
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting lethal EKG rhythms)
**System Focus:** Lethal Arrhythmias

A client admitted following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction suddenly reports severe dizziness and profound weakness. The client's blood pressure is 78/40 mmHg. The telemetry monitor shows regular P waves at a rate of 88 bpm, regular widened QRS complexes at a rate of 34 bpm, and absolutely no correlation between the P waves and the QRS complexes. What is the priority, data-driven nursing intervention?

A) Prepare the client for immediate transcutaneous pacing and notify the provider.
B) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of atropine sulfate 1 mg.
C) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of amiodarone 300 mg.
D) Initiate a continuous intravenous infusion of diltiazem.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** 
- **A is correct:** The EKG description (regular P waves, regular wide QRS complexes, rate of 34, no correlation between P and QRS) is the hallmark of a **Third-Degree (Complete) Heart Block**. The client is highly symptomatic and hemodynamically unstable (BP 78/40). The definitive and immediate emergency treatment for a symptomatic complete heart block is **transcutaneous pacing**.
- **B is incorrect:** While atropine is the first-line drug for symptomatic sinus bradycardia, it works by blocking vagal stimulation at the SA and AV nodes. Incomplete/Third-degree heart blocks—especially wide-complex blocks associated with anterior wall MIs (which occur below the AV node in the bundle of His or Purkinje fibers)—will typically NOT respond to atropine and it may inadvertently worsen ischemia.
- **C is incorrect:** Amiodarone is an antiarrhythmic used for ventricular tachycardia or ventricular fibrillation. It is contraindicated in complete heart block as it can further depress automaticity and cause asystole.
- **D is incorrect:** Diltiazem is a calcium channel blocker used to slow heart rate in supraventricular tachycardias (like atrial fibrillation). It is absolutely contraindicated in bradycardia and heart blocks.

</details>

---

### Question 124
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)
**System Focus:** Cardiogenic Shock

A client is in the intensive care unit in profound cardiogenic shock following ischemic damage to the left ventricle. The client’s cardiac index is severely reduced at 1.5 L/min/m². Over the last 12 hours, the client’s urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr, BUN is 62 mg/dL, and liver enzymes (AST/ALT) are steeply rising. Which advanced pathophysiological mechanism best explains these multi-system findings?

A) A massive systemic inflammatory response is causing widespread distributive vasodilation, resulting in relative hypovolemia.
B) Widespread microvascular thrombosis is resulting in a consumptive coagulopathy and secondary ischemic organ necrosis.
C) Intense sympathetic nervous system activation is causing profound splanchnic and renal vasoconstriction to preserve cerebral flow.
D) Aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation has caused extreme hemodilution and massive retroperitoneal third-spacing.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** 
- **C is correct:** In cardiogenic shock, pump failure leads to severely decreased cardiac output. The body compensates via massive **sympathetic nervous system (SNS) activation** and the Renin-Angiotensin-Aldosterone System (RAAS). This causes intense peripheral, renal, and splanchnic (GI/liver) vasoconstriction to shunt the limited blood volume to the vital organs (heart and brain). This prolonged hypoperfusion causes pre-renal acute kidney injury (oliguria, high BUN) and ischemic hepatitis ("shock liver," rising AST/ALT).
