- **A is incorrect:** Distributive shock with systemic vasodilation describes septic, neurogenic, or anaphylactic shock, not cardiogenic shock. Cardiogenic shock is characterized by severe compensatory *vasoconstriction* (high systemic vascular resistance).
- **B is incorrect:** This describes Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation (DIC), which can be a terminal complication of shock but does not represent the primary compensatory mechanism driving pre-renal AKI and shock liver in early cardiogenic shock.
- **D is incorrect:** Cardiogenic shock treatment requires cautious fluid management or diuresis, not aggressive fluid resuscitation, as the failing heart cannot handle volume (leading to pulmonary edema). Furthermore, hemodilution does not cause rising liver enzymes.

</details>

---

### Question 125
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')
**System Focus:** Cardiac Tamponade

A client is 4 hours post-coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG). The nurse observes that the mediastinal chest tube drainage, which was steadily draining 100 mL/hr, has abruptly ceased over the past 30 minutes. The client’s heart rate is slowly trending upward. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the client is at imminent risk for cardiac tamponade?

A) A gradual decrease in central venous pressure (CVP) from 10 mmHg to 6 mmHg.
B) A loud, harsh systolic murmur auscultated at the right second intercostal space.
C) Bounding peripheral pulses accompanied by flushed, abnormally warm extremities.
D) A pulsus paradoxus of 18 mmHg measured during normal inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** 
- **D is correct:** The sudden cessation of chest tube drainage post-CABG strongly suggests a clot in the tube, which can cause blood to rapidly accumulate in the pericardial space, leading to **cardiac tamponade**. An early and subtle hallmark sign of tamponade is **pulsus paradoxus**—an abnormally large decrease in systolic blood pressure (greater than 10 mmHg) during inspiration. 
- **A is incorrect:** Cardiac tamponade causes an *increase* in central venous pressure (CVP), not a decrease, because the accumulated pericardial fluid compresses the right side of the heart, preventing venous return and causing blood to back up into the systemic circulation (manifesting as elevated CVP and JVD).
- **B is incorrect:** A loud, harsh systolic murmur at the right second intercostal space is a classic finding of aortic stenosis, a chronic valvular condition, not an acute sign of cardiac tamponade.
- **C is incorrect:** Tamponade causes obstructive shock, which leads to severely reduced cardiac output resulting in weak, thready pulses and cool, clammy extremities. Bounding pulses and warm extremities are signs of early distributive (septic) shock.

</details>
### Question 126
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION**
The nurse has just received the shift report in the cardiovascular intensive care unit (CVICU). Which of the following four clients should the nurse assess **FIRST**?

A) A client with atrial fibrillation receiving a continuous diltiazem infusion, who is asymptomatic with an apical heart rate of 108 bpm.  
B) A client in a hypertensive emergency receiving a nicardipine drip, who complains of a mild, throbbing headache and has a blood pressure of 168/92 mm Hg.  
C) A client 4 hours post-coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) whose mediastinal chest tube output abruptly dropped from 110 mL/hr to 0 mL/hr over the last hour, accompanied by a rising heart rate.  
D) A client in cardiogenic shock receiving continuous milrinone, who has a mean arterial pressure (MAP) of 64 mm Hg and a urine output of 25 mL/hr.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) A client 4 hours post-coronary artery bypass grafting (CABG) whose mediastinal chest tube output abruptly dropped from 110 mL/hr to 0 mL/hr over the last hour, accompanied by a rising heart rate.

**Rationale:** 
This client is exhibiting the classic, early warning signs of developing cardiac tamponade, a rapidly lethal complication post-cardiac surgery. The sudden cessation of chest tube drainage indicates a potential blockage (clot), causing blood to accumulate in the pericardial space. The rising heart rate is an early compensatory response to the falling stroke volume caused by ventricular compression. This requires immediate intervention (e.g., milking the tube if hospital protocol allows, or immediately notifying the surgeon for re-exploration). 
Option A is stable; rate control is actively being managed. Option B is expected; mild headaches are a common side effect of calcium channel blockers (nicardipine) and rapid blood pressure shifts, and the BP is safely trending down. Option D is chronically critically ill, but a MAP of 64 mm Hg and marginal urine output are expected baselines currently being managed by the milrinone drip; this client is stable in their instability compared to the acute mechanical failure in Option C.
</details>

---

### Question 127
**LENS: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**
A client with a history of severe heart failure is admitted for acute decompensation and is receiving intravenous amiodarone and furosemide. Suddenly, the telemetry monitor alarms, displaying a rapid, polymorphic ventricular tachycardia with a continuously shifting QRS axis. The client remains faintly responsive but lethargic. Which of the following morning laboratory results requires the most immediate clinical integration to explain this rhythm?

A) Serum digoxin level of 1.1 ng/mL  
B) Serum potassium level of 4.0 mEq/L  
C) Serum sodium level of 134 mEq/L  
D) Serum magnesium level of 1.1 mEq/L  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Serum magnesium level of 1.1 mEq/L

**Rationale:** 
The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (polymorphic ventricular tachycardia), a lethal arrhythmia associated with a prolonged QT interval. Amiodarone is a class III antiarrhythmic that significantly prolongs the QT interval. Furosemide is a loop diuretic that causes the renal depletion of potassium and magnesium. A critically low serum magnesium level (normal is 1.7-2.2 mEq/L) heavily potentiates the QT-prolonging effects of amiodarone, triggering Torsades de Pointes. 
Option A is a normal therapeutic digoxin level (0.8-2.0 ng/mL) and does not typically cause Torsades. Option B is a normal potassium level. Option C is a mild hyponatremia that does not acutely trigger polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. Recognizing the triad of QT-prolonging drugs, loop diuretics, and hypomagnesemia is critical in advanced cardiac data analysis.
</details>

---

### Question 128
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
A client in a hypertensive emergency is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside at 5 mcg/kg/min. Over the past hour, the client has developed profound confusion, hyperreflexia, and a faint almond odor on their breath. The latest arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals severe, uncompensated metabolic acidosis. What is the nurse's priority pharmacological intervention?

A) Immediately stop the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer intravenous sodium thiosulfate.  
B) Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate by half and draw a stat serum thiocyanate level.  
C) Administer a rapid 500 mL bolus of 0.9% normal saline to dilute the systemic toxicity.  
D) Administer intravenous sodium bicarbonate to correct the severe metabolic acidosis.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Immediately stop the nitroprusside infusion and prepare to administer intravenous sodium thiosulfate.

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting the classic signs of cyanide toxicity, a life-threatening complication of high-dose or prolonged sodium nitroprusside therapy. Symptoms include altered mental status, hyperreflexia, a bitter almond odor on the breath, and severe lactic (metabolic) acidosis due to the halting of cellular respiration. The absolute priority is to halt the administration of the offending agent immediately and administer the specific antidote, sodium thiosulfate (which converts cyanide to thiocyanate for renal excretion). 
Option B delays life-saving treatment; a stat lab draw is secondary to immediate rescue. Options C and D are symptomatic treatments that completely fail to address the underlying lethal toxicity causing the metabolic blockade.
</details>

---

### Question 129
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**
A client being monitored in the CVICU for an evolving acute myocardial infarction suddenly rolls their eyes back and loses consciousness. The cardiac monitor immediately displays coarse ventricular fibrillation. Defibrillation pads are already attached to the client from admission. What is the nurse's immediate, priority life-saving action?

A) Administer a rapid 2 g intravenous bolus of magnesium sulfate.  
B) Deliver an immediate unsynchronized shock (defibrillation).  
C) Initiate synchronized cardioversion at 100 Joules.  
D) Administer 1 mg of intravenous epinephrine while preparing a bag-valve mask.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Deliver an immediate unsynchronized shock (defibrillation).

**Rationale:** 
For a witnessed cardiac arrest caused by ventricular fibrillation (VF) or pulseless ventricular tachycardia (pVT) where a defibrillator is immediately available and already attached, immediate unsynchronized defibrillation is the absolute highest priority life-saving action. Early defibrillation provides the best chance of restoring an organized, perfusing rhythm. 
Option A is incorrect because magnesium sulfate is indicated for Torsades de Pointes, not standard coarse VF. Option C is a fatal error; a defibrillator cannot synchronize to the disorganized electrical activity of VF, meaning the shock will be withheld by the machine, delaying life-saving therapy. Option D is incorrect; while epinephrine is part of the ACLS algorithm for VF, it is administered only after the first shock and initiation of high-quality CPR, never before immediate defibrillation if the machine is ready.
</details>

---

### Question 130
**LENS: EVALUATION OF CRISIS**
A client is admitted in profound cardiogenic shock following a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction. The healthcare team initiates an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) alongside continuous intravenous infusions of dobutamine and norepinephrine. Which of the following clinical findings provides the best evidence that these rescue interventions are successful?

A) The client's central venous pressure (CVP) increases from 12 mm Hg to 16 mm Hg.  
B) The client's pulmonary artery wedge pressure (PAWP) increases to 22 mm Hg.  
C) The client's capillary refill time is 2 seconds, and hourly urine output increases to 45 mL/hr.  
D) The client's apical heart rate rises from 110 bpm to 140 bpm with occasional premature ventricular contractions (PVCs).  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The client's capillary refill time is 2 seconds, and hourly urine output increases to 45 mL/hr.

**Rationale:** 
The ultimate goal of treating cardiogenic shock with an IABP and vasoactive/inotropic drips (like norepinephrine and dobutamine) is to restore forward cardiac output and adequate end-organ tissue perfusion. A normal capillary refill time (under 3 seconds) and a urine output greater than 30 mL/hr (which requires an adequate mean arterial pressure and renal perfusion) are the most direct physical indicators that systemic tissue perfusion has been successfully restored. 
Option A indicates worsening right-sided fluid overload or failure. Option B (PAWP) reflects left ventricular end-diastolic pressure; an increase indicates worsening pulmonary congestion and left ventricular failure, not success. Option D indicates an adverse effect of the inotropic/vasopressor therapy (tachycardia and dysrhythmias), which dangerously increases myocardial oxygen demand in an already ischemic heart.
</details>
### Question 131
A client admitted with a massive anterior wall myocardial infarction is recovering in the Cardiac Care Unit (CCU). The nurse is monitoring for signs of cardiogenic shock. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the highest risk for imminent hemodynamic deterioration?

A) The client's pulse pressure narrows from 45 mm Hg to 25 mm Hg.
B) The monitor displays new-onset premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) at 4 per minute.
C) The client's hourly urine output decreases from 60 mL/hr to 45 mL/hr.
D) Auscultation reveals fine crackles in the bilateral lung bases that clear with a cough.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The client's pulse pressure narrows from 45 mm Hg to 25 mm Hg.

**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')

**Rationale:** A narrowing pulse pressure (the difference between systolic and diastolic blood pressure) is an early, highly sensitive indicator of impending cardiogenic shock. As the damaged left ventricle fails, stroke volume decreases (lowering the systolic pressure). In response, the sympathetic nervous system triggers profound peripheral vasoconstriction to maintain organ perfusion, which raises the diastolic pressure. This narrowing occurs long before profound hypotension is observed. While 45 mL/hr of urine is lower than previous, it remains above the critical 30 mL/hr threshold. Rare PVCs and clearable crackles do not immediately herald cardiogenic shock as strongly as a narrowing pulse pressure.
</details>

---

### Question 132
A client with a hypertensive emergency is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of sodium nitroprusside. The client's initial blood pressure was 240/130 mm Hg. After 30 minutes, the blood pressure is 150/90 mm Hg, but the client acutely develops profound confusion, ringing in the ears (tinnitus), and reports a bitter almond taste in their mouth. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A) Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate by half.
B) Stop the nitroprusside infusion and notify the healthcare provider.
C) Administer prescribed PRN intravenous labetalol to maintain blood pressure.
D) Obtain an arterial blood gas to assess for compensatory respiratory alkalosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Stop the nitroprusside infusion and notify the healthcare provider.

**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)

**Rationale:** Sodium nitroprusside metabolism produces cyanide and thiocyanate. Toxicity can develop rapidly, especially with high doses or prolonged use. Classic signs of cyanide toxicity include altered mental status (confusion, delirium), tinnitus, hyperreflexia, and occasionally the hallmark "bitter almond" breath or taste. This is an acute, life-threatening emergency. The infusion must be stopped immediately, and the provider notified to initiate antidotal therapy (e.g., sodium thiosulfate). Decreasing the rate or simply obtaining lab work delays life-saving intervention. 
</details>

---

### Question 133
A client receiving intravenous amiodarone for atrial fibrillation suddenly loses consciousness. The telemetry monitor displays a chaotic, undulating ventricular rhythm that twists around the isoelectric baseline. The nurse confirms the client has no palpable carotid pulse. What is the nurse's *first* life-saving action?

A) Administer intravenous magnesium sulfate 2 grams over 5 minutes.
B) Administer intravenous epinephrine 1 mg rapid push.
C) Deliver an unsynchronized defibrillation shock.
D) Prepare the client for immediate transcutaneous pacing.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Deliver an unsynchronized defibrillation shock.

**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)

**Rationale:** The rhythm described is Torsades de Pointes (polymorphic ventricular tachycardia), and the client is pulseless. A pulseless ventricular tachycardia is a lethal arrhythmia that requires immediate CPR and unsynchronized defibrillation. While intravenous magnesium sulfate is the pharmacological treatment of choice for Torsades de Pointes in a stable patient or as an adjunct during cardiac arrest, the absolute primary rescue action for a pulseless client is immediate electrical defibrillation. Epinephrine is given during the resuscitation algorithm but is secondary to immediate electrical shock. 
</details>

---

### Question 134
A client develops acute cardiac tamponade following coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery. The nurse understands that the profound restriction of ventricular filling leads to an acute multi-system ischemic cascade. Which integrated laboratory finding best reflects the secondary organ impairment caused by this sudden hemodynamic collapse?

A) Elevated blood urea nitrogen (BUN) with a normal serum creatinine.
B) Severe hypokalemia combined with metabolic alkalosis.
C) Decreased serum albumin and a prolonged prothrombin time (PT).
D) Rapidly rising serum lactate and acutely elevated liver transaminases (AST/ALT).

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Rapidly rising serum lactate and acutely elevated liver transaminases (AST/ALT).

**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)

**Rationale:** Cardiac tamponade severely restricts diastolic filling, dropping cardiac output and causing profound systemic hypoperfusion (cardiogenic/obstructive shock). A rapidly rising serum lactate is a direct result of inadequate tissue oxygenation, forcing cells into anaerobic metabolism. Concurrently, acute hepatic hypoperfusion leads to ischemic hepatitis (often termed "shock liver"), characterized by a rapid, massive spike in transaminases (AST/ALT). Elevated BUN with normal creatinine implies dehydration rather than acute ischemic kidney injury. Hypokalemia and decreased albumin are not direct, immediate indicators of acute shock-induced organ hypoperfusion.
</details>

---

### Question 135
The healthcare provider has just performed a bedside emergency pericardiocentesis on a client with acute cardiac tamponade resulting from penetrating chest trauma. Which hemodynamic and physical assessment finding provides the most definitive evidence that the rescue intervention was successful?

A) The client's pulse pressure widens and jugular venous distention (JVD) resolves.
B) The client exhibits an inspiratory drop in systolic blood pressure of 18 mm Hg.
C) Muffled heart sounds are immediately replaced by a harsh systolic murmur.
D) The central venous pressure (CVP) increases from 12 mm Hg to 16 mm Hg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The client's pulse pressure widens and jugular venous distention (JVD) resolves.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS (Identifying physical signs that a rescue intervention was successful)

**Rationale:** Cardiac tamponade presents with Beck's triad: hypotension (with narrow pulse pressure), jugular venous distention (JVD), and muffled heart sounds. A successful pericardiocentesis relieves the external pressure on the heart. Resolving JVD indicates that right-sided filling pressures have normalized, and a widening pulse pressure indicates that left ventricular stroke volume and cardiac output have improved. An inspiratory drop in systolic BP > 10 mm Hg is pulsus paradoxus, a hallmark of *ongoing* tamponade. CVP should decrease, not increase, as the backup of blood in the venous system is relieved.
</details>
### Question 136
A client with a hypertensive emergency has been receiving a sodium nitroprusside continuous IV infusion at 5 mcg/kg/min for the past 48 hours. The nurse notes the client is newly disoriented, breathing rapidly, and displaying a profound, therapy-resistant metabolic acidosis on their recent arterial blood gas (ABG) panel. 

Based on high-alert pharmacological principles, which immediate action should the nurse take?

A. Administer an IV bolus of sodium bicarbonate to chemically correct the metabolic acidosis.
B. Decrease the nitroprusside infusion rate to 2.5 mcg/kg/min and reassess the client's mental status.
C. Stop the nitroprusside infusion immediately and prepare to administer IV sodium thiosulfate.
D. Administer a 500 mL bolus of 0.9% normal saline to increase systemic vascular perfusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Stop the nitroprusside infusion immediately and prepare to administer IV sodium thiosulfate.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens 6: High-Alert Pharmacology.** Sodium nitroprusside is a potent vasodilator metabolized into cyanide and thiocyanate. High infusion rates (>3 mcg/kg/min) or prolonged use (>48 hours) significantly increase the risk of cyanide toxicity. 
- Clinical manifestations of cyanide toxicity include altered mental status, profound metabolic acidosis (lactic acidosis due to blocked cellular respiration), tachycardia, and sometimes an almond-like odor to the breath.
- The immediate life-saving intervention is to stop the offending agent (nitroprusside) and administer an antidote, such as sodium thiosulfate, sodium nitrite, or hydroxocobalamin, to bind and excrete the cyanide.
- Option A addresses the symptom (acidosis) but not the lethal root cause (cyanide toxicity).
- Option B is incorrect; decreasing the rate is insufficient when profound toxicity is already present. The drug must be stopped.
- Option D does not treat the underlying cellular asphyxiation caused by cyanide.

</details>

---

### Question 137
A client recovering from an acute anterior myocardial infarction is placed on continuous cardiac telemetry. Due to severe, agitated delirium, the client was given a dose of intravenous haloperidol. The nurse analyzes the latest 12-lead EKG and notes a markedly prolonged QTc interval of 0.58 seconds accompanied by frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs).

Based on this advanced data analysis, which lethal arrhythmia is this client at highest risk for developing, and what is the priority nursing action?

A. Ventricular fibrillation; prepare for emergent endotracheal intubation.
B. Pulseless electrical activity; rapidly administer a normal saline bolus.
C. Third-degree atrioventricular block; prepare for transcutaneous pacing.
D. Torsades de pointes; hold the haloperidol and urgently notify the provider.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Torsades de pointes; hold the haloperidol and urgently notify the provider.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens 3: Advanced Data Analysis.** A prolonged QTc interval (normal is <0.44 sec in men, <0.46 sec in women) places a client at extreme risk for developing Torsades de Pointes, a lethal polymorphic ventricular tachycardia. 
- IV haloperidol is a known high-risk medication for prolonging the QT interval. When combined with myocardial ischemia (acute MI), the risk for the "R-on-T" phenomenon precipitating Torsades is critical.
- The most appropriate, proactive nursing action is to hold any QT-prolonging medications (haloperidol) and notify the provider to prevent a sudden cardiac arrest. The provider may also order IV magnesium sulfate to stabilize the cardiac membrane.
- Options A, B, and C identify incorrect arrhythmias related to a prolonged QT interval. While V-Fib can follow Torsades, Torsades is the specific primary threat linked to QT prolongation.

</details>

---

### Question 138
A client in the intensive care unit is being managed for profound cardiogenic shock. Hemodynamic support includes an intra-aortic balloon pump (IABP) and an intravenous dobutamine infusion. Over the last 6 hours, the nurse notes the client's urine output has dropped to 12 mL/hr, and the serum creatinine has doubled to 2.6 mg/dL. 

Which statement best describes the integrated pathophysiological mechanism causing this secondary system failure?

A. Upward migration of the IABP balloon has occluded the renal arteries, precipitating an acute post-renal obstruction.
B. Dobutamine has triggered profound alpha-1 receptor activation, causing severe renal artery vasospasm.
C. Severe left ventricular dysfunction has critically reduced mean arterial pressure, causing pre-renal hypoperfusion and impending acute tubular necrosis.
D. Systemic venous congestion from cardiogenic shock has caused right-sided heart pressures to rupture the renal parenchyma.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Severe left ventricular dysfunction has critically reduced mean arterial pressure, causing pre-renal hypoperfusion and impending acute tubular necrosis.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens 5: Multi-System Integration.** Cardiogenic shock is characterized by pump failure, leading to a drastically reduced cardiac output and mean arterial pressure (MAP). 
- When MAP falls below the autoregulatory threshold of the kidneys (typically < 60-65 mm Hg), renal perfusion ceases. This initially causes pre-renal acute kidney injury (AKI). If the severe hypoperfusion is sustained, it rapidly progresses to ischemic intra-renal failure, known as acute tubular necrosis (ATN).
- Option A is incorrect anatomically. The renal arteries branch off the *abdominal* aorta. The IABP sits in the *descending thoracic* aorta. If the IABP migrates *upward*, it occludes the left subclavian artery (causing a diminished left radial pulse). If it migrates *downward*, it can occlude the renal arteries. Furthermore, arterial occlusion causes pre-renal or intra-renal injury, not post-renal obstruction.
- Option B is incorrect; dobutamine is primarily a beta-1 agonist (increasing contractility) with mild beta-2 properties (vasodilation). It does not cause intense alpha-1 vasoconstriction (unlike high-dose norepinephrine or epinephrine).
- Option D is physiologically inaccurate; venous congestion does not rupture the renal parenchyma.

</details>

---

### Question 139
A client admitted with a massive malignant pericardial effusion suddenly develops Beck's triad, severe dyspnea, and profound hypotension. The critical care physician performs an emergent bedside pericardiocentesis, draining 450 mL of serosanguinous fluid. 

Which clinical finding provides the nurse with the most reliable indication that the emergency intervention was successful in reversing the cardiac tamponade?

A. The client's pulsus paradoxus resolves and systemic cardiac output increases.
B. The client's central venous pressure (CVP) increases from 14 mm Hg to 18 mm Hg.
C. The client's systolic blood pressure decreases by 15 mm Hg during inspiration.
D. The muffled heart sounds are immediately replaced by a harsh pericardial friction rub.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The client's pulsus paradoxus resolves and systemic cardiac output increases.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens 8: Evaluation of Crisis.** Cardiac tamponade occurs when fluid rapidly accumulates in the pericardial sac, compressing the heart, severely restricting ventricular filling, and drastically reducing cardiac output.
- Pulsus paradoxus (an exaggerated drop in systolic blood pressure >10 mm Hg during inspiration) is a hallmark sign of cardiac tamponade. Its resolution, along with an increase in cardiac output and systemic blood pressure, is the definitive sign that the mechanical compression has been relieved and the rescue intervention (pericardiocentesis) was successful.
- Option B is incorrect; CVP is markedly elevated *during* tamponade due to right-sided heart compression. A successful intervention would cause the CVP to *decrease* toward normal.
- Option C describes the *presence* of pulsus paradoxus (>10 mm Hg drop), indicating that the tamponade is still present or worsening, not resolved.
- Option D is incorrect; while a rub may be present in pericarditis, relying on it as a primary indicator of hemodynamic recovery is inaccurate. Hemodynamic stabilization (resolution of paradoxus, improved CO) is the clinical priority.

</details>

---

### Question 140
The charge nurse in the cardiovascular intensive care unit is reviewing the status of four assigned clients. Which client should the nurse prioritize for immediate assessment and intervention?

A. A client in cardiogenic shock receiving a norepinephrine infusion whose fingertips are cool, pale, and exhibit delayed capillary refill.
B. A client with a known thoracic aortic aneurysm who reports a sudden onset of tearing mid-back pain and has unequal blood pressures in the upper extremities.
C. A client admitted with a hypertensive emergency whose blood pressure has decreased from 230/125 mm Hg to 175/95 mm Hg over the last hour via a labetalol drip.
D. A client with chronic heart failure who is displaying occasional unifocal premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) with a serum potassium of 3.9 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. A client with a known thoracic aortic aneurysm who reports a sudden onset of tearing mid-back pain and has unequal blood pressures in the upper extremities.

**Rationale:** 
- **Lens 1: Prioritization.** This client is exhibiting the classic, hallmark signs of an acute aortic dissection or impending rupture (sudden "tearing" or "ripping" back pain and a blood pressure differential >20 mm Hg between arms due to compromised subclavian arterial flow). This is a catastrophic, life-threatening surgical emergency requiring immediate blood pressure control (e.g., esmolol) and emergent operative repair.
- Option A describes a client experiencing peripheral vasoconstriction, which is an expected, although undesirable, side effect of profound shock and high-dose vasopressor therapy (norepinephrine stimulates alpha-1 receptors). While it requires monitoring, it does not supersede a rupturing aorta.
- Option C describes a client whose blood pressure is being successfully and safely managed. In a hypertensive emergency, the goal is to reduce MAP by no more than 20-25% in the first hour to prevent cerebral ischemia; dropping from 230/125 to 175/95 is the exact therapeutic goal.
- Option D describes a client with chronic heart failure experiencing stable, unifocal PVCs with a normal potassium level (3.5-5.0 mEq/L). This is a highly stable, expected baseline presentation for this chronic condition.

</details>


# SECTION: Endocrine System (Count: 140 Questions - ADVANCED)


### Question 141
A client with a history of Addison's disease is brought to the emergency department unresponsive with a palpable blood pressure of 60/doppler. The nurse analyzes the client's arterial blood gas (ABG) and comprehensive metabolic panel. Which set of laboratory findings indicates an impending lethal complication of acute adrenal crisis requiring immediate, life-saving intervention?

A) pH 7.31, PaCO2 38 mmHg, HCO3 18 mEq/L, Na⁺ 130 mEq/L, K⁺ 5.2 mEq/L, Glucose 65 mg/dL
B) pH 7.48, PaCO2 30 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L, Na⁺ 145 mEq/L, K⁺ 3.1 mEq/L, Glucose 140 mg/dL
C) pH 7.25, PaCO2 35 mmHg, HCO3 14 mEq/L, Na⁺ 122 mEq/L, K⁺ 7.4 mEq/L, Glucose 48 mg/dL
D) pH 7.38, PaCO2 45 mmHg, HCO3 26 mEq/L, Na⁺ 138 mEq/L, K⁺ 4.5 mEq/L, Glucose 250 mg/dL

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS

**Rationale:** Acute adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis) involves profound glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid (aldosterone) deficiency. Without aldosterone, the kidneys severely waste sodium and retain potassium, leading to dangerous hyponatremia and life-threatening hyperkalemia. Glucocorticoid deficiency causes profound hypoglycemia and inability to maintain vascular tone. The resultant shock state causes lactic acidosis (metabolic acidosis). Option C represents severe uncompensated metabolic acidosis, critically low sodium (122 mEq/L), profoundly lethal hyperkalemia (7.4 mEq/L, posing an immediate risk of ventricular fibrillation or asystole), and severe hypoglycemia (48 mg/dL). Option A shows mild derangements not yet at a lethal threshold. Option B shows respiratory alkalosis and hypokalemia, inconsistent with Addisonian crisis. Option D represents relatively normal electrolytes with mild hyperglycemia.

</details>

---

### Question 142
A 19-year-old client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion and 0.9% sodium chloride. A repeat metabolic panel reveals the client's blood glucose has rapidly decreased from 550 mg/dL to 220 mg/dL over the past 60 minutes. The client suddenly reports an excruciating headache, exhibits bradycardia, and has a widening pulse pressure. What is the nurse's *priority* pharmacological intervention?

A) Administer prescribed intravenous mannitol or 3% hypertonic saline immediately.
B) Discontinue the regular insulin infusion and administer subcutaneous insulin lispro.
C) Transition the intravenous fluids to 0.45% sodium chloride without dextrose.
D) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of 1,000 mL 0.9% sodium chloride.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of cerebral edema, a highly lethal complication of severe DKA therapy, especially common in adolescents and young adults. It is precipitated by a rapid drop in blood glucose (greater than 100 mg/dL/hr), causing a rapid osmolar shift of water into the cerebrospinal fluid and brain cells. An excruciating headache, bradycardia, and widening pulse pressure are components of Cushing's triad, signaling increased intracranial pressure (ICP) and impending brain herniation. The priority, life-saving pharmacological intervention is hyperosmolar therapy to pull fluid out of the brain tissue, achieved via IV mannitol or hypertonic (3%) saline. Discontinuing the insulin abruptly (Option B) is inappropriate as the client is likely still acidotic; rather, dextrose should be added to the IV fluids. Administering hypotonic fluid (Option C) will worsen the cellular swelling. A fluid bolus (Option D) will exacerbate fluid overload and increase intracranial pressure further.

</details>

---

### Question 143
The intensive care nurse is managing a client admitted with profound myxedema coma who is intubated and receiving intravenous levothyroxine, hydrocortisone, and passive rewarming. Which assessment finding represents the *earliest* indication of a failure to rescue this client from a life-threatening complication of the prescribed therapies?

A) The client's core body temperature slowly rises to 97.2°F (36.2°C).
B) The client exhibits hypoactive bowel sounds in all four abdominal quadrants.
C) The client's blood glucose level drops from 105 mg/dL to 94 mg/dL.
D) Frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) appear on the cardiac monitor.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION

**Rationale:** Myxedema coma is the extreme, life-threatening manifestation of hypothyroidism. Treatment requires the administration of intravenous levothyroxine to correct the severe metabolic depression. However, rapid replacement of thyroid hormone drastically increases the basal metabolic rate and myocardial oxygen demand. Because clients with profound hypothyroidism often have underlying myxedematous cardiomyopathy and unrecognized coronary artery disease, this sudden surge in cardiac workload can precipitate acute myocardial ischemia, arrhythmias, or heart failure. The appearance of frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) is the earliest, subtlest sign of myocardial irritability and an impending lethal arrhythmia or cardiac collapse (failure to rescue). A slow rise in temperature (Option A) is an expected and desired outcome of passive rewarming. Hypoactive bowel sounds (Option B) are an expected baseline finding in severe hypothyroidism. A mild decrease in blood glucose to 94 mg/dL (Option C) remains within normal limits and does not indicate deterioration.

</details>

---

### Question 144
The emergency department charge nurse receives handoff report for four critically ill clients experiencing endocrine or systemic emergencies. Which client must the nurse assess *first*?

A) A client with a history of a recent transsphenoidal hypophysectomy reporting continuous clear nasal drainage that tests positive for glucose.
B) A client with Graves' disease exhibiting extreme agitation, a heart rate of 152 bpm, and a temperature of 104.8°F (40.4°C).
C) A client with Addison's disease reporting diffuse abdominal pain, nausea, and a blood pressure of 96/58 mmHg.
D) A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) demonstrating Kussmaul respirations at 30 breaths/min and a serum pH of 7.28.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION

**Rationale:** The client in Option B is exhibiting classic signs of Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis), a hypermetabolic, life-threatening emergency characterized by extreme hyperpyrexia (104.8°F), severe tachycardia (152 bpm), and central nervous system dysfunction (extreme agitation/delirium). Without immediate interventions (cooling blankets, beta-blockers, antithyroid medications, and iodine), this client will rapidly progress to cardiovascular collapse, heart failure, and death. This client requires priority assessment and intervention. Option A describes a cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) leak following pituitary surgery; while serious and requiring intervention to prevent meningitis, it is not as immediately lethal in the next 10 minutes as thyroid storm. Option C describes an Addisonian crisis; the client is hypotensive and requires IV hydrocortisone and fluids, but a BP of 96/58 mmHg indicates they are not yet in profound shock compared to the impending cardiovascular collapse in Option B. Option D describes a client with DKA utilizing a compensatory respiratory mechanism (Kussmaul respirations) to blow off CO2 due to metabolic acidosis (pH 7.28); this is an expected finding for this disease process and is actively compensating, making them a lower immediate priority than the client in active thyroid storm.

</details>

### Question 145
A client is admitted in a profound Myxedema Coma with severe hypoventilation, hypothermia, and bradycardia. While aggressively initiating continuous intravenous levothyroxine, the nurse closely monitors for the failure of a secondary organ system directly precipitated by the treatment of this primary endocrine collapse. Which clinical manifestation indicates this secondary multi-system failure is occurring?

A) A decrease in urine specific gravity to 1.002 with profound polyuria.
B) The presence of a positive Chvostek's sign and a prolonged QT interval on the ECG.
C) The sudden development of bilateral basilar crackles, jugular venous distention, and an S3 heart sound.
D) An abrupt elevation in serum aspartate aminotransferase (AST) and total bilirubin levels.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION

**Rationale:** The treatment of myxedema coma requires high-dose intravenous levothyroxine. This rapidly accelerates the body's metabolic rate, dramatically increasing cardiac oxygen consumption and systemic tissue demands. Clients with long-standing, severe hypothyroidism often suffer from a weakened heart muscle (myxedematous cardiomyopathy) and generalized fluid retention. When the metabolic demand suddenly spikes from IV levothyroxine therapy, the severely compromised heart can easily fail, leading to acute decompensated heart failure (Cardiovascular System Failure). Bilateral basilar crackles, jugular venous distention, and an S3 gallop are classic, definitive signs of heart failure and fluid overload. Option A describes Diabetes Insipidus, which is a complication of pituitary trauma/surgery, not myxedema treatment. Option B indicates hypocalcemia, which is a risk following thyroidectomy (due to parathyroid removal), not a complication of treating myxedema. Option D indicates acute liver injury, which is not the primary secondary failure risk associated with levothyroxine administration in myxedema coma.

</details>
### Question 146
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**

The nurse receives the shift report on an endocrine step-down unit. Which client should the nurse assess *first*?

A) A client with Myxedema Coma receiving intravenous levothyroxine who has a core temperature of 95.2°F (35.1°C) and a heart rate of 54 bpm. 
B) A client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) on a continuous insulin infusion whose latest anion gap decreased from 24 mEq/L to 14 mEq/L.
C) A client with Graves' disease post-operative day 1 from a subtotal thyroidectomy who has a new 1.2°F (0.7°C) increase in body temperature. 
D) A client with Addison's disease complaining of profound fatigue and dizziness with a supine blood pressure of 105/70 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) A client with Graves' disease post-operative day 1 from a subtotal thyroidectomy who has a new 1.2°F (0.7°C) increase in body temperature.

**Rationale:** 
In a client with Graves' disease, especially following thyroid surgery, even a slight increase in temperature (as small as 1°F) is a critical red flag indicating the early onset of a life-threatening **Thyroid Storm** (thyrotoxic crisis). Surgical manipulation of the thyroid gland can cause a massive release of thyroid hormones into the bloodstream. This client requires immediate assessment and intervention (e.g., beta-blockers, antithyroid medications, and cooling). 

**Why the others are incorrect:**
- **Option A:** Bradycardia and hypothermia are hallmark baseline findings in Myxedema Coma. While this client requires intensive care, the provided vitals are expected and do not indicate immediate deterioration.
- **Option B:** A decreasing anion gap (moving toward the normal range of 8-12 mEq/L) indicates that the DKA is resolving and the insulin therapy is effective. This client is stabilizing. 
- **Option D:** Fatigue and mild hypotension are chronic, expected findings in Addison's disease (adrenal insufficiency) due to decreased aldosterone and cortisol. While the dizziness is a concern, it is not an impending lethal crisis like a developing Thyroid Storm.

</details>

---

### Question 147
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A pediatric client is receiving aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation and a regular insulin infusion for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The nurse is closely monitoring the client for neurological complications. Which subtle clinical finding is the *earliest* indicator of a life-threatening failure to rescue?

A) The client begins to complain of a new, progressively worsening headache and exhibits sudden lethargy.
B) The client's pupillary response becomes sluggish and unequal to light.
C) The client's heart rate drops from 110 bpm to 58 bpm while the pulse pressure acutely widens.
D) The client exhibits deep, rapid Kussmaul respirations and a fruity breath odor.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The client begins to complain of a new, progressively worsening headache and exhibits sudden lethargy.

**Rationale:** 
**Cerebral Edema** is the most lethal complication of severe DKA, particularly in younger clients. It typically develops 4 to 12 hours after treatment is initiated, often due to rapid fluid shifts or a precipitous drop in blood glucose levels. The *earliest* and subtlest signs of developing increased intracranial pressure (ICP) from cerebral edema are a new-onset headache, progressive lethargy, and behavioral changes. Identifying this early allows for immediate life-saving interventions like administering Mannitol or hypertonic saline.

**Why the others are incorrect:**
- **Option B:** Sluggish, unequal, or fixed pupils are late, ominous signs of brainstem herniation secondary to severe increased ICP, indicating a failure to rescue early enough.
- **Option C:** Bradycardia accompanied by a widening pulse pressure (and irregular respirations) constitutes Cushing's Triad. This is a very late and terminal sign of impending brain herniation, not an early indicator.
- **Option D:** Kussmaul respirations and fruity breath are classic, expected compensatory findings of the underlying metabolic acidosis in DKA, not a new sign of neurological deterioration.

</details>

---

### Question 148
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr and 0.9% Normal Saline. The client's latest metabolic panel reveals a blood glucose of 245 mg/dL (13.6 mmol/L), potassium of 3.8 mEq/L (3.8 mmol/L), and an anion gap of 19 mEq/L. Which advanced pharmacological intervention is the nurse's priority?

A) Stop the continuous intravenous insulin infusion and administer a subcutaneous dose of long-acting glargine.
B) Add 5% dextrose (D5W) to the continuous intravenous maintenance fluids while maintaining the insulin infusion. 
C) Administer 50% dextrose (D50) IV push immediately to prevent the development of cerebral edema.
D) Increase the insulin infusion rate to 0.15 units/kg/hr to rapidly close the elevated anion gap.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Add 5% dextrose (D5W) to the continuous intravenous maintenance fluids while maintaining the insulin infusion.

**Rationale:** 
In the management of severe DKA, intravenous insulin must be continued until the ketoacidosis resolves, which is indicated by the closure of the anion gap (normal is typically 8-12 mEq/L). In this scenario, the blood glucose has fallen below 250 mg/dL, but the anion gap remains wide open at 19 mEq/L. To prevent severe hypoglycemia while continuing the essential insulin drip needed to stop lipolysis and resolve the acidosis, the nurse must add dextrose (usually D5W) to the intravenous maintenance fluids.

**Why the others are incorrect:**
- **Option A:** Stopping the IV insulin while the anion gap is still 19 mEq/L will immediately halt the resolution of ketoacidosis and cause a lethal rebound into severe DKA. Insulin must not be stopped until the gap is closed.
- **Option C:** D50 IV push is an emergency rescue drug for profound hypoglycemia. The client's glucose is 245 mg/dL; giving D50 will cause dramatic hyperglycemia and massive fluid shifts.
- **Option D:** Increasing the insulin rate will cause blood glucose to plummet too rapidly (precipitating cerebral edema or severe hypoglycemia). The standard protocol is to maintain the insulin rate and add dextrose to the IV fluids once glucose reaches ~250 mg/dL.

</details>

---

### Question 149
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is brought to the emergency department in acute Thyroid Storm with a core temperature of 105.2°F (40.7°C), extreme agitation, and a heart rate of 170 bpm. Thirty minutes into treatment, the nurse assesses new-onset bibasilar lung crackles, prominent jugular venous distention (JVD), and a drop in blood pressure to 85/48 mmHg. What is the pathophysiological basis for this multi-system complication, and what is the immediate priority?

A) Severe volume depletion from hyperthermia-induced diaphoresis; prioritize rapid administration of warmed intravenous crystalloids.
B) Rapid intracellular shifts of potassium causing lethal dysrhythmias; prioritize administering intravenous calcium gluconate.
C) Non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema secondary to increased capillary permeability; prioritize administering high-dose intravenous corticosteroids.
D) Thyrotoxicosis-induced high-output heart failure leading to cardiogenic shock; prioritize administering prescribed beta-blockers.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Thyrotoxicosis-induced high-output heart failure leading to cardiogenic shock; prioritize administering prescribed beta-blockers.

**Rationale:** 
Thyroid Storm drastically increases metabolic demand, forcing the heart to work at extreme rates (tachycardia) to deliver oxygen. This massive workload severely limits diastolic filling time, ultimately causing **high-output heart failure** and cardiogenic shock. The presence of crackles, JVD, and hypotension are classic signs that the pump is failing. Beta-blockers (such as propranolol) are the primary life-saving intervention because they immediately decrease the heart rate, allowing for ventricular filling, and uniquely block the peripheral conversion of T4 to the active T3 hormone. 

**Why the others are incorrect:**
- **Option A:** While volume depletion is common in Thyroid Storm, the development of crackles and JVD indicates fluid overload in the cardiopulmonary circuit due to pump failure, not a simple volume deficit. Giving a rapid fluid bolus will exacerbate the heart failure.
- **Option B:** Intracellular potassium shifts do not directly cause fluid backup (crackles and JVD). Calcium gluconate is used for hyperkalemia stabilization, which is not the primary issue described here.
- **Option C:** While corticosteroids are used in Thyroid Storm to block T4 to T3 conversion and treat relative adrenal insufficiency, the pulmonary edema here is *cardiogenic* (pump failure), not non-cardiogenic (ARDS). Beta-blockade is the priority to fix the underlying high-output state.

</details>

---

### Question 150
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

An older adult client with a long-standing history of Hashimoto's thyroiditis is brought to the emergency department in a profound Myxedema Coma. The client is unarousable, with a core temperature of 92.4°F (33.5°C), a respiratory rate of 6 breaths/min, and severe bradycardia. In addition to securing the airway, which immediate emergency rescue intervention must the nurse anticipate?

A) Administration of intravenous levothyroxine simultaneously with intravenous stress-dose hydrocortisone.
B) Aggressive active core rewarming using forced-air warming blankets and heated intravenous fluids.
C) Rapid administration of a 1,000 mL bolus of warmed 0.45% Sodium Chloride to correct cellular dehydration.
D) Administration of a continuous epinephrine infusion to directly reverse the profound bradycardia and hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Administration of intravenous levothyroxine simultaneously with intravenous stress-dose hydrocortisone.

**Rationale:** 
Myxedema Coma is a life-threatening complication of severe hypothyroidism. Treatment requires immediate replacement of thyroid hormone via **IV levothyroxine**. However, because autoimmune hypothyroidism (Hashimoto's) often coexists with autoimmune adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease), initiating thyroid hormone replacement accelerates the metabolic clearance of whatever minimal cortisol the client has left. This will precipitate an acute, lethal **Adrenal Crisis**. Therefore, empirical IV corticosteroids (like hydrocortisone) *must* be administered simultaneously with or just prior to IV levothyroxine.

**Why the others are incorrect:**
- **Option B:** Active core rewarming (forced-air blankets) is strictly contraindicated in Myxedema Coma. It causes rapid peripheral vasodilation, which leads to immediate cardiovascular collapse and shock. Passive rewarming (warm room, regular blankets) is the standard of care.
- **Option C:** Clients with Myxedema Coma characteristically suffer from severe dilutional hyponatremia due to decreased water excretion. Administering a hypotonic fluid (0.45% NaCl) will drastically worsen the hyponatremia, potentially causing fatal seizures or brain edema.
- **Option D:** The bradycardia and hypotension in Myxedema Coma are completely refractory to standard vasopressors and chronotropes (like epinephrine) until the underlying lack of thyroid hormone is corrected.

</details>
### Question 151
The nurse receives the shift report on an endocrine step-down unit. Which client should the nurse assess first?

A) A client with diabetes insipidus receiving intravenous desmopressin whose most recent urine specific gravity is 1.004.
B) A client with primary hyperparathyroidism who has a serum calcium level of 11.2 mg/dL and reports mild flank pain.
C) A client with Addison's disease who is reporting sudden, severe, penetrating pain in the lower back and legs.
D) A client with Cushing's syndrome who has a capillary blood glucose of 245 mg/dL and 2+ bilateral pitting edema.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION

**Rationale:** 
A client with Addison's disease experiencing sudden, severe, penetrating pain in the lower back, abdomen, or legs is exhibiting classic early signs of an acute Adrenal Crisis (Addisonian crisis). This is a life-threatening medical emergency characterized by profound glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid deficiencies, leading to rapid vascular collapse, severe hypotension, and death if not treated immediately with IV fluids and hydrocortisone. 
*Option A* is incorrect because a specific gravity of 1.004 indicates dilute urine, meaning the diabetes insipidus may need further titration of desmopressin, but this is not immediately life-threatening. 
*Option B* is incorrect as mild flank pain and elevated calcium (11.2 mg/dL) are expected, chronic findings of hyperparathyroidism (risk for kidney stones), requiring intervention but not taking priority over impending cardiovascular collapse. 
*Option D* is incorrect because hyperglycemia and fluid retention are chronic, expected manifestations of hypercortisolism (Cushing's syndrome).

---

### Question 152
A 19-year-old client is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the past 3 hours, the client's blood glucose has dropped rapidly from 680 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL. Which assessment finding represents the earliest, most subtle sign of a life-threatening complication?

A) New-onset lethargy and a report of a dull, diffuse headache.
B) Development of deep, rapid, and unlabored Kussmaul respirations.
C) A capillary blood glucose reading that decreases by 60 mg/dL in one hour.
D) Serum potassium level decreasing from 5.4 mEq/L to 4.2 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION

**Rationale:** 
When blood glucose drops too rapidly during DKA treatment (exceeding a drop of 50-75 mg/dL/hr), the sudden shift in serum osmolality causes water to rush into the hyperosmolar brain cells, resulting in Cerebral Edema. This is a lethal complication, particularly common in adolescents and young adults. The earliest and subtlest signs of cerebral edema are neurological changes such as headache, lethargy, confusion, or irritability. If unnoticed, this progresses to pupillary changes, bradycardia, and brain herniation.
*Option B* is incorrect; Kussmaul respirations are an expected compensatory mechanism in DKA to blow off excess CO2 and do not represent a new complication. 
*Option C* is incorrect; a decrease of 60 mg/dL/hr is the optimal and safe target rate for glucose reduction in DKA to prevent cerebral edema.
*Option D* is incorrect; potassium shifting back into the intracellular space is an expected and monitored physiological response to insulin therapy.

---

### Question 153
A client in thyrotoxic crisis (thyroid storm) is prescribed several medications to manage the hypermetabolic state. In what specific sequence or manner must the nurse administer the prescribed pharmacological interventions to prevent a lethal exacerbation of the storm?

A) Administer aspirin to manage profound hyperthermia concurrently with propylthiouracil (PTU).
B) Administer Lugol's iodine solution exactly one hour before administering propylthiouracil (PTU).
C) Initiate a continuous infusion of regular insulin prior to administering oral propranolol.
D) Administer propylthiouracil (PTU) first, followed by Lugol's iodine solution one hour later.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY

**Rationale:** 
In the pharmacological management of a thyroid storm, thionamides like propylthiouracil (PTU) or methimazole must be administered *before* iodine compounds (such as Lugol's solution or SSKI). PTU blocks the synthesis of new thyroid hormones. Iodine blocks the release of preformed thyroid hormones. If iodine is administered first, the highly active thyroid gland will use the sudden influx of iodine substrate to synthesize massive amounts of new thyroid hormone, catastrophically worsening the thyroid storm. 
*Option A* is incorrect because aspirin is strictly contraindicated in thyroid storm; salicylates displace thyroid hormone from binding proteins, increasing the level of free, active thyroid hormone in the blood. Acetaminophen is the preferred antipyretic. 
*Option B* reverses the required sequence, which is a dangerous error. 
*Option C* is irrelevant; while beta-blockers (propranolol) are used to block sympathetic overactivity, insulin is not a primary or sequenced treatment for thyroid storm unless concurrent severe hyperglycemia exists.

---

### Question 154
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with a diagnosis of myxedema coma. The nurse notes a respiratory rate of 8 breaths/min, a PaCO2 of 65 mmHg, severe bradycardia, and a core body temperature of 94.2°F (34.5°C). As the client becomes increasingly somnolent, which integrated multi-system intervention is the priority?

A) Actively rewarm the client with a forced-air warming blanket to improve cardiac output.
B) Prepare for immediate endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation while maintaining passive rewarming.
C) Administer intravenous levothyroxine rapidly to immediately reverse the hypothermic and hypoventilatory state.
D) Initiate a continuous infusion of vasopressin to counteract profound bradycardia and hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION

**Rationale:** 
Myxedema coma represents the extreme, life-threatening manifestation of hypothyroidism, characterized by multi-system failure. The primary cause of death is profound hypoventilation leading to respiratory failure, hypercapnia, and hypoxia. Securing the airway and providing mechanical ventilation is the immediate priority. Additionally, passive rewarming (using regular blankets and warm room temperature) is required; active rewarming (Option A) is strictly contraindicated because it causes rapid peripheral vasodilation, leading to severe cardiovascular collapse and shock in a client who already has compromised cardiac output and depleted blood volume. 
*Option C* is incorrect because while IV levothyroxine is the definitive treatment, it takes hours to days to reverse the metabolic crisis; it does not replace the immediate need for airway rescue. 
*Option D* is incorrect; vasopressors may be ineffective in myxedema until thyroid hormone levels are restored, and airway management takes precedence over pressor administration.

---

### Question 155
A client with a history of Addison's disease is brought to the emergency department after a sudden syncopal episode. The client is profoundly hypotensive (blood pressure 68/40 mmHg), tachycardic (heart rate 138 bpm), and unresponsive to verbal stimuli. Which is the nurse's immediate life-saving rescue action?

A) Rapidly infuse 0.9% sodium chloride and administer IV hydrocortisone.
B) Initiate a continuous intravenous infusion of norepinephrine starting at 2 mcg/min.
C) Administer 1 mg of epinephrine IV push and initiate continuous CPR.
D) Rapidly administer a bolus of 5% Dextrose in Water (D5W) to correct anticipated hypoglycemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE

**Rationale:** 
The client is in acute Adrenal Crisis, manifesting as profound distributive and hypovolemic shock. The lack of aldosterone causes massive sodium and water loss, while the lack of cortisol prevents the maintenance of vascular tone. The immediate, life-saving rescue action is aggressive volume resuscitation with isotonic crystalloids (0.9% Normal Saline) to restore intravascular volume, combined with an immediate IV push of high-dose glucocorticoids (hydrocortisone) to restore vascular tone and address the endocrine failure. 
*Option B* is incorrect; vasopressors are notoriously ineffective in adrenal crisis until both vascular volume and circulating cortisol levels are replaced. Cortisol is required for catecholamines (like norepinephrine) to exert their vasoconstrictive effects on blood vessels. 
*Option C* is incorrect; the client is hypotensive and tachycardic, but not in cardiac arrest, so CPR and IV push epinephrine are inappropriate. 
*Option D* is incorrect; while hypoglycemia is common in adrenal crisis, 5% Dextrose in Water (D5W) is a hypotonic fluid in the body that will rapidly leave the intravascular space, failing to correct the life-threatening hypotensive shock. 

---
### Question 156
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)
**System Focus:** Myxedema Coma

A client with a history of poorly managed Hashimoto’s thyroiditis is brought to the emergency department in a suspected Myxedema Coma. The client is unarousable, with a core body temperature of 89.2°F (31.7°C), a heart rate of 38 bpm, and a respiratory rate of 6 breaths per minute. Arterial blood gas (ABG) analysis reveals a PaCO2 of 72 mmHg and an O2 saturation of 82%. What is the most immediate life-saving nursing action?

A) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of 500 mcg of levothyroxine.
B) Initiate rapid active external rewarming using a forced-air warming blanket.
C) Prepare the equipment and client for immediate endotracheal intubation.
D) Administer intravenous hydrocortisone to prevent concurrent adrenal collapse.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** In Myxedema Coma, profound hypothyroidism leads to a severe depression of the central nervous system and respiratory drive, resulting in life-threatening alveolar hypoventilation, respiratory acidosis, and hypoxia. Securing the airway and initiating mechanical ventilation (Option C) is the absolute first priority (Airway, Breathing, Circulation) before administering medications. While IV levothyroxine (Option A) and IV hydrocortisone (Option D) are critical medical interventions for Myxedema Coma, they cannot precede establishing a patent airway in a client with a respiratory rate of 6 and severe hypercapnia. Rapid active external rewarming (Option B) is contraindicated; it can cause sudden peripheral vasodilation, leading to cardiovascular collapse and severe shock. Rewarming must be done passively and slowly.
</details>
