### Question 157
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)
**System Focus:** Severe DKA

A client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr. The nurse reviews the client’s 1-hour follow-up laboratory results: Blood glucose is 380 mg/dL (decreased from 610 mg/dL), venous pH is 7.22, HCO3 is 12 mEq/L, and serum potassium is 3.1 mEq/L. Which high-alert pharmacological action must the nurse take immediately?

A) Stop the continuous insulin infusion and request an order for intravenous potassium replacement.
B) Decrease the insulin infusion rate by half and add 5% Dextrose to the primary intravenous fluids.
C) Continue the insulin infusion at the current rate and prepare to administer intravenous sodium bicarbonate.
D) Administer 10 mEq of potassium chloride via rapid intravenous push over 5 minutes.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Advanced DKA management protocols dictate that if the serum potassium falls below 3.3 mEq/L, the continuous insulin infusion must be **held immediately**. Insulin drives both glucose and potassium into the intracellular space. Continuing the drip will rapidly worsen hypokalemia, putting the client at extreme risk for lethal ventricular arrhythmias and respiratory muscle paralysis. Potassium must be replenished to at least 3.3 mEq/L before resuming insulin (Option A). Dextrose is not added until the blood glucose reaches approximately 200-250 mg/dL (Option B). Routine use of sodium bicarbonate is not recommended for a pH > 6.9, as it can cause paradoxical intracellular acidosis (Option C). Potassium chloride must never be administered via rapid IV push, as this is a lethal action (Option D).
</details>

---

### Question 158
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')
**System Focus:** Severe DKA with Cerebral Edema

A 19-year-old client is admitted with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Initially, the blood glucose was 880 mg/dL, and the serum osmolality was 345 mOsm/kg. After 3 hours of aggressive fluid resuscitation with 0.9% Normal Saline and a continuous regular insulin infusion, the client's blood glucose has rapidly dropped to 250 mg/dL. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the client is developing a lethal complication of rapid treatment?

A) The client's respiratory rate steadily decreases from 34 breaths per minute to 22 breaths per minute.
B) The client complains of a new-onset, dull headache and exhibits slightly delayed verbal responses.
C) The client continues to pass 800 mL of pale, dilute urine over a two-hour period.
D) The client reports persistent generalized abdominal cramping and moderate nausea.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** The rapid lowering of blood glucose and serum osmolality during DKA treatment can cause a dangerous osmotic shift, drawing fluid into the intracellular space of the brain and causing Cerebral Edema. This is a highly lethal complication, particularly in young adults and adolescents. A new-onset headache and subtle changes in the level of consciousness (delayed responses, lethargy) are the earliest signs of increasing intracranial pressure (Option B) and warrant immediate intervention (e.g., stopping fluids, administering Mannitol). A decreasing respiratory rate (Option A) is an expected finding as the metabolic acidosis (Kussmaul respirations) resolves. Polyuria (Option C) is an expected manifestation of osmotic diuresis until glucose is fully normalized. Abdominal pain and nausea (Option D) are common presenting symptoms of DKA due to circulating ketones and usually resolve slowly with treatment.
</details>

---

### Question 159
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting lethal EKG rhythms and toxic labs)
**System Focus:** Adrenal Crisis

A client with a history of Addison's disease is admitted to the Intensive Care Unit after sustaining severe trauma in a motor vehicle accident. The client is uncooperative and confused. The nurse rapidly analyzes the admission telemetry and laboratory data. Which combination of findings confirms the client has progressed into an acute Adrenal Crisis requiring immediate resuscitation?

A) A prolonged QT interval on the EKG, serum calcium of 8.1 mg/dL, and an arterial pH of 7.32.
B) Depressed ST segments on the EKG, serum potassium of 2.8 mEq/L, and blood glucose of 65 mg/dL.
C) A heart rate of 112 bpm, serum sodium of 148 mEq/L, and a core temperature of 101.4°F (38.5°C).
D) Peaked T-waves on the EKG, serum sodium of 124 mEq/L, and a blood pressure of 72/40 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** An acute Adrenal Crisis (Addisonian Crisis) is triggered by severe stress (e.g., trauma, infection) in an individual with adrenal insufficiency. It is characterized by an absolute lack of cortisol and aldosterone. A lack of aldosterone causes the kidneys to excrete massive amounts of sodium and water while retaining potassium, leading to severe hyponatremia, profound hypovolemic shock (hypotension), and severe hyperkalemia (evidenced by peaked T-waves on the EKG). Option D accurately reflects the lethal triad of Adrenal Crisis: Hyperkalemia, Hyponatremia, and Refractory Hypotension. Option A suggests hypocalcemia. Option B suggests hypokalemia (seen in hyperaldosteronism, not adrenal insufficiency). Option C suggests hypernatremia, whereas Addisonian crisis causes hyponatremia.
</details>

---

### Question 160
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)
**System Focus:** Thyroid Storm

A client is admitted to the critical care unit in a Thyroid Storm secondary to untreated Graves' disease. Vital signs are: Temperature 106.1°F (41.1°C), Heart Rate 198 bpm (atrial fibrillation), and Blood Pressure 185/95 mmHg. The hypermetabolic state places immense demand on the myocardium. Which clinical manifestation indicates that this endocrine emergency has now triggered a secondary, life-threatening cardiovascular failure?

A) The client develops progressive, bilateral exophthalmos and an unblinking stare.
B) The client complains of severe nausea, vomiting, and diffuse abdominal pain.
C) The client expectorates pink, frothy sputum and exhibits a drop in oxygen saturation to 81%.
D) The client’s systolic blood pressure increases from 145 mmHg to 180 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** In Thyroid Storm, the massive release of thyroid hormones causes an extreme hypermetabolic state, driving the heart rate to dangerous levels (often >140 bpm). Over time, the sustained tachycardia and hyperdynamic circulation cause the heart muscle to exhaust, leading to high-output heart failure and acute pulmonary edema. Expectorating pink, frothy sputum with profound hypoxia (Option C) is the classic hallmark of acute pulmonary edema, indicating the endocrine system failure has now caused catastrophic cardiovascular and respiratory failure. Exophthalmos (Option A) and gastrointestinal distress (Option B) are classic signs of Graves' disease and thyroid storm, but do not represent secondary cardiovascular collapse. Hypertension (Option D) is common in early thyroid storm due to increased cardiac output, whereas high-output heart failure would eventually lead to a severe drop in blood pressure and cardiogenic shock.
</details>

---
### Question 161
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A 19-year-old client is being treated for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) with an intravenous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr and 0.45% NaCl. Over the past three hours, the client's blood glucose has rapidly decreased from 680 mg/dL to 250 mg/dL. Suddenly, the client reports a "blinding headache," becomes lethargic, and the cardiac monitor reveals new-onset sinus bradycardia at 46 bpm with widening pulse pressure. What is the immediate life-saving nursing action?

A) Increase the 0.45% NaCl infusion rate to maximize cerebral perfusion.
B) Administer 50 mL of IV 50% Dextrose (D50W) rapid push. 
C) Administer intravenous mannitol 20% or hypertonic saline as prescribed. 
D) Prepare the client for immediate emergency endotracheal intubation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Administer intravenous mannitol 20% or hypertonic saline as prescribed.

**Rationale:** 
This client is exhibiting classic signs of **cerebral edema** (headache, lethargy, Cushing's triad of bradycardia and widening pulse pressure), a lethal complication of severe DKA caused by rapid fluid shifts when blood glucose drops too quickly. The immediate emergency rescue action is to reduce intracranial pressure using an osmotic diuretic like IV mannitol or hypertonic saline. Increasing hypotonic fluids (0.45% NaCl) will severely worsen the cerebral edema (Option A). While D50W is used for severe hypoglycemia, this client's glucose is 250 mg/dL, so hypoglycemia is not the cause of the neurological collapse (Option B). While intubation may eventually be necessary if the airway is lost, the direct pharmacological rescue to reverse the cerebral herniation process is osmotic therapy (Option D).

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing  
**Client Need:** Physiological Adaptation  
**Nursing Process:** Implementation
</details>

---

### Question 162
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client with severe Addisonian Crisis is admitted to the Intensive Care Unit with a blood pressure of 70/38 mmHg. Over the past hour, the nurse has rapidly infused 3 liters of 0.9% NaCl and initiated a continuously titrating norepinephrine infusion, currently at 0.5 mcg/kg/min. Despite these interventions, the client’s Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) remains critically low at 45 mmHg. Which pharmacological intervention is the *priority* to restore the client's hemodynamic stability?

A) Initiate a secondary continuous infusion of epinephrine.
B) Administer intravenous hydrocortisone sodium succinate.
C) Switch the primary IV fluid bolus to 5% Dextrose in 0.9% NaCl (D5NS).
D) Administer oral fludrocortisone acetate via a nasogastric tube.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Administer intravenous hydrocortisone sodium succinate.

**Rationale:** 
In an acute adrenal crisis, the absolute lack of cortisol leads to profound vasodilation and down-regulation of alpha-1 adrenergic receptors in the vasculature. Because of this, the cardiovascular system becomes **refractory to fluid resuscitation and vasopressors** (like norepinephrine). Hemodynamic stability cannot be achieved until glucocorticoids (IV hydrocortisone) are administered to restore vascular tone and re-sensitize the receptors to catecholamines. Adding a second pressor (Option A) will be ineffective without cortisol. While D5NS helps correct the hypoglycemia associated with adrenal crisis, it will not fix the refractory shock (Option C). Fludrocortisone is a mineralocorticoid given orally for long-term maintenance, not in an acute shock state; IV hydrocortisone already possesses sufficient mineralocorticoid activity for the acute phase (Option D).

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing  
**Client Need:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies  
**Nursing Process:** Implementation
</details>

---

### Question 163
**LENS: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A client is in the critical care unit receiving multi-modal treatment for a Thyroid Storm, including propylthiouracil (PTU), Lugol's iodine solution, IV hydrocortisone, and a continuous esmolol infusion. Over the last two hours, the client's heart rate has decreased from 175 bpm to 112 bpm, and their temperature has reduced to 100.8°F (38.2°C). Which new assessment finding provides the earliest indication of a critical failure to rescue requiring immediate intervention?

A) The new onset of an S3 ventricular gallop on cardiac auscultation.
B) Persistent fine resting tremors of the bilateral upper extremities.
C) The development of a mild maculopapular rash on the client's torso.
D) Serum free T4 levels that remain significantly above the normal reference range.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The new onset of an S3 ventricular gallop on cardiac auscultation.

**Rationale:** 
Thyroid storm puts immense strain on the cardiovascular system, drastically increasing the risk of **high-output heart failure** and cardiogenic shock. The sudden appearance of an S3 ventricular gallop is an early, subtle indicator of ventricular volume overload and impending congestive heart failure. This risk is further compounded by the administration of beta-blockers (esmolol), which decrease myocardial contractility. Tremors (Option B) and elevated free T4 levels (Option D) are expected findings of the disease process that will take days to fully resolve, not signs of acute deterioration. A maculopapular rash (Option C) is a known, non-life-threatening side effect of PTU therapy that does not immediately denote a failure to rescue. 

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing  
**Client Need:** Reduction of Risk Potential  
**Nursing Process:** Evaluation
</details>

---

### Question 164
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

An adult client is brought to the emergency department in a profound Myxedema Coma. The nurse notes a respiratory rate of 6 breaths/min, oxygen saturation of 84% on room air, a core body temperature of 93.8°F (34.3°C), and a heart rate of 42 bpm. The client is unresponsive. How does this primary endocrine failure directly trigger a secondary critical system collapse in this client?

A) Profound hypothermia triggers systemic arterial vasoconstriction, leading to a sudden hypertensive crisis and ischemic stroke.
B) The absence of thyroid hormone induces a rapid breakdown of muscle tissue, causing acute kidney injury secondary to rhabdomyolysis.
C) A depressed metabolic rate causes severe intracellular shifts of potassium, initiating lethal ventricular dysrhythmias.
D) Severely reduced cellular metabolism depresses the medullary respiratory center, causing hypercapnic respiratory failure and subsequent anoxic cardiac arrest.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Severely reduced cellular metabolism depresses the medullary respiratory center, causing hypercapnic respiratory failure and subsequent anoxic cardiac arrest.

**Rationale:** 
Myxedema coma is the extreme, life-threatening manifestation of hypothyroidism. The profound lack of thyroid hormone leads to critically reduced cellular metabolism across all body systems. In the central nervous system, this directly depresses the medullary respiratory center, leading to hypoventilation, severe hypercapnia (CO2 narcosis), and hypoxemia. This hypercapnic respiratory failure inevitably leads to systemic hypoxia, respiratory acidosis, and cardiovascular collapse (anoxic cardiac arrest). Myxedema causes hypotension, not a hypertensive crisis (Option A). Hypothyroidism does not acutely cause massive rhabdomyolysis as a primary pathway to death (Option B). While hyponatremia is common in myxedema, it does not typically cause massive intracellular potassium shifts leading to dysrhythmias (Option C).

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing  
**Client Need:** Physiological Adaptation  
**Nursing Process:** Assessment
</details>

---

### Question 165
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION**

The critical care charge nurse is reviewing the status of four unstable clients experiencing severe endocrine emergencies. Which client must the nurse assess and intervene with *first*?

A) A client with Diabetes Insipidus (DI) who has an hourly urine output of 450 mL and a morning serum sodium level of 152 mEq/L.
B) A client with Thyroid Storm who is agitated, diaphoretic, and has a current core temperature of 103.2°F (39.5°C).
C) A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) receiving an IV regular insulin infusion whose most recent serum potassium is 3.1 mEq/L.
D) A client with Myxedema Coma receiving IV levothyroxine who has a core body temperature of 95.8°F (35.4°C) and a heart rate of 54 bpm.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) receiving an IV regular insulin infusion whose most recent serum potassium is 3.1 mEq/L.

**Rationale:** 
This is a high-alert safety emergency. IV insulin drives potassium intracellularly, rapidly decreasing serum potassium levels. Strict DKA protocols mandate that **insulin infusions must be held if the serum potassium is less than 3.3 mEq/L**. Continuing the insulin drip in a client with a potassium of 3.1 mEq/L will cause profound, life-threatening hypokalemia, leading to immediate lethal cardiac dysrhythmias (e.g., ventricular fibrillation). The nurse must immediately stop the insulin and administer IV potassium. The client with DI (Option A) is hypovolemic and hypernatremic, but 152 mEq/L does not take priority over impending fatal dysrhythmias. The clients with Thyroid Storm (Option B) and Myxedema Coma (Option D) are exhibiting expected, hallmark signs of their respective conditions that are currently being managed, making them a lower immediate priority than the client facing an active, iatrogenic lethal threat.

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing  
**Client Need:** Safe and Effective Care Environment / Management of Care  
**Nursing Process:** Planning
</details>
### Question 166
A client with Addison's disease arrives in the emergency department lethargic and unresponsive. Vital signs are BP 72/40 mmHg, HR 128 bpm, and RR 24/min. The cardiac monitor displays a rapidly widening QRS complex. Based on the suspected underlying pathology, what is the *most* immediate life-saving nursing intervention?

A. Administer an intravenous bolus of 1,000 mL 0.9% Normal Saline.
B. Administer intravenous calcium gluconate.
C. Administer 100 mg of intravenous hydrocortisone succinate.
D. Administer a rapid infusion of 10 units of regular insulin and 50% dextrose.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Administer intravenous calcium gluconate.

**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE

**Rationale:** This client is in an acute Adrenal Crisis (Addisonian crisis), which causes severe, life-threatening hyperkalemia due to absolute aldosterone deficiency. A widening QRS complex is an ominous sign of extreme hyperkalemia and impending ventricular fibrillation or asystole. While fluids (A), hydrocortisone (C), and insulin/dextrose to shift potassium (D) are all required components of adrenal crisis management, intravenous calcium gluconate is the *most immediate* life-saving action. Calcium acts instantly to stabilize the myocardial resting membrane potential and prevent lethal arrhythmias, buying time for the other medications to lower the serum potassium and reverse the shock.
</details>

---

### Question 167
A client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr. After 4 hours of treatment, the client's blood glucose has decreased from 580 mg/dL to 240 mg/dL. The client's anion gap is currently 18 mEq/L (normal: 8-12 mEq/L). Which pharmacological intervention should the nurse anticipate *next*?

A. Discontinue the intravenous insulin infusion and transition to subcutaneous glargine.
B. Increase the regular insulin infusion rate to rapidly close the anion gap.
C. Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous maintenance fluids.
D. Administer a rapid bolus of 1,000 mL of 0.9% Normal Saline to clear serum ketones.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous maintenance fluids.

**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY

**Rationale:** In the management of DKA, the goal of the insulin drip is to clear serum ketones and close the anion gap, not just to lower blood glucose. Because the blood glucose frequently drops faster than the ketones clear, stopping the insulin drip when glucose reaches normal levels would cause a relapse into ketosis. According to high-alert DKA protocols, once the blood glucose drops to 250 mg/dL (13.9 mmol/L) or below, 5% dextrose must be added to the IV maintenance fluids. This prevents severe iatrogenic hypoglycemia while allowing the continuous insulin infusion to remain running until the anion gap is fully closed (≤ 12 mEq/L).
</details>

---

### Question 168
A 19-year-old client is being treated in the ICU for Severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the last 6 hours, rapid fluid resuscitation and a continuous insulin infusion have reduced the client's blood glucose from 750 mg/dL to 300 mg/dL. Which of the following assessment findings is the *earliest* indicator of a fatal complication requiring immediate intervention?

A. The client complains of a sudden, severe headache.
B. The client's Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score drops from 15 to 8.
C. The client's respiratory rate decreases from 32 to 20 breaths per minute.
D. The client develops a newly widened pulse pressure and bradycardia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The client complains of a sudden, severe headache.

**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION

**Rationale:** Rapid fluid shifts and rapid reduction of serum osmolality during the treatment of DKA can lead to cerebral edema, a highly fatal complication most common in adolescents and young adults. The *earliest* and subtlest signs of cerebral edema include a sudden, severe headache, lethargy, confusion, or behavioral changes. A drop in GCS to 8 (B) and Cushing's triad (widened pulse pressure, bradycardia, irregular respirations) (D) are late, ominous signs of brainstem herniation. A decrease in respiratory rate from 32 to 20 (C) is an expected, positive sign indicating the resolution of Kussmaul respirations as the metabolic acidosis clears.
</details>

---

### Question 169
A client with a history of Hashimoto's thyroiditis was admitted to the intensive care unit in myxedema coma. The client has received intravenous levothyroxine, hypertonic saline, and passive rewarming over the last 12 hours. Which assessment finding most reliably indicates that the immediate life-threatening pathophysiology of this crisis is resolving?

A. Deep tendon reflexes improve from absent to 1+ bilaterally.
B. Serum thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) levels drop from 85 mU/L to 45 mU/L.
C. The client reports feeling warmer and requests the removal of a warming blanket.
D. Arterial blood gas results demonstrate a decrease in PaCO2 from 65 mmHg to 42 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Arterial blood gas results demonstrate a decrease in PaCO2 from 65 mmHg to 42 mmHg.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS

**Rationale:** Myxedema coma is characterized by profound metabolic slowing, which leads to severe respiratory depression, hypercapnia (high PaCO2), respiratory acidosis, and subsequent CO2 narcosis. Normalization of the PaCO2 indicates the reversal of central respiratory depression, which is one of the primary causes of mortality in this crisis. While improved reflexes (A) and feeling warmer (C) are positive signs, they do not reflect the stabilization of a primary life-threatening system failure. TSH levels (B) take weeks to normalize and are not used to evaluate acute crisis resolution.
</details>

---

### Question 170
A client in a thyrotoxic crisis (thyroid storm) develops severe bibasilar crackles, profound jugular venous distention, and a sudden drop in blood pressure from 155/85 mmHg to 88/50 mmHg over 30 minutes. The cardiac monitor shows sinus tachycardia at 165 bpm. The nurse understands that this multi-system failure is best managed by anticipating which immediate combination of therapies?

A. Administer intravenous hydrocortisone and 100% oxygen via non-rebreather mask.
B. Discontinue propylthiouracil (PTU) and prepare for emergent synchronized cardioversion.
C. Administer an intravenous bolus of 0.9% normal saline and start a norepinephrine infusion.
D. Administer a continuous esmolol infusion and prepare for intravenous furosemide.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Administer a continuous esmolol infusion and prepare for intravenous furosemide.

**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION

**Rationale:** Thyroid storm causes extreme systemic adrenergic hyperactivity. The heart is overstimulated, leading to severe sinus tachycardia, dramatically increased cardiac workload, and ultimately high-output heart failure (manifested here by crackles, JVD, and cardiogenic shock/hypotension). An ultra-short-acting beta-blocker like esmolol is critical to immediately block the sympathetic overdrive, slow the heart rate, and reduce myocardial oxygen demand. Diuretics (furosemide) are required to clear the acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema. Administering a fluid bolus (C) would fatally exacerbate the pulmonary edema. Synchronized cardioversion (B) is ineffective for sinus tachycardia driven by a systemic endocrine storm. Oxygen and steroids (A) are supportive but do not fix the acute pump failure driven by beta-adrenergic overstimulation.
</details>
### Question 171
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A 19-year-old client is admitted to the intensive care unit with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The client is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion and aggressive fluid resuscitation. Which assessment finding represents the earliest, most subtle sign that the client is experiencing a life-threatening complication of the current therapies?

A. A widening pulse pressure and an abrupt drop in heart rate to 48 bpm.
B. Blood glucose rapidly decreasing by 80 mg/dL after the first hour of treatment. 
C. The client complains of a dull, new-onset headache and becomes uncharacteristically irritable.
D. A transition from deep, rapid Kussmaul respirations to shallow breathing with an SpO2 of 94%.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is at extreme risk for developing cerebral edema, a highly lethal complication of severe DKA (especially in pediatric and young adult populations) caused by rapid intracellular fluid shifts during fluid resuscitation and insulin administration. The *earliest* and most subtle signs of impending cerebral edema are neurological changes such as a new headache, irritability, lethargy, or confusion. Option A describes Cushing's triad (bradycardia, widening pulse pressure, altered respirations), which is a late and often irreversible sign of brainstem herniation. Option B is an expected and safe rate of glucose reduction (optimal is 50-75 mg/dL/hr; 80 mg/dL is slightly fast but not an immediate clinical sign of failure to rescue). Option D reflects the expected resolution of severe metabolic acidosis as the body no longer needs to blow off excess CO2.

</details>

---

### Question 172
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client with severe DKA has been receiving a continuous IV regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr. The client's initial blood glucose was 720 mg/dL and is currently 240 mg/dL. The most recent basic metabolic panel (BMP) reveals a serum potassium of 3.1 mEq/L and an anion gap of 18 mEq/L. Which high-alert pharmacological intervention must the nurse execute immediately?

A. Hold the continuous insulin infusion and notify the provider to initiate intravenous potassium replacement.
B. Transition the primary IV fluid to 5% Dextrose in 0.45% NaCl and maintain the current insulin infusion rate.
C. Discontinue the insulin drip and administer a rapid-acting subcutaneous insulin dose to close the anion gap.
D. Increase the insulin infusion rate slightly to accelerate the resolution of the metabolic acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Insulin drives potassium into the intracellular space, rapidly depleting intravascular serum potassium. While a client with DKA still has an open anion gap (normal is 8-12 mEq/L) and acidosis, insulin is normally required. However, the strict high-alert protocol for DKA dictates that if the serum potassium falls below 3.3 mEq/L, the continuous insulin infusion *must be held immediately*, and aggressive IV potassium replacement must be initiated. Continuing the insulin (even with dextrose) will precipitate lethal hypokalemia, leading to fatal cardiac arrhythmias. Option B is partially correct in DKA management (adding Dextrose when blood glucose reaches 200-250 mg/dL), but maintaining the insulin rate while potassium is critically low is lethal. Options C and D are completely contraindicated as they will worsen the hypokalemia.

</details>

---

### Question 173
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is admitted to the progressive care unit in a state of Thyroid Storm following an abrupt discontinuation of antithyroid medications. The severe hypermetabolic state is overwhelming the client’s physiological reserves. Which clinical presentation definitively demonstrates that the primary endocrine failure has progressed into a secondary, life-threatening cardiovascular failure?

A. Severe agitation, delirium, and tremors progressing to a generalized tonic-clonic seizure.
B. A core body temperature of 105.2°F (40.6°C) accompanied by profound diaphoresis and dehydration.
C. An acute decrease in urine output to 15 mL/hr with a rapidly increasing serum creatinine level.
D. New-onset pink frothy sputum, jugular venous distention, and a mean arterial pressure (MAP) of 55 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Thyroid storm induces a profound hypermetabolic state resulting in severe tachycardia, increased oxygen demand, and excessive cardiac workload. Over time, this overwhelming demand leads to secondary high-output heart failure and cardiogenic shock. The presence of pink frothy sputum (pulmonary edema), jugular venous distention (right-sided failure), and a profoundly low MAP indicates severe biventricular cardiovascular collapse. Option A reflects primary neurological decompensation from the storm itself. Option B is a direct, primary manifestation of the extreme hypermetabolism of thyrotoxicosis. Option C reflects acute kidney injury (likely pre-renal from dehydration), but Option D specifically illustrates the classic, often fatal, multi-system integration complication of high-output heart failure secondary to thyrotoxicosis. 

</details>

### Question 174
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A client is brought into the emergency department unresponsive. The medical history indicates long-standing, untreated Hashimoto's thyroiditis. Assessment reveals a core body temperature of 92.8°F (33.7°C), a heart rate of 42 bpm, and a respiratory rate of 6 breaths per minute with an SpO2 of 81% on room air. The arterial blood gas shows a PaCO2 of 72 mmHg. What is the nurse's *immediate* life-saving action?

A. Apply a forced-air warming blanket to rapidly correct the profound hypothermia.
B. Prepare the client for immediate endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation.
C. Administer intravenous levothyroxine (T4) as prescribed to reverse the hypothyroid state.
D. Administer a rapid 1,000 mL bolus of warmed 0.9% Normal Saline to support perfusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** This client is presenting with Myxedema Coma, characterized by extreme hypothermia, bradycardia, altered mental status, and profound hypoventilation leading to severe respiratory acidosis (PaCO2 72 mmHg) and hypoxia. Following the ABCs (Airway, Breathing, Circulation), the immediate life-saving action is securing the airway and providing mechanical ventilation, as respiratory failure is the most rapid cause of death in myxedema coma. Option A is contraindicated; active, rapid rewarming causes sudden peripheral vasodilation, which can trigger irreversible cardiovascular collapse and shock in these clients (passive rewarming is preferred). Option C is the definitive endocrine treatment but does not supersede airway control. Option D poses a severe risk for fluid overload and heart failure in a profoundly hypometabolic, bradycardic heart.

</details>

### Question 175
**Lens: PRIORITIZATION**

The intensive care unit charge nurse is reviewing the status of four clients experiencing severe endocrine emergencies. Which client requires the nurse's *immediate* assessment and intervention?

A. A client with Myxedema Coma receiving IV levothyroxine who develops new-onset jaw pain and ST-segment elevation on the monitor.
B. A client with Severe DKA on a continuous regular insulin infusion whose anion gap decreased from 24 mEq/L to 14 mEq/L over the last 6 hours.
C. A client in an Addisonian Crisis who exhibits profound weakness, a blood pressure of 90/58 mmHg, and a serum potassium of 5.9 mEq/L.
D. A client with Thyroid Storm receiving Lugol's iodine and propylthiouracil (PTU) who reports an intense metallic taste in their mouth.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Clients with profound hypothyroidism (Myxedema Coma) often have underlying, undiagnosed coronary artery disease. Administering high-dose IV levothyroxine abruptly increases the resting metabolic rate and myocardial oxygen demand. If the coronary arteries cannot supply adequate blood flow, the client will suffer an acute myocardial infarction (manifested by jaw pain and ST-segment elevation). This is a lethal, immediate priority. Option B demonstrates expected, positive progress (the anion gap is closing, meaning the acidosis is resolving). Option C describes a client with expected baseline findings of an Addisonian Crisis (hypotension, hyperkalemia, weakness); while unstable, they do not take precedence over an active myocardial infarction. Option D describes a normal, expected adverse effect of Lugol's iodine administration.

</details>
### Question 176
**Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A 14-year-old client is admitted to the pediatric intensive care unit with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The client is receiving a continuous infusion of regular IV insulin at 0.1 units/kg/hr and 0.9% normal saline. Which assessment finding is the subtlest, earliest indicator that the client is experiencing a lethal failure-to-rescue complication?

A) The client's urinary output steadily increases to 150 mL/hr. 
B) The client's respiratory rate increases to 32 breaths per minute with deep, regular breaths.  
C) The client complains of a mild, persistent headache and exhibits sudden irritability.  
D) The telemetry monitor demonstrates sinus tachycardia with peaked T-waves.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Rationale:** In children and young adults with severe DKA, rapid fluid shifts and overly rapid decreases in blood glucose or serum osmolality can lead to cerebral edema, a highly lethal complication. The earliest and subtlest signs of increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) from cerebral edema are a new onset of a mild headache, behavioral changes, sudden irritability, or lethargy. Option A is expected initially due to osmotic diuresis from hyperglycemia. Option B describes Kussmaul respirations, which are expected in severe metabolic acidosis. Option D indicates hyperkalemia, which is a risk during DKA, but it is not a "failure to rescue" sign of cerebral edema; rather, it is an expected initial finding requiring careful electrolyte management alongside insulin.
</details>

### Question 177
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

An adult client is admitted to the ICU in a life-threatening Thyroid Storm. The provider orders a continuous esmolol infusion, IV hydrocortisone, oral propylthiouracil (PTU), and Lugol's iodine solution. As the nurse coordinates the administration of these high-alert medications, which sequence or action is absolute priority?

A) Administer the Lugol's iodine solution exactly 1 hour before administering the PTU.  
B) Administer the PTU exactly 1 hour before administering the Lugol's iodine solution.  
C) Hold the IV hydrocortisone if the client's capillary blood glucose exceeds 180 mg/dL.  
D) Titrate the esmolol infusion upward to maintain a heart rate slightly above 100 bpm to support cardiac output.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B  
**Rationale:** In the pharmacological management of Thyroid Storm, it is critical to administer the thionamide (propylthiouracil or methimazole) at least 1 hour *before* administering iodine solutions (Lugol's solution or SSKI). The PTU blocks the synthesis of new thyroid hormone. If iodine is given first, it provides the thyroid gland with the exact substrate it needs to rapidly synthesize and release even more thyroid hormone, drastically worsening the storm (Jod-Basedow phenomenon). Option C is incorrect; hydrocortisone is life-saving in thyroid storm (it blocks peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 and treats relative adrenal insufficiency) and should never be held for mild hyperglycemia. Option D is incorrect; beta-blockers like esmolol are titrated to *reduce* the heart rate below 100 bpm to prevent high-output cardiac failure.
</details>

---

### Question 178
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**

A client is admitted to the emergency department in an acute Adrenal Crisis. The client is lethargic, severely hypotensive, and complaining of profound weakness. Which set of laboratory and diagnostic findings requires the most immediate, advanced intervention by the nurse?

A) Sodium 132 mEq/L, Potassium 5.1 mEq/L, rare premature ventricular contractions (PVCs).  
B) Glucose 68 mg/dL, Calcium 9.0 mg/dL, sinus tachycardia at 110 bpm.  
C) Glucose 140 mg/dL, Potassium 3.8 mEq/L, flattened T-waves and prominent U-waves.  
D) Sodium 128 mEq/L, Potassium 6.8 mEq/L, widened QRS complexes on the EKG.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D  
**Rationale:** Adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis) results in a profound deficiency of aldosterone and cortisol. Lack of aldosterone causes massive sodium excretion and potassium retention. A potassium level of 6.8 mEq/L combined with widened QRS complexes indicates severe hyperkalemia leading to impending ventricular fibrillation or asystole. This is a lethal rhythm change requiring immediate administration of IV calcium gluconate (to stabilize the myocardium) followed by agents to shift potassium intracellularly (e.g., IV regular insulin with dextrose). Option A shows mild lab derangements. Option B shows mild hypoglycemia expected with cortisol deficiency, but not as immediately lethal as a widening QRS. Option C indicates hypokalemia, which is opposite to what typically occurs in an adrenal crisis.
</details>

---

### Question 179
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A client with a long-standing history of Hashimoto's thyroiditis is brought to the emergency department. The client is profoundly lethargic, has a core temperature of 93.8°F (34.3°C), a heart rate of 42 bpm, and a respiratory rate of 6 breaths/min with poor chest rise. The client suddenly loses consciousness. What is the nurse's immediate life-saving action?

A) Prepare the equipment for immediate endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation.  
B) Administer the prescribed IV levothyroxine push to reverse the metabolic collapse.  
C) Apply active external rewarming devices such as a forced-air warming blanket to normalize core temperature.  
D) Administer IV hydrocortisone 100 mg to prevent concurrent adrenal collapse.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A  
**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting signs of Myxedema Coma, a life-threatening complication of extreme hypothyroidism characterized by severe hypoventilation, bradycardia, and hypothermia. A respiratory rate of 6 with loss of consciousness indicates acute respiratory failure and impending arrest. Following the ABCs (Airway, Breathing, Circulation), airway protection and mechanical ventilation are the absolute first priorities. Options B and D are necessary pharmacological interventions for Myxedema Coma but secondary to securing a patent airway and providing ventilation. Option C is contraindicated; active external rewarming can cause massive peripheral vasodilation, leading to irreversible shock and cardiovascular collapse. Passive rewarming (e.g., warm blankets in a warm room) is the standard of care.
</details>

---

### Question 180
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is receiving aggressive ICU care for Thyroid Storm. Due to the extreme hypermetabolic state, the nurse monitors closely for multi-organ complications. Which assessment finding indicates that the primary endocrine crisis has precipitated a severe, secondary cardiovascular failure?

A) A core body temperature of 105.2°F (40.6°C) accompanied by profound diaphoresis.  
B) Severe nausea, projective vomiting, and diffuse, cramping abdominal pain.  
C) New-onset jugular venous distension (JVD), crackles in bilateral lung bases, and an S3 gallop.  
D) Fine motor tremors, extreme psychomotor agitation, and a Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score of 13.  

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C  
**Rationale:** Thyroid storm causes an extreme hypermetabolic state, exponentially increasing tissue oxygen demand. The heart is forced to pump at an excessive rate and volume to meet these demands, which rapidly depletes cardiac reserve and often precipitates high-output heart failure. The presence of JVD, bibasilar crackles (pulmonary edema), and an S3 gallop strongly indicates that the left and right ventricles are failing to handle the massive volume and workload (secondary cardiovascular failure). Options A, B, and D describe classic, primary manifestations of the thyroid storm itself (hyperpyrexia, GI distress, and central nervous system agitation), rather than the secondary integration of cardiovascular pump failure.
</details>
### Question 181
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION**

The nurse receives the shift report on an endocrine step-down unit. Which client must the nurse assess **FIRST**?

A) A client with hyperparathyroidism who reports generalized bone pain and has a serum calcium of 11.2 mg/dL.
B) A client with adrenal insufficiency whose blood pressure dropped from 110/70 to 92/58 mmHg after receiving a dose of IV hydrocortisone.
C) A client in diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) receiving an insulin infusion who suddenly reports a severe headache and exhibits a declining level of consciousness. 
D) A client with myxedema coma whose current arterial blood gas (ABG) shows a PaCO2 of 52 mm Hg and a pH of 7.31.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** A sudden headache and a declining level of consciousness in a client being treated for DKA are classic, ominous signs of cerebral edema. This is a rapid, life-threatening complication resulting from rapid fluid shifts during the correction of hyperglycemia. It requires immediate hyperosmolar therapy (e.g., mannitol or 3% hypertonic saline) to prevent brain herniation and death. This is the most unstable client.
*Incorrect Options:*
A: A calcium level of 11.2 mg/dL is elevated (normal 8.6-10.3 mg/dL) and bone pain is expected in hyperparathyroidism, but this is not an immediate life threat compared to cerebral edema.
B: A drop in blood pressure requires assessment and possible fluid resuscitation, but the client is already receiving the definitive treatment (IV hydrocortisone) for adrenal insufficiency. 
D: A PaCO2 of 52 mm Hg and pH of 7.31 indicates mild to moderate respiratory acidosis, which is an expected finding in myxedema coma due to hypoventilation. While this requires continuous monitoring and potential ventilatory support, an actively herniating brain takes ultimate priority.
</details>

---

### Question 182
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client is receiving continuous intravenous regular insulin and 0.9% Normal Saline for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The client's initial blood glucose was 650 mg/dL with a serum pH of 7.15. Current laboratory results show a blood glucose of 220 mg/dL, a pH of 7.24, and an anion gap of 18 mEq/L. Which advanced pharmacological intervention should the nurse anticipate next?

A) Discontinue the continuous regular insulin infusion and transition to subcutaneous glargine.
B) Administer a rapid IV bolus of 50% Dextrose (D50) to prevent profound hypoglycemia.
C) Decrease the insulin infusion rate by half and switch the IV fluid to 0.45% Sodium Chloride.
D) Add 5% Dextrose (D5W) to the current IV fluid regimen while maintaining the insulin infusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** In the treatment of DKA, the continuous insulin infusion must run until the ketoacidosis resolves, which is indicated by the closure of the anion gap (normal is typically 8-12 mEq/L). Because the anion gap is still 18 mEq/L, the client is still in ketoacidosis. However, the blood glucose has dropped below 250 mg/dL. To prevent hypoglycemia while allowing the insulin drip to continue halting lipolysis and ketogenesis, a dextrose-containing solution (like D5W or D5.45NS) must be added to the IV fluids.
*Incorrect Options:*
A: Stopping the insulin drip while the anion gap is open will cause an immediate relapse into severe ketoacidosis. Subcutaneous insulin is only started after the gap is closed and the client can eat.
B: A D50 bolus is reserved for acute, severe hypoglycemic emergencies, not for a controlled glucose level of 220 mg/dL.
C: Switching to 0.45% NaCl without adding dextrose will lead to hypoglycemia since the glucose is already nearing 200 mg/dL and the insulin drip is still running. 
</details>

---

### Question 183
**LENS: RISK OF DETERIORATION**

A client is admitted with myxedema coma and is receiving initial emergency treatment with intravenous levothyroxine and hydrocortisone. The nurse closely monitors the client for early, subtle signs of a "failure to rescue" complication related to the rapid pharmacological reversal of this condition. Which clinical finding represents the greatest risk of immediate deterioration?

A) An increase in core body temperature from 95.2°F (35.1°C) to 97.8°F (36.5°C).
B) Development of occasional premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) and mild chest pressure.
C) A decrease in thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) levels compared to admission.
D) Mild tremors in the hands and transient flushing of the face.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** IV levothyroxine rapidly increases the basal metabolic rate and myocardial oxygen demand. Clients with myxedema coma often have underlying, undiagnosed coronary artery disease. A sudden increase in metabolic demand can precipitate acute myocardial ischemia, angina, or a fatal myocardial infarction. Chest pressure and new dysrhythmias (PVCs) are early signs of cardiac ischemia and represent a critical risk of deterioration.
*Incorrect Options:*
A: An increase in body temperature is an expected and desired outcome of treating myxedema coma, which presents with profound hypothermia.
C: A decrease in TSH is the expected physiological response as exogenous levothyroxine (T4) exerts negative feedback on the pituitary gland.
D: Mild tremors and flushing may indicate a mild hyperthyroid response to the IV levothyroxine, but they are not immediately life-threatening compared to the risk of acute myocardial infarction.
</details>

---

### Question 184
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client in an acute adrenal crisis presents with profound lethargy, a blood pressure of 72/40 mmHg, and a heart rate of 128 bpm. Laboratory results reveal a serum sodium of 126 mEq/L, potassium of 6.8 mEq/L, and BUN of 45 mg/dL. The client begins exhibiting peaked T waves and absent P waves on the electrocardiogram (EKG). Which pathophysiological sequence explains this multi-system failure?

A) Mineralocorticoid deficiency leads to absolute sodium and water loss, culminating in hypovolemic shock and renal retention of hydrogen and potassium ions.
B) Glucocorticoid excess causes massive renal potassium retention, leading to myocardial depolarization failure.
C) Elevated adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) directly suppresses sinoatrial node conduction, causing secondary cardiogenic shock.
D) Severe hyponatremia induces cerebral fluid shifts, causing sympathetic nervous system collapse and subsequent hyperkalemic acute tubular necrosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** Adrenal crisis involves a critical lack of mineralocorticoids (aldosterone) and glucocorticoids (cortisol). Aldosterone normally functions to retain sodium and water while excreting potassium and hydrogen ions in the kidneys. Its absence leads to profound sodium and water depletion (resulting in severe hypovolemia, shock, and elevated BUN indicating pre-renal acute kidney injury) and the dangerous retention of potassium (hyperkalemia). This severe hyperkalemia directly results in lethal cardiac dysrhythmias (peaked T waves, loss of P waves, and ultimately ventricular fibrillation).
*Incorrect Options:*
B: Adrenal crisis is characterized by glucocorticoid *deficiency*, not excess.
C: While ACTH is elevated in primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison's disease), it does not directly suppress sinoatrial node conduction; the hyperkalemia causes the EKG changes.
D: Hyponatremia can cause cerebral edema, but the shock in this scenario is hypovolemic/distributive due to cortisol and aldosterone deficiency, not due to a sympathetic collapse from fluid shifts.
</details>

---

### Question 185
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A client with Graves' disease is admitted with a severe exacerbation. During the shift, the client suddenly becomes extremely agitated, confused, and delirious. Vital signs reveal a temperature of 105.8°F (41.0°C), a heart rate of 188 bpm, and a blood pressure of 80/40 mmHg. What is the immediate life-saving emergency rescue action?

A) Administer an intravenous bolus of high-dose aspirin to rapidly reduce the hyperpyrexia.
B) Prepare for synchronized cardioversion to mechanically override the profound tachycardia.
C) Administer intravenous esmolol to block sympathetic nervous system overactivity. 
D) Immediately administer intravenous iodine solution to block the release of preformed thyroid hormones.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** This client is experiencing Thyroid Storm, characterized by extreme hyperpyrexia, cardiovascular collapse, and altered mental status due to a massive surge of thyroid hormones. The immediate life-saving priority is to control the peripheral effects of the massive sympathetic surge, which is best achieved with a rapid-acting beta-blocker like IV esmolol (or propranolol). This prevents fatal arrhythmias and reduces the heart rate, allowing for improved cardiac output.
*Incorrect Options:*
A: Aspirin is strictly contraindicated in thyroid storm. Salicylates displace thyroid hormone from its binding proteins, increasing the amount of free, active thyroid hormone in the blood and worsening the storm.
B: Cardioversion is not the primary intervention for sinus tachycardia or atrial fibrillation caused by severe thyrotoxicosis; the underlying sympathetic drive must be blocked first. 
D: Iodine solutions (like Lugol's solution) are used to block the release of thyroid hormone, but they *must* be administered 1-2 hours *after* giving antithyroid medications (like PTU or methimazole). If given before, the iodine will be used by the thyroid gland to synthesize even more thyroid hormone.
</details>
### Question 186
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**
**SCENARIO:** A client with a known history of Addison's disease is rushed to the emergency department after three days of severe gastroenteritis. The client is unresponsive, with a blood pressure of 68/40 mmHg, a heart rate of 132 bpm, and a thready pulse. The client’s skin is cool and clammy. Which immediate life-saving action must the nurse execute first?

A) Infuse a continuous norepinephrine drip to stabilize the rapidly dropping mean arterial pressure.
B) Administer broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics to empirically treat the underlying infection.
C) Administer a rapid IV bolus of hydrocortisone sodium succinate and 0.9% Normal Saline. 
D) Infuse 50 mL of 50% dextrose (D50W) to correct anticipated profound hyperglycemia.

<details>
<summary>View Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer: C**

**Rationale:** 
This client is in an acute Adrenal (Addisonian) Crisis triggered by gastrointestinal illness and volume depletion. The profound hypotension (shock) in adrenal crisis is primarily driven by an absolute deficiency of cortisol and aldosterone, resulting in extreme sodium and water loss, as well as vasoplegia (loss of vascular tone). Vasopressors (Option A) are largely ineffective if the underlying glucocorticoid deficiency is not corrected first. The immediate, life-saving intervention is the aggressive administration of isotonic crystalloids (0.9% Normal Saline) to restore intravascular volume, administered concurrently with high-dose intravenous glucocorticoids (hydrocortisone) to reverse the shock state. Option B is important but secondary to cardiovascular collapse. Option D is incorrect because Addisonian crisis causes profound *hypoglycemia*, not hyperglycemia, though fluids and steroids take immediate precedence.

</details>

---

### Question 187
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
**SCENARIO:** A client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr. The initial serum potassium was 5.2 mEq/L. The latest laboratory results reveal a blood glucose of 240 mg/dL, a pH of 7.15, and a serum potassium of 3.1 mEq/L. What is the priority nursing action?

A) Suspend the regular insulin infusion and request an order for IV potassium replacement.
B) Transition the intravenous fluids from 0.9% normal saline to 5% dextrose in water (D5W).
C) Administer IV sodium bicarbonate to buffer the profound metabolic acidosis.
D) Continue the insulin infusion and administer a STAT dose of oral potassium chloride.

<details>
<summary>View Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer: A**

**Rationale:** 
In the management of DKA, continuous insulin therapy drives both glucose and potassium intracellularly, causing serum potassium levels to drop rapidly. Strict clinical protocols dictate that if the serum potassium falls below 3.3 mEq/L, the insulin infusion MUST be held immediately. Continuing the insulin infusion (Option D) will drive the potassium even lower, placing the client at imminent risk for lethal cardiac dysrhythmias (e.g., ventricular fibrillation, cardiac arrest). The nurse must stop the insulin and prioritize intravenous potassium replacement until levels are > 3.3 mEq/L. Option B is an expected step when glucose drops to ~200-250 mg/dL, but addressing the critically low potassium is the life-saving priority. Option C is generally contraindicated as bicarbonate can paradoxically worsen intracellular acidosis and further drive potassium into the cells.

</details>

---

### Question 188
**LENS: RISK OF DETERIORATION**
**SCENARIO:** A 19-year-old client is being treated in the intensive care unit for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Due to aggressive fluid resuscitation and insulin therapy, the client's blood glucose has decreased rapidly from 850 mg/dL to 300 mg/dL over the past two hours. Which assessment finding represents the earliest, most critical sign of a failure to rescue in this specific client?

A) The client develops rapid, deep respirations with a fruity breath odor.
B) The client exhibits decorticate posturing in response to tactile stimuli.
C) The client's telemetry monitor displays peaked T waves and a widened QRS complex.
D) The client complains of a progressively worsening headache and feels increasingly lethargic.

<details>
<summary>View Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer: D**

**Rationale:** 
A rapid decline in blood glucose levels during DKA treatment (exceeding a drop of 50-75 mg/dL per hour), combined with aggressive fluid administration, drastically changes serum osmolality. This causes a massive osmotic shift of water into the brain cells, leading to Cerebral Edema—a highly lethal complication that disproportionately affects young adults and children. A progressively worsening headache and a subtle change in the level of consciousness (lethargy, irritability) are the earliest indicators of rising intracranial pressure (ICP). Option B (decorticate posturing) is a late, often irreversible sign of brainstem herniation. Option A describes Kussmaul respirations, which are expected in severe DKA acidosis. Option C indicates hyperkalemia, which is a risk early in DKA, but the rapid drop in glucose specifically elevates the risk of cerebral edema.

</details>

---

### Question 189
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**
**SCENARIO:** An older adult client is admitted to the emergency department in a Myxedema Coma. Assessment reveals profound bradycardia (HR 42 bpm), a core body temperature of 93.8°F (34.3°C), extreme lethargy, and a respiratory rate of 8 breaths/min with shallow effort. Arterial blood gas results show uncompensated respiratory acidosis. Which integrated intervention is the priority for managing this multi-system failure?

A) Vigorously rewarm the client using forced-air warming devices to rapidly reverse the severe hypothermia.
B) Administer intravenous levothyroxine and prepare the client for immediate endotracheal intubation.
C) Administer multiple doses of IV atropine to stimulate the sinoatrial node and correct the bradycardia.
D) Infuse 3% hypertonic saline as a rapid bolus to aggressively correct the anticipated hyponatremia.

<details>
<summary>View Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** 
Myxedema Coma is a life-threatening complication of severely decompensated hypothyroidism, resulting in multi-organ failure primarily characterized by severely depressed central respiratory drive (alveolar hypoventilation) and cardiovascular collapse. The definitive management requires addressing the endocrine root cause by administering IV thyroid hormone replacement (levothyroxine) while simultaneously securing the airway and providing mechanical ventilation for respiratory failure. Option A is contraindicated; active, rapid rewarming causes peripheral vasodilation, which will precipitate profound hypotension, shock, and cardiovascular collapse in a client who is already bradycardic and volume-depleted. Option C is incorrect as the bradycardia in myxedema coma is hormonally driven, not vagally mediated, rendering atropine largely ineffective. Option D is incorrect because rapid correction of hyponatremia using hypertonic saline risks osmotic demyelination syndrome.

</details>

---

### Question 190
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION**
**SCENARIO:** The nurse is receiving shift report on an endocrine step-down unit. Which client must the nurse assess FIRST?

A) A client with Addison's disease reporting generalized weakness and mild abdominal pain, with a blood pressure of 102/68 mmHg.
B) A client with Graves' disease exhibiting extreme agitation, a temperature of 104.5°F (40.3°C), and a heart rate of 168 bpm.
C) A client with Myxedema Coma whose core body temperature increased from 94.0°F (34.4°C) to 96.5°F (35.8°C) over the last 4 hours.
D) A client with severe diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) on an insulin infusion whose anion gap has successfully closed to 10 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary>View Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer: B**

**Rationale:** 
Option B describes a client in Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis), an acute, life-threatening hypermetabolic state characterized by extreme hyperthermia, severe tachycardia, altered mental status (agitation/delirium), and impending high-output heart failure. This is an absolute medical emergency requiring immediate assessment and intervention (antithyroid medications, beta-blockers, cooling). Option A describes a client with Addison's disease displaying expected, chronic symptoms; while they need assessment to prevent an Addisonian crisis, the hemodynamics (BP 102/68) are currently stable. Option C indicates successful, gradual medical intervention for myxedema coma (rising core body temperature), meaning the client is stabilizing. Option D indicates the DKA is resolving (closure of the anion gap is the clinical marker of resolution), so this client is stable and ready to transition to subcutaneous insulin. 

</details>
### Question 191
A client with a history of Addison's disease is admitted with severe gastroenteritis and rapidly develops an adrenal crisis. The client is profoundly lethargic, with a blood pressure of 72/40 mmHg, a serum potassium of 6.9 mEq/L, and a serum sodium of 126 mEq/L. Which pathophysiological consequence of this acute endocrine failure requires the most immediate multi-system intervention?

A. Diminished renal perfusion leading to acute tubular necrosis.
B. Loss of vascular tone and fatal dysrhythmias from mineralocorticoid deficiency.
C. Severe hypoglycemia resulting in irreversible cerebral ischemia.
D. Fluid shifts causing massive pulmonary edema and right-sided heart failure.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Multi-System Integration</strong><br>
An adrenal crisis causes a profound absence of aldosterone (a mineralocorticoid) and cortisol (a glucocorticoid). The lack of aldosterone leads to massive sodium and water loss (causing severe hypovolemia and vasoplegia) and retention of potassium. A potassium level of 6.9 mEq/L places the client at imminent risk for lethal ventricular dysrhythmias (cardiovascular collapse). While acute tubular necrosis (A) and hypoglycemia (C) are risks, the immediate cause of death in adrenal crisis is cardiovascular collapse due to hypovolemic shock and hyperkalemia-induced dysrhythmias. Pulmonary edema (D) is not a primary risk; these clients are profoundly dehydrated, not fluid overloaded.
</details>

---

### Question 192
A 14-year-old client is receiving a continuous regular insulin infusion and 0.9% normal saline for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the past two hours, the client's blood glucose has rapidly dropped from 680 mg/dL to 250 mg/dL. Which assessment finding is the most critical early indicator of a life-threatening complication?

A. The client's heart rate decreases from 125 bpm to 95 bpm.
B. The client reports intense thirst and a dry, sticky mouth.
C. The client's respiratory rate is 26 breaths per minute with deep, rapid breaths.
D. The client complains of an isolated, sudden onset, severe headache.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Risk of Deterioration</strong><br>
A severe and rapid drop in blood glucose during DKA treatment causes a rapid shift in serum osmolality. Water shifts from the intravascular space into the cerebrospinal fluid and brain cells, leading to cerebral edema—a highly lethal complication, particularly in pediatric and adolescent populations. A sudden, severe headache is often the earliest and subtlest sign of increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) and impending herniation. Decreased heart rate (A) indicates improving fluid volume status. Intense thirst (B) is an expected finding of dehydration. Deep, rapid respirations (Kussmaul breathing) (C) are an expected compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis and should gradually resolve with treatment.
</details>

---

### Question 193
A client in a suspected thyroid storm is prescribed propylthiouracil (PTU), Lugol's iodine solution, propranolol, and IV hydrocortisone. To prevent worsening the thyrotoxic crisis, the nurse must ensure which high-alert pharmacological principle is strictly followed?

A. Administer Lugol's iodine solution exactly 1 to 2 hours after administering the propylthiouracil (PTU).
B. Titrate the propranolol infusion to maintain a heart rate below 60 bpm before initiating the PTU.
C. Administer the IV hydrocortisone only after the client's core temperature has dropped below 38.0°C (100.4°F).
D. Administer Lugol's iodine solution immediately upon arrival, prior to the propylthiouracil (PTU).

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology</strong><br>
In the management of thyroid storm, synthesis of new thyroid hormone must be blocked *before* the release of preformed hormone is inhibited. Propylthiouracil (PTU) blocks the synthesis of new thyroid hormones. Lugol's iodine solution blocks the release of stored thyroid hormone. If iodine is given before PTU (Option D), the iodine can be used as a substrate by the thyroid gland to synthesize even *more* thyroid hormone, exacerbating the storm. Therefore, PTU must be given first, followed by iodine 1 to 2 hours later. Propranolol targets symptom control (beta-blockade) but aiming for a heart rate below 60 bpm in a hypermetabolic state can cause cardiovascular collapse (B). Hydrocortisone is given immediately to treat relative adrenal insufficiency and block peripheral conversion of T4 to T3; it is not dependent on temperature (C).
</details>

---

### Question 194
An older adult client is brought to the emergency department by emergency medical services (EMS) in a state of myxedema coma. The client is unarousable, with a core body temperature of 33.8°C (92.8°F), a heart rate of 42 bpm, and a respiratory rate of 6 breaths per minute with extremely shallow effort. Arterial blood gas results show a PaCO2 of 72 mmHg and a PaO2 of 54 mmHg. What is the nurse's immediate life-saving action?

A. Administer an intravenous bolus of 50% dextrose to treat severe hypoglycemia.
B. Apply active forced-air warming blankets to rapidly correct the systemic hypothermia.
C. Prepare for immediate endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation.
D. Administer a high-dose IV levothyroxine push to reverse the metabolic collapse.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Emergency Rescue</strong><br>
Myxedema coma is a life-threatening complication of severe hypothyroidism characterized by drastically decreased metabolic rate, leading to hypoventilation, hypothermia, bradycardia, and altered mental status. The client's respiratory rate of 6 breaths/min and PaCO2 of 72 mmHg indicate severe type 2 respiratory failure (hypercapnia and hypoxia). The immediate life-saving priority is securing the airway and supporting ventilation via intubation. While IV levothyroxine (D) is required to treat the underlying cause, airway takes precedence (ABCs). Active rapid warming (B) is contraindicated because it can cause severe peripheral vasodilation, leading to profound shock and cardiovascular collapse; warming must be done passively and slowly. Hypoglycemia may be present, but dextrose (A) does not supersede securing a failing airway.
</details>

### Question 195
The rapid response nurse assumes care for four clients experiencing acute endocrine instability. Which client must the nurse assess FIRST?

A. A client with syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone (SIADH) who has a serum sodium of 122 mEq/L and reports mild lethargy.
B. A client with hyperthyroidism reporting an abrupt onset of severe agitation, a temperature of 40.0°C (104.0°F), and a heart rate of 165 bpm.
C. A client being treated for diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) whose blood glucose is currently 250 mg/dL and potassium is 4.0 mEq/L.
D. A client with hypoparathyroidism who reports circumoral numbness and tingling in the fingertips.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br>
<strong>Lens: Prioritization</strong><br>
The client in Option B is exhibiting classic signs of Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis), characterized by extreme hyperthermia, severe tachycardia, and altered mental status (agitation/delirium). This is an absolute medical emergency with a high mortality rate due to the risk of imminent heart failure, arrhythmias, and cardiovascular collapse; this client must be seen first. The client with SIADH (A) has hyponatremia and requires intervention (hypertonic saline, fluid restriction) to prevent seizures, but mild lethargy indicates they are not yet actively seizing or herniating. The DKA client (C) has stabilizing numbers and is not the immediate priority. The hypoparathyroid client (D) is showing early signs of hypocalcemia (Chvostek/Trousseau precursors) and needs IV calcium, but this is less immediately life-threatening than an active thyroid storm.
</details>
### Question 196
A client with a history of severe, untreated hypothyroidism is brought to the emergency department presenting with a core body temperature of 94.1°F (34.5°C), a heart rate of 42 bpm, and profound lethargy. Within minutes of arrival, the client's respiratory rate drops to 6 breaths/min with an oxygen saturation of 81%. Based on the principles of **EMERGENCY RESCUE**, what is the nurse's immediate priority?

A) Administer IV levothyroxine sodium to reverse the metabolic crisis.
B) Apply forced-air warming blankets to rapidly increase the core body temperature.
C) Administer IV hydrocortisone to prevent a simultaneous adrenal crisis.
D) Prepare for immediate endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE

**Rationale:** 
This client is exhibiting classic signs of Myxedema Coma, a life-threatening endocrine emergency characterized by severe metabolic depression, hypothermia, bradycardia, and profound respiratory failure. In any emergency rescue scenario, the definitive airway takes absolute priority (ABCs). A respiratory rate of 6 breaths/min with severe hypoxia requires immediate mechanical ventilation to prevent respiratory arrest and death. 
Option A is incorrect because while IV levothyroxine is the definitive treatment for the underlying myxedema coma, airway management must precede medication administration. 
Option B is contraindicated; rapid active rewarming can cause massive peripheral vasodilation, leading to severe vascular collapse and profound shock. Passive rewarming is the standard of care.
Option C is incorrect; although IV corticosteroids are routinely given before thyroid replacement to prevent precipitating an adrenal crisis (as the two conditions often coexist), managing the collapsing airway is still the primary life-saving action.

</details>

---

### Question 197
A client is admitted with Severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) and is currently receiving a continuous IV regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr. The client's blood glucose has decreased from 520 mg/dL to 280 mg/dL over the past two hours. The latest basic metabolic panel reveals a serum potassium level of 3.1 mEq/L. Applying **HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY** principles, which action must the nurse take immediately?

A) Hold the continuous regular insulin infusion and notify the healthcare provider.
B) Continue the insulin infusion and add 5% Dextrose to the maintenance IV fluids.
C) Increase the insulin infusion rate to clear serum ketones more rapidly.
D) Administer a slow IV push of 10 mEq potassium chloride to correct the deficit.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY

**Rationale:** 
In the management of DKA, insulin drives both glucose and potassium intracellularly. If a client's serum potassium falls below 3.3 mEq/L, the strict standard of care is to *hold* the insulin infusion immediately and provide potassium replacement. Continuing the insulin drip while the client is hypokalemic will further deplete intravascular potassium, precipitating lethal cardiac dysrhythmias (such as ventricular fibrillation). 
Option B is incorrect; while 5% Dextrose is added to IV fluids when glucose reaches 200-250 mg/dL to prevent hypoglycemia, continuing the insulin without correcting the critically low potassium is fatal.
Option C is incorrect because increasing the insulin will worsen the hypokalemia. 
Option D is completely contraindicated; potassium chloride must NEVER be administered via IV push, as it will cause immediate cardiac arrest. It must be administered as a diluted piggyback infusion.

</details>

---

### Question 198
The nurse is receiving the shift report for an endocrine step-down unit. Based on **PRIORITIZATION** principles, which of the following unstable clients must the nurse assess first?

A) A client with primary hyperparathyroidism reporting sudden, severe flank pain and hematuria.
B) A client with Cushing's syndrome who has a capillary blood glucose of 280 mg/dL and new 2+ bilateral pitting edema.
C) A client with a pheochromocytoma complaining of a pounding headache, diaphoresis, and a blood pressure of 215/118 mmHg.
D) A client with diabetes insipidus receiving IV desmopressin whose urine output was 400 mL over the past 8 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION

**Rationale:** 
A pheochromocytoma is a rare catecholamine-secreting tumor of the adrenal medulla. The client's triad of a pounding headache, diaphoresis, and severe hypertension (215/118 mmHg) indicates a hypertensive crisis. This client is at imminent risk for a hemorrhagic stroke, myocardial infarction, or fatal dysrhythmia, making them the absolute highest priority. 
Option A describes a client passing a renal calculus (kidney stone) due to hypercalcemia. While painful and requiring intervention, it is not immediately life-threatening.
Option B describes expected findings in Cushing's syndrome (hyperglycemia and fluid retention from excess cortisol); these are chronic, stable issues that do not require emergency intervention. 
Option D describes a client whose diabetes insipidus is well-controlled; a urine output of 50 mL/hr is a sign that the desmopressin is effectively concentrating the urine, indicating stabilization.

</details>

---

### Question 199
A 19-year-old client is being treated for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the last 3 hours, massive isotonic fluid resuscitation and an IV insulin drip have rapidly reduced the client's blood glucose from 850 mg/dL to 250 mg/dL. Applying the **RISK OF DETERIORATION** lens, which subtle assessment finding strongly indicates the client is developing a lethal complication of therapy?

A) The client's respiratory rate transitions from deep, rapid breaths at 32/min to 20 breaths/min.
B) The client complains of a sudden, progressive headache and exhibits a subtle decline in Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS).
C) The client reports intense, persistent thirst and exhibits dry oral mucous membranes.
D) The client's hourly urine output decreases from 250 mL/hr to 120 mL/hr.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION

**Rationale:** 
A sudden, rapid drop in blood glucose during DKA treatment—especially in younger clients—creates an osmotic gradient that pulls water rapidly into the brain cells, causing Cerebral Edema. This is a lethal "failure to rescue" complication. The earliest, subtlest signs of cerebral edema are a new headache, lethargy, confusion, or any decline in the GCS. 
Option A is an expected, positive finding; a decrease in Kussmaul respirations indicates that the metabolic acidosis is resolving. 
Option C is an expected finding in DKA due to profound dehydration and does not indicate an immediate new life-threatening complication. 
Option D indicates successful fluid resuscitation and normalization of kidney function, as the osmotic diuresis (polyuria) associated with severe hyperglycemia is resolving.

</details>

---

### Question 200
A client with Addison's disease is admitted to the ICU with an acute Adrenal Crisis triggered by severe pneumonia. The client is profoundly hypotensive (BP 70/40 mmHg) and is failing to respond to aggressive IV fluid boluses. Utilizing **MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**, which pharmacological intervention corrects the root cause of this refractory cardiovascular collapse?

A) Administer IV hydrocortisone immediately while continuing fluid resuscitation.
B) Initiate a continuous infusion of norepinephrine to artificially restore vascular tone.
C) Administer IV glucagon to stimulate hepatic glycogenolysis and raise blood pressure.
D) Prepare for emergency hemodialysis to aggressively correct hyperkalemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION

**Rationale:** 
In an acute Adrenal Crisis (Addisonian Crisis), the multi-system failure is driven by a profound lack of cortisol and aldosterone. Cortisol is essential for maintaining vascular tone and allowing the blood vessels to respond to endogenous and exogenous catecholamines. Without it, massive vasodilation occurs, leading to refractory hypotensive shock that will *not* respond to fluids or vasopressors alone. IV hydrocortisone acts as both a glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid, reversing the shock state by restoring vascular sensitivity.
Option B is incorrect as a primary action; while vasopressors (like norepinephrine) may be used, they are notoriously ineffective in an adrenal crisis unless corticosteroids are administered first or simultaneously, as the vascular receptors require cortisol to function.
Option C is incorrect; while hypoglycemia is a component of adrenal crisis, correcting it with glucagon does not address the lethal cardiovascular collapse. 
Option D is incorrect; while hyperkalemia occurs due to aldosterone deficiency, it is managed medically (e.g., insulin/dextrose, calcium gluconate, steroids) before invasive hemodialysis is considered.

</details>
### Question 201
A 12-year-old client is admitted to the pediatric intensive care unit with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The nurse is infusing continuous IV regular insulin and 0.45% normal saline. Over the last 2.5 hours, the client's blood glucose has fallen rapidly from 720 mg/dL to 310 mg/dL. Which assessment finding represents the earliest, most subtle sign of a life-threatening complication requiring immediate intervention?

A. The client's blood pressure drops slightly from 115/75 mmHg to 105/65 mmHg.
B. The client's deep, rapid Kussmaul respirations decrease from 32 to 22 breaths per minute.
C. The client complains of a mild headache and suddenly becomes irritable and confused.
D. The client's urine output remains elevated at 120 mL/hr despite fluid replacement.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue').<br><br>
