<strong>Rationale:</strong> The most feared and lethal complication of severe DKA treatment, particularly in children and adolescents, is cerebral edema. It typically occurs when blood glucose drops too rapidly (faster than 50-100 mg/dL/hr), causing rapid fluid shifts into the hyperosmolar brain cells. The earliest and most subtle signs of cerebral edema include a sudden change in level of consciousness, mild headache, irritability, and lethargy. If missed, it progresses to Cushing's triad (bradycardia, widened pulse pressure, altered respirations) and brain herniation. <br>Option A is an expected finding as osmotic diuresis resolves. Option B indicates clinical improvement, as the body no longer needs to aggressively blow off CO2 to compensate for metabolic acidosis. Option D is an expected finding due to ongoing osmotic diuresis from hyperglycemia.
</details>

---

### Question 202
A client with a history of Addison's disease is rushed to the emergency department experiencing sudden, profound weakness, severe vomiting, and back pain following a bacterial infection. Vital signs are: BP 70/38 mmHg, HR 142 bpm, and RR 28/min. Laboratory results reveal a serum sodium of 126 mEq/L and potassium of 6.4 mEq/L. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse must execute?

A. Administer 10 units of IV regular insulin concurrently with 50 mL of 50% dextrose.
B. Rapidly infuse 0.9% normal saline and administer IV hydrocortisone sodium succinate.
C. Prepare the client for emergency synchronized cardioversion for unstable tachycardia.
D. Administer a continuous infusion of norepinephrine to rapidly correct the profound hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse).<br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> This client is in acute Adrenal Crisis (Addisonian crisis), a severe lack of cortisol and aldosterone triggered by the stress of an infection. The lack of aldosterone causes massive sodium and water loss (hypovolemic shock) and potassium retention (hyperkalemia), while the lack of cortisol eliminates vascular tone. The definitive, immediate life-saving rescue is rapid volume replacement with isotonic crystalloids (0.9% normal saline or D5NS) AND immediate IV glucocorticoid replacement (hydrocortisone). <br>Option A treats the hyperkalemia but ignores the fatal hypovolemic/distributive shock that will kill the client first. Option C is inappropriate as the tachycardia is a compensatory sinus response to hypovolemia, not an inherent arrhythmia. Option D is ineffective; in an adrenal crisis, the vasculature loses its sensitivity to catecholamines (vasopressors) until circulating corticosteroids are replaced. Fluids and steroids must come first.
</details>

---

### Question 203
A client in acute Thyroid Storm is admitted to the ICU. The provider orders stat administrations of propylthiouracil (PTU), Lugol's iodine solution, propranolol, and hydrocortisone. To prevent exacerbation of this hypermetabolic crisis, what is the correct pharmacological sequencing and rationale for the thyroid-specific medications?

A. Administer Lugol's iodine first to block thyroid hormone release, followed immediately by PTU to stop new synthesis.
B. Administer hydrocortisone first to prevent adrenal shock, followed by simultaneous iodine and PTU administration.
C. Administer simultaneous Lugol's iodine and PTU to rapidly halt the peripheral conversion of T4 to T3.
D. Administer PTU at least 1 to 2 hours before Lugol's iodine to prevent the iodine from serving as a substrate for new thyroid hormone synthesis.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating/sequencing high-alert meds in a crisis).<br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> In a Thyroid Storm, the sequence of anti-thyroid medications is absolutely critical. Propylthiouracil (PTU) or methimazole (thioamides) must be administered *first* to block the synthesis of new thyroid hormones. Lugol's iodine solution or potassium iodide is then given 1 to 2 hours *later*. Iodine blocks the release of pre-formed thyroid hormone. If iodine is administered before the thioamide, the hyperactive thyroid gland will rapidly use the sudden influx of iodine as a substrate to synthesize and release massive amounts of *new* thyroid hormone (Jod-Basedow phenomenon), catastrophically worsening the thyroid storm. <br>Options A, B, and C fail to recognize this critical chronological requirement, placing the client at severe risk of worsening cardiovascular collapse.
</details>

---

### Question 204
An ICU nurse is caring for a client with Myxedema Coma. Upon admission, the client presented with a core body temperature of 93.8°F (34.3°C), extreme lethargy, bradycardia (HR 42 bpm), and a blood pressure of 82/50 mmHg. After 12 hours of administering IV levothyroxine, corticosteroids, and applying passive rewarming techniques, which finding best indicates the advanced rescue interventions are successful?

A. The client's mean arterial pressure (MAP) stabilizes at 72 mmHg without the use of vasopressor support.
B. The client's core body temperature rapidly increases to 99.2°F (37.3°C) via forced-air active rewarming blankets.
C. The client's cardiac monitor displays frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs).
D. The client demonstrates shallow respirations with an arterial PaCO2 of 54 mmHg.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> EVALUATION OF CRISIS (Identifying physical signs that a rescue intervention was successful).<br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Myxedema Coma is a severe, life-threatening decompensated state of hypothyroidism characterized by cardiovascular collapse, hypothermia, and hypoventilation. The goal of IV levothyroxine is to restore the body's metabolic rate, thereby improving myocardial contractility and vascular tone. Stabilization of the MAP > 65 mmHg without chemical support indicates that cardiac output and tissue perfusion are improving, reflecting a successful rescue response. <br>Option B is incorrect and dangerous; active/rapid rewarming (forced-air) is contraindicated in Myxedema Coma as it causes rapid peripheral vasodilation, precipitating cardiovascular collapse. Option C (PVCs) is a sign of levothyroxine toxicity and myocardial ischemia, an adverse event. Option D (PaCO2 54 mmHg) indicates ongoing respiratory failure and hypoventilation, meaning the crisis has not resolved.
</details>

---

### Question 205
The ICU charge nurse is reviewing the status of four assigned clients currently experiencing acute endocrine crises. Which client represents the most critical risk for immediate mortality and must be assessed FIRST?

A. A client with Myxedema Coma who has a core body temperature of 95.5°F (35.3°C) and is receiving mechanical ventilation.
B. A client with severe DKA whose blood glucose dropped from 750 mg/dL to 250 mg/dL and now exhibits bradycardia and a widening pulse pressure.
C. A client with Thyroid Storm exhibiting a heart rate of 138 bpm, diaphoresis, and severe agitation while receiving a continuous propranolol infusion.
D. A client in an Adrenal Crisis who received a 1-liter normal saline bolus and IV hydrocortisone, now reporting a decrease in abdominal pain.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong><br><br>
<strong>Lens:</strong> PRIORITIZATION (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?).<br><br>
<strong>Rationale:</strong> Option B describes a client exhibiting classic signs of Cushing's Triad (bradycardia, widening pulse pressure, and potentially altered respirations), which is a late and lethal sign of increased intracranial pressure (ICP) due to cerebral edema. Cerebral edema is a fatal complication of treating severe DKA, especially when blood glucose is lowered too rapidly, causing massive osmotic shifts into the brain tissue. This client is actively herniating and requires immediate life-saving intervention (e.g., IV mannitol or hypertonic saline). <br>Option A is stable relative to their diagnosis, as their airway is secured via the ventilator and hypothermia is expected. Option C is expected in Thyroid Storm; while highly unstable, they are already receiving targeted therapy (propranolol) and are not exhibiting signs of imminent brain death. Option D is demonstrating a positive response to rescue therapy (decreased pain implies improving perfusion and resolution of the crisis).
</details>
### Question 206
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology**

An adolescent client is receiving continuous IV regular insulin and 0.9% normal saline for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the last two hours, the blood glucose drops rapidly from 780 mg/dL to 300 mg/dL. The client suddenly becomes highly lethargic and develops bradycardia with a widening pulse pressure. Which advanced pharmacological intervention is the priority?

A. Initiate a secondary infusion of 5% dextrose in 0.45% sodium chloride.
B. Increase the rate of the 0.9% normal saline infusion to maximize brain perfusion.
C. Administer intravenous atropine 0.5 mg to treat the symptomatic bradycardia.
D. Administer an IV bolus of 3% hypertonic saline or mannitol as prescribed.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Analysis</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D. Administer an IV bolus of 3% hypertonic saline or mannitol as prescribed.**

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of cerebral edema (lethargy, bradycardia, widening pulse pressure—Cushing's triad), which is a lethal complication of severe DKA, especially in children and adolescents. It occurs when blood glucose is lowered too rapidly, causing a rapid osmotic shift of water into the intracellular space of the brain. The immediate life-saving pharmacological intervention is hyperosmolar therapy with 3% hypertonic saline or mannitol to draw fluid out of the cerebral tissue and reduce intracranial pressure (ICP). 

**Why other options are incorrect:**
*   **A:** While adding 5% dextrose to IV fluids is standard protocol when blood glucose reaches 250-300 mg/dL in DKA to prevent hypoglycemia, it will not treat an active, life-threatening cerebral herniation. 
*   **B:** Increasing hypotonic or isotonic fluids can exacerbate the cerebral edema by providing more free water to shift into the brain cells. Fluids are typically restricted once cerebral edema is suspected.
*   **C:** The bradycardia is a reflex response to increased intracranial pressure (Cushing's reflex), not a primary cardiac issue. Atropine will not fix the underlying cause and is contraindicated as it distracts from treating the fatal ICP spike.
</details>

---

### Question 207
**Lens: Risk of Deterioration**

A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with thyroid storm. The client has been receiving propylthiouracil (PTU), intravenous propranolol, hydrocortisone, and cooling blankets. Which subtle assessment finding indicates to the nurse that the client is experiencing a critical "failure to rescue" related to cardiovascular collapse?

A. High-amplitude tremors of the hands and severe generalized agitation.
B. A rise in core body temperature from 103.1°F (39.5°C) to 103.8°F (39.9°C).
C. Development of a new S3 heart sound and fine bilateral basilar crackles.
D. A decrease in heart rate from 160 bpm to 110 bpm after propranolol administration.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Analysis</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: C. Development of a new S3 heart sound and fine bilateral basilar crackles.**

**Rationale:** Thyroid storm places an immense hypermetabolic demand on the body, leading to extremely high cardiac output. Prolonged tachycardia and extreme metabolic demands can rapidly exhaust the myocardium, leading to high-output heart failure and cardiogenic shock. A new S3 heart sound and basilar crackles are early, critical signs of ventricular failure and pulmonary edema. If not immediately addressed, the client will enter fatal cardiovascular collapse.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
*   **A:** Tremors and agitation are expected, baseline neurological symptoms of severe hyperthyroidism and thyroid storm, not indicators of impending cardiovascular collapse.
*   **B:** A highly elevated temperature is a hallmark of thyroid storm. While a slight increase shows the storm is ongoing, it is an expected systemic finding of the primary disease process, whereas heart failure (Option C) represents a lethal secondary deterioration (failure to rescue).
*   **C:** A decrease in heart rate to 110 bpm demonstrates a positive, expected response to the beta-blocker (propranolol) therapy.
</details>

---

### Question 208
**Lens: Multi-System Integration**

A client in acute adrenal crisis presents with a blood pressure of 72/40 mmHg, lethargy, and severe abdominal pain. Despite aggressive administration of intravenous 0.9% normal saline and hydrocortisone, the client's urine output has been 10 mL/hr for the past three hours. The latest serum potassium is 6.9 mEq/L, and the ECG shows widened QRS complexes and peaked T-waves. How should the nurse manage this multi-system failure?

A. Administer a 500 mL bolus of 5% dextrose in water (D5W) to dilute the potassium.
B. Prepare for urgent hemodialysis while administering IV calcium gluconate.
C. Initiate an IV infusion of fludrocortisone to restore sodium and potassium balance.
D. Administer intravenous furosemide 40 mg to stimulate renal potassium excretion.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Analysis</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: B. Prepare for urgent hemodialysis while administering IV calcium gluconate.**

**Rationale:** This client is experiencing multi-system failure: Addisonian crisis has led to severe hypovolemic shock, which subsequently caused an acute kidney injury (AKI) evidenced by profound oliguria (10 mL/hr). Because the kidneys have failed, they cannot excrete the rapidly rising potassium. The potassium of 6.9 mEq/L with widened QRS complexes indicates impending cardiac arrest. IV calcium gluconate must be given immediately to stabilize the myocardium, and because the kidneys are failing, urgent hemodialysis is the definitive treatment to clear the lethal hyperkalemia. 

**Why other options are incorrect:**
*   **A:** Administering D5W alone does not adequately shift or remove potassium, and giving large volumes of free water can cause severe hyponatremia, worsening the Addisonian crisis. 
*   **C:** Fludrocortisone is an oral mineralocorticoid used for long-term maintenance in Addison's disease, not an emergency IV medication. Hydrocortisone (already given) has sufficient mineralocorticoid activity for acute crisis.
*   **D:** Furosemide requires functioning kidneys to work. Given the client's oliguria (10 mL/hr) and profound hypotension (72/40 mmHg), a loop diuretic will be ineffective and will fatally worsen the hypovolemic shock.
</details>

---

### Question 209
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis**

A nurse is analyzing the laboratory and diagnostic data for a client found unresponsive with suspected myxedema coma. The client’s core temperature is 93.2°F (34.0°C), and the skin is severely edematous. Which set of data requires the *most* immediate life-saving intervention?

A. Arterial Blood Gas (ABG): pH 7.21, PaCO2 78 mmHg, PaO2 54 mmHg.
B. Serum sodium 124 mEq/L and a blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg.
C. Total T4 2.1 mcg/dL (Normal: 4.6-12) and TSH 45 mIU/L (Normal: 0.4-4.0).
D. Sinus bradycardia on the ECG at 42 beats per minute with a first-degree AV block.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Analysis</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: A. Arterial Blood Gas (ABG): pH 7.21, PaCO2 78 mmHg, PaO2 54 mmHg.**

**Rationale:** Myxedema coma causes a severe depression of the respiratory drive, leading to global hypoventilation, hypercapnia, and hypoxemia. The ABG reveals severe, uncompensated respiratory acidosis (pH 7.21) with profound hypercapnia (PaCO2 78) and hypoxia (PaO2 54). Respiratory failure is the leading cause of sudden death in myxedema coma. This client requires immediate intubation and mechanical ventilation.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
*   **B:** Hyponatremia and hypotension are classic, expected findings in myxedema coma due to decreased cardiac output and altered water excretion. While they require treatment (IV thyroid hormone and fluid restriction/hypertonic saline), airway and breathing take physiological precedence.
*   **C:** These labs confirm primary hypothyroidism (low T4, high TSH) but are diagnostic, not an acute emergency parameter requiring immediate action over severe respiratory failure.
*   **D:** Bradycardia is a direct result of the severely decreased metabolic rate in myxedema coma. It will typically resolve once IV Levothyroxine and warming measures are initiated; however, without an airway, the client will die before the bradycardia becomes the primary lethal issue.
</details>

---

### Question 210
**Lens: Prioritization**

The critical care charge nurse is reviewing the status of four assigned clients with endocrine emergencies. Which client must the nurse assess *first*?

A. A client with myxedema coma receiving IV levothyroxine who has a core body temperature of 95.0°F (35.0°C).
B. A client in DKA with a current blood glucose of 280 mg/dL whose anion gap has decreased to 10 mEq/L.
C. A client with a toxic multinodular goiter who has a heart rate of 115 bpm and is profusely diaphoretic.
D. A client with Addison's disease reporting sudden, severe lower back pain and intractable vomiting.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Analysis</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer: D. A client with Addison's disease reporting sudden, severe lower back pain and intractable vomiting.**

**Rationale:** Sudden, severe pain in the lower back, abdomen, or legs, combined with intractable vomiting, are the classic hallmark signs of an impending acute adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis). Without immediate intervention (rapid IV fluids and IV hydrocortisone push), this client will rapidly deteriorate into fatal cardiovascular collapse/shock. This client is the most unstable.

**Why other options are incorrect:**
*   **A:** A core temperature of 95.0°F in a client with myxedema coma is expected and often represents an *improving* trend since profound hypothermia (often < 90°F) is common on admission. The client is already receiving the correct treatment (IV levothyroxine).
*   **B:** An anion gap of 10 mEq/L (Normal: 8-12 mEq/L) and a blood glucose of 280 mg/dL indicate that the DKA is resolving. This client is stabilizing.
*   **C:** Tachycardia (115 bpm) and diaphoresis are expected, baseline findings for a client with hyperthyroidism (toxic multinodular goiter). While requiring monitoring, this client does not face immediate vascular collapse like the client in Option D.
</details>

---
### Question 211
The intensive care nurse receives the shift report for four clients experiencing acute endocrine emergencies. Applying the principles of prioritization, which client must the nurse assess *first*?

A) A client with hyperthyroidism reporting an abrupt onset of severe agitation, vomiting, and a core temperature of 104.2°F (40.1°C).
B) A client admitted for an adrenal crisis who has a blood pressure of 92/58 mmHg and is requesting oral fluids due to severe thirst.
C) A client with Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) receiving an insulin infusion whose latest capillary blood glucose is 245 mg/dL.
D) A client being treated for myxedema coma who is intubated, has a heart rate of 58 bpm, and exhibits absent bowel sounds.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** 
**A is correct.** This client is exhibiting classic, acute signs of Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis), a life-threatening hypermetabolic state. The extreme hyperthermia (104.2°F), severe agitation, and gastrointestinal distress indicate impending cardiovascular collapse and require immediate, aggressive intervention (e.g., beta-blockers, antithyroid medications, cooling blankets). 
**B is incorrect.** While a blood pressure of 92/58 mmHg in a client with adrenal crisis warrants close monitoring and continued fluid resuscitation, it is not as immediately lethal as the hypermetabolic crisis seen in the client with impending thyroid storm. 
**C is incorrect.** A blood glucose of 245 mg/dL in a DKA client on an insulin drip shows expected clinical improvement and indicates the client is nearing the threshold to add dextrose to their IV fluids. This client is stable. 
**D is incorrect.** Bradycardia and decreased gastrointestinal motility are expected, baseline findings in a client recovering from myxedema coma. Because the client's airway is already secured (intubated), they are stable enough to wait.

**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION
</details>

---

### Question 212
A 22-year-old client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of regular insulin at 0.1 units/kg/hr. The initial serum glucose was 820 mg/dL, and after 4 hours of treatment, it has dropped to 235 mg/dL. The client’s anion gap remains elevated at 18 mEq/L, and venous pH is 7.28. Which high-alert pharmacological intervention is the priority at this time?

A) Discontinue the continuous insulin infusion immediately and transition the client to subcutaneous long-acting glargine.
B) Increase the insulin infusion rate to 0.15 units/kg/hr to aggressively correct the remaining metabolic acidosis.
C) Administer a 50 mL bolus of 50% dextrose (D50) IV push to rapidly prevent iatrogenic hypoglycemic shock.
D) Append 5% dextrose to the IV maintenance fluids while continuing the regular insulin infusion at the current rate.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** 
**D is correct.** In the management of DKA, the continuous insulin infusion must be maintained until the metabolic acidosis resolves (indicated by a closed anion gap and normalized pH), not just until blood glucose normalizes. Because the blood glucose has fallen below 250 mg/dL but the anion gap remains elevated, 5% dextrose must be added to the maintenance IV fluids. This prevents life-threatening hypoglycemia while allowing the insulin drip to continue halting ketogenesis.
**A is incorrect.** Stopping the insulin drip before the anion gap closes will cause a rapid rebound of ketogenesis and worsen the DKA.
**B is incorrect.** Increasing the insulin rate while the glucose is rapidly dropping increases the risk of severe hypoglycemia and massive fluid shifts, leading to cerebral edema. 
**C is incorrect.** Administering D50 IV push is reserved for severe, symptomatic hypoglycemia. The client's blood glucose is currently 235 mg/dL, which does not warrant an emergency dextrose push.

**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY
</details>

---

### Question 213
A 19-year-old client is admitted to the ICU with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) and an initial blood glucose of 950 mg/dL. Over the last two hours, the client has received massive fluid resuscitation and an aggressive IV insulin infusion, causing the blood glucose to drop rapidly to 380 mg/dL. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the *earliest* risk of a catastrophic deterioration?

A) A decrease in hourly urine output from 150 mL/hr to 60 mL/hr over the past hour.
B) Kussmaul respirations beginning to transition to a more normalized respiratory rate.
C) New-onset sluggish pupillary response to light and the client complaining of a dull headache.
D) The sudden development of decorticate posturing and a heart rate dropping to 42 bpm.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** 
**C is correct.** Rapid shifts in serum osmolality during aggressive DKA treatment (especially when blood glucose drops too quickly) can cause fluid to shift into the brain cells, leading to cerebral edema. A dull headache, lethargy, and sluggish pupillary responses are the *earliest* and subtlest neurological indicators of increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) and require immediate intervention (e.g., mannitol or hypertonic saline).
**A is incorrect.** A decrease in urine output from polyuric levels (150 mL/hr) to normal levels (60 mL/hr) indicates that the osmotic diuresis caused by hyperglycemia is resolving, which is an expected sign of improvement.
**B is incorrect.** A transition from Kussmaul respirations to a normal respiratory pattern indicates that the profound metabolic acidosis is correcting, which is an expected and positive finding.
**D is incorrect.** Decorticate posturing and severe bradycardia (Cushing's triad) are very late, often irreversible signs of brain herniation due to cerebral edema. The nurse must recognize the deterioration much earlier.

**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION
</details>

---

### Question 214
A client with a history of Addison's disease is rushed to the emergency department after a severe systemic infection. The client is profoundly lethargic with a blood pressure of 68/38 mmHg, a heart rate of 135 bpm, and a core temperature of 96.2°F (35.6°C). The cardiac monitor reveals peaked T waves and widened QRS complexes. After establishing large-bore IV access, what is the immediate life-saving rescue action?

A) Prepare the client for immediate synchronized cardioversion.
B) Administer intravenous hydrocortisone sodium succinate and rapidly infuse 0.9% normal saline.
C) Initiate a continuous infusion of regular insulin without dextrose to lower serum potassium.
D) Administer 0.1 mg of oral fludrocortisone and position the client in high Fowler’s.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** 
**B is correct.** This client is in severe Adrenal Crisis, characterized by profound distributive shock, hypoglycemia, and hyperkalemia (indicated by the peaked T waves and widened QRS). The immediate, life-saving rescue action is the rapid administration of IV corticosteroids (hydrocortisone) to replace the missing cortisol, alongside aggressive volume resuscitation with isotonic crystalloids (0.9% normal saline) to reverse the shock.
**A is incorrect.** Synchronized cardioversion is indicated for unstable tachyarrhythmias (like SVT or VT with a pulse), not for sinus tachycardia resulting from hyperkalemic ECG changes or hypovolemic shock.
**C is incorrect.** While insulin shifts potassium intracellularly, administering it without dextrose in an Addisonian crisis will cause lethal hypoglycemia, as these clients are inherently hypoglycemic due to a lack of glucocorticoids.
**D is incorrect.** Oral fludrocortisone will not absorb quickly enough to rescue a client in cardiovascular collapse, and high Fowler's position will drastically worsen the client's severe hypotension.

**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE
</details>

---

### Question 215
An elderly client is admitted to the ICU in myxedema coma, exhibiting profound hypothermia, severe bradycardia, respiratory depression, and an altered level of consciousness. The healthcare team initiates mechanical ventilation, passive rewarming, and intravenous levothyroxine (T4) with hydrocortisone. Which combination of clinical data best indicates that the advanced rescue interventions are successful?

A) Core body temperature gradually increases to 97.8°F (36.5°C), heart rate stabilizes at 72 bpm, and PaCO2 normalizes to 42 mmHg.
B) Blood glucose levels increase from 65 mg/dL to 140 mg/dL, while serum sodium decreases from 130 mEq/L to 124 mEq/L.
C) Central venous pressure (CVP) reading drops to 1 mmHg, and the mean arterial pressure (MAP) remains at 55 mmHg.
D) The client suddenly develops fine resting tremors, profound diaphoresis, and a heart rate of 138 bpm.

<details>
<summary>Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** 
**A is correct.** Myxedema coma is the extreme manifestation of decompensated hypothyroidism, resulting in hypothermia, bradycardia, and severe hypoventilation leading to hypercapnia (retained CO2). A temperature of 97.8°F, a normal heart rate of 72 bpm, and a normal PaCO2 (35-45 mmHg) indicate a successful reversal of the metabolic suppression and respiratory failure.
**B is incorrect.** While the correction of hypoglycemia is positive, a decrease in serum sodium to 124 mEq/L indicates worsening dilutional hyponatremia, a severe complication of myxedema coma that can lead to seizures and cerebral edema.
**C is incorrect.** A CVP of 1 mmHg and a MAP of 55 mmHg indicate persistent, dangerous hypovolemia and inadequate tissue perfusion (MAP should be >65 mmHg). 
**D is incorrect.** Fine tremors, diaphoresis, and severe tachycardia (138 bpm) are signs of profound thyrotoxicosis (Thyroid Storm). This indicates that the IV levothyroxine dose was excessive and has pushed the client from one life-threatening extreme to the other.

**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS
</details>

---
### Question 216
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY
**Topic:** Severe DKA with Cerebral Edema

A 19-year-old client with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr alongside 0.9% Normal Saline. Over the last 90 minutes, the client's blood glucose precipitously dropped from 680 mg/dL to 250 mg/dL. The client suddenly becomes somnolent, develops a heart rate of 48 bpm, and has a blood pressure of 175/60 mmHg. Which immediate high-alert pharmacological intervention is required?

A. Administer IV atropine 1 mg to reverse the symptomatic bradycardia.
B. Increase the regular insulin infusion to 0.15 units/kg/hr to clear ketones.
C. Switch the primary IV fluids to 5% Dextrose in Water (D5W) to stop the glucose drop.
D. Administer prescribed IV mannitol and prepare to adjust the fluid infusion rate.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Administer prescribed IV mannitol and prepare to adjust the fluid infusion rate.

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting signs of Cushing's triad (bradycardia, widened pulse pressure, and hypertension) along with an altered level of consciousness (somnolence). This indicates cerebral edema, a lethal and rapid complication of DKA treatment caused by precipitous drops in serum osmolality and blood glucose. The immediate life-saving rescue is reducing intracranial pressure using an osmotic diuretic like mannitol or hypertonic saline. While dextrose is typically added to IV fluids when glucose reaches 250 mg/dL to prevent hypoglycemia (Option C), addressing the active cerebral herniation with mannitol takes absolute priority. Treating the bradycardia in isolation (Option A) ignores the underlying elevated intracranial pressure. Increasing the insulin (Option B) would fatally worsen the osmolarity shift. 

</details>

---

### Question 217
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION
**Topic:** Myxedema Coma

A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with myxedema coma. The client presents with a core body temperature of 93.8°F (34.3°C), extreme lethargy, and non-pitting facial edema. The nurse recognizes that severe hypothyroidism can precipitate a lethal secondary system failure. Which finding indicates the client requires immediate multi-system rescue?

A. Arterial blood gas results showing pH 7.26, PaCO2 65 mmHg, and PaO2 55 mmHg.
B. Serum sodium of 128 mEq/L combined with generalized muscle weakness.
C. Heart rate of 52 bpm with flattened T waves on the 12-lead electrocardiogram.
D. Central venous pressure (CVP) of 4 mmHg with delayed capillary refill.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Arterial blood gas results showing pH 7.26, PaCO2 65 mmHg, and PaO2 55 mmHg.

**Rationale:** Myxedema coma represents the most extreme, life-threatening form of hypothyroidism, causing a profound slowing of all metabolic processes. A critical multi-system complication is the depression of the respiratory drive, leading to alveolar hypoventilation, severe hypercapnia, and hypoxemia. The ABG results in Option A indicate acute decompensated respiratory acidosis and impending respiratory failure. This requires immediate multi-system rescue, including intubation and mechanical ventilation, alongside intravenous levothyroxine administration. While hyponatremia (Option B) and bradycardia (Option C) are expected findings in myxedema coma, the acute failure of the respiratory system poses the most immediate threat to life.

</details>

---

### Question 218
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION
**Topic:** Adrenal Crisis

The nurse receives the morning handoff report for an endocrine progressive care unit. Which unstable client must the nurse see FIRST?

A. A client with hyperthyroidism reporting a resting heart rate of 110 bpm and fine hand tremors.
B. A client with diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) whose blood glucose is 240 mg/dL and needs 5% dextrose added to their IV fluids.
C. A client with Addison's disease who complains of sudden, severe lower back pain and has a blood pressure of 86/48 mmHg.
D. A client with diabetes insipidus receiving IV vasopressin who has a urine output of 350 mL over the last 8 hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. A client with Addison's disease who complains of sudden, severe lower back pain and has a blood pressure of 86/48 mmHg.

**Rationale:** The client with Addison's disease is exhibiting classic signs of acute adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis). Severe, unexplained lower back, abdominal, or leg pain combined with profound hypotension indicates imminent vascular collapse and hypovolemic shock due to a massive depletion of cortisol and aldosterone. This client requires immediate life-saving IV hydrocortisone and aggressive fluid resuscitation. Option B is important to prevent hypoglycemia as the DKA resolves, but this client is currently stable enough to wait while the adrenal crisis is managed. Options A and D present expected or stabilizing findings for their respective conditions.

</details>

---

### Question 219
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION
**Topic:** Thyroid Storm

A client with untreated Graves’ disease is admitted for a severe bacterial pneumonia. The nurse is closely monitoring the client for the development of thyroid storm. Which subtle assessment finding is the *earliest* and most critical indicator of impending clinical deterioration?

A. The client exhibits new-onset scleral icterus and elevated liver enzymes.
B. The client becomes suddenly agitated, severely anxious, and unable to sit still in bed.
C. The client’s core body temperature abruptly spikes to 104.5°F (40.3°C).
D. The client develops a wide-complex tachycardia at a sustained rate of 170 bpm.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. The client becomes suddenly agitated, severely anxious, and unable to sit still in bed.

**Rationale:** Thyroid storm is an extreme, life-threatening hypermetabolic crisis often triggered by a stressor (such as an infection) in a patient with hyperthyroidism. While hyperpyrexia (Option C) and extreme tachycardia (Option D) are classic, devastating hallmarks of a fully developed thyroid storm, the *earliest* signs of clinical deterioration often manifest as sudden neurological and behavioral changes. New-onset severe agitation, restlessness, tremors, or delirium are highly sensitive, early indicators of central nervous system overstimulation. Recognizing these subtle neurobehavioral shifts allows the nurse to intervene (e.g., administering beta-blockers, antithyroid medications, and cooling measures) before catastrophic cardiovascular collapse or severe hyperpyrexia occurs. Liver involvement (Option A) is a later, less frequent complication.

</details>

---

### Question 220
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS
**Topic:** Adrenal Crisis

A client in an acute adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis) presents to the emergency department with profound lethargy, a blood pressure of 70/40 mmHg, and a serum potassium of 6.4 mEq/L. The team aggressively administers IV hydrocortisone, 0.9% normal saline, and 50% dextrose. Which subsequent assessment finding provides the best evidence that the primary life-saving goal of these interventions was successful?

A. The client’s blood pressure stabilizes at 115/72 mmHg with a urine output of 45 mL/hr.
B. The client's serum potassium decreases to 4.9 mEq/L and peaked T waves disappear.
C. The client reports that their severe abdominal cramping has completely resolved.
D. The client's blood glucose level rises steadily from 48 mg/dL to 102 mg/dL.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The client’s blood pressure stabilizes at 115/72 mmHg with a urine output of 45 mL/hr.

**Rationale:** Acute adrenal crisis is primarily characterized by severe mineralocorticoid and glucocorticoid deficiency, which rapidly leads to massive sodium and water loss, profound vascular collapse, and lethal hypovolemic shock. The primary goal of emergency resuscitation is to restore intravascular volume and tissue perfusion. A stabilized blood pressure (MAP > 65 mmHg) and adequate end-organ perfusion (urine output > 30 mL/hr) provide the definitive clinical evidence that life-threatening shock has been reversed. While resolving hyperkalemia (Option B), relieving severe pain (Option C), and managing hypoglycemia (Option D) are highly positive secondary outcomes indicating the medication is working, overcoming the irreversible vascular collapse remains the ultimate metric of successful system rescue.

</details>

---
### Question 221
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')
**Topic:** Severe DKA with Cerebral Edema

A 19-year-old client is admitted with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The initial blood glucose was 780 mg/dL, and the client has been receiving a continuous IV regular insulin infusion and 0.9% Sodium Chloride for the past 6 hours. The current blood glucose is 280 mg/dL. Which of the following subtle assessment findings represents the earliest indication of a lethal complication that requires immediate intervention?

A) Blood glucose decreases at a rate of 75 mg/dL per hour.
B) Serum potassium decreases from 5.5 mEq/L to 4.3 mEq/L.
C) The client complains of a persistent headache and becomes uncharacteristically irritable.
D) Urine output remains high at 150 mL/hr over the last two hours.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:**
- **A & B are incorrect:** A blood glucose drop of 50–75 mg/dL/hr is the safe, targeted rate to prevent osmotic shifts. A decrease in serum potassium is an expected physiological response to insulin therapy, which drives potassium intracellularly. 
- **C is correct:** Rapid fluid resuscitation and insulin administration in severe DKA can cause rapid shifts in serum osmolality. If the blood glucose drops too quickly, water moves into the cerebrospinal fluid and brain cells, leading to cerebral edema. The earliest and subtlest signs of cerebral edema (a life-threatening complication) are neurological changes, such as a worsening headache, irritability, lethargy, or a sudden change in sensorium.
- **D is incorrect:** High urine output (polyuria) is an expected finding of osmotic diuresis caused by hyperglycemia and is not an early sign of cerebral edema.

</details>

---

### Question 222
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating/Administering High-Alert medications in a crisis)
**Topic:** Thyroid Storm

A client is admitted to the intensive care unit in a state of Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis). The provider orders propylthiouracil (PTU), an iodine solution (Lugol's solution), IV propranolol, and IV hydrocortisone. To prevent a sudden, paradoxical worsening of thyrotoxicosis, which administration protocol must the nurse strictly follow?

A) Administer the iodine solution 1 to 2 hours *after* giving the propylthiouracil (PTU).
B) Administer the iodine solution 1 to 2 hours *before* giving the propylthiouracil (PTU).
C) Administer the propylthiouracil (PTU) and iodine solution concurrently through separate IV lines.
D) Withhold the propylthiouracil (PTU) until the client's heart rate drops below 100 bpm using propranolol.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:**
- **A is correct:** In the pharmacological management of thyroid storm, an antithyroid medication (like PTU or methimazole) must be administered *before* iodine. PTU blocks the synthesis of new thyroid hormones. If iodine is given first, the thyroid gland will use the sudden influx of iodine as a substrate to synthesize and release *more* thyroid hormone, severely worsening the thyrotoxic crisis (Jod-Basedow phenomenon). Iodine should be given 1 to 2 hours after PTU to utilize the Wolff-Chaikoff effect (inhibiting hormone release).
- **B & C are incorrect:** Giving iodine before or at the exact same time as PTU risks providing the overactive thyroid gland with the substrate needed to create more hormone before the synthetic pathways are effectively blocked.
- **D is incorrect:** PTU should not be delayed. Propranolol manages the peripheral sympathetic effects (tachycardia) but does not stop hormone synthesis. Both should be initiated promptly.

</details>

---

### Question 223
**Lens:** PRIORITIZATION (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?)
**Topic:** Endocrine Emergencies

The nurse receives the morning handoff report for an endocrinology step-down unit. Which client requires the nurse's IMMEDIATE assessment and intervention?

A) A client with Addison's disease reporting generalized abdominal pain and nausea, with a blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg.
B) A client in Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) exhibiting Kussmaul respirations with a blood glucose of 420 mg/dL.
C) A client with Graves' disease scheduled for a thyroidectomy who has a resting heart rate of 112 bpm and reports severe anxiety.
D) A client with severe, untreated hypothyroidism who presents with a core body temperature of 94.1°F (34.5°C), shallow respirations of 8 breaths/min, and profound lethargy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:**
- **A is incorrect:** Abdominal pain and nausea are warning signs of an impending adrenal crisis, but the client is currently normotensive and hemodynamically stable. This client needs prompt attention, but is not the immediate priority.
- **B is incorrect:** Kussmaul respirations and hyperglycemia are expected findings in DKA representing a compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis. While requiring ongoing critical care, this does not override an acute airway/breathing crisis.
- **C is incorrect:** Tachycardia and anxiety are expected baseline findings in an active hyperthyroid state (Graves' disease). 
- **D is correct:** This client is exhibiting signs of Myxedema Coma, a life-threatening decompensation of hypothyroidism. Hypoventilation (8 breaths/min) leads to severe hypoxemia and hypercapnia, which can cause respiratory arrest. According to the ABCs (Airway, Breathing, Circulation), the client with acute respiratory failure and altered mental status requires immediate life-saving intervention (intubation/mechanical ventilation and IV levothyroxine).

</details>

---

### Question 224
**Lens:** EVALUATION OF CRISIS (Identifying physical signs that a rescue intervention was successful)
**Topic:** Adrenal Crisis

A client was admitted in an acute Adrenal Crisis (Addisonian Crisis) exhibiting profound hypotension, hypoglycemia, and hyperkalemia. After 4 hours of aggressive resuscitation with IV 5% Dextrose in 0.9% Sodium Chloride (D5NS) and high-dose IV hydrocortisone, which assessment finding indicates that the life-saving interventions have been successful?

A) The client's serum sodium level rapidly increases by 14 mEq/L within the 4-hour window.
B) The mean arterial pressure (MAP) stabilizes at 74 mmHg and the client's sensorium clears.
C) The electrocardiogram reveals the sudden appearance of prominent U-waves and flattened T-waves.
D) The blood glucose decreases to 65 mg/dL after the continuous infusion of IV fluids is slowed.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:**
- **A is incorrect:** A rapid increase in serum sodium (greater than 8-10 mEq/L in 24 hours) is a dangerous complication that puts the client at risk for Osmotic Demyelination Syndrome (central pontine myelinolysis).
- **B is correct:** Adrenal crisis is characterized by severe hypovolemic/distributive shock due to a lack of cortisol and aldosterone. A successful rescue is evidenced by hemodynamic stability (MAP > 65 mmHg) and improved tissue perfusion, which directly results in clearing of the client's mental status (sensorium).
- **C is incorrect:** Prominent U-waves and flattened T-waves are signs of *hypokalemia*. While treating the hyperkalemia of adrenal crisis is necessary, shifting potassium too rapidly can cause life-threatening hypokalemia and cardiac arrhythmias. This indicates a complication, not a successful resolution.
- **D is incorrect:** Hypoglycemia is a key feature of adrenal crisis. Blood glucose dropping back to 65 mg/dL indicates ongoing deficiency of glucocorticoids, meaning the treatment has not fully stabilized the client's metabolic state.

</details>

---

### Question 225
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)
**Topic:** Thyroid Storm

A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with Thyroid Storm. The extreme hypermetabolic state places massive physiological demands on the body. Which combination of advanced assessment findings indicates that the thyrotoxicosis has precipitated acute decompensated heart failure?

A) Profound weight loss, a widened pulse pressure, and hyperactive bowel sounds.
B) Extreme psychomotor agitation, hyperthermia of 105.4°F (40.8°C), and profuse diaphoresis.
C) Jugular venous distension (JVD), pink frothy sputum, and a new S3 gallop.
D) Hypoactive deep tendon reflexes, non-pitting facial edema, and delayed capillary refill.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:**
- **A & B are incorrect:** These are classic manifestations of the primary endocrine failure (Thyroid Storm / extreme hyperthyroidism) itself. While life-threatening, they describe the hypermetabolic state, not the secondary failure of the cardiovascular system.
- **C is correct:** Thyroid storm dramatically increases cardiac workload, leading to high-output heart failure or toxic cardiomyopathy. The development of Jugular Venous Distension (right-sided failure), pink frothy sputum (pulmonary edema/left-sided failure), and an S3 gallop (fluid volume overload/ventricular dysfunction) directly indicates that the cardiovascular system has acutely failed secondary to the endocrine crisis.
- **D is incorrect:** These findings describe myxedema (severe hypothyroidism), which is the exact opposite of the clinical picture seen in a hypermetabolic thyroid storm.

</details>
### Question 226
A 19-year-old client is admitted to the intensive care unit with severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The client has been receiving continuous intravenous regular insulin and aggressive isotonic fluid resuscitation for the past 6 hours. The client's blood glucose has decreased from 710 mg/dL to 260 mg/dL. Which subtle assessment finding indicates the highest risk for a sudden, life-threatening complication?

A) The client complains of a dull, diffuse headache and appears increasingly drowsy.
B) The client exhibits Kussmaul respirations at a rate of 26 breaths per minute.
C) The client's continuous ECG monitor displays occasional premature atrial contractions (PACs).
D) The client reports mild periumbilical abdominal pain and nausea.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Risk of Deterioration (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')
**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing
**Client Need:** Physiological Adaptation

**Rationale:** 
In severe DKA, particularly in younger clients, rapid shifts in serum osmolality due to aggressive fluid resuscitation and insulin therapy can lead to cerebral edema. A dull headache, lethargy, drowsiness, or any acute change in the level of consciousness is the earliest and most critical clinical sign of increasing intracranial pressure (ICP) from cerebral edema. This requires immediate intervention (e.g., mannitol, hypertonic saline, reducing fluid rates) to prevent herniation and death. Kussmaul respirations (Option B) are an expected compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis and will gradually resolve as the anion gap closes. Mild arrhythmias like PACs (Option C) require monitoring but are not the earliest sign of a profound systemic collapse compared to cerebral edema. Abdominal pain (Option D) is a common, expected presentation in severe DKA due to the acidosis and typically resolves with treatment.
</details>

---

### Question 227
A client in severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/hr. The client's initial blood glucose was 820 mg/dL. Four hours into treatment, the blood glucose level drops to 240 mg/dL. The client's anion gap remains elevated at 18 mEq/L, and serum ketones are strongly positive. What is the nurse's priority pharmacological intervention?

A) Discontinue the regular insulin infusion and administer a subcutaneous dose of long-acting glargine.
B) Increase the regular insulin infusion rate to rapidly clear the remaining serum ketones.
C) Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous replacement fluids while maintaining the insulin infusion.
D) Administer 1 ampule of 50% dextrose (D50W) intravenous push to prevent imminent hypoglycemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** High-Alert Pharmacology (Titrating IV drips like Insulin in a crisis)
**Cognitive Level:** Applying
**Client Need:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies

**Rationale:** 
During the management of DKA, the primary goal of the continuous insulin infusion is to suppress lipolysis, clear serum ketones, and close the anion gap (normal is 8-12 mEq/L). When the blood glucose falls to approximately 200-250 mg/dL, the anion gap is often still open, meaning the insulin infusion must continue. To prevent iatrogenic hypoglycemia and a rapid drop in serum osmolality (which triggers cerebral edema), the nurse must add an exogenous glucose source (like D5W) to the IV fluids. Discontinuing the insulin (Option A) is strictly contraindicated while the anion gap remains open, as this will cause rebound ketoacidosis. Increasing the insulin rate (Option B) risks severe hypoglycemia. Administering D50W push (Option D) is reserved for acute, symptomatic hypoglycemic emergencies, not for protocol-driven glycemic maintenance in resolving DKA.
</details>

---

### Question 228
An elderly client with a known history of Hashimoto's thyroiditis is brought to the emergency department unresponsive. Assessment reveals profound hypothermia (89.6°F / 32°C), a heart rate of 38 bpm, a respiratory rate of 6 breaths/min, and non-pitting edema of the face and extremities. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse should anticipate performing first?

A) Administering a rapid intravenous bolus of levothyroxine (T4) to reverse the metabolic collapse.
B) Preparing for immediate endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation.
C) Applying active external rewarming devices, such as a forced-air warming blanket (Bair Hugger).
D) Administering an intravenous bolus of hydrocortisone to prevent concurrent adrenal crisis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Emergency Rescue (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)
**Cognitive Level:** Synthesizing
**Client Need:** Management of Care / Physiological Adaptation

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting classic signs of Myxedema Coma, a life-threatening complication of severe hypothyroidism. The immediate cause of death in myxedema coma is respiratory failure from profound hypoventilation, hypercapnia, and subsequent respiratory arrest. Following the ABCs (Airway, Breathing, Circulation), airway management via intubation and mechanical ventilation is the absolute highest priority. While IV levothyroxine (Option A) and IV hydrocortisone (Option D) are critical medical treatments that will follow immediately, they do not supersede securing an airway in a client breathing 6 times a minute. Active external rewarming (Option C) is completely contraindicated in myxedema coma; rapid external rewarming causes peripheral vasodilation, which will instantly precipitate cardiovascular collapse and refractory shock in a client with a severely depressed myocardium. Slow, passive rewarming is required.
</details>

---

### Question 229
The nurse is receiving shift report on a high-acuity endocrine step-down unit. Which client must the nurse assess first?

A) A client with diabetes insipidus (DI) who reports extreme thirst, has a urine output of 350 mL/hr, and a serum sodium of 148 mEq/L.
B) A client recovering from severe DKA whose current blood glucose is 180 mg/dL and who has occasional premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) with a potassium level of 3.4 mEq/L.
C) A client with severe, unmedicated hypothyroidism who has a resting heart rate of 55 bpm and is complaining of severe constipation and fatigue.
D) A client with Graves' disease who suddenly developed severe agitation, severe diaphoresis, a temperature of 104.2°F (40.1°C), and an irregular pulse of 148 bpm.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Prioritization (Who do you see first among 4 unstable system-specific clients?)
**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing
**Client Need:** Management of Care

**Rationale:** 
The client in Option D is exhibiting cardinal signs of Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis), a massive surge of thyroid hormones leading to a hypermetabolic state. The clinical presentation of severe hyperpyrexia (104.2°F), severe tachycardia/arrhythmias, and profound agitation indicates imminent cardiovascular collapse, congestive heart failure, and death if not immediately treated with beta-blockers, antithyroid medications, and cooling measures. This client is the most unstable. The client with DI (Option A) has expected symptoms that require ongoing hydration and vasopressin/desmopressin, but the sodium is only mildly elevated. The client recovering from DKA (Option B) requires potassium replacement for mild hypokalemia, but is not in immediate danger of collapse compared to the client in thyroid storm. The hypothyroid client (Option C) is exhibiting chronic, expected symptoms of the disease and is currently stable.
</details>

---

### Question 230
A client presents to the emergency department in acute adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis) following abrupt discontinuation of chronic corticosteroid therapy. The nurse evaluates the client's continuous ECG monitor, arterial blood gases (ABGs), and basic metabolic panel. Which combination of diagnostic data requires the most immediate life-saving intervention by the nurse?

A) Sinus tachycardia on ECG, a serum glucose of 58 mg/dL, and a blood pH of 7.32.
B) Absent P waves with a widened QRS complex on ECG, a serum sodium of 124 mEq/L, and a serum potassium of 7.2 mEq/L.
C) Flat T waves with prominent U waves on ECG, a serum potassium of 2.8 mEq/L, and a blood pH of 7.48.
D) Premature atrial contractions (PACs) on ECG, a serum sodium of 130 mEq/L, and a blood urea nitrogen (BUN) of 45 mg/dL.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Advanced Data Analysis (Interpreting lethal EKG rhythms, compensated ABGs, or toxic labs)
**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing
**Client Need:** Reduction of Risk Potential

**Rationale:** 
In acute adrenal crisis, the profound lack of aldosterone causes the kidneys to excrete massive amounts of sodium and water while retaining potassium, leading to severe hyponatremia, dehydration, and life-threatening hyperkalemia. A serum potassium of 7.2 mEq/L paired with ECG changes such as absent P waves and a widened QRS complex indicates impending lethal arrhythmias (ventricular fibrillation or asystole). This client requires emergency administration of intravenous calcium gluconate (to stabilize the myocardium), followed by IV regular insulin with 50% dextrose (to shift potassium intracellularly). Option A shows hypoglycemia and mild metabolic acidosis, which is expected in adrenal crisis and needs treatment, but is not as immediately lethal as the wide-QRS hyperkalemia. Option C describes hypokalemia, which contradicts the classic presentation of Addisonian crisis. Option D describes mild hyponatremia and prerenal azotemia (dehydration), which requires fluid resuscitation but does not present an immediate risk of cardiac arrest like Option B.
</details>
### Question 231
**LENS: PRIORITIZATION**
The nurse has just received the shift report on an endocrine step-down unit. Which of the following clients must the nurse assess FIRST?

A. A client with Addison's disease complaining of mild, generalized fatigue and a strong craving for salty foods.
B. A client with diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) on a continuous insulin infusion whose blood glucose dropped from 400 mg/dL to 250 mg/dL over the past two hours.
C. A client with a history of Graves' disease who has become acutely agitated, with a heart rate of 145 bpm and a temperature of 103.8°F (39.9°C).
D. A client with severe hypothyroidism whose morning TSH is 15 mU/L and complains of chronic, severe constipation.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click here for the rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** This client is exhibiting classic, life-threatening signs of a Thyroid Storm (thyrotoxic crisis), an extreme state of hyperthyroidism characterized by high fever, severe tachycardia, altered mental status (agitation/delirium), and cardiovascular collapse. This requires immediate emergency intervention (beta-blockers, antithyroid medications, cooling measures). Option A describes expected, chronic symptoms of Addison's disease (aldosterone deficiency causes salt cravings). Option B describes a safe and expected rate of blood glucose decline during DKA treatment (an optimal drop is 50-75 mg/dL per hour; dropping 150 mg/dL in two hours is exactly on target). Option D describes expected findings for uncontrolled hypothyroidism that do not pose an immediate threat to life. 
**Lens Focus:** Prioritization (Who do you see first among 4 unstable/system-specific clients?).
</details>

---

### Question 232
**LENS: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**
A client in severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of regular insulin at 0.1 units/kg/hr and 0.9% NaCl. Four hours into the treatment, the client’s blood glucose level reaches 240 mg/dL (13.3 mmol/L). What is the most critical pharmacological intervention at this time to prevent lethal neurological complications?

A. Add 5% dextrose to the continuous intravenous fluids while maintaining the insulin infusion.
B. Discontinue the regular insulin infusion immediately and administer subcutaneous glargine.
C. Change the insulin infusion to a rapid-acting analogue such as lispro to clear the ketones faster.
D. Increase the 0.9% NaCl infusion rate to rapidly correct the remaining intracellular dehydration.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click here for the rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** In the management of DKA, when the blood glucose falls to approximately 200–250 mg/dL, intravenous fluids must be switched to include 5% dextrose (e.g., D5 0.45% NaCl). The insulin drip is maintained to continue clearing ketones and resolving the acidosis. Adding dextrose prevents hypoglycemia and sudden drops in serum osmolality, which can rapidly lead to fluid shifting into the brain cells, causing lethal cerebral edema. Option B is incorrect; stopping the insulin drip prematurely will halt the reversal of ketoacidosis. Option C is incorrect; regular insulin is the standard of care for IV infusions. Option D is incorrect; aggressively increasing isotonic or hypotonic fluids without dextrose at this stage drastically increases the risk of cerebral edema.
**Lens Focus:** High-Alert Pharmacology (Titrating IV drips in a crisis).
</details>

---

### Question 233
**LENS: RISK OF DETERIORATION**
A client is admitted with an exacerbation of severe, long-standing Hashimoto's thyroiditis. Which of the following subtle assessment findings is the earliest indicator that the client is deteriorating into a life-threatening Myxedema Coma?

A. Increasing generalized, non-pitting edema (myxedema) and macroglossia.
B. Progressive somnolence accompanied by an arterial PaCO2 of 58 mm Hg.
C. A core body temperature drop from 96.5°F (35.8°C) to 95.0°F (35.0°C) with absent shivering.
D. A steady decline in heart rate from 62 to 55 beats per minute with normal sinus rhythm.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click here for the rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Myxedema coma is the extreme, life-threatening stage of hypothyroidism characterized by a drastic decrease in metabolic rate, leading to depressed respiratory drive, decreased cardiac output, and hypothermia. The hallmark of impending respiratory failure in myxedema coma is progressive alveolar hypoventilation, evidenced by somnolence, lethargy, and hypercapnia (elevated PaCO2, such as 58 mm Hg). This requires immediate intubation and mechanical ventilation. Options A, C, and D are all classic signs of severe hypothyroidism, but hypercapnia coupled with declining mental status represents acute failure of the respiratory system and is the most urgent indicator of clinical deterioration ("failure to rescue").
**Lens Focus:** Risk of Deterioration (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of 'failure to rescue').
</details>

---

### Question 234
**LENS: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**
A client with a history of autoimmune adrenalitis (Addison's disease) is brought to the emergency department in an acute Adrenal Crisis following a severe gastrointestinal viral infection. The client is lethargic, with a blood pressure of 70/40 mm Hg. How does this primary endocrine failure directly precipitate a life-threatening secondary system failure?

A. Cortisol deficiency induces massive hepatic glycogenolysis, leading to a hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state and neurotoxicity.
B. Epinephrine depletion causes profound bradycardia, leading to cardiogenic shock and a subsequent myocardial infarction.
C. Mineralocorticoid excess causes rapid potassium depletion, triggering lethal ventricular dysrhythmias and cardiac arrest.
D. Aldosterone deficiency causes profound sodium and water loss, leading to severe hypovolemic shock and acute kidney injury.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click here for the rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** Adrenal crisis is characterized by a severe lack of glucocorticoids (cortisol) and mineralocorticoids (aldosterone). Aldosterone is responsible for sodium and water retention and potassium excretion in the kidneys. An acute deficiency results in massive renal sodium and water loss (diuresis), hyperkalemia, and severe hypovolemia. This profound volume depletion leads directly to hypovolemic shock (evidenced by the BP of 70/40 mm Hg) and reduced renal perfusion, causing Acute Kidney Injury (AKI). Option A is incorrect because cortisol deficiency causes hypoglycemia, not a hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state. Option B is incorrect; adrenal crisis involves the adrenal cortex, not the adrenal medulla (which produces epinephrine). Option C is incorrect; adrenal crisis causes mineralocorticoid *deficiency*, resulting in potassium *retention* (hyperkalemia), not depletion.
**Lens Focus:** Multi-System Integration (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail).
</details>

---

### Question 235
**LENS: EMERGENCY RESCUE**
A 19-year-old client is in the intensive care unit receiving continuous IV regular insulin and 0.9% NaCl for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). The nurse notes the client suddenly complains of a severe, "splitting" headache, becomes increasingly lethargic, and exhibits a new blood pressure of 170/60 mm Hg with a heart rate of 52 bpm. What is the immediate life-saving action?

A. Administer 50% dextrose (D50) IV push to rapidly reverse cerebral hypoglycemia.
B. Place the client in a supine position and elevate the lower extremities to support systemic perfusion.
C. Prepare for the immediate administration of IV mannitol or hypertonic saline.
D. Increase the rate of the continuous regular insulin infusion to urgently decrease serum osmolarity.

<details>
<summary><strong>Click here for the rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of Cerebral Edema, a highly lethal complication of DKA treatment (most common in children and young adults). Symptoms include sudden severe headache, altered mental status/lethargy, and Cushing's triad (widened pulse pressure [170/60 = pulse pressure of 110], bradycardia [52 bpm], and irregular respirations) indicating dangerously elevated intracranial pressure (ICP) and impending brain herniation. The immediate life-saving rescue action is the administration of an osmotic diuretic (IV mannitol) or hypertonic saline (3% NaCl) to pull fluid out of the cerebral tissue. Option A is incorrect; the client is experiencing cerebral edema, not isolated hypoglycemia. Option B is incorrect; laying the client flat and elevating the legs increases venous return to the head, further increasing ICP (the head of the bed should be elevated). Option D is completely contraindicated; increasing insulin will drop serum osmolarity faster, driving *more* fluid into the brain cells and worsening the edema.
**Lens Focus:** Emergency Rescue (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse).
</details>
### Question 236
A 12-year-old client is admitted with severe diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) and is receiving an intravenous regular insulin infusion alongside aggressive fluid resuscitation. Which assessment finding represents the earliest, subtlest indicator of a catastrophic neurological complication requiring immediate intervention?

A) The client exhibits deep, rapid respirations at a rate of 34 breaths/minute.
B) A sudden drop in serum potassium from 5.9 mEq/L to 4.8 mEq/L.
C) A decrease in capillary blood glucose from 680 mg/dL to 500 mg/dL in one hour.
D) The client reports a new-onset dull headache and exhibits delayed responses to questions.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** Risk of Deterioration (Failure to rescue)
**Rationale:** The development of a headache and mild lethargy (delayed responses) are the earliest, subtlest signs of cerebral edema—a highly lethal complication of DKA treatment, particularly in children and adolescents. Rapid fluid shifts and sudden changes in serum osmolality can cause swelling in the brain. Deep, rapid respirations (Kussmaul respirations) are an expected compensatory mechanism for severe metabolic acidosis. A drop in potassium and a decrease in blood glucose are expected findings during DKA treatment and insulin administration, requiring monitoring but not indicating a catastrophic neurological event.
</details>

---

### Question 237
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit in acute thyroid storm. Despite initial interventions, the client develops profound tachycardia (168 bpm), a core temperature of 105.4°F (40.8°C), jugular venous distention, and sudden-onset bibasilar crackles with frothy sputum. The nurse recognizes that the client's severe hypermetabolic state has triggered which secondary system failure?

A) Acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) secondary to widespread alveolar capillary destruction.
B) High-output heart failure due to the cardiac muscle's inability to meet massive peripheral tissue oxygen demands.
C) A massive pulmonary embolism resulting from severe hypercoagulability and rapid atrial fibrillation.
D) Status asthmaticus triggered by a catastrophic surge in circulating endogenous catecholamines.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** Multi-System Integration
**Rationale:** Thyroid storm causes a massive, life-threatening surge in the body's metabolic rate. To deliver enough oxygen to the hypermetabolic tissues, the heart must drastically increase cardiac output. Over time, the myocardium cannot sustain this extreme workload, leading to high-output heart failure. The development of jugular venous distention, bibasilar crackles, and frothy sputum are classic signs of pulmonary edema secondary to this heart failure. While arrhythmias like atrial fibrillation are common, the primary multi-system complication presenting with these specific fluid-overload symptoms is high-output cardiac failure.
</details>

---

### Question 238
A client with undiagnosed Addison's disease arrives at the emergency department lethargic and in profound shock following a severe gastrointestinal infection. Vital signs are BP 70/38 mmHg, HR 132 bpm, and RR 28. Capillary blood glucose is 42 mg/dL. Which immediate life-saving rescue action is the nurse's priority?

A) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of 5% Dextrose in 0.9% Normal Saline and IV hydrocortisone.
B) Prepare immediately for rapid sequence intubation (RSI) to secure the airway before treating the shock.
C) Initiate a continuous intravenous infusion of norepinephrine (Levophed) to restore systemic vascular resistance.
D) Administer 1 mg of intramuscular glucagon and prepare to transfuse packed red blood cells.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** Emergency Rescue
**Rationale:** The client is experiencing an acute adrenal crisis (Addisonian crisis), characterized by profound hypovolemia, vascular collapse, hypoglycemia, and an acute absence of glucocorticoids and mineralocorticoids. The immediate life-saving intervention is rapid volume resuscitation combined with dextrose to correct the fatal hypoglycemia, alongside the administration of IV hydrocortisone (which acts as both a glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid). Vasopressors (like norepinephrine) will be ineffective if the fluid volume is not restored and the cortisol deficit is not corrected, because cortisol is required to maintain vascular reactivity to catecholamines.
</details>

---

### Question 239
The nurse analyzes the admission arterial blood gas (ABG) and metabolic panel for a client presenting in suspected myxedema coma. Which set of data strongly correlates with the profound physiological collapse expected in this severe endocrine emergency?

A) pH 7.52, PaCO2 28 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L; Serum Sodium 155 mEq/L
B) pH 7.29, PaCO2 35 mmHg, HCO3 16 mEq/L; Serum Sodium 148 mEq/L
C) pH 7.18, PaCO2 72 mmHg, HCO3 26 mEq/L; Serum Sodium 116 mEq/L
D) pH 7.48, PaCO2 45 mmHg, HCO3 32 mEq/L; Serum Sodium 135 mEq/L

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** Advanced Data Analysis
**Rationale:** Myxedema coma is the extreme, life-threatening manifestation of hypothyroidism, characterized by a profound slowing of all metabolic processes. This includes severe hypoventilation (which leads to carbon dioxide retention, causing severe respiratory acidosis, as seen in a PaCO2 of 72 and pH of 7.18) and a drastically decreased glomerular filtration rate paired with an SIADH-like state (which leads to severe dilutional hyponatremia, as seen in a sodium level of 116 mEq/L). The other options represent respiratory alkalosis, metabolic acidosis without severe hyponatremia, and metabolic alkalosis, none of which fit the classic profile of myxedema coma.
</details>

---

### Question 240
A client in severe diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of regular insulin. The client's blood glucose has decreased steadily from 780 mg/dL to 230 mg/dL. However, the current arterial pH is 7.27, and the anion gap remains elevated at 18 mEq/L. Which high-alert pharmacological action should the nurse take next?

A) Stop the continuous insulin infusion immediately and administer a scheduled subcutaneous dose of insulin glargine.
B) Add 5% Dextrose to the intravenous maintenance fluids and continue the regular insulin infusion.
C) Decrease the insulin drip rate by half and change the intravenous fluid directly to 0.45% Sodium Chloride.
D) Administer a rapid intravenous push of 50 mL of 50% Dextrose (D50) to prevent severe hypoglycemic brain injury.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** High-Alert Pharmacology
**Rationale:** In DKA, the goal of continuous IV insulin therapy is to halt ketogenesis and close the anion gap, not merely to lower blood glucose. Because the anion gap is still wide open (18 mEq/L) and acidosis is still present (pH 7.27), the insulin infusion MUST be continued. To prevent hypoglycemia while the insulin continues to clear the ketones, the standard of care requires adding dextrose (usually D5W or D5 in 0.45% NS) to the maintenance IV fluids once the blood glucose falls to approximately 200–250 mg/dL. Stopping the insulin would cause the patient to rapidly revert to worsening ketoacidosis. 
</details>
### Question 241
**A client in an acute adrenal (Addisonian) crisis is admitted to the intensive care unit with profound hypotension, severe flank pain, and refractory hypoglycemia. Despite aggressive IV isotonic fluid resuscitation and vasopressor initiation, the client's urine output drops to 10 mL/hr, and serum creatinine rises rapidly. Which pathophysiological mechanism best explains the secondary system failure occurring in this client?**

A) Severe glucocorticoid deficiency induces massive peripheral vasodilation and decreased vascular sensitivity to catecholamines, leading to refractory shock and acute tubular necrosis.
B) Excessive mineralocorticoid release causes profound sodium retention, precipitating right-sided cardiogenic shock and subsequent renal hypoperfusion.
C) Destruction of the adrenal medulla prevents epinephrine synthesis, directly causing neurogenic shock and immediate cessation of glomerular filtration.
D) Hypoglycemia triggers a hypermetabolic state that rapidly depletes renal adenosine triphosphate (ATP), halting the cellular pumps required for urine production.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)

**Rationale:** 
An adrenal crisis is characterized by a severe lack of cortisol (a glucocorticoid) and aldosterone (a mineralocorticoid). Cortisol is essential for maintaining vascular tone because it permits catecholamines (like epinephrine and norepinephrine) to exert their vasoconstrictive effects on blood vessels. Without cortisol, blood vessels profoundly dilate, and the body becomes refractory (unresponsive) to endogenous and exogenous vasopressors. This leads to profound distributive shock and severe hypoperfusion of the kidneys, culminating in acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and renal failure. 
Option B is incorrect because adrenal crisis involves a *deficiency*, not an excess, of mineralocorticoids. Option C is incorrect because adrenal crisis primarily affects the adrenal cortex, not the medulla. Option D is incorrect; while hypoglycemia occurs due to decreased gluconeogenesis, the renal failure is caused by ischemic shock, not ATP depletion from hypoglycemia.

</details>

---

### Question 242
**A 19-year-old client is receiving a continuous IV regular insulin infusion and 0.9% normal saline for severe Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA). Over the last two hours, the client's blood glucose has decreased rapidly from 780 mg/dL to 350 mg/dL. Which assessment finding indicates the highest risk for an impending, life-threatening complication?**

A) The client continues to exhibit deep, rapid respirations at 28 breaths per minute.
B) The client's serum potassium level decreases from 5.4 mEq/L to 4.2 mEq/L.
C) The client reports a mild, generalized headache and increasing drowsiness.
D) The client's capillary refill time is 3 seconds with cool, pale extremities.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION (Identifying the earliest, subtlest sign of a 'failure to rescue')

**Rationale:** 
The rapid reduction of blood glucose in DKA (a drop of 430 mg/dL over 2 hours is far above the recommended safe reduction rate of 50-75 mg/dL/hr) causes a sudden shift in serum osmolality. As the blood rapidly becomes less hypertonic, water shifts via osmosis across the blood-brain barrier and into the relatively hypertonic brain cells, precipitating cerebral edema. A mild headache and increasing drowsiness or lethargy are the earliest, subtlest signs of cerebral edema and rising intracranial pressure (ICP), requiring immediate intervention (such as mannitol or hypertonic saline) before herniation occurs.
Option A is an expected finding; Kussmaul respirations will persist until the metabolic acidosis clears. Option B is an expected effect of insulin driving potassium into the cells. Option D indicates mild peripheral vasoconstriction/dehydration, which is common in DKA but not an immediate harbinger of sudden death like cerebral edema.

</details>

---

### Question 243
**A client in an acute thyroid storm is admitted to the progressive care unit. The provider has prescribed multiple high-alert medications, including propylthiouracil (PTU), Lugol's iodine solution, IV hydrocortisone, and an esmolol continuous IV infusion. Which pharmacological action by the nurse is critical for safely managing this client's crisis?**

A) Withhold the IV hydrocortisone if the client's capillary blood glucose level exceeds 200 mg/dL to prevent a hyperosmolar crisis.
B) Administer the propylthiouracil (PTU) at least one hour prior to initiating the Lugol's iodine solution.
C) Administer the Lugol's iodine solution immediately upon admission to rapidly halt thyroid hormone synthesis.
D) Titrate the esmolol infusion to maintain a systolic blood pressure strictly above 160 mmHg to ensure adequate cerebral perfusion.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale & Lens</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips or sequencing high-alert meds in a crisis)

**Rationale:** 
In the pharmacological management of thyroid storm, the sequence of medication administration is critical. Propylthiouracil (PTU) or methimazole blocks the *synthesis* of new thyroid hormone. Iodine solutions (like Lugol's or SSKI) block the *release* of preformed thyroid hormone.
