### Question 287
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism and subsequent hemodynamic collapse is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of alteplase (tPA). The client's initial blood pressure was 82/50 mmHg. Which clinical finding requires the nurse to IMMEDIATELY stop the infusion and activate the rapid response team?

A. A sudden decline in the client’s Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score from 15 to 9.
B. Sustained oozing of dark blood from the client's peripheral IV insertion site.
C. An increase in the client's blood pressure from 82/50 mmHg to 108/64 mmHg.
D. Sinus tachycardia with a heart rate increase from 115 bpm to 122 bpm.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY (Titrating IV drips in a crisis)

**Rationale:** 
Alteplase is a thrombolytic agent used in life-threatening, massive pulmonary embolisms presenting with hemodynamic collapse. The most lethal complication of tPA is intracranial hemorrhage (ICH). A sudden, significant drop in the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score highly suggests acute intracranial bleeding, requiring the immediate cessation of the infusion, a stat head CT, and administration of reversal agents (such as cryoprecipitate or aminocaproic acid). 
Option B (oozing from puncture sites) is an expected, manageable side effect of thrombolytic therapy that does not typically warrant stopping a life-saving drip for massive PE. 
Option C represents a positive response to therapy (resolution of obstructive shock). 
Option D is an expected, mild fluctuation in heart rate for a client in acute cardiopulmonary distress.

</details>

---

### Question 288
A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is mechanically ventilated. The ventilator settings are: Assist-Control (AC), rate 18, tidal volume 400 mL (6 mL/kg), FiO2 80%, and PEEP 15 cm H2O. The latest arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals: pH 7.28, PaCO2 52 mmHg, PaO2 48 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L. Peak inspiratory pressure (PIP) is elevated at 45 cm H2O. What is the nurse's most accurate advanced interpretation of these data?

A. The client is experiencing uncompensated metabolic acidosis and requires immediate administration of intravenous sodium bicarbonate.
B. The client's ventilator tidal volume must be immediately increased to 800 mL to blow off excess carbon dioxide and correct the pH.
C. The client's gas exchange is steadily improving on current settings, and the nurse should prepare the client for an impending weaning trial.
D. The client is experiencing refractory hypoxemia and high barotrauma risk, warranting consideration of prone positioning and neuromuscular blockade.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS (Interpreting compensated ABGs and toxic parameters)

**Rationale:** 
The ABG indicates uncompensated respiratory acidosis (pH 7.28, PaCO2 52) and severe hypoxemia (PaO2 48) despite aggressive ventilator support (80% FiO2, PEEP 15). This is the hallmark of ARDS: refractory hypoxemia caused by non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema and massive intrapulmonary shunting. The high PIP (45 cm H2O) indicates severely decreased lung compliance and a high risk of barotrauma (pneumothorax). Increasing PEEP or FiO2 further carries immense toxicity and barotrauma risks. Therefore, advanced interventions such as prone positioning (to recruit posterior alveoli and improve V/Q matching) and neuromuscular blockade (to decrease oxygen demand and improve ventilator synchrony) are indicated. 
Option A is incorrect because the acidosis is respiratory, not metabolic. 
Option B is lethal; increasing tidal volumes in ARDS (volutrauma) destroys fragile alveoli; ARDS requires lung-protective low tidal volumes (4-6 mL/kg). 
Option C is dangerously incorrect, as the client is actively failing current therapy.

</details>

---

### Question 289
An intubated client receiving positive pressure mechanical ventilation suddenly triggers high-pressure alarms. The client develops profound hypotension, tachycardia, absent breath sounds on the right side, a tracheal deviation to the left, and an SpO2 drop to 78%. Which of the following is the most appropriate IMMEDIATE rescue action for the nurse to facilitate?

A. Administration of a rapid 1,000 mL normal saline bolus and initiation of a norepinephrine infusion.
B. Immediate preparation for and assistance with a right-sided needle thoracostomy.
C. Coordination of a stat portable chest X-ray to confirm the diagnosis of pneumothorax.
D. Increasing the ventilator's FiO2 to 100% and raising the positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP).

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE (The immediate life-saving action for a sudden system collapse)

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting the classic triad of a tension pneumothorax (absent breath sounds, severe hypotension/hemodynamic collapse, and tracheal deviation away from the affected side), which is a known complication of positive pressure ventilation (barotrauma). A tension pneumothorax is an absolute clinical emergency caused by trapped air compressing the heart and great vessels, preventing venous return. The immediate life-saving action is a needle thoracostomy (decompression) on the affected side (right side), followed by chest tube placement. 
Option A attempts to treat the shock symptoms but ignores the structural, obstructive cause. 
Option C is fatal; you never delay emergency decompression for radiographic confirmation when classic signs of tension pneumothorax with hemodynamic collapse are present. 
Option D is contraindicated; increasing PEEP will force more air into the pleural space, exacerbating the tension pneumothorax and worsening the cardiovascular collapse.

</details>

---

### Question 290
A client with a massive saddle pulmonary embolism remains intubated in the intensive care unit. Despite maintaining adequate oxygenation on the ventilator, the client develops newly distended jugular veins, marked peripheral edema, a palpably enlarged liver, and a central venous pressure (CVP) of 22 mm Hg. Which pathophysiological process best explains this secondary multi-system complication?

A. The pulmonary embolus has triggered a massive systemic inflammatory response, leading to distributive shock and fluid third-spacing.
B. Intrapulmonary shunting from the pulmonary embolism has resulted in left-sided heart failure, dramatically reducing cardiac output.
C. Increased pulmonary vascular resistance has caused acute right ventricular failure, resulting in systemic venous congestion.
D. Positive pressure from the mechanical ventilator is directly obstructing the inferior vena cava, causing a fluid backup into the systemic circulation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION (Managing how this system failure is causing a second system to fail)

**Rationale:** 
A massive pulmonary embolism creates a sudden, severe mechanical obstruction in the pulmonary arterial bed. This dramatically increases pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR). The right ventricle, which is not designed to pump against high resistance, acutely dilates and fails (acute cor pulmonale). As the right ventricle fails, blood backs up into the systemic venous system, causing highly elevated central venous pressure (normal CVP is 2-8 mm Hg, the client's is 22 mm Hg), jugular venous distension (JVD), hepatomegaly, and peripheral edema. 
Option A describes sepsis/distributive shock, which is characterized by vasodilation and a *low* CVP, not elevated. 
Option B describes left-sided heart failure, which would present with pulmonary edema (crackles, high pulmonary artery wedge pressure), not primary systemic venous congestion. 
Option D is incorrect; while high PEEP can decrease venous return mildly, it does not cause acute, massive hepatomegaly and a CVP of 22 mm Hg in the context of a massive PE.

</details>
### Question 291
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis**

A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is receiving pressure-controlled mechanical ventilation utilizing a low tidal volume and high positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) strategy. The nurse reviews the morning arterial blood gas (ABG) results: pH 7.32, PaCO2 56 mm Hg, PaO2 64 mm Hg, and HCO3 28 mEq/L. Based on these advanced data parameters, what is the most appropriate action by the nurse?

A. Document the findings as an expected outcome of permissive hypercapnia related to the ventilator strategy.
B. Administer an intravenous push of sodium bicarbonate to immediately correct the respiratory acidosis.
C. Request an order to increase the tidal volume to rapidly blow off the excess carbon dioxide.
D. Increase the FiO2 to 100% immediately to treat the severe hypoxemia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Document the findings as an expected outcome of permissive hypercapnia related to the ventilator strategy.

**Rationale:** 
In the management of ARDS, the primary ventilator strategy utilizes low tidal volumes (usually 4-8 mL/kg of ideal body weight) to prevent alveolar overdistention and volutrauma. A well-known consequence of this protective lung strategy is the retention of carbon dioxide, known as *permissive hypercapnia*. As long as the pH remains above 7.20 to 7.25, the hypercapnia (PaCO2 of 56 mm Hg) is tolerated and does not require intervention. A PaO2 of 64 mm Hg is also acceptable in ARDS, as the target is typically 55–80 mm Hg to prevent oxygen toxicity while maintaining adequate tissue perfusion. Therefore, documenting the findings is the correct action. Increasing tidal volume (Option C) would defeat the purpose of the protective lung strategy and risk barotrauma. Sodium bicarbonate (Option B) is not indicated for this mild respiratory acidosis. Increasing the FiO2 to 100% (Option D) is unnecessary and increases the risk of oxygen toxicity, as the current PaO2 is within the ARDS target range.

</details>

---

### Question 292
**Lens: Emergency Rescue**

A client with bilateral pneumonia is receiving mechanical ventilation when the high peak inspiratory pressure (PIP) alarm suddenly sounds. The client immediately exhibits a drop in blood pressure from 112/68 mm Hg to 70/40 mm Hg, a heart rate of 145 bpm, absent breath sounds on the right side, and the trachea is visibly deviated to the left. Which life-saving action must be prioritized?

A. Disconnect the mechanical ventilator and manually ventilate the client with a bag-valve-mask.
B. Prepare the necessary equipment for immediate needle decompression in the right second intercostal space.
C. Administer a rapid IV fluid bolus of 0.9% normal saline and prepare a continuous norepinephrine infusion.
D. Contact radiology for a STAT portable chest X-ray to formally diagnose a pneumothorax.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Prepare the necessary equipment for immediate needle decompression in the right second intercostal space.

**Rationale:** 
This client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax: high PIP alarms (indicating severe resistance to ventilation), severe hypotension (obstructive shock), tachycardia, absent breath sounds on the affected side (right), and tracheal deviation to the unaffected side (left). Positive pressure ventilation increases the risk of alveolar rupture, which can quickly evolve into a tension pneumothorax. The immediate emergency rescue action for a tension pneumothorax causing hemodynamic collapse is needle decompression (typically at the 2nd intercostal space, midclavicular line, or 5th intercostal space, anterior axillary line) followed by chest tube placement. Manual ventilation (Option A) will continue to push positive pressure into the chest, exacerbating the tension pneumothorax. Fluids and vasopressors (Option C) do not fix the underlying obstructive cause of the shock. Waiting for a chest X-ray (Option D) will fatally delay life-saving intervention.

</details>

---

### Question 293
**Lens: Risk of Deterioration**

A young adult client is admitted to the intensive care unit with status asthmaticus. The client is receiving continuous nebulized albuterol, systemic methylprednisolone, and supplemental oxygen. Which assessment finding represents the *subtlest*, earliest indicator that the client is experiencing impending respiratory failure and requires immediate escalation of care?

A. The client's heart rate increases from 115 bpm to 135 bpm over a 30-minute period.
B. The client is actively using the sternocleidomastoid and intercostal muscles during inspiration.
C. The client's PaCO2 increases from 30 mm Hg to 42 mm Hg on successive arterial blood gases.
D. The nurse auscultates widespread, scattered expiratory wheezes and a significantly prolonged expiratory phase.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. The client's PaCO2 increases from 30 mm Hg to 42 mm Hg on successive arterial blood gases.

**Rationale:** 
Asthma exacerbations typically cause hyperventilation, resulting in respiratory alkalosis and a low PaCO2 (e.g., 30 mm Hg). If a client with severe status asthmaticus demonstrates a rising PaCO2 that enters the "normal" range (35-45 mm Hg), this is a critical "failure to rescue" sign. It indicates that the respiratory muscles are profoundly fatigued and the client is no longer able to maintain the work of breathing necessary to blow off CO2. This "pseudo-normal" PaCO2 is an ominous sign of impending respiratory arrest and signals the need for potential mechanical ventilation. Tachycardia (Option A) is an expected side effect of continuous albuterol and the physiological stress of the attack. Accessory muscle use (Option B) and expiratory wheezing (Option D) are expected, classic signs of an active asthma attack, not the subtlest indicator of the shift toward sudden respiratory collapse.

</details>

---

### Question 294
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology**

A client with a massive saddle pulmonary embolism (PE) develops severe hemodynamic collapse (BP 68/38 mm Hg, HR 138 bpm). The critical care team initiates an intravenous infusion of systemic alteplase (tPA). Thirty minutes into the infusion, the nurse observes that the client's Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) drops from 15 to 9, and the right pupil is 6 mm and sluggish compared to the left pupil at 3 mm. What is the most appropriate immediate action?

A. Administer a STAT dose of intravenous protamine sulfate to reverse the pharmacological effects.
B. Decrease the alteplase infusion rate by half and urgently notify the intensive care provider.
C. Immediately place the client in a modified Trendelenburg position to improve cerebral perfusion.
D. Stop the alteplase infusion immediately and prepare to administer cryoprecipitate or fresh frozen plasma.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Stop the alteplase infusion immediately and prepare to administer cryoprecipitate or fresh frozen plasma.

**Rationale:** 
Systemic alteplase (tPA) is a potent thrombolytic used in massive PE with hemodynamic instability. The most devastating complication of tPA is intracranial hemorrhage (ICH). A sudden drop in GCS and anisocoria (unequal pupils) are classic signs of increased intracranial pressure secondary to ICH. The absolute priority is to immediately discontinue the tPA infusion. Subsequent management involves ordering a STAT non-contrast CT of the head and preparing to administer blood products such as cryoprecipitate, fresh frozen plasma, or antifibrinolytics (like tranexamic acid) to restore clotting factors. Protamine sulfate (Option A) is the reversal agent for heparin, not tPA. Decreasing the rate (Option B) is inappropriate; a fatal bleed requires immediate cessation of the drug. Placing the client in Trendelenburg (Option C) would dangerously increase intracranial pressure and worsen the bleeding.

</details>

---

### Question 295
**Lens: Complex Delegation**

The charge nurse is managing an intensive care unit where multiple clients are experiencing severe respiratory compromise. When delegating tasks to a highly experienced licensed practical nurse (LPN/LVN), which task is UNSAFE and outside the LPN/LVN's scope of practice in this high-acuity environment?

A. Performing routine sterile tracheostomy care for a stable client who requires long-term mechanical ventilation.
B. Administering a scheduled dose of subcutaneous enoxaparin to a client recovering from a pulmonary embolism.
C. Troubleshooting a sudden high peak inspiratory pressure (PIP) alarm for a hemodynamically unstable client with ARDS.
D. Emptying and recording the output from a chest tube drainage system for a client with a resolving tension pneumothorax.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Troubleshooting a sudden high peak inspiratory pressure (PIP) alarm for a hemodynamically unstable client with ARDS.

**Rationale:** 
Troubleshooting a mechanical ventilator alarm in an unstable client with ARDS requires advanced clinical assessment, critical thinking, and the ability to intervene immediately in a life-threatening crisis (e.g., assessing for a tension pneumothorax, severe bronchospasm, or mucous plugging). Assessment, evaluation, and management of unstable clients fall strictly under the scope of practice of the Registered Nurse (RN) and cannot be delegated. Performing routine tracheostomy care (Option A), administering subcutaneous medications (Option B), and emptying/recording drainage (Option D) are standard procedures with predictable outcomes that can safely be delegated to a competent LPN/LVN, even in critical care settings, provided the clients are relatively stable regarding those specific tasks.

</details>
### Question 296
**LENS:** EMERGENCY RESCUE

A client in the Intensive Care Unit is receiving positive pressure mechanical ventilation for acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS). Suddenly, the ventilator's high-pressure alarm sounds, and the client’s blood pressure plummets from 115/75 mm Hg to 70/40 mm Hg. The nurse notes absent breath sounds on the right side, severe jugular venous distention, and the trachea is deviated to the left. What is the immediate, life-saving rescue action the nurse must anticipate?

A. Obtain a STAT portable chest X-ray to confirm the diagnosis.
B. Prepare the client for an immediate needle thoracostomy.
C. Disconnect the ventilator and ventilate manually with a bag-valve-mask.
D. Administer a rapid 1-liter bolus of 0.9% Normal Saline.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Prepare the client for an immediate needle thoracostomy.

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (sudden hypotension, absent breath sounds on the affected side, high peak airway pressures, jugular venous distention, and tracheal deviation away from the affected side). Positive pressure ventilation increases the risk of barotrauma, which can easily cause a tension pneumothorax. This is an absolute medical emergency causing obstructive shock. The immediate life-saving intervention is a needle thoracostomy (decompression), usually at the 2nd intercostal space in the midclavicular line or 4th/5th intercostal space in the anterior axillary line, followed by chest tube insertion. Waiting for a chest X-ray (Option A) delays life-saving treatment and can lead to cardiac arrest. Disconnecting the ventilator (Option C) or giving fluids (Option D) does not fix the underlying obstructive intrathoracic pressure that is preventing venous return and cardiac output.

</details>

---

### Question 297
**LENS:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS

A client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is intubated. The ventilator settings are: FiO2 80%, PEEP 15 cm H2O, and Tidal Volume 6 mL/kg. The most recent arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals: pH 7.28, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, PaO2 55 mm Hg, and HCO3 26 mEq/L. Hemodynamic monitoring indicates a central venous pressure (CVP) of 15 mm Hg (normal 2-8 mm Hg) and a blood pressure of 86/52 mm Hg. How should the nurse accurately interpret this clinical data?

A. The client is hyperventilating and requires a decrease in the ventilator's respiratory rate.
B. The client has developed a pulmonary embolism and requires an immediate V/Q scan.
C. The client is tolerating permissive hypercapnia, but immediate fluid resuscitation is needed.
D. The client is exhibiting uncompensated respiratory acidosis and hemodynamic compromise from high PEEP.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. The client is exhibiting uncompensated respiratory acidosis and hemodynamic compromise from high PEEP.

**Rationale:** The ABG indicates uncompensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, normal HCO3) combined with refractory hypoxemia (PaO2 55 mm Hg despite 80% FiO2), which is hallmark for severe ARDS. The high PEEP (15 cm H2O) required to recruit alveoli significantly increases intrathoracic pressure. This elevated intrathoracic pressure compresses the vena cava, reducing venous return to the right heart, which elevates the CVP (15 mm Hg) and decreases overall cardiac output, leading to hypotension (86/52 mm Hg). Option A is incorrect because the client is retaining CO2 (hypoventilating or experiencing severe dead-space ventilation), not hyperventilating. Option B is unlikely the primary cause given the ARDS and high PEEP context. Option C is incorrect because while permissive hypercapnia is a strategy in ARDS to prevent barotrauma, administering fluids is not the primary solution for PEEP-induced hypotension when CVP is already significantly elevated; vasopressors or adjusting PEEP would be more appropriate.

</details>

---

### Question 298
**LENS:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY

A client presents to the emergency department with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) resulting in obstructive shock, severe right ventricular dysfunction, and impending hemodynamic collapse. The provider orders a systemic alteplase (tPA) infusion. While preparing this high-alert medication, the nurse reviews the client’s medical history. Which finding requires the nurse to withhold the alteplase and contact the provider immediately?

A. Subcutaneous administration of enoxaparin 12 hours ago.
B. A current blood pressure of 155/90 mm Hg.
C. An ischemic stroke that occurred 6 weeks ago.
D. A reported penicillin allergy causing anaphylaxis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. An ischemic stroke that occurred 6 weeks ago.

**Rationale:** Systemic thrombolytic therapy (alteplase) carries a profound risk of life-threatening hemorrhage. An ischemic stroke within the last 3 months is an *absolute contraindication* for the administration of thrombolytics because the ischemic brain tissue is highly susceptible to hemorrhagic conversion. Administering tPA to this client could cause a fatal intracranial hemorrhage. Option A is incorrect; while the client received prophylactic or therapeutic enoxaparin recently, systemic tPA can still be administered in massive PE with hemodynamic collapse, though coagulation status is closely monitored. Option B is incorrect; a blood pressure of 155/90 mm Hg is not an absolute contraindication (severe uncontrolled hypertension, typically >185/110 mm Hg, is a contraindication). Option D is an allergy to a completely unrelated medication class and does not affect tPA administration.

</details>

---

### Question 299
**LENS:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION

A client is admitted to the progressive care unit with status asthmaticus. Despite receiving continuous albuterol nebulization, intravenous methylprednisolone, and magnesium sulfate, the client remains in severe respiratory distress with accessory muscle use. Which of the following assessment findings most strongly indicates to the nurse that the severe respiratory failure is directly precipitating a secondary cardiovascular compromise?

A. A drop in systolic blood pressure of 18 mm Hg during inspiration.
B. A sudden decrease in wheezing with a persistent respiratory rate of 34 breaths/min.
C. An increase in heart rate from 110 to 130 beats per minute.
D. Fine bilateral tremors in the upper extremities.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. A drop in systolic blood pressure of 18 mm Hg during inspiration.

**Rationale:** A drop in systolic blood pressure of greater than 10 mm Hg during inspiration is known as *pulsus paradoxus*. In status asthmaticus, severe air trapping leads to hyperinflation of the lungs. This creates extreme negative intrathoracic pressure during inspiration, which increases venous return to the right ventricle, bowing the intraventricular septum into the left ventricle. This severely decreases left ventricular end-diastolic volume, stroke volume, and cardiac output, demonstrating cardiovascular compromise directly caused by the respiratory mechanics. Option B indicates a "silent chest," which is a sign of complete airway obstruction and impending respiratory arrest, but it does not represent cardiovascular integration. Options C and D are expected side effects of continuous albuterol administration (a beta-2 agonist with some beta-1 crossover). 

</details>

---

### Question 300
**LENS:** RISK OF DETERIORATION

A client with severe acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) is undergoing prone positioning therapy while receiving mechanical ventilation. The nurse performs a comprehensive assessment after the client has been prone for 8 hours. Which of the following findings represents the earliest, most critical sign of a "failure to rescue" indicating the prone therapy is failing?

A. The client exhibits non-pitting facial and periorbital edema.
B. Moderate thick white secretions are suctioned from the endotracheal tube.
C. The client's ventilator requires a positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) of 12 cm H2O.
D. The PaO2/FiO2 ratio drops from 145 to 88 over a 4-hour period.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. The PaO2/FiO2 ratio drops from 145 to 88 over a 4-hour period.

**Rationale:** The PaO2/FiO2 (P/F) ratio is the primary objective metric used to classify the severity of ARDS and evaluate oxygenation status. A normal P/F ratio is >400. A ratio falling from 145 (moderate ARDS) to 88 (severe ARDS, defined as <100) indicates worsening intrapulmonary shunting and refractory hypoxemia despite prone positioning. This is a critical sign of deterioration and a potential "failure to rescue" requiring immediate intervention (e.g., neuromuscular blockade, ECMO consideration). Option A is an expected, dependent physiological response to being in the prone position for prolonged periods and is not life-threatening. Option B is an expected finding, as prone positioning helps mobilize secretions; the nurse simply needs to clear the airway. Option C is an expected and standard PEEP setting for a client with severe ARDS.

</details>
### Question 301
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism and hemodynamic collapse is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of alteplase (tPA). The nurse is closely monitoring the client's physiological response to the thrombolytic therapy. Which assessment finding requires the *most* immediate action by the nurse?

A) Oozing of dark blood around the client's peripheral intravenous catheter site.
B) New-onset microscopic hematuria noted in the urinary catheter drainage system.
C) Sudden onset of a severe, localized headache and ipsilateral pupillary sluggishness.
D) An increase in sinus tachycardia from 110 to 125 beats per minute.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Sudden onset of a severe, localized headache and ipsilateral pupillary sluggishness.

**Lens:** High-Alert Pharmacology (#6)

**Rationale:** Hemorrhagic stroke is the most lethal and severe complication of systemic thrombolytic therapy (such as alteplase). A sudden, severe headache accompanied by neurological changes (e.g., sluggish pupils, altered level of consciousness) strongly indicates an acute intracranial hemorrhage. The infusion must be stopped instantly, and an emergency CT scan of the head should be obtained. Oozing at IV sites (Option A) and microscopic hematuria (Option B) are expected, non-life-threatening side effects of tPA that do not warrant stopping the life-saving infusion; bleeding at superficial sites can be managed with direct pressure. An increase in tachycardia (Option D) is a compensatory response to shock or anxiety, and while it requires monitoring, it does not mandate immediate cessation of the tPA drip like a suspected brain bleed does.
</details>

---

### Question 302
A client is admitted to the emergency department with status asthmaticus. Despite receiving continuous albuterol nebulizers, intravenous methylprednisolone, and magnesium sulfate over the last hour, the client remains visibly distressed with severe dyspnea. Which subtle assessment finding indicates an impending respiratory arrest and a high risk of "failure to rescue"?

A) The client's most recent arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals a PaCO2 of 42 mm Hg (5.6 kPa).
B) The client exhibits prominent, continuous use of the sternocleidomastoid muscles.
C) High-pitched expiratory wheezes are auscultated bilaterally over the lung bases.
D) The client's pulse oximetry reading fluctuates between 90% and 92% on high-flow oxygen.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The client's most recent arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals a PaCO2 of 42 mm Hg (5.6 kPa).

**Lens:** Risk of Deterioration (#4)

**Rationale:** During the early stages of a severe asthma attack, patients hyperventilate to compensate for hypoxemia, which reliably "blows off" carbon dioxide and results in a low PaCO2 (respiratory alkalosis). If the PaCO2 level normalizes (35–45 mm Hg) or rises during a prolonged attack, it is an ominous, advanced sign of extreme respiratory muscle fatigue. A "normal" PaCO2 in status asthmaticus indicates the client can no longer maintain the work of breathing and is rapidly progressing toward hypercapnic respiratory failure and arrest. Immediate preparation for mechanical ventilation is required. Use of accessory muscles (Option B), expiratory wheezing (Option C), and borderline hypoxia (Option D) are expected findings in acute asthma; the normalization of PaCO2 is the uniquely critical sign of deterioration.
</details>

---

### Question 303
A critical care nurse is managing a client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) who is mechanically ventilated. The ventilator is set to a fraction of inspired oxygen (FiO2) of 0.80 and positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) of 18 cm H2O. Over the last hour, the client’s blood pressure drops to 82/45 mm Hg, urine output falls to 10 mL/hr, and extremities are cool to the touch. What is the primary pathophysiological mechanism responsible for this multi-system deterioration?

A) Systemic microemboli from right ventricular failure occluding the renal and peripheral arteries.
B) Increased intrathoracic pressure from high PEEP drastically reducing venous return and cardiac output.
C) Severe alveolar hypoxemia directly causing acute tubular necrosis and subsequent distributive shock.
D) Alveolar overdistension leading to vagal nerve stimulation and profound neurogenic hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Increased intrathoracic pressure from high PEEP drastically reducing venous return and cardiac output.

**Lens:** Multi-System Integration (#5)

**Rationale:** High levels of PEEP (typically >15 cm H2O), which are often required to keep alveoli open in severe ARDS, cause a significant increase in intrathoracic pressure. This elevated pressure compresses the superior and inferior vena cava, drastically reducing venous return (preload) to the right side of the heart. The drop in preload causes a profound decrease in overall cardiac output, leading to systemic hypotension, diminished peripheral perfusion (cool extremities), and renal hypoperfusion (oliguria). Understanding how respiratory interventions mechanically compromise the cardiovascular and renal systems is essential for advanced multi-system management.
</details>

---

### Question 304
The nurse is caring for a mechanically ventilated client recovering from a pulmonary contusion. Suddenly, the high-pressure ventilator alarm sounds, and the client develops extreme respiratory distress. Assessment reveals marked tracheal deviation to the left, completely absent breath sounds on the right hemithorax, and a precipitous drop in blood pressure to 70/40 mm Hg. The physician is en route. Which emergency rescue action should the nurse facilitate immediately?

A) Disconnecting the client from the ventilator and delivering rescue breaths with a bag-valve-mask.
B) Silencing the ventilator alarms, suctioning the airway, and increasing the FiO2 to 1.0.
C) Administering a rapid intravenous fluid bolus to support the plummeting blood pressure.
D) Preparing equipment for an immediate needle thoracostomy in the right anterior chest.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Preparing equipment for an immediate needle thoracostomy in the right anterior chest.

**Lens:** Emergency Rescue (#2)

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (tracheal deviation away from the affected side, absent breath sounds, profound hypotension, and high peak airway pressures on the ventilator). This is an immediate life-threatening emergency caused by trapped air compressing the heart and great vessels. The definitive rescue action prior to chest tube insertion is a needle thoracostomy (needle decompression), typically performed in the 2nd intercostal space at the midclavicular line or 5th intercostal space at the midaxillary line on the affected side. Disconnecting the vent to bag the patient (Option A) can actually force more air into the pleural space, worsening the tension. Suctioning (Option B) delays life-saving treatment. Fluid administration (Option C) does not resolve the mechanical compression of the heart preventing cardiac output.
</details>

---

### Question 305
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism is receiving a continuous intravenous heparin infusion. The client is tachycardic, diaphoretic, and severely dyspneic. The healthcare team includes a Registered Nurse (RN), a Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN), and an unlicensed assistive personnel (UAP). Which task is UNSAFE for the RN to delegate to the LPN in this unstable environment?

A) Drawing a STAT peripheral blood sample for a partial thromboplastin time (PTT).
B) Adjusting the rate of the continuous intravenous heparin infusion based on the newly resulted PTT.
C) Assisting the client with repositioning in bed to optimize respiratory effort and comfort.
D) Obtaining a 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) to evaluate the new-onset tachycardia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Adjusting the rate of the continuous intravenous heparin infusion based on the newly resulted PTT.

**Lens:** Complex Delegation (#9)

**Rationale:** Titrating high-alert intravenous continuous medications (such as heparin, insulin, or vasopressors) requires advanced clinical judgment, interpretation of lab values, and assessment of an unstable patient. This falls strictly within the scope of practice of the Registered Nurse (RN) and is inherently unsafe and legally out of scope to delegate to a Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN/LVN) or a UAP. Depending on facility policy and state practice acts, an LPN may draw peripheral blood (Option A) and perform an ECG (Option D). Assisting with repositioning (Option C) is safe to delegate to an LPN or UAP.
</details>
### Question 306
The intensive care nurse receives handoff report for four clients experiencing acute respiratory compromise. Which client should the nurse assess **FIRST**?

A. A client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) on mechanical ventilation with a PaO2 of 64 mmHg while on 60% FiO2.
B. A client with a massive pulmonary embolism receiving a heparin infusion, who has an aPTT of 110 seconds and reports mild bleeding from the gums.
C. A client admitted for severe status asthmaticus whose wheezing has suddenly ceased despite an increase in suprasternal retractions.
D. A client with a right-sided chest tube for a spontaneous pneumothorax who exhibits intermittent bubbling in the water-seal chamber.

<details>
<summary>Correct Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. A client admitted for severe status asthmaticus whose wheezing has suddenly ceased despite an increase in suprasternal retractions.

**Rationale:** 
This is a **Prioritization** question. The sudden cessation of wheezing in a client with status asthmaticus who is still exhibiting increased work of breathing (retractions) indicates the development of a "silent chest." This is an ominous clinical sign demonstrating that airflow is so severely restricted by bronchospasm and mucosal edema that wheezing can no longer be produced. This client is in impending respiratory arrest and requires immediate life-saving intervention (e.g., preparation for intubation and mechanical ventilation). 

*Option A* is incorrect because a PaO2 of 64 mmHg on 60% FiO2 yields a PaO2/FiO2 ratio of approximately 106, which is an expected finding in a client being managed for ARDS (permissive hypoxemia target is often a PaO2 of 55-80 mmHg). 
*Option B* is incorrect because while an aPTT of 110 seconds is critically high and requires the nurse to pause the infusion and adjust the dose, mild gum bleeding is not immediately life-threatening compared to an airway collapse. 
*Option D* is incorrect because intermittent bubbling in the water-seal chamber corresponding with respirations is an expected, normal finding indicating that air from the pleural space is actively draining. 
</details>

---

### Question 307
A client receiving high-frequency positive pressure ventilation for severe ARDS suddenly becomes acutely agitated. The ventilator's high-pressure alarm sounds continuously. The client's blood pressure drops from 112/74 mmHg to 68/38 mmHg, heart rate spikes to 148 bpm, and SpO2 rapidly drops to 76%. The nurse notes that the trachea is deviated to the left. Based on this multi-system presentation, what is the priority nursing intervention?

A. Prepare for an immediate right-sided needle thoracostomy.
B. Initiate a prescribed norepinephrine infusion to support mean arterial pressure.
C. Disconnect the ventilator and manually ventilate using a bag-valve-mask at 15 L/min.
D. Administer a 1,000 mL IV fluid bolus of 0.9% Normal Saline to increase right ventricular preload.

<details>
<summary>Correct Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Prepare for an immediate right-sided needle thoracostomy.

**Rationale:** 
This is a **Multi-System Integration** question. The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax secondary to barotrauma (a known complication of high positive end-expiratory pressure [PEEP] used in ARDS). The trapped air in the right pleural space increases intrathoracic pressure, collapsing the vena cava and shifting the mediastinum (tracheal deviation to the left). This causes obstructive shock, completely halting venous return to the heart and precipitating hemodynamic collapse. The only definitive, life-saving intervention to restore both respiratory function and cardiac output is to immediately decompress the chest via needle thoracostomy, followed by chest tube insertion. 

*Option B* is incorrect because vasopressors will not fix the underlying mechanical obstruction blocking venous return; cardiac output will remain critically low until the pressure is relieved. 
*Option C* is incorrect because manually bagging the client will continue to force air into the pleural defect, exacerbating the tension pneumothorax. 
*Option D* is incorrect because while fluid resuscitation is sometimes used temporarily in obstructive shock, it does not correct the lethal intrathoracic pressure and delays definitive, life-saving decompression.
</details>

---

### Question 308
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism and severe hemodynamic collapse (blood pressure 70/40 mmHg) is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of alteplase (tPA). Thirty minutes into the infusion, the client suddenly complains of a severe, localized headache and the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) drops from 15 to 11. What is the nurse's priority action?

A. Elevate the head of the bed to 45 degrees, assess pupillary reaction, and notify the provider.
B. Immediately stop the alteplase infusion and prepare the client for a STAT head CT.
C. Administer prescribed IV protamine sulfate to rapidly reverse the thrombolytic agent.
D. Decrease the alteplase infusion rate by half to limit systemic toxicity while maintaining clot lysis.

<details>
<summary>Correct Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Immediately stop the alteplase infusion and prepare the client for a STAT head CT.

**Rationale:** 
This is a **High-Alert Pharmacology** question. Alteplase (tPA) is a potent thrombolytic used in massive pulmonary embolisms presenting with obstructive shock. The most devastating complication of tPA is intracranial hemorrhage (ICH). A sudden, severe headache accompanied by a precipitous drop in neurological status (GCS dropping from 15 to 11) is the classic presentation of an acute brain bleed. The absolute priority is to stop the infusion immediately to prevent further bleeding, followed by urgent neuroimaging (STAT CT without contrast) to confirm the hemorrhage.

*Option A* is incorrect because while neurological assessment is important, it delays the critical action of stopping the drug that is actively causing the life-threatening bleed.
*Option C* is incorrect because protamine sulfate is the antidote for heparin, not alteplase. (Antidotes for tPA include aminocaproic acid or tranexamic acid, alongside cryoprecipitate).
*Option D* is incorrect because a thrombolytic infusion must be halted entirely, never simply decreased, when a lethal bleeding complication is suspected.
</details>

---

### Question 309
A client with sepsis-induced Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is on mechanical ventilation. Current settings: Assist-Control (AC), Rate 18, Tidal Volume (Vt) 380 mL, FiO2 80%, PEEP 16 cm H2O. An arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals: pH 7.28, PaCO2 56 mmHg, PaO2 48 mmHg, HCO3 25 mEq/L. Which analysis of this clinical data is most accurate?

A. The client's PaO2/FiO2 ratio indicates mild ARDS, allowing for immediate weaning of the FiO2.
B. The client is experiencing uncompensated metabolic acidosis requiring a sodium bicarbonate push.
C. The ABG reveals compensated respiratory acidosis, and the PEEP should be decreased to lower intrathoracic pressure.
D. The client is exhibiting refractory hypoxemia with a severely low PaO2/FiO2 ratio, indicating the need for prone positioning or ECMO.

<details>
<summary>Correct Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. The client is exhibiting refractory hypoxemia with a severely low PaO2/FiO2 ratio, indicating the need for prone positioning or ECMO.

**Rationale:** 
This is an **Advanced Data Analysis** question. Refractory hypoxemia (low PaO2 despite high levels of oxygen delivery and PEEP) is the hallmark pathophysiological mechanism of ARDS. To calculate the severity, the nurse evaluates the PaO2/FiO2 (P/F) ratio. Here, the PaO2 is 48 and the FiO2 is 0.80 (80%). The calculation is 48 / 0.80 = 60. A P/F ratio less than 100 indicates *severe* ARDS. Because conventional mechanical ventilation is failing to oxygenate the client, advanced rescue therapies such as prone positioning, neuromuscular blockade, or Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation (ECMO) are clinically indicated.

*Option A* is incorrect because a P/F ratio of 60 reflects severe ARDS, not mild (which would be 200-300).
*Option B* is incorrect because the ABG demonstrates uncompensated *respiratory* acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, normal HCO3), not metabolic. 
*Option C* is incorrect because the ABG is uncompensated, and decreasing the PEEP in severe ARDS would cause catastrophic alveolar de-recruitment and worsen the hypoxemia.
</details>

---

### Question 310
A client is admitted to the progressive care unit with status asthmaticus and is receiving continuous albuterol nebulization, IV methylprednisolone, and magnesium sulfate. Upon admission, the client was highly agitated, tachypneic at 36 breaths/min, with an ABG showing: pH 7.54, PaCO2 26 mmHg, PaO2 68 mmHg. Two hours later, the client appears lethargic, respirations are shallow at 14 breaths/min, and a repeat ABG shows: pH 7.36, PaCO2 42 mmHg, PaO2 60 mmHg. What is the nurse's priority interpretation of these findings?

A. The client's respiratory drive is stabilizing, indicating a successful therapeutic response to the magnesium sulfate.
B. The rising PaCO2 level is a critical indicator of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending failure to rescue.
C. The downward shift in pH demonstrates the development of lactic acidosis secondary to continuous beta-agonist use.
D. The client is experiencing central nervous system depression from oxygen toxicity, requiring immediate FiO2 reduction.

<details>
<summary>Correct Answer & Rationale</summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. The rising PaCO2 level is a critical indicator of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending failure to rescue.

**Rationale:** 
This is a **Risk of Deterioration** question. During an acute asthma attack, clients initially hyperventilate in an effort to overcome airway resistance, blowing off large amounts of carbon dioxide (resulting in a low PaCO2 and respiratory alkalosis). If a subsequent ABG shows a "normalizing" or rising PaCO2 (such as 42 mmHg) while the client's clinical appearance worsens (becoming lethargic with shallow breathing), it is an ominous, life-threatening sign. It indicates that the client has exhausted their respiratory muscles and can no longer maintain the extreme work of breathing. This "pseudonormalization" of PaCO2 is the earliest warning sign of impending hypercapnic respiratory failure and the immediate need for intubation.

*Option A* is incorrect because the client is deteriorating, not stabilizing. The lethargy and shallow breathing are signs of collapse, not therapeutic success.
*Option C* is incorrect because the ABG changes are driven by the retention of CO2 (a respiratory parameter), not a primary metabolic lactic acidosis.
*Option D* is incorrect because the lethargy is caused by hypercapnia and respiratory failure, not oxygen toxicity (which takes prolonged exposure to extremely high FiO2 to develop, and the client remains hypoxemic with a PaO2 of 60 mmHg).
</details>
### Question 311
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis**

A client admitted to the emergency department with status asthmaticus has been receiving continuous nebulized albuterol and intravenous methylprednisolone for 2 hours. The client is diaphoretic, sits leaning forward, and has a respiratory rate of 36/min. The nurse evaluates the client's latest arterial blood gas (ABG) results. Which ABG result requires the nurse to *immediately* prepare the client for endotracheal intubation?

A) pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mm Hg, PaO2 75 mm Hg
B) pH 7.36, PaCO2 42 mm Hg, PaO2 65 mm Hg
C) pH 7.52, PaCO2 30 mm Hg, PaO2 88 mm Hg
D) pH 7.45, PaCO2 33 mm Hg, PaO2 72 mm Hg

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) pH 7.36, PaCO2 42 mm Hg, PaO2 65 mm Hg

**Rationale:**
In the early stages of an acute asthma exacerbation, clients hyperventilate in an attempt to pull in more oxygen, which blows off carbon dioxide and typically results in respiratory alkalosis (a low PaCO2, as seen in options A, C, and D). If a client who is severely tachypneic and visibly working hard to breathe presents with a "normal" PaCO2 (35-45 mm Hg), this is an ominous sign known as pseudo-normalization. It indicates profound diaphragmatic muscle fatigue; the client no longer has the energy to maintain the high respiratory drive needed to clear CO2. Without immediate intervention (endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation), this will rapidly progress to hypercapnic respiratory failure, respiratory acidosis, and cardiac arrest.

</details>

---

### Question 312
**Lens: Emergency Rescue**

An ICU nurse is caring for a client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) who is heavily sedated and receiving mechanical ventilation with a high Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) of 18 cm H2O. Suddenly, the ventilator's high-pressure alarm sounds continuously. The client's blood pressure drops from 110/70 mm Hg to 68/38 mm Hg, the heart rate spikes to 148 bpm, and the nurse notes absent breath sounds on the right side with the trachea deviated to the left. What is the nurse's *immediate* life-saving action?

A) Disconnect the client from the ventilator and manually ventilate with a bag-valve-mask at 100% FiO2.
B) Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of 500 mL 0.9% normal saline to restore perfusion.
C) Instruct the respiratory therapist to immediately decrease the PEEP setting to 5 cm H2O.
D) Prepare the client for an emergent right-sided needle thoracostomy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Prepare the client for an emergent right-sided needle thoracostomy.

**Rationale:**
The client is exhibiting the classic triad of a tension pneumothorax (absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, and hemodynamic collapse/obstructive shock). This is a known, severe complication of high PEEP (barotrauma) in ARDS clients. The trapped air in the pleural space applies immense pressure on the mediastinum, collapsing the superior and inferior vena cava, which abruptly halts venous return to the heart and causes severe hypotension. The *immediate* life-saving rescue action is decompression of the pleural space via an emergent needle thoracostomy, followed by chest tube insertion. Manual ventilation (Option A) could worsen the tension. Fluids (Option B) will not fix the mechanical obstruction of the heart. Decreasing PEEP (Option C) is too late to reverse the already collapsed lung and mediastinal shift.

</details>

---

### Question 313
**Lens: Multi-System Integration**

A client recovering from a pelvic fracture suddenly develops severe dyspnea, pleuritic chest pain, and a sense of impending doom. A computed tomography pulmonary angiography (CTPA) confirms a massive saddle pulmonary embolism. The client’s blood pressure is 72/40 mm Hg, and the central venous pressure (CVP) reading has acutely spiked to 18 mm Hg. Which underlying pathophysiological mechanism best explains the cascade causing this client's hemodynamic collapse?

A) Massive vasodilation from the inflammatory cascade pooling blood in the peripheral vascular bed.
B) Extravasation of fluid into the alveolar spaces causing an absolute hypovolemic state.
C) Sudden increase in pulmonary vascular resistance leading to right ventricular failure and decreased left ventricular preload.
D) Sudden loss of sympathetic vascular tone due to severe hypoxia and hypercapnia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Sudden increase in pulmonary vascular resistance leading to right ventricular failure and decreased left ventricular preload.

**Rationale:**
A massive pulmonary embolism causes mechanical obstruction of the pulmonary arterial bed, which abruptly and drastically increases pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR). The right ventricle, unaccustomed to pumping against such high pressure, acutely dilates and fails (acute cor pulmonale). This is evidenced by the acute spike in CVP (normal is 2-8 mm Hg), indicating backup of blood into the venous system. Because blood cannot effectively pass through the lungs to reach the left side of the heart, left ventricular preload critically drops. This results in a plummeting cardiac output and severe obstructive shock (blood pressure of 72/40 mm Hg). Option A describes distributive/septic shock. Option B describes ARDS/hypovolemia. Option D describes neurogenic shock.

</details>

---

### Question 314
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology**

A client with severe ARDS is intubated and receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of the neuromuscular blocking agent (NMBA) cisatracurium to prevent ventilator dyssynchrony. The nurse is titrating the infusion using a peripheral nerve stimulator (Train-of-Four monitoring) on the ulnar nerve. Which assessment finding requires the nurse to *decrease* the infusion rate of the high-alert medication?

A) The client demonstrates spontaneous respiratory efforts overriding the ventilator settings.
B) The peripheral nerve stimulator yields zero twitches out of four sequential stimuli.
C) The peripheral nerve stimulator yields two twitches out of four sequential stimuli.
D) The continuous Bispectral Index (BIS) monitor displays a value of 85.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) The peripheral nerve stimulator yields zero twitches out of four sequential stimuli.

**Rationale:**
When administering a continuous continuous neuromuscular blockade (such as cisatracurium), the nurse must carefully titrate the drip to avoid prolonged paralysis, muscle atrophy, and prolonged mechanical ventilation. Train-of-Four (TOF) monitoring delivers four electrical impulses to a nerve (usually ulnar or facial). The standard therapeutic goal for ARDS management is typically 1 to 2 twitches out of 4 (representing approximately 80-90% receptor blockade). Zero twitches indicate 100% blockade (over-paralysis); the nurse must decrease the infusion rate to prevent toxicity and long-term neuromuscular complications. Spontaneous breathing (Option A) indicates under-paralysis, requiring an increase in the rate. Two twitches (Option C) is the target goal, so the rate should be maintained. A BIS monitor value of 85 (Option D) indicates the client is awake and aware (target for deep sedation is usually 40-60); NMBAs do not provide sedation or analgesia, so this finding requires an increase in *sedation*, not an adjustment to the NMBA drip.

</details>

---

### Question 315
**Lens: Complication Prevention**

The critical care nurse is managing a client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) on mechanical ventilation. To prevent secondary lung complications, the provider has prescribed a lung-protective ventilation strategy utilizing low tidal volumes. The client's latest arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals a pH of 7.31, a PaCO2 of 56 mm Hg, and a PaO2 of 68 mm Hg. Which action is the most appropriate for the nurse to take?

A) Document the findings as permissive hypercapnia and maintain the current ventilator settings.
B) Disconnect the client from the ventilator and manually ventilate to blow off excess carbon dioxide.
C) Request a provider order to increase the tidal volume to 10 mL/kg of the client's ideal body weight.
D) Administer intravenous sodium bicarbonate to promptly correct the respiratory acidosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Document the findings as permissive hypercapnia and maintain the current ventilator settings.

**Rationale:**
In severe ARDS, the lungs become stiff and non-compliant ("baby lungs"). Traditional, high tidal volumes can cause severe barotrauma (injury from high pressure) and volutrauma (injury from overdistension), worsening the inflammatory cascade. Therefore, advanced lung-protective strategies strictly utilize low tidal volumes (e.g., 4-6 mL/kg of ideal body weight). A known, expected consequence of low tidal volume ventilation is the retention of carbon dioxide, resulting in mild to moderate respiratory acidosis. This is called *permissive hypercapnia*. As long as the pH remains above a certain threshold (typically >7.20 to 7.25), this acid-base imbalance is tolerated to protect the lungs from catastrophic mechanical injury. Increasing tidal volumes (Option C) would directly cause the volutrauma the strategy is trying to prevent. Sodium bicarbonate (Option D) is generally not indicated for mild respiratory acidosis and can paradoxically worsen intracellular acidosis.

</details>
### Question 316
A mechanically ventilated client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) suddenly deteriorates. The ventilator is alarming "high pressure," oxygen saturation drops to 78%, heart rate increases to 145 bpm, and blood pressure drops to 72/40 mmHg. The nurse notes absent breath sounds on the right side and the trachea shifted to the left. What is the immediate life-saving nursing action?

A. Remove the client from the ventilator and manually ventilate using a bag-valve-mask at 15 L/min oxygen.
B. Increase the positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) on the ventilator to stent the collapsing alveoli.
C. Gather a 14-gauge IV catheter and prep the 2nd intercostal space at the midclavicular line for decompression.
D. Administer a rapid 1-liter IV fluid bolus of 0.9% Normal Saline to correct the severe hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Lens:** EMERGENCY RESCUE

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (sudden hemodynamic collapse, high peak inspiratory pressures, absent breath sounds on one side, and tracheal deviation away from the affected side). This is a known complication of mechanical ventilation, especially in ARDS where high airway pressures are used. The immediate life-saving intervention is an emergent needle thoracostomy (decompression) to release the trapped air and restore venous return to the heart. Manual ventilation (Option A) or increasing PEEP (Option B) will worsen the tension pneumothorax by forcing more air into the pleural space. While fluids (Option D) may be ordered eventually, they do not fix the mechanical obstruction of blood flow caused by the intrathoracic pressure; decompression must happen first.

</details>

---

### Question 317
A client in status asthmaticus is receiving continuous nebulized albuterol, IV methylprednisolone, and magnesium sulfate. Over the last hour, the client has become drowsy and less responsive. The respiratory therapist draws an arterial blood gas (ABG) on room air. Which ABG result indicates an impending respiratory arrest requiring immediate intubation?

A. pH 7.26, PaCO2 68 mmHg, PaO2 52 mmHg, HCO3 26 mEq/L
B. pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mmHg, PaO2 70 mmHg, HCO3 22 mEq/L
C. pH 7.35, PaCO2 40 mmHg, PaO2 65 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L
D. pH 7.41, PaCO2 35 mmHg, PaO2 80 mmHg, HCO3 23 mEq/L

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS

**Rationale:** In an acute asthma exacerbation, clients initially hyperventilate, leading to respiratory alkalosis (Option B). As the client fatigues, their breathing slows, and their PaCO2 begins to rise back to "normal" (Option C), which is a deceptive and dangerous transition phase indicating exhaustion. An ABG showing severe respiratory acidosis with hypercapnia (high PaCO2) and hypoxemia (Option A) in the setting of increasing lethargy indicates impending respiratory failure and arrest. The client's respiratory muscles have failed, and emergent endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation are required. 

</details>

---

### Question 318
A client is diagnosed with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) resulting in right ventricular failure and hemodynamic collapse. The physician orders a continuous infusion of Alteplase (tPA). The nurse reviews the client’s medical history. Which concurrent clinical finding requires the nurse to immediately withhold the tPA infusion and contact the physician?

A. The client is currently receiving a continuous IV unfractionated heparin infusion.
B. The client sustained a closed-head concussion with a brief loss of consciousness 14 days ago.
C. The client’s arterial blood gas reveals a PaO2 of 55 mmHg on a 100% non-rebreather mask.
D. The client has an active prescription for daily 81 mg enteric-coated aspirin.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Lens:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY

**Rationale:** Alteplase (tPA) is a potent thrombolytic agent used in life-threatening, massive pulmonary embolisms to dissolve the clot and restore pulmonary perfusion. However, it carries a severe risk of lethal hemorrhage. Absolute contraindications for thrombolytic therapy include recent head trauma or traumatic brain injury within the past 3 months. Administering tPA to a client with a recent concussion (Option B) risks a fatal intracranial hemorrhage. Hypoxemia (Option C) is the indication for the medication, not a contraindication. Heparin (Option A) and aspirin (Option D) increase bleeding risk, but in the setting of massive PE with hemodynamic collapse, a recent head injury is the absolute contraindication that stops the administration.

</details>

---

### Question 319
A client is mechanically ventilated for severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS). Due to refractory hypoxemia, the provider increases the positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) to 18 cm H2O. Which subtle assessment finding is the earliest indicator of a life-threatening complication related to these ventilator settings?

A. Diffuse fine crackles auscultated bilaterally in the lower lung fields.
B. Intermittent high-pressure ventilator alarms triggering when the client coughs.
C. A gradual decrease in the fraction of inspired oxygen (FiO2) requirement.
D. An unexplained drop in hourly urine output and a narrowing pulse pressure.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Lens:** RISK OF DETERIORATION

**Rationale:** High PEEP levels (such as 18 cm H2O) severely increase intrathoracic pressure. This pressure compresses the vena cava, drastically reducing venous return to the heart (preload), which in turn decreases cardiac output. An early and subtle sign of diminished cardiac output is a narrowing pulse pressure and a drop in renal perfusion, leading to decreased hourly urine output (Option D). Failure to recognize this can lead to profound hemodynamic collapse. Crackles (Option A) are an expected baseline finding in ARDS due to alveolar edema. High-pressure alarms during coughing (Option B) are normal mechanical responses. Decreasing FiO2 needs (Option C) indicates an improvement in oxygenation, not a complication.

</details>

---

### Question 320
The intensive care charge nurse is leading the resuscitation of a client in severe Status Asthmaticus. The client is receiving continuous bronchodilators and is stabilized on non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP). An experienced LPN/LVN is floated to the unit to assist. Which task is UNSAFE to delegate to the LPN/LVN during this crisis?

A. Titrating the expiratory positive airway pressure (EPAP) setting on the BiPAP based on the client's work of breathing.
B. Administering a scheduled dose of subcutaneous enoxaparin for deep vein thrombosis prophylaxis.
C. Inserting an indwelling urinary catheter to monitor strict hourly urine output during the crisis.
D. Performing a point-of-care capillary blood glucose check due to high-dose systemic corticosteroid therapy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Lens:** COMPLEX DELEGATION

**Rationale:** Adjusting and titrating settings on mechanical ventilation devices (including BiPAP) requires advanced respiratory assessment, critical thinking, and clinical judgment to evaluate the client's tolerance, work of breathing, and gas exchange. This falls strictly within the scope of the Registered Nurse (RN) or Respiratory Therapist (RT) and is unsafe to delegate to an LPN/LVN. Administering subcutaneous medications (Option B), inserting a Foley catheter (Option C), and checking blood glucose (Option D) are standard, task-oriented procedures that fall safely within the scope of practice for an experienced LPN/LVN.

</details>
### Question 321
A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is receiving mechanical ventilation with a positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) of 15 cm H₂O. The nurse reviews the client’s latest clinical data: 
- Arterial Blood Gas: pH 7.31, PaCO₂ 52 mm Hg, PaO₂ 58 mm Hg, HCO₃ 26 mEq/L
- Hemodynamics: Blood pressure 84/52 mm Hg, Heart rate 118 bpm, Central Venous Pressure (CVP) 18 mm Hg
- Urine Output: 12 mL over the last hour

Which advanced physiological interpretation best explains the relationship between the ventilator settings and the client's current hemodynamic status?

A) The client is exhibiting uncompensated metabolic acidosis resulting from profound tissue hypoxia.
B) High intrathoracic pressure has severely compromised right ventricular preload and systemic cardiac output.
C) The client has developed a sudden tension pneumothorax secondary to severe ventilator barotrauma.
D) Massive intrapulmonary shunting has resulted in a compensatory hyperdynamic cardiac output state.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) High intrathoracic pressure has severely compromised right ventricular preload and systemic cardiac output.

**Lens:** Advanced Data Analysis
**Rationale:** In ARDS, high levels of PEEP (e.g., 15 cm H₂O) are often required to recruit collapsed alveoli and improve oxygenation. However, high PEEP drastically increases intrathoracic pressure. This elevated pressure compresses the superior and inferior vena cava, severely restricting venous return (preload) to the right side of the heart. This is evidenced by the high CVP (18 mm Hg) representing backup of pressure, coupled with a drop in cardiac output leading to systemic hypotension (84/52 mm Hg) and poor organ perfusion (oliguria of 12 mL/hr). 
*Option A is incorrect* because the ABG shows a respiratory acidosis (low pH, high CO₂), not a metabolic acidosis. 
*Option C is incorrect* because while barotrauma is a risk of high PEEP, a tension pneumothorax would typically present with absent breath sounds unilaterally and tracheal deviation; high CVP and low BP are expected systemic effects of the PEEP itself. 
*Option D is incorrect* because the client is in a hypodynamic state (low cardiac output), not a hyperdynamic one.

</details>

---

### Question 322
A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with a massive saddle pulmonary embolism and obstructive shock. The client is receiving a continuous intravenous norepinephrine infusion at 15 mcg/min and an IV heparin infusion at 1,500 units/hr. The latest laboratory results reveal an activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) of 115 seconds (control: 30 seconds). Current vital signs are BP 82/45 mm Hg and HR 128 bpm. 

Which represents the priority nursing action regarding the high-alert pharmacology management?

A) Pause the heparin drip for 1 hour and decrease the infusion rate according to the prescribed nomogram.
B) Decrease the norepinephrine drip gradually to reduce right ventricular myocardial oxygen demand.
C) Maintain the current heparin rate as the massive pulmonary embolism requires aggressive anticoagulation.
D) Administer protamine sulfate IV push immediately to reverse the supratherapeutic aPTT.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Pause the heparin drip for 1 hour and decrease the infusion rate according to the prescribed nomogram.

**Lens:** High-Alert Pharmacology
**Rationale:** An aPTT of 115 seconds is significantly supratherapeutic (normal target is typically 1.5 to 2.5 times the control, or roughly 45–75 seconds). Even in the presence of a massive pulmonary embolism, maintaining a critically high aPTT places the client at imminent risk for spontaneous and fatal hemorrhage (e.g., intracranial hemorrhage). The standard high-alert medication protocol requires pausing the infusion and decreasing the rate. 
*Option B is incorrect* because decreasing the norepinephrine drip in a client with a BP of 82/45 mm Hg in obstructive shock would worsen systemic perfusion and cause cardiovascular collapse. 
*Option C is incorrect* because ignoring a toxic laboratory value violates safe pharmacological practice, regardless of the underlying clot burden. 
*Option D is incorrect* because protamine sulfate is an antidote for heparin but is typically reserved for active, life-threatening bleeding, not solely for an elevated aPTT without evidence of hemorrhage.

</details>

---

### Question 323
A client presents to the emergency department in status asthmaticus. Upon admission, the client was profusely diaphoretic, utilizing sternocleidomastoid muscles to breathe, and exhibited loud, bilateral expiratory wheezing. Initial ABG revealed a PaCO₂ of 28 mm Hg. Two hours after receiving continuous nebulized albuterol and intravenous methylprednisolone, the nurse reassesses the client. 

Which assessment finding represents the highest risk of imminent respiratory failure requiring mechanical ventilation?

A) The client develops a fine tremor in both hands and a resting heart rate of 118 bpm.
B) The client reports a sharp, localized pain in the chest wall when coughing vigorously.
C) The client's repeat arterial blood gas reveals a PaCO₂ of 44 mm Hg and a pH of 7.37.
D) The client’s peak expiratory flow rate (PEFR) increases to 55% of their personal best.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The client's repeat arterial blood gas reveals a PaCO₂ of 44 mm Hg and a pH of 7.37.

**Lens:** Risk of Deterioration
**Rationale:** In an acute asthma exacerbation, the client typically hyperventilates, blowing off carbon dioxide and causing a respiratory alkalosis (evident by the admission PaCO₂ of 28 mm Hg). If the PaCO₂ begins to rise and enter the "normal" range (35-45 mm Hg), this is a phenomenon known as "pseudonormalization." It is an ominous, subtle sign indicating profound respiratory muscle fatigue. The client is losing the ability to maintain the work of breathing, and respiratory arrest is imminent. 
*Option A is incorrect* because tachycardia and fine tremors are expected, common side effects of high-dose continuous albuterol (a beta-2 agonist). 
*Option B is incorrect* because musculoskeletal chest wall pain is a common, non-lethal consequence of vigorous coughing and accessory muscle use. 
*Option D is incorrect* because an increase in PEFR indicates an improvement in airway patency and response to therapy.

</details>

---

### Question 324
A client with ARDS is sedated and receiving pressure-controlled mechanical ventilation. Suddenly, the ventilator’s high-pressure alarm sounds continuously. The client's oxygen saturation abruptly drops from 94% to 76%, and blood pressure plummets to 68/40 mm Hg. The nurse notes rapid, shallow chest excursions, absent breath sounds on the right side, and the trachea is deviated to the left. 

What is the immediate life-saving nursing action?

A) Disconnect the client from the ventilator and manually ventilate using a bag-valve-mask.
B) Increase the ventilator’s FiO₂ to 100% and initiate a continuous norepinephrine infusion.
C) Administer the prescribed PRN dose of intravenous propofol to treat ventilator dyssynchrony.
D) Maintain ventilator connection and immediately prepare for a right-sided needle thoracostomy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Maintain ventilator connection and immediately prepare for a right-sided needle thoracostomy.

**Lens:** Emergency Rescue
**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting the classic clinical triad of a tension pneumothorax: severe hypoxemia, obstructive shock (hypotension), and tracheal deviation away from the affected side (absent breath sounds on the right). This is a life-threatening emergency caused by trapped air in the pleural space compressing the heart and great vessels. The definitive immediate rescue intervention is a needle thoracostomy (needle decompression) to release the trapped air, followed by chest tube insertion. 
*Option A is incorrect and highly dangerous*; manually ventilating with a bag-valve-mask provides unmeasured positive pressure, which will force more air into the pleural space and rapidly worsen the tension pneumothorax. 
*Option B is incorrect* because while oxygen and pressors may be utilized, they do not fix the structural obstruction causing the shock; decompression is the only life-saving measure. 
*Option C is incorrect* because sedation will rapidly worsen the hypotension and does nothing to relieve the pleural pressure.

</details>

---

### Question 325
A client who suffered a massive pulmonary embolism 24 hours ago has been stabilized on mechanical ventilation. Hemodynamics remain borderline despite fluid resuscitation. During the morning assessment, the nurse notes the client’s urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr over the past 4 hours, serum creatinine has risen from 0.9 mg/dL to 2.2 mg/dL, and there is significant jugular venous distention (JVD). 

The nurse understands that this secondary organ failure is primarily driven by which pathophysiological mechanism?

A) Acute right ventricular failure causing profound systemic venous congestion and compromised renal perfusion.
B) Embolization of fragmented pulmonary clots through the systemic circulation directly into the renal arteries.
C) Massive release of pro-inflammatory cytokines causing direct necrotic damage to the renal tubules.
D) Hypoxia-induced breakdown of muscle tissue resulting in myoglobin precipitation within the glomeruli.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Acute right ventricular failure causing profound systemic venous congestion and compromised renal perfusion.

**Lens:** Multi-System Integration
**Rationale:** A massive pulmonary embolism causes a severe obstruction in the pulmonary arterial bed, abruptly increasing pulmonary vascular resistance. This leads to acute right ventricular failure (Cor Pulmonale). The failing right ventricle cannot pump blood forward effectively, causing massive systemic venous backup (indicated by the JVD). This venous congestion significantly elevates central venous pressure (CVP). Because renal perfusion pressure is determined by the Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) minus the CVP (Renal Perfusion Pressure = MAP - CVP), a very high CVP severely compromises blood flow across the kidneys, resulting in acute kidney injury (Cardiorenal Syndrome Type 1). 
*Option B is incorrect* because pulmonary emboli originate in the venous system; they cannot reach the systemic arterial circulation (like the renal arteries) unless the client has a structural heart defect like a patent foramen ovale, which is not stated. 
*Options C and D are incorrect*; while systemic inflammation and hypoxia are present, the classic triad of PE, Cor Pulmonale (JVD), and subsequent AKI is hemodynamically driven by right heart failure and venous congestion.

</details>
### Question 326
**LENS: Emergency Rescue**
A client in the Intensive Care Unit is receiving positive pressure mechanical ventilation for acute respiratory failure. Suddenly, the ventilator alarms for high peak airway pressures. The nurse assesses the client and notes a rapid drop in blood pressure to 72/40 mm Hg, a heart rate of 145 bpm, absent breath sounds on the right side of the chest, and the trachea visibly shifted toward the left. What is the nurse's immediate life-saving action?

A) Administer a rapid 500 mL IV fluid bolus of 0.9% Normal Saline to correct the profound hypotension.
B) Prepare a 14-gauge angiocatheter for immediate insertion into the client's second intercostal space.
C) Increase the FiO2 on the mechanical ventilator to 100% to treat impending hypoxic cardiac arrest.
D) Call for an emergency portable chest X-ray to definitively confirm a right-sided lung collapse.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) Prepare a 14-gauge angiocatheter for immediate insertion into the client's second intercostal space.

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (absent breath sounds on the affected side, tracheal deviation to the unaffected side, severe hypotension, and tachycardia) secondary to barotrauma from positive pressure ventilation. This is a life-threatening obstructive shock crisis. It is a clinical diagnosis, and delaying treatment for a chest X-ray (Option D) can be fatal. The immediate emergency rescue action is a needle thoracostomy (decompression) using a large-bore needle in the 2nd intercostal space midclavicular line (or 4th/5th intercostal space anterior axillary line), followed by chest tube insertion. Fluids (Option A) and oxygen (Option C) will not reverse the underlying extreme intrathoracic pressure that is preventing venous return and causing cardiovascular collapse.
</details>

---

### Question 327
**LENS: Advanced Data Analysis**
A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is intubated and sedated. The mechanical ventilator is set to Assist-Control, Tidal Volume (TV) 4 mL/kg, FiO2 70%, and PEEP 16 cm H2O. The latest Arterial Blood Gas (ABG) reveals: pH 7.28, PaCO2 58 mm Hg, PaO2 62 mm Hg, and HCO3 25 mEq/L. Based on these advanced data parameters, what is the priority action for the nurse?

A) Increase the tidal volume to 8 mL/kg to correct the respiratory acidosis.
B) Administer IV sodium bicarbonate to buffer the arterial pH back to a normal range.
C) Increase the FiO2 to 100% to treat the refractory hypoxemia.
D) Maintain the current settings and document the findings as acceptable permissive hypercapnia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Maintain the current settings and document the findings as acceptable permissive hypercapnia.

**Rationale:** In the management of ARDS, evidence-based protocols require low tidal volumes (4-6 mL/kg) to prevent ventilator-induced lung injury (volutrauma/barotrauma). A known and accepted consequence of this lung-protective strategy is the retention of CO2, leading to "permissive hypercapnia" (elevated PaCO2 and a mildly acidotic pH). Unless the pH drops to dangerously low levels (typically below 7.15), this respiratory acidosis is tolerated, making sodium bicarbonate unnecessary (Option B). Increasing the tidal volume (Option A) would directly violate the ARDS net protocol and worsen lung injury. A PaO2 of 62 mm Hg is within the acceptable target range (55-80 mm Hg) for ARDS patients; increasing the FiO2 to 100% (Option C) unnecessarily increases the risk of oxygen toxicity. Therefore, maintaining current settings is the correct advanced action.
</details>

---

### Question 328
