**LENS: High-Alert Pharmacology**
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) and hemodynamic collapse is receiving an intravenous infusion of alteplase (tPA). Thirty minutes into the infusion, the client suddenly vomits, reports a severe "thunderclap" headache, and exhibits a Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) score drop from 15 to 11. Which pharmacological safety intervention must the nurse execute first?

A) Immediately halt the alteplase infusion and prepare for an emergency non-contrast head CT.
B) Decrease the alteplase infusion rate by half and assess the client's pupillary response.
C) Administer the prescribed PRN IV labetalol to protect the brain from intracranial hypertension.
D) Administer a rapid 1-liter IV fluid bolus to prevent further hemodynamic collapse.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) Immediately halt the alteplase infusion and prepare for an emergency non-contrast head CT.

**Rationale:** Alteplase (tPA) is a potent thrombolytic used for massive PE with hemodynamic instability. The most severe, life-threatening complication of fibrinolytic therapy is intracranial hemorrhage (ICH). A sudden severe headache, vomiting, and a declining level of consciousness are classic signs of a hemorrhagic stroke. The absolute highest priority is to stop the high-alert medication immediately to prevent further bleeding and prepare the client for a CT scan to confirm the bleed. Titrating the drip down (Option B) is unsafe when a lethal bleed is suspected. Blood pressure control (Option C) and fluid resuscitation (Option D) are secondary to stopping the offending agent causing the intracranial bleed.
</details>

---

### Question 329
**LENS: Risk of Deterioration**
A client is admitted to the emergency department in status asthmaticus. Initial assessment reveals a respiratory rate of 38 breaths/min, heart rate of 132 bpm, severe intercostal retractions, and loud bilateral expiratory wheezing. The client is given continuous albuterol/ipratropium nebulizer treatments and IV methylprednisolone. Which reassessment finding is the most critical indicator of "failure to rescue" and impending respiratory arrest?

A) The client’s respiratory rate decreases from 38 to 32 breaths per minute.
B) The client exhibits bilateral hand tremors and complains of a racing heart.
C) The wheezing suddenly ceases but the client remains diaphoretic and lethargic.
D) The client insists on leaning over the bedside table and refuses to lie supine.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) The wheezing suddenly ceases but the client remains diaphoretic and lethargic.

**Rationale:** The sudden cessation of wheezing in a severe asthma exacerbation, without a corresponding clinical improvement, is a highly ominous sign known as a "silent chest." It indicates that airflow is severely restricted due to complete airway obstruction, and the client is no longer moving enough air to generate a wheeze. Combined with lethargy (indicating worsening hypercapnia/hypoxia), this points to impending respiratory failure and the need for immediate intubation. Hand tremors and tachycardia (Option B) are expected side effects of high-dose albuterol. A slight decrease in respiratory rate (Option A) could be early improvement or fatigue, but without context, "silent chest" is the ultimate red flag. The tripod position (Option D) is an expected compensatory posture for severe respiratory distress, not an indication of immediate failure like a silent chest.
</details>

---

### Question 330
**LENS: Multi-System Integration**
A client is mechanically ventilated for severe ARDS with a Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) of 20 cm H2O. Over the past two hours, breath sounds remain equal bilaterally, but the nurse notes the Central Venous Pressure (CVP) has acutely risen from 8 to 19 mm Hg. Simultaneously, the Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) has dropped to 52 mm Hg, and urine output has fallen to 10 mL/hr. What is the primary cause of this multi-system deterioration?

A) Fluid overload from aggressive resuscitation has caused left-sided heart failure and secondary renal injury.
B) A spontaneous pneumothorax has occurred, resulting in sudden lung collapse and obstructive shock.
C) High intrathoracic pressure from PEEP has caused right ventricular failure, reducing systemic cardiac output.
D) Systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS) has caused profound vasodilation and distributive shock.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) High intrathoracic pressure from PEEP has caused right ventricular failure, reducing systemic cardiac output.

**Rationale:** High levels of PEEP significantly increase intrathoracic pressure. This compresses the pulmonary capillary bed, increasing pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR) and right ventricular afterload. Consequently, the right ventricle can fail (acute cor pulmonale), causing blood to back up into the venous system (manifested by a dramatically elevated CVP). Because the right heart cannot pump blood effectively through the lungs to the left heart, left ventricular preload and systemic cardiac output drop precipitously (manifested by hypotension/low MAP). The resulting poor perfusion to the kidneys causes acute oliguria. Equal breath sounds rule out a tension pneumothorax (Option B). Distributive shock (Option D) typically presents with a low or normal CVP due to massive vasodilation, not a severely elevated one. Left-sided heart failure (Option A) would primarily cause pulmonary edema (crackles) and elevated wedge pressures, rather than isolated rapid right-sided back-up without related left-sided structural disease in this context.
</details>
### Question 331
A client receiving positive pressure mechanical ventilation suddenly develops severe tachycardia, hypotension, absent breath sounds on the right side, and tracheal deviation to the left. The nurse suspects a tension pneumothorax. What is the immediate life-saving action?

A. Prepare for immediate needle decompression at the right anterior chest.
B. Obtain a stat portable chest X-ray to confirm the extent of lung collapse.
C. Administer a rapid IV bolus of 0.9% normal saline to stabilize blood pressure.
D. Increase the FiO2 on the ventilator to 100% and notify the respiratory therapist.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong>
<strong>Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE</strong>
Tension pneumothorax is a life-threatening emergency characterized by trapped air in the pleural space, causing mediastinal shift, compression of the great vessels, and rapid hemodynamic collapse. Immediate needle decompression is the required emergency rescue to release the trapped air, followed by chest tube insertion. Delaying for an X-ray (B) can be fatal. While fluids (C) and O2 (D) are supportive, they do not resolve the mechanical compression of the heart and great vessels causing the shock state.
</details>

---

### Question 332
A client is admitted with status asthmaticus and is receiving continuous albuterol nebulizers and intravenous methylprednisolone. Which assessment finding represents the earliest, most ominous sign of impending respiratory failure requiring immediate intervention?

A. Persistent tachycardia with a heart rate of 128 beats per minute.
B. Continued loud, high-pitched expiratory wheezing over the upper lung lobes.
C. An arterial PaCO2 rising from 28 mmHg to 42 mmHg.
D. Vigorous use of the sternocleidomastoid and intercostal muscles during inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: C</strong>
<strong>Lens: RISK OF DETERIORATION</strong>
In the early stages of a severe acute asthma attack, hyperventilation typically leads to "blowing off" carbon dioxide, resulting in a low PaCO2 (respiratory alkalosis). If the PaCO2 begins to rise and "normalize" (e.g., reaching 42 mmHg) despite aggressive treatment, it indicates severe respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory arrest. This is a critical, subtle "failure to rescue" sign. Wheezing, tachycardia, and accessory muscle use are expected findings in a severe attack, but a normalizing or elevating PaCO2 signals that the client can no longer sustain the work of breathing.
</details>

---

### Question 333
An ICU nurse evaluates the arterial blood gases (ABGs) and ventilator settings of a client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) on pressure-controlled ventilation. Current settings: FiO2 80%, PEEP 15 cm H2O. ABG results: pH 7.28, PaCO2 58 mmHg, PaO2 52 mmHg, HCO3 26 mEq/L. Which interpretation and subsequent action is most appropriate?

A. The client is in compensated respiratory acidosis; maintain the current ventilator settings.
B. The client has refractory hypoxemia; prepare to place the client in a prone position.
C. The client is hyperventilating; decrease the set respiratory rate on the ventilator.
D. The client is experiencing oxygen toxicity; decrease the FiO2 to 50% immediately.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: B</strong>
<strong>Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS</strong>
The ABG demonstrates uncompensated respiratory acidosis with severe hypoxemia (PaO2 52 mmHg) despite a high FiO2 (80%) and high PEEP (15 cm H2O). This is the hallmark of refractory hypoxemia in ARDS (a PaO2/FiO2 ratio of less than 100). The most appropriate advanced intervention for severe ARDS with refractory hypoxemia is prone positioning to improve V/Q matching, recruit dependent alveoli, and improve oxygenation. Lowering the FiO2 or decreasing the respiratory rate would exacerbate the client's lethal hypoxic and acidotic state.
</details>

---

### Question 334
A client with a massive pulmonary embolism develops sudden hemodynamic collapse with a blood pressure of 72/40 mmHg and a heart rate of 145 bpm. The provider orders an emergent intravenous alteplase infusion. Before initiating the thrombolytic drip, which finding is an absolute contraindication requiring the nurse to withhold the medication and contact the provider?

A. An INR of 1.4 from baseline lab work drawn upon admission.
B. A reported severe allergic reaction to intravenous contrast dye.
C. A platelet count of 120,000/mm3 from the morning metabolic panel.
D. A recent history of closed head trauma sustained in a motor vehicle accident 2 weeks ago.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: D</strong>
<strong>Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY</strong>
Alteplase (tPA) is a potent thrombolytic used to dissolve clots in cases of massive pulmonary embolism accompanied by hemodynamic instability. However, it carries a severe risk of catastrophic hemorrhage. Recent significant head trauma (within the past 3 months) is an absolute contraindication due to the risk of fatal intracranial bleeding. An INR of 1.4 and mild thrombocytopenia (120,000/mm3) are not absolute contraindications in a life-or-death PE scenario. An allergy to contrast dye has no relevance to alteplase administration.
</details>

---

### Question 335
A client is receiving mechanical ventilation for severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) requiring high levels of Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) at 18 cm H2O. The nurse notes a sudden drop in urine output to 10 mL/hr, a central venous pressure (CVP) rise from 8 to 16 mm Hg, and a blood pressure decrease from 110/70 to 82/50 mm Hg. Which pathophysiological process best explains this multi-system deterioration?

A. The high intrathoracic pressure from PEEP is decreasing venous return, leading to decreased cardiac output and renal hypoperfusion.
B. The client is developing severe sepsis from ventilator-associated pneumonia, causing widespread peripheral vasodilation.
C. The client has developed a tension pneumothorax, causing immediate compression of the descending thoracic aorta.
D. Acute kidney injury from prolonged hypoxemia is causing massive fluid retention and acute cor pulmonale.

<details>
<summary><strong>Rationale</strong></summary>
<strong>Correct Answer: A</strong>
<strong>Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION</strong>
High levels of PEEP significantly increase intrathoracic pressure. This elevated pressure compresses the vena cava, reducing venous return (preload) to the right side of the heart, causing blood to pool in the venous system (manifested by the elevated CVP). Because less blood returns to the heart, cardiac output drops, resulting in systemic hypotension and subsequent decreased renal perfusion (manifesting as oliguria). This highlights how an aggressive respiratory intervention can directly precipitate cardiovascular and renal multi-system failure. 
</details>
### Question 336
**Lens: EMERGENCY RESCUE**

A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is receiving mechanical ventilation in pressure-control mode. Suddenly, the high-pressure alarm sounds continuously. The nurse assesses the client and notes the SpO2 has dropped from 92% to 76%, blood pressure is 70/40 mmHg, breath sounds are absent on the right side, and the trachea is visibly deviated to the left. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse must take?

A) Administer a rapid 500 mL normal saline bolus to correct the hypotension.
B) Disconnect the client from the ventilator and suction the endotracheal tube.
C) Notify the provider immediately while preparing a 14-gauge needle for emergency thoracostomy.
D) Obtain a stat portable chest X-ray to confirm the cause of the respiratory collapse.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (sudden hypoxemia, high peak airway pressures, unilateral absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation to the unaffected side, and profound hypotension due to compromised venous return). This is an absolute medical emergency. The immediate life-saving rescue intervention is a needle thoracostomy (decompression) followed by chest tube insertion. The nurse must urgently gather the supplies (a large-bore 14-gauge needle) and notify the critical care provider/rapid response team to perform the procedure instantly. Waiting for an X-ray (Option D) will result in cardiac arrest. Suctioning (Option B) will not fix the pleural pressure and delays life-saving care. Fluid resuscitation (Option A) does not correct the obstructive cause of the shock.
**System Focus:** Tension Pneumothorax
</details>

---

### Question 337
**Lens: ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS**

The nurse is caring for a client admitted with bilateral viral pneumonia who has developed severe respiratory distress. The client is placed on a high-flow nasal cannula at an FiO2 of 0.80 (80%). The latest arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals: pH 7.28, PaCO2 56 mmHg, HCO3 24 mEq/L, and PaO2 52 mmHg. The chest X-ray shows diffuse, bilateral "white-out" infiltrates. Based on the calculation of the client’s PaO2/FiO2 (P/F) ratio, how should the nurse interpret this data?

A) The client has Severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) and needs preparation for intubation and prone positioning.
B) The client is experiencing an acute exacerbation of COPD with fully compensated respiratory acidosis.
C) The client is demonstrating uncomplicated lobar pneumonia with adequate tissue oxygenation.
D) The client is exhibiting acute pulmonary embolism characterized by respiratory alkalosis and profound hypoxia.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A
**Rationale:** The nurse must calculate the P/F ratio to determine the severity of hypoxia. PaO2 (52) divided by FiO2 (0.80) equals 65. A P/F ratio < 100 with bilateral infiltrates indicates Severe ARDS. Furthermore, the ABG shows uncompensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high CO2, normal bicarb) with severe refractory hypoxemia. This client is failing non-invasive support and requires immediate mechanical ventilation and likely prone positioning to recruit collapsed alveoli. Option B is incorrect because the pH is not compensated. Option C is incorrect; a P/F ratio of 65 is severe, life-threatening hypoxia, not adequate oxygenation. Option D is incorrect because the ABG shows respiratory acidosis, not alkalosis. 
**System Focus:** ARDS
</details>

---

### Question 338
**Lens: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION**

A client is admitted to the Intensive Care Unit with a massive saddle pulmonary embolism (PE). Two hours after admission, the nurse notes that the client’s urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr, blood pressure is 82/50 mmHg, extremities are cool and mottled, and severe jugular venous distension (JVD) is present. The nurse understands that this multi-system deterioration is primarily caused by which pathophysiological mechanism?

A) Microvascular thrombosis in the renal tubules due to heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT).
B) Development of acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) leading to widespread alveolar-capillary leakage.
C) Severe hypovolemia resulting from occult internal bleeding secondary to anticoagulation therapy.
D) Right ventricular failure leading to obstructive shock, which severely decreases left ventricular preload and cardiac output.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D
**Rationale:** A massive pulmonary embolism dramatically increases pulmonary vascular resistance, causing the right ventricle to pump against extreme pressure. This leads to acute right ventricular failure (cor pulmonale) and obstructive shock. Because blood cannot pass through the lungs to the left side of the heart, left ventricular preload plummets, causing a drastic drop in systemic cardiac output, profound hypotension, and subsequent organ failure (evidenced by the drop in urine output and mottled extremities). The JVD is a classic sign of blood backing up from the failing right ventricle. Options A, B, and C do not account for the combination of obstructive right-sided failure (JVD) and left-sided low output (hypotension, poor perfusion) specific to a massive PE.
**System Focus:** PE with Hemodynamic Collapse
</details>

---

### Question 339
**Lens: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY**

A client experiencing refractory status asthmaticus is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of magnesium sulfate to promote bronchodilation. Which assessment finding requires the nurse to immediately STOP the magnesium sulfate infusion and prepare to administer calcium gluconate?

A) The client reports a sensation of intense facial flushing and total body warmth.
B) The client's patellar deep tendon reflexes become completely absent.
C) The client develops fine motor tremors in both hands.
D) The client's heart rate increases from 115 to 132 beats per minute.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B
**Rationale:** Magnesium sulfate is a high-alert medication used in severe status asthmaticus for its smooth muscle-relaxing properties. However, magnesium toxicity can be lethal, leading to neuromuscular blockade, respiratory paralysis, and cardiac arrest. The loss of deep tendon reflexes (DTRs) is an early and hallmark sign of magnesium toxicity. The infusion must be stopped immediately, and the antidote (calcium gluconate) should be prepared. Flushing and warmth (Option A) are expected, normal side effects of IV magnesium administration. Fine tremors (Option C) and tachycardia (Option D) are common side effects of continuous albuterol nebulization, not signs of magnesium toxicity.
**System Focus:** Status Asthmaticus
</details>

---

### Question 340
**Lens: COMPLEX DELEGATION**

An ICU nurse is coordinating care with an experienced LPN/LVN and an Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) for a client who is receiving a tissue plasminogen activator (tPA) infusion and intravenous norepinephrine for a massive pulmonary embolism causing obstructive shock. Which task is UNSAFE for the RN to delegate in this scenario?

A) Delegating the UAP to empty the urinary catheter collection bag and report the hourly volume.
B) Delegating the LPN/LVN to obtain a stat 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) for sudden new-onset tachycardia.
C) Delegating the LPN/LVN to titrate the continuous norepinephrine infusion to maintain a MAP greater than 65 mmHg.
D) Delegating the UAP to obtain the client's blood pressure using a manual sphygmomanometer.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C
**Rationale:** The titration of high-alert, continuous vasoactive medications (like norepinephrine) in a hemodynamically unstable client requires advanced assessment, continuous critical thinking, and clinical judgment. This falls strictly within the scope of practice of the Registered Nurse (RN) and is unsafe and illegal to delegate to an LPN/LVN. An LPN/LVN can perform skills such as obtaining an ECG (Option B). A UAP can empty a foley catheter (Option A) and obtain vital signs (Option D), even in critical situations, provided the RN interprets the data and remains responsible for the overall assessment. 
**System Focus:** PE with Hemodynamic Collapse
</details>
### Question 341
**Lens: Emergency Rescue**

A client receiving mechanical ventilation for Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) suddenly develops a heart rate of 145 bpm, a blood pressure of 78/40 mmHg, and oxygen saturations plummeting to 78%. The ventilator alarms for high peak inspiratory pressure (PIP). The nurse assesses absent breath sounds on the right side and notes the trachea is visibly shifted to the left. Which of the following is the immediate, life-saving action the nurse must take?

A) Increase the FiO2 to 100% on the ventilator and suction the client.
B) Obtain a stat portable chest x-ray to definitively confirm the diagnosis.
C) Prepare the client for immediate needle decompression and chest tube insertion.
D) Administer a rapid infusion of isotonic crystalloids to correct the hypotension.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Prepare the client for immediate needle decompression and chest tube insertion.

**Rationale:** The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (absent lung sounds, tracheal deviation, hemodynamic collapse, and high PIP on a ventilator). The positive pressure from the ventilator has caused a severe accumulation of trapped air in the pleural space, compressing the heart and great vessels, leading to obstructive shock. Immediate needle decompression followed by chest tube insertion is the emergency rescue intervention to release the trapped air and restore cardiac output. Waiting for a chest x-ray (Option B) will delay life-saving treatment and lead to cardiac arrest. Increasing FiO2 (Option A) or administering fluids (Option D) will not fix the mechanical obstruction preventing cardiac filling and ventilation.

**Cognitive Level:** Application/Analysis
**Client Need:** Physiological Integrity: Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies / Reduction of Risk Potential
</details>

---

### Question 342
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis**

A client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is mechanically ventilated on an assist-control mode. The client is receiving an FiO2 of 80% and a Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) of 5 cm H2O. The latest arterial blood gas (ABG) reveals: pH 7.31, PaCO2 50 mmHg, PaO2 48 mmHg, and HCO3 24 mEq/L. Based on this advanced data, which intervention should the nurse anticipate next?

A) The client is hyperventilating; decrease the set respiratory rate.
B) The client has refractory hypoxemia; anticipate an order to increase PEEP.
C) The client is developing a tension pneumothorax; prepare for thoracentesis.
D) The client has uncompensated metabolic acidosis; administer sodium bicarbonate.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B) The client has refractory hypoxemia; anticipate an order to increase PEEP.

**Rationale:** The ABG shows a PaO2 of 48 mmHg despite receiving 80% FiO2. This is the hallmark of ARDS: severe, refractory hypoxemia caused by non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema and alveolar collapse (shunting). Increasing the FiO2 further will likely cause oxygen toxicity without improving oxygenation. Instead, increasing the PEEP will stent the collapsed alveoli open, recruiting more surface area for gas exchange and improving the PaO2. The ABG shows respiratory acidosis, not metabolic acidosis (Option D). Decreasing the respiratory rate (Option A) would worsen the retention of CO2 (PaCO2 is already elevated at 50 mmHg). There is no data here directly indicating a tension pneumothorax (Option C).

**Cognitive Level:** Analysis/Evaluation
**Client Need:** Physiological Integrity: Physiological Adaptation
</details>

---

### Question 343
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology**

A client is admitted to the intensive care unit with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) causing right ventricular failure and hemodynamic collapse. A continuous intravenous infusion of alteplase (tPA) is initiated. One hour into the infusion, the client’s Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) drops suddenly from 15 to 9, and the right pupil becomes sluggish. What is the priority nursing action?

A) Administer a rapid IV fluid bolus to correct cerebral hypoperfusion.
B) Decrease the alteplase infusion rate by half and reassess neurologic status.
C) Prepare to administer protamine sulfate as an immediate reversal agent.
D) Immediately stop the alteplase infusion and prepare for a stat head CT.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D) Immediately stop the alteplase infusion and prepare for a stat head CT.

**Rationale:** Alteplase is a potent thrombolytic used in life-threatening pulmonary embolisms to dissolve the clot. However, it carries a catastrophic risk of intracranial hemorrhage (ICH). A sudden drop in GCS and pupillary changes strongly indicate bleeding in the brain. The first and most critical action is to immediately stop the infusion to prevent further bleeding, followed by an urgent head CT to confirm ICH. Decreasing the rate (Option B) is unsafe; the drug must be stopped entirely. Fluids (Option A) will not treat an active intracranial bleed. Protamine sulfate (Option C) is the reversal agent for heparin, not alteplase (tPA reversal typically involves cryoprecipitate or aminocaproic acid depending on protocols).

**Cognitive Level:** Application/Analysis
**Client Need:** Physiological Integrity: Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 344
**Lens: Risk of Deterioration**

A young adult client is admitted with status asthmaticus. Upon admission, the client was highly agitated, using pronounced accessory muscles, with a respiratory rate of 36 breaths/min and a loud, high-pitched expiratory wheeze. Two hours later, following continuous albuterol and IV methylprednisolone, the nurse evaluates the client. Which finding represents the earliest, most subtle sign of impending respiratory failure?

A) The client's PaCO2 level rises from 30 mmHg to 42 mmHg.
B) The client's heart rate decreases from 125 bpm to 110 bpm.
C) The client's wheezing becomes slightly louder during inspiration.
D) The client requests the head of the bed to be elevated to 90 degrees.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A) The client's PaCO2 level rises from 30 mmHg to 42 mmHg.

**Rationale:** In the early stages of an acute asthma exacerbation, the client hyperventilates, causing them to blow off carbon dioxide, resulting in a low PaCO2 (respiratory alkalosis). If the client continues to struggle and the PaCO2 level begins to rise back to a "normal" range (35-45 mmHg), it is a dangerous, subtle sign of severe respiratory muscle fatigue. A normalizing PaCO2 in the context of an ongoing severe asthma attack indicates that the client is no longer able to maintain the work of breathing and is rapidly progressing toward hypercapnic respiratory failure and arrest. Decreased heart rate (Option B) could indicate improvement or impending arrest, but a normalizing CO2 is the classic physiologic warning sign. Louder wheezing (Option C) indicates air is still moving (a silent chest would be alarming). Sitting upright (Option D) is a standard orthopneic position for distress, not an early sign of impending failure compared to the ABG shift.

**Cognitive Level:** Analysis/Evaluation
**Client Need:** Physiological Integrity: Reduction of Risk Potential
</details>

---

### Question 345
**Lens: Complex Delegation**

The critical care nurse is managing a client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) who is heavily sedated, receiving a continuous cisatracurium (neuromuscular blocking agent) infusion, and placed on a prone-positioning protocol to improve oxygenation. Which task is strictly UNSAFE to delegate to the Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP) in this highly unstable environment?

A) Emptying the indwelling urinary catheter bag and recording the hourly output.
B) Assisting the healthcare team with the physical turning of the client into the prone position.
C) Reconnecting the ventilator circuit if it accidentally detaches during repositioning.
D) Applying protective hydrocolloid dressings to the client's knees and elbows before a turn.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C) Reconnecting the ventilator circuit if it accidentally detaches during repositioning.

**Rationale:** A client receiving a neuromuscular blocking agent (cisatracurium) is completely paralyzed and entirely dependent on the mechanical ventilator for survival. If the ventilator circuit disconnects, the client cannot breathe and will immediately suffer anoxic brain injury or death. Managing, assessing, and reconnecting an artificial airway and ventilator circuit requires clinical judgment and assessment to ensure patency, proper connection, and effective ventilation. This falls outside the scope of practice for a UAP and is critically unsafe to delegate. The UAP can safely empty Foley catheters (Option A), assist a team in physical repositioning (Option B), and apply prophylactic barrier/hydrocolloid dressings to pressure points (Option D) under the direction of the RN.

**Cognitive Level:** Application/Analysis
**Client Need:** Safe and Effective Care Environment: Management of Care
</details>

---
### Question 346
A client admitted with status asthmaticus has been receiving continuous albuterol nebulizers and intravenous methylprednisolone for the past two hours. The client remains tachypneic with severe retractions. Which of the following arterial blood gas (ABG) findings represents the earliest, most critical sign of a "failure to rescue" in this client?

A. Sudden normalization of PaCO2 to 40 mm Hg from a previous reading of 28 mm Hg. 
B. A persistent PaO2 of 75 mm Hg while receiving 4 L/min via nasal cannula.
C. A serum pH increasing from 7.42 to 7.46 over the last hour.
D. A steady bicarbonate (HCO3-) level of 23 mEq/L.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Sudden normalization of PaCO2 to 40 mm Hg from a previous reading of 28 mm Hg.

**Rationale:** *[Lens 4: RISK OF DETERIORATION]* In the early stages of an acute asthma exacerbation, hyperventilation causes the client to blow off carbon dioxide, resulting in a low PaCO2 (respiratory alkalosis). As the client becomes exhausted, the respiratory muscles fatigue and air trapping worsens, causing the PaCO2 to rise. A "normal" or rising PaCO2 in a client with status asthmaticus who is still exhibiting severe distress is a glaring, ominous sign of impending respiratory failure and arrest. The nurse must anticipate immediate intubation and mechanical ventilation.

</details>

---

### Question 347
A client is mechanically ventilated in the ICU for severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS). The ventilator suddenly sounds a continuous "High Peak Inspiratory Pressure" alarm. Upon assessment, the nurse notes profound hypotension, severe tachycardia, absent breath sounds on the right side, and the trachea is visibly deviated to the left. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse must take?

A. Disconnect the client from the ventilator and manually bag them with 100% oxygen.
B. Prepare to assist the provider with an immediate needle decompression in the right second intercostal space.
C. Order a STAT portable chest X-ray to confirm the presence of a collapsed lung.
D. Administer a rapid 500 mL bolus of 0.9% normal saline to stabilize the blood pressure.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Prepare to assist the provider with an immediate needle decompression in the right second intercostal space.

**Rationale:** *[Lens 2: EMERGENCY RESCUE]* The client is demonstrating classic, lethal signs of a tension pneumothorax (absent unilateral breath sounds, high ventilator peak pressures, severe hypotension, and contralateral tracheal deviation). Positive pressure ventilation increases the risk of this complication in ARDS. Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis; waiting for an X-ray (Option C) or giving fluids (Option D) will fatally delay definitive treatment. The immediate life-saving intervention is needle decompression to release the trapped air, followed by chest tube insertion.

</details>

---

### Question 348
A client in the ICU with a massive pulmonary embolism and cardiogenic shock is receiving a continuous alteplase (tPA) infusion alongside a titratable heparin drip. While performing a neurological check, the nurse notes the client has suddenly become unresponsive and has a unilaterally fixed and dilated right pupil. Which action should the nurse take *first*?

A. Administer intravenous protamine sulfate and aminocaproic acid immediately.
B. Lower the head of the bed to a flat position to maximize cerebral perfusion.
C. Stop both the continuous alteplase and heparin infusions immediately.
D. Prepare the client for an emergency non-contrast computed tomography (CT) scan of the head.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. Stop both the continuous alteplase and heparin infusions immediately.

**Rationale:** *[Lens 6: HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY]* A sudden change in level of consciousness accompanied by a blown (fixed and dilated) pupil in a client receiving systemic thrombolytics (alteplase) and anticoagulants (heparin) strongly indicates a catastrophic acute intracranial hemorrhage. The absolute first action the nurse must take is to stop the offending infusions to prevent further bleeding. Only after the drips are stopped should the nurse notify the provider, prepare for a STAT head CT, and anticipate the administration of reversal agents.

</details>

---

### Question 349
A client with diffuse Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) requires mechanical ventilation with a high Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) of 18 cm H2O. The nurse understands that while high PEEP recruits collapsed alveoli, it can precipitate secondary organ dysfunction. Which clinical finding indicates that the respiratory intervention is causing a secondary cardiovascular system failure?

A. Diffuse bilateral crackles persistently auscultated in the lung bases.
B. Capillary blood glucose steadily rising from 110 mg/dL to 160 mg/dL.
C. An increasing PaO2/FiO2 (P/F) ratio from 120 to 180.
D. A marked elevation in central venous pressure (CVP) with new-onset jugular venous distention.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. A marked elevation in central venous pressure (CVP) with new-onset jugular venous distention.

**Rationale:** *[Lens 5: MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION]* High PEEP significantly increases intrathoracic pressure. This elevated pressure compresses the vena cava and pulmonary vessels, reducing venous return to the right side of the heart and sharply increasing right ventricular afterload. This can rapidly precipitate acute right-sided heart failure (cor pulmonale) and significantly drop cardiac output. An elevated CVP with jugular venous distention reflects this acute right ventricular failure secondary to the aggressive mechanical ventilation settings.

</details>

---

### Question 350
After receiving the shift report in the medical-surgical intensive care unit, which client should the nurse assess *first*?

A. A client recovering from a massive pulmonary embolism who reports a sudden onset of blood-tinged sputum.
B. A client with status asthmaticus who is restless, sweating, and whose loud wheezing has abruptly stopped.
C. A client with a resolving tension pneumothorax whose chest tube water-seal chamber shows intermittent bubbling.
D. A client with ARDS on a ventilator whose latest arterial PaO2 is 62 mm Hg while receiving 60% FiO2.

<details>
<summary><strong>Correct Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. A client with status asthmaticus who is restless, sweating, and whose loud wheezing has abruptly stopped.

**Rationale:** *[Lens 1: PRIORITIZATION]* The abrupt cessation of wheezing in a restless and sweating client with status asthmaticus indicates a "silent chest." This occurs when bronchoconstriction is so severe that there is insufficient airflow to even produce a wheeze. It is a sign of complete airway obstruction and impending respiratory arrest, requiring immediate intervention (intubation). Hemoptysis is a known complication of PE and requires evaluation, but is not immediately terminal. Intermittent bubbling in a water-seal chamber is normal for a resolving pneumothorax. A PaO2 of 62 mm Hg in an ARDS patient is low but often falls within acceptable permissive hypoxemia parameters for this disease process. 

</details>
### Question 351
*Emergency Rescue*

A mechanically ventilated client with Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) suddenly develops high peak inspiratory pressures alarms. Upon assessment, the nurse notes absent breath sounds on the right side, profound hypotension (70/40 mm Hg), tachycardia (142 bpm), and the trachea is noticeably deviated to the left. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse must take?

A. Prepare the equipment and assist the provider with an immediate needle thoracostomy.
B. Disconnect the client from the ventilator and manually ventilate using a bag-valve-mask.
C. Administer a 500 mL normal saline bolus to correct the sudden hypotension.
D. Request an emergent STAT portable chest X-ray to confirm the diagnosis.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Prepare the equipment and assist the provider with an immediate needle thoracostomy.

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation, hemodynamic collapse), a severe complication of positive pressure ventilation (barotrauma) in ARDS. This is an absolute medical emergency. Waiting for a chest X-ray (Option D) will result in fatal cardiac arrest. Fluid resuscitation (Option C) does not treat the underlying obstructive shock. Manually ventilating (Option B) will not fix the issue and may worsen the intrathoracic pressure. The immediate rescue action is a needle decompression (thoracostomy) to release the trapped air, followed by chest tube placement.

**Lens Focus:** EMERGENCY RESCUE. Identifying the definitive, immediate life-saving intervention for sudden respiratory and hemodynamic collapse.
</details>

---

### Question 352
*Risk of Deterioration*

A young adult client is admitted with status asthmaticus and has been receiving continuous albuterol nebulization and IV methylprednisolone for the past two hours. The client is tachypneic and using accessory muscles. Which assessment finding indicates the highest risk for an impending "failure to rescue" respiratory arrest?

A. Fine hand tremors and a resting heart rate of 124 bpm.
B. An arterial PaCO2 rising from 28 mm Hg to 42 mm Hg despite therapy.
C. Loud, high-pitched expiratory wheezing heard throughout the upper lung fields.
D. Oxygen saturation fluctuating intermittently between 91% and 93% on room air.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. An arterial PaCO2 rising from 28 mm Hg to 42 mm Hg despite therapy.

**Rationale:** 
During the early stages of an acute asthma exacerbation, clients hyperventilate, leading to respiratory alkalosis and a low PaCO2 (e.g., 28 mm Hg). If the PaCO2 begins to "normalize" (rise to 35-45 mm Hg) or become elevated while the client is still exhibiting increased work of breathing, it is a hallmark sign of severe respiratory muscle fatigue. This indicates the client can no longer maintain the compensatory hyperventilation and is at imminent risk for complete respiratory failure and arrest. Tremors and tachycardia (Option A) are expected side effects of continuous albuterol. Loud wheezing (Option C) indicates air is still moving; absent wheezing ("silent chest") would be a grave sign. Fluctuating SpO2 (Option D) is expected in an exacerbation, but the rising PaCO2 is the specific precursor to mechanical failure.

**Lens Focus:** RISK OF DETERIORATION. Identifying the subtlest, earliest physiologic marker that a client's compensatory mechanisms are failing.
</details>

---

### Question 353
*Multi-System Integration*

A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is intubated and mechanically ventilated with a Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) of 18 cm H2O. Over the last two hours, the nurse notes the client's urine output has dropped to 10 mL/hr, Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) is 55 mm Hg, and peripheral pulses are weak. What is the primary pathophysiological cause of this new multi-system complication?

A. Systemic inflammatory response causing vasodilation and acute tubular necrosis.
B. Compensatory renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system activation from persistent hypoxemia.
C. High intrathoracic pressure drastically decreasing venous return and cardiac output.
D. Alveolar-capillary membrane damage leaking protein and fluid into the interstitium.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. High intrathoracic pressure drastically decreasing venous return and cardiac output.

**Rationale:** 
High levels of PEEP (such as 18 cm H2O, which is common in severe ARDS to keep alveoli open) severely increase intrathoracic pressure. This high pressure compresses the superior and inferior vena cava, which dramatically decreases venous return (preload) to the right side of the heart. Decreased preload leads to severely decreased cardiac output and systemic hypotension (MAP of 55 mm Hg). The resulting hypoperfusion to the kidneys causes an acute drop in urine output (oliguria/prerenal acute kidney injury). While ARDS involves capillary leakage (Option D) and inflammation (Option A), the direct mechanical consequence of high PEEP driving this specific cascade of sudden cardiovascular and renal failure is decreased venous return.

**Lens Focus:** MULTI-SYSTEM INTEGRATION. Understanding how therapeutic interventions for respiratory failure directly induce cardiovascular and renal dysfunction.
</details>

---

### Question 354
*High-Alert Pharmacology*

A client is admitted to the Intensive Care Unit with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) causing right ventricular failure and a blood pressure of 72/40 mm Hg. An alteplase (tPA) infusion is initiated. Fifteen minutes into the infusion, the client suddenly becomes unresponsive to verbal stimuli, and the nurse notes unequal pupils. What is the nurse's priority action?

A. Elevate the head of the bed to 45 degrees and assess oxygenation status.
B. Prepare the intubation tray for emergent endotracheal airway management.
C. Administer the prescribed PRN dose of IV protamine sulfate immediately.
D. Stop the alteplase infusion immediately and notify the rapid response team.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Stop the alteplase infusion immediately and notify the rapid response team.

**Rationale:** 
Alteplase (tPA) is a high-alert thrombolytic given to dissolve the massive PE causing hemodynamic collapse. However, a major, life-threatening adverse effect is hemorrhage, particularly intracranial hemorrhage (ICH). A sudden decrease in the level of consciousness and unequal pupils are classic signs of increased intracranial pressure secondary to a cerebral bleed. The absolute priority is to halt the infusion of the offending drug immediately to prevent further bleeding. Protamine sulfate (Option C) is the reversal agent for heparin, not tPA (cryoprecipitate or aminocaproic acid may be used for tPA). While airway management (Option B) and positioning (Option A) may be necessary, stopping the root cause of the active bleeding takes absolute precedence.

**Lens Focus:** HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY. Managing severe, life-threatening complications of crisis medications through immediate recognition and discontinuation.
</details>

---

### Question 355
*Prioritization*

The charge nurse receives the shift report on a respiratory step-down unit. Which client must the nurse assess FIRST?

A. A client with status asthmaticus receiving an IV magnesium sulfate infusion whose deep tendon reflexes are 1+.
B. A client with ARDS utilizing a high-flow nasal cannula whose morning arterial PaO2 is 58 mm Hg.
C. A client with a chest tube placed for a spontaneous pneumothorax exhibiting intermittent bubbling in the water seal chamber.
D. A client recovering from a pulmonary embolism receiving a continuous heparin drip with an aPTT of 85 seconds.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. A client with status asthmaticus receiving an IV magnesium sulfate infusion whose deep tendon reflexes are 1+.

**Rationale:** 
IV magnesium sulfate is used as a smooth muscle relaxant in severe status asthmaticus. However, diminishing deep tendon reflexes (1+) are an early, critical sign of magnesium toxicity. If left unaddressed, this will rapidly progress to respiratory muscle paralysis and cardiac arrest. This client is actively deteriorating and requires immediate assessment and potential administration of calcium gluconate. Option B is abnormal but expected in severe ARDS; while requiring monitoring, it is a chronic/stable severity. Option C describes a normal, expected finding for a chest tube treating a pneumothorax. Option D represents a slightly supratherapeutic aPTT (normal therapeutic range is typically 1.5-2.5 times the baseline, or 60-80 seconds); this requires a drip adjustment but is not an immediate life-threatening emergency compared to impending respiratory paralysis.

**Lens Focus:** PRIORITIZATION. Determining which unstable system-specific client requires the most immediate intervention based on the highest risk of imminent death or severe injury.
</details>
### Question 356
**Lens: Multi-System Integration**

A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is intubated and receiving mechanical ventilation. The healthcare provider increases the Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) from 10 cm H2O to 18 cm H2O to improve oxygenation. Ten minutes later, the nurse notes sudden changes in the client's condition. Which assessment finding indicates that the respiratory intervention is causing a secondary, life-threatening system failure?

A. Bilateral fine crackles auscultated at the lung bases on inspiration.
B. The client's systolic blood pressure drops from 110 mm Hg to 78 mm Hg with weak peripheral pulses.
C. PaO2 remains at 55 mm Hg despite an FiO2 of 80% on the ventilator.
D. The endotracheal tube requires frequent suctioning due to thick, tenacious secretions.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B

**Rationale:** 
In the management of ARDS, high levels of Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) are often required to keep the alveoli open and improve gas exchange. However, a major complication of high PEEP is the significant increase in intrathoracic pressure. This elevated pressure compresses the superior and inferior vena cava, severely decreasing venous return (preload) to the heart. The resulting drop in preload causes a profound reduction in cardiac output, leading to secondary cardiovascular collapse, manifested by severe hypotension and weak peripheral pulses. 
Option A is an expected respiratory finding in ARDS due to pulmonary edema. Option C indicates persistent respiratory failure (refractory hypoxemia), not a secondary system failure. Option D is related to airway clearance, not a new multi-system complication.

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing
**Client Need:** Physiological Adaptation
</details>

---

### Question 357
**Lens: Advanced Data Analysis**

A 28-year-old client is admitted to the emergency department in status asthmaticus. Upon arrival, the client is severely tachypneic, and the initial arterial blood gas (ABG) on room air reveals: pH 7.50, PaCO2 28 mm Hg, and PaO2 65 mm Hg. After one hour of continuous albuterol nebulization and intravenous methylprednisolone, the client appears lethargic. A repeat ABG on a non-rebreather mask reveals: pH 7.37, PaCO2 43 mm Hg, and PaO2 58 mm Hg. How must the nurse interpret these advanced clinical data?

A. The client is experiencing a compensated metabolic alkalosis due to the corticosteroid administration.
B. The client's respiratory drive is normalizing, indicating a successful response to bronchodilator therapy.
C. The client requires immediate administration of an intravenous sodium bicarbonate infusion.
D. The client is fatiguing and developing impending hypercapnic respiratory failure.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
During the initial phase of an acute asthma exacerbation, clients hyperventilate in an attempt to maintain oxygenation, which blows off CO2 and causes respiratory alkalosis (hypocapnia), as seen in the first ABG. As the client's respiratory muscles fatigue from the severe work of breathing against bronchoconstriction, their respiratory rate and depth decrease. The PaCO2 begins to rise back to a "normal" level (43 mm Hg) and eventually climbs into hypercapnia. In the context of status asthmaticus, a "normalizing" PaCO2 alongside clinical lethargy and worsening hypoxemia (PaO2 58 mm Hg despite high-flow oxygen) is an ominous sign of extreme muscle fatigue and impending hypercapnic respiratory arrest. The client requires immediate preparation for intubation and mechanical ventilation.

**Cognitive Level:** Evaluating
**Client Need:** Reduction of Risk Potential
</details>

---

### Question 358
**Lens: High-Alert Pharmacology**

A client with a massive pulmonary embolism (PE) complicated by acute right ventricular failure and hemodynamic collapse is receiving a continuous intravenous infusion of Alteplase (tPA). Fifteen minutes into the infusion, the client's blood pressure drops to 70/40 mm Hg, heart rate increases to 135 bpm, and the nurse notes acute gross hematuria in the urinary catheter tubing. What is the nurse's immediate priority action?

A. Discontinue the Alteplase infusion immediately and prepare to administer ordered cryoprecipitate or fresh frozen plasma.
B. Administer a 500 mL 0.9% normal saline bolus rapidly and notify the healthcare provider.
C. Decrease the Alteplase infusion rate by half and draw stat coagulation studies.
D. Prepare to administer protamine sulfate intravenously to rapidly reverse the coagulopathy.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A

**Rationale:** 
Alteplase is a potent thrombolytic (fibrinolytic) agent used in massive pulmonary embolisms with hemodynamic instability. A major, life-threatening complication of tPA is severe, uncontrolled hemorrhage. The client's sudden hemodynamic collapse (BP 70/40, HR 135) combined with gross hematuria strongly indicates systemic internal bleeding. The absolute priority is to stop the causative agent immediately. Following the discontinuation of tPA, the nurse should anticipate the administration of blood products (such as PRBCs, cryoprecipitate, or fresh frozen plasma) or antifibrinolytic agents (like aminocaproic acid or tranexamic acid) to restore clotting factors. 
Protamine sulfate (Option D) is the reversal agent for Heparin, not Alteplase. Decreasing the rate (Option C) or just giving fluids (Option B) delays the life-saving intervention of stopping the hemorrhage-inducing medication.

**Cognitive Level:** Synthesizing / Creating
**Client Need:** Pharmacological and Parenteral Therapies
</details>

---

### Question 359
**Lens: Emergency Rescue**

A mechanically ventilated client with advanced ARDS is receiving high positive-pressure ventilation. Suddenly, the ventilator triggers a continuous high-pressure alarm and ceases delivering tidal volume. The nurse assesses the client and notes profound cyanosis, a heart rate of 148 bpm, a blood pressure of 65/38 mm Hg, absent breath sounds on the right side of the chest, and a noticeable leftward deviation of the trachea. What is the immediate life-saving action the nurse must take or anticipate?

A. Administer an immediate intravenous push of epinephrine 1 mg (1:10,000).
B. Detach the client from the ventilator and attempt manual ventilation using a bag-valve-mask.
C. Anticipate an emergent needle decompression in the right second intercostal space at the midclavicular line.
D. Increase the positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) to stent the collapsed right lung open.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax, a known complication of high positive-pressure ventilation (barotrauma) in ARDS patients. High pressures can rupture overdistended alveoli, leaking air into the pleural space. This trapped air rapidly accumulates, compressing the affected lung (absent breath sounds on the right), shifting mediastinal structures away from the affected side (leftward tracheal deviation), and compressing the great vessels, which drastically reduces venous return and causes obstructive shock (hemodynamic collapse). The immediate life-saving rescue action is to perform a needle thoracostomy (needle decompression) on the affected side (the right side) to vent the trapped air, converting the tension pneumothorax into a simple pneumothorax until a chest tube can be inserted.

**Cognitive Level:** Applying
**Client Need:** Management of Care / Physiological Adaptation
</details>

---

### Question 360
**Lens: Risk of Deterioration**

A young adult client is being treated in the intensive care unit for status asthmaticus triggered by an acute respiratory infection. For the past two hours, the client has been highly anxious, diaphoretic, and exhibiting loud, diffuse inspiratory and expiratory wheezes. Upon the most recent assessment, the nurse notes the client is suddenly drowsy. The respiratory rate has decreased from 36 to 14 breaths per minute, and the chest is moving with extreme effort, but no wheezing or breath sounds are audible upon auscultation. Which conclusion correctly identifies this deterioration?

A. The client is demonstrating an expected, positive response to the continuous bronchodilator therapy.
B. The client's airway edema is resolving, allowing for silent, laminar airflow.
C. The client requires an immediate reduction in supplemental oxygen to prevent oxygen toxicity.
D. The client is exhibiting a "silent chest," indicating complete airway obstruction and imminent respiratory arrest.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer & Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D

**Rationale:** 
In the context of severe status asthmaticus, loud wheezing indicates that air is forcefully moving through drastically narrowed airways. If the client suddenly stops wheezing and breath sounds become inaudible despite continued or increased respiratory effort (often accompanied by a decreased level of consciousness), this is known as a "silent chest." It is a catastrophic sign representing a "failure to rescue" if missed. It indicates that the airways are so severely constricted and occluded with mucus plugs that there is virtually zero air movement occurring. The client's sudden drowsiness and decreased respiratory rate signal profound exhaustion and hypercapnia. Immediate intubation and mechanical ventilation are required to prevent death.

**Cognitive Level:** Analyzing
**Client Need:** Reduction of Risk Potential
</details>
### Question 361
A trauma client receiving positive-pressure mechanical ventilation suddenly exhibits a sharp drop in blood pressure to 68/40 mm Hg, a heart rate of 145 bpm, absent breath sounds on the right hemithorax, and noticeable leftward tracheal deviation. Applying the **EMERGENCY RESCUE** lens, what is the nurse's *immediate* life-saving action?

A. Initiate manual bag-valve-mask ventilation with 100% oxygen.
B. Order a stat portable chest radiograph to confirm the lung collapse.
C. Administer a rapid 1-liter IV bolus of 0.9% normal saline.
D. Prepare for an emergent needle thoracostomy on the right side.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** D. Prepare for an emergent needle thoracostomy on the right side.

**Rationale:** 
The client is exhibiting classic signs of a tension pneumothorax (absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, and hemodynamic collapse), which is a life-threatening complication often exacerbated by positive-pressure ventilation. The increased intrathoracic pressure compresses the superior vena cava, severely restricting venous return and causing obstructive shock (dropping BP, severe tachycardia). The immediate life-saving rescue action is decompression of the pleural space, typically via a needle thoracostomy (large-bore needle inserted into the 2nd intercostal space at the midclavicular line), followed by chest tube insertion. Waiting for a chest x-ray (Option B) delays life-saving treatment and is contraindicated in an unstable tension pneumothorax. Manual ventilation (Option A) and fluids (Option C) do not fix the underlying obstructive pressure causing the cardiovascular collapse.

</details>

---

### Question 362
A client with severe Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) is mechanically ventilated with an FiO2 of 0.80 and PEEP of 18 cm H2O. Current hemodynamics reveal a sudden drop in Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) from 72 to 52 mm Hg, while the Central Venous Pressure (CVP) has acutely risen from 8 to 19 mm Hg. ABGs show a PaO2 of 48 mm Hg. Applying the **ADVANCED DATA ANALYSIS** lens, what is the *best* interpretation of this clinical data?

A. The high PEEP is causing severe right ventricular strain (acute cor pulmonale) and decreasing left ventricular preload.
B. The client's ARDS is actively resolving, as indicated by the elevated CVP reflecting optimized fluid status.
C. The client has developed a systemic inflammatory response syndrome (SIRS), requiring immediate broad-spectrum antibiotics.
D. The oxygenation failure is driving a compensatory respiratory alkalosis, leading to peripheral vasodilation.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. The high PEEP is causing severe right ventricular strain (acute cor pulmonale) and decreasing left ventricular preload.

**Rationale:** 
In severe ARDS, high levels of Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) are often required to recruit alveoli and improve oxygenation. However, high PEEP significantly increases intrathoracic pressure and pulmonary vascular resistance. This creates a massive afterload for the right ventricle, potentially leading to acute cor pulmonale (right ventricular failure), evidenced here by the sudden spike in Central Venous Pressure (CVP to 19 mm Hg). Because the right ventricle cannot pump effectively through the compressed pulmonary beds, left ventricular preload drastically falls, causing a dangerous drop in cardiac output and Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP to 52 mm Hg). This represents a severe hemodynamic complication of mechanical ventilation in ARDS, requiring immediate intervention (such as vasopressors/inotropes or adjusting the PEEP), not a resolving condition (Option B) or simply a systemic infection (Option C).

</details>

---

### Question 363
A 26-year-old client is admitted to the progressive care unit with status asthmaticus. The client is receiving continuous nebulized albuterol and intravenous methylprednisolone. Applying the **RISK OF DETERIORATION** lens, which assessment finding represents the *earliest, most subtle* indicator that the client is experiencing a "failure to rescue" and requires impending intubation?

A. The client becomes increasingly agitated and attempts to pull off the oxygen mask.
B. Auscultation reveals a sudden "silent chest" with no audible wheezing or breath sounds.
C. An arterial blood gas reveals the PaCO2 has shifted from 28 mm Hg to 42 mm Hg.
D. The client demonstrates a pulsus paradoxus of 15 mm Hg during inspiration.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** C. An arterial blood gas reveals the PaCO2 has shifted from 28 mm Hg to 42 mm Hg.

**Rationale:** 
During the early stages of a severe asthma attack, the client hyperventilates to compensate for hypoxemia, typically resulting in respiratory alkalosis and a low PaCO2 (e.g., 28 mm Hg). If the client continues to struggle to breathe and their PaCO2 begins to rise into the "normal" range (35-45 mm Hg), this is a highly ominous, subtle sign of respiratory muscle fatigue and impending respiratory failure. The client is no longer able to maintain the work of breathing required to blow off CO2. A "silent chest" (Option B) is a very late and overt sign of complete airway obstruction, not an early or subtle sign. Agitation (Option A) and pulsus paradoxus (Option D) are expected signs of severe asthma, but a "normalizing" PaCO2 in the face of ongoing respiratory distress is the earliest definitive warning of systemic deterioration requiring mechanical ventilation.

</details>

---

### Question 364
A client is diagnosed with a massive pulmonary embolism and progresses to hemodynamic collapse with a blood pressure of 70/40 mm Hg. The critical care physician orders systemic Alteplase (tPA). The client is currently receiving a continuous IV Heparin infusion at 18 units/kg/hr. Applying the **HIGH-ALERT PHARMACOLOGY** lens, which action regarding the pharmacologic management must the nurse take?

A. Administer the Alteplase through the same IV line as the Heparin to potentiate clot lysis.
B. Stop the continuous Heparin infusion immediately prior to initiating the Alteplase.
C. Continue the Heparin infusion at the current rate and infuse Alteplase through a separate peripheral line.
D. Administer a reversal dose of protamine sulfate 15 minutes before starting the Alteplase.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** B. Stop the continuous Heparin infusion immediately prior to initiating the Alteplase.

**Rationale:** 
Systemic thrombolytics like Alteplase (tPA) carry a massive risk for catastrophic hemorrhage. When tPA is ordered for a massive pulmonary embolism causing hemodynamic instability, concurrent anticoagulation with unfractionated Heparin must be temporarily suspended to minimize bleeding risks. Heparin and tPA should never run simultaneously during the initial thrombolytic infusion. Heparin is typically restarted without a bolus after the tPA infusion is completed and the aPTT falls below a specific threshold. Administering protamine sulfate (Option D) is generally unnecessary and contraindicated unless severe, life-threatening bleeding from Heparin specifically has already occurred, as the client still requires anticoagulation post-tPA to prevent further emboli. 

</details>

### Question 365
The critical care team is preparing to manually prone a highly unstable client intubated for severe ARDS. The client is receiving a continuous cisatracurium (Nimbex) infusion for therapeutic paralysis. The RN is delegating tasks to an experienced Licensed Practical Nurse (LPN/LVN) and two Unlicensed Assistive Personnel (UAP). Applying the **COMPLEX DELEGATION** lens, which task is UNSAFE to delegate and MUST be performed by the RN or Respiratory Therapist?

A. Securing and managing the endotracheal tube at the head of the bed during the turning maneuver.
B. Applying hydrocolloid protective dressings to the client's forehead, cheeks, and chest prior to the turn.
C. Disconnecting and temporarily capping the continuous enteral feeding tube before the procedure.
D. Placing the specialized positioning pillows beneath the client's shins and shoulders after proning.

<details>
<summary><strong>View Answer and Rationale</strong></summary>

**Correct Answer:** A. Securing and managing the endotracheal tube at the head of the bed during the turning maneuver.

**Rationale:** 
Proning a client with severe ARDS who is pharmacologically paralyzed is a high-risk procedure. The most critical risk during the manual turn is accidental extubation or loss of the airway. Because the client is paralyzed and heavily sedated, losing the airway would result in immediate, profound hypoxemia and potential cardiac arrest. Securing, supporting, and managing the endotracheal tube requires advanced assessment, specialized airway management skills, and clinical judgment that falls solely within the scope of practice of the RN, Respiratory Therapist, or critical care provider. Applying protective dressings (Option B), pausing enteral feedings (Option C), and placing positioning pillows (Option D) are routine tasks that can be safely delegated to an LPN/LVN or UAP under the direction of the RN.

</details>
